# Rudolf Bernet, *La vie du sujet: Recherches sur l'interprétation de Husserl dans la phénoménologie* — Commentary

References are to the pagination of the Presses Universitaires de France edition (Épiméthée, 1994). Where Bernet cites Fink's *Sixth Cartesian Meditation* or Heidegger's *Sein und Zeit*, the page numbers are those of the German texts, and this is noted.

Five confusions beset this book and are guarded against throughout. First, the double life of the subject is not two subjects: constituting consciousness and phenomenologizing spectator are one transcendental subject living in two ways, and splitting them into a pair of egos dissolves the thesis. Second, Bernet's extended expositions of Heidegger, Derrida, Henry and Merleau-Ponty are not endorsements; his recurring move is to show the critic standing on ground Husserl had already prepared, so their objections must not be read off as his conclusions. Third, Fink's *Sixth Cartesian Meditation* serves as an authorized presentation of Husserl, but Husserl's marginalia mark precisely where Fink hardens the split into dualism — Fink's abyss is not Husserl's. Fourth, the reduction at issue is ontological rather than epistemological: it discloses the being of the world and the being of the subject instead of isolating a residue of consciousness, and Heidegger's charge that Husserl neglects the being of consciousness is what the book contests. Fifth, "life" here is neither biological nor a principle of *Lebensphilosophie*; it names the temporal, intentional accomplishing of subjectivity, and "transcendence within immanence" is the formula Bernet defends against both Henry's pure immanence and Merleau-Ponty's pure transcendence.

## Foreword

Phenomenology stands accused of clinging to the subject after structuralism, deconstruction and postmodernism had disposed of it; a rival industry shows how much those movements still owe phenomenology (pp. 1–2). Bernet declines both tasks. The era of inter-school polemic is closed, and what outlasts it are the questions the polemic left unpaid — the subject among them. The critiques were aimed at a Cartesian or Kantian subjectivity, or at what a hurried reader retained of it, so phenomenology was condemned in the name of a conception of the subject it had itself set out to overcome. The concession that follows is the real position: phenomenology was not sheltered from the crisis of modern subjectivity, but it looked for answers rather than issuing verdicts on the subject's death.

The hasty critique is not confined to phenomenology's opponents (p. 2). Heidegger accentuates the Cartesian features of Husserlian transcendental consciousness so exclusively that his critique is not merely partly unjust but conceals what Dasein still owes to Husserl's conception of transcendental life. Sartre attacks the egological subject while resting on a position Husserl had himself defended and then abandoned, with reasons given at length. Derrida's minute analysis of the tensions in Husserl's account of the subject's self-manifestation and its dependence on the empirical world does not always avoid abusive simplification. Merleau-Ponty and Henry, closer to Husserl's implicit aspirations, press transcendence and immanence respectively until they endanger what was most original in a subjectivity understood as "transcendence within immanence". That formula is the criterion by which all four judgments have just been made, and it will govern the book.

Since phenomenology's entire history has been nothing but the interrogation of modern subjectivity, the charge that it neglected that question collapses; anyone who thinks the question of the subject's life is not exhausted will retrace the road backwards, to Husserl (pp. 2–3). Husserl became a phenomenologist precisely by turning from the subject as thinking substance, as omnipotent creator of the world, and as formal principle of a purely spiritual life. The flesh of the perceiving subject, the intentional bond to the world, the encounter with other subjects took priority early. Intentionality guided the exploration but was itself transformed by having to accommodate both the intimacy of impressional affects and the exteriority of symbolic systems — sign language, cultural inheritance, the pregiven order of nature. Temporality and passive genesis were decisive in that transformation. What emerges is not only a subject always in becoming but a mode of individuation that does not exclude self-transcending, self-loss, and desire of the infinite. Finitude, splitting and difference stamp a manifestation in which the self becomes phenomenon while refusing direct, immediate, objective perception. The subject's life is an effort of appropriation still answerable to a distant *telos* and never abolishing self-distance; the reduction that brings subjectivity to light therefore reveals its duplicity as much as its unity, its symbolic surroundings as much as its uniqueness, its facticity as much as its attraction to the supratemporal.

Collecting the essays, rather more than half of them previously published, Bernet was struck by their shared preoccupation with the subject, which was surprising given that most were written for specific occasions and external requests (pp. 3–4). He resisted the temptation to standardize them into a single system, refusing to conceal that the writing spread over nearly a decade during which his attitude to Husserl shifted. What held constant was the option of approaching Husserl critically — that is, from what his successors had made intelligible. This is the book's method stated plainly: the reading is retrospective by design, not by accident. Each of the three chapters is arranged to give first a relatively technical exposition of Husserl's position and then a freer, more personal interpretation of selected aspects, while Introduction and Conclusion address the underlying understanding of the subject's life directly.

The acknowledgments record a debt to the community of thinkers who claim Husserlian phenomenology in one way or another, encouragement and friendship as much as instruction, thanks to the journals and collective volumes that published earlier versions, particular thanks to Jean-Luc Marion for the invitation to collect the essays for Épiméthée, and a dedication (p. 4). The genre is thereby fixed: a collection, gathered under a series whose director is himself one of the book's interlocutors.

## Introduction — The Phenomenological Reduction and the Subject's Double Life

After a long purgatory the problem of the reduction again occupies the front of the phenomenological stage, above all in France — Marion, Henry, Courtine (pp. 5–6). Three points of agreement organize the field. Everyone treats the reduction as a leading-back (*Rückführung*) of philosophical thought to phenomena; but the phenomena in question are those whose appearing requires the reduction itself, so that the reduction phenomenalizes what would otherwise never have become a phenomenon at all, and in waking dormant phenomena it appeals to the subject's vigilance. Everyone agrees, second, that the reduction is unthinkable without a subject able to receive the givenness it first opens. And third, phenomenological phenomena are not merely covered over by those of ordinary life but constitute the ground from which ordinary phenomena receive their signification, so the datum the reduction offers founds every other givenness. Beyond these three the agreement ends: what the phenomenological phenomena show, what form of appearing belongs to them, and how they differ from and connect with ordinary phenomena are all disputed.

What appears through the reduction? (pp. 6–7.) For Husserl, transcendental subjectivity as constituting the world — which makes both the world as constituted and that subjectivity as constituting into phenomena, so that the reduction lights up the transcendental correlation between the being of the constituting subject and the being of the constituted world, while showing that this constitution was already at work in natural life without appearing. For Heidegger too what appears is being: of tools, of the surrounding world, of Dasein in its modes of existence, finally the sense of being in general. But the appearing no longer answers to a detached gaze; it is accomplished within Dasein's factical existence, and from his earliest phenomenological writings Heidegger asks after the phenomenality of these phenomena. Marion attempts to pass beyond both the Husserlian transcendental and the Heideggerian existential conception, to make appear the gift of giving oneself or withdrawing from the claim of the call — a gift addressed to the *interloqué*, accomplished whenever there is a call whatever its content, opening a field of givenness wider than either the transcendental correlation or the ontological phenomenon.

The question of what appears has received explicit answers across phenomenology's history, even if they differ and the nature of their difference is far from obvious (p. 7). Henry is right that the question of what "appearing phenomenologically" means has been neglected, and right about the reason: phenomenologists thought they knew the answer, assuming that all appearing necessarily takes the form of intentionality or ecstatic transcendence. Against the presupposition that every givenness is an affection of the subject's immanence by something exceeding it, Henry demands room for the duplicity of appearing and the recognition that the appearing of transcendence is a derived form: affection by the world, and so by any being, would never occur unless that ecstatic affection auto-affected itself in Life, which is nothing but this primitive auto-affection.

Bernet will not adjudicate Marion's and Henry's reductions but keeps them in view for his own rereading of Husserl and Heidegger (pp. 7–8). He does not aim to pass beyond the ontological phenomenon toward a givenness "otherwise than being", in Levinas's and Marion's manner, nor to interrogate the ambiguities in the identity of being and appearing claimed by both Husserl and Heidegger. His design does meet Henry's "duplicity of appearing" at one point: he wants to exhibit the double life of the transcendental subject and of Dasein as it shows itself in a reduction that turns out to pursue a double objective. The reduction makes a subject appear that both holds to the world and turns away from it, and more precisely makes the transcendental subject or Dasein appear as leading a double life and therefore appearing to itself in two ways. The scission the reduction introduces thus falls not between rational phenomenological phenomena and irrational ordinary ones but within the phenomenological phenomenon itself — that is, within the appearing of the transcendental subject or of Dasein.

Husserl accounts for this duplicity by distinguishing the subject that constitutes the world from the subject that attends that constitution as "impartial spectator": the first is what the reduction gives to be seen, the second is the agent who performs it (pp. 8–9). The first appears to itself through the work it accomplishes in and for the world; the second appears to itself immediately, outside all reference to the world. One and the same transcendental subject in both cases. In Heidegger the same scission takes the form of two modes of Dasein's existence: inauthentically, Dasein occupies itself with the practical affairs of its familiar world; authentically, it is brought to care about its own being. The reduction accordingly reveals first the readiness-to-hand of tools and the significance of the familiar world, then Dasein's own being as this appears when that significance collapses. Across the two moments Dasein appears to itself differently and as different — implicated in the world and exiled from it, in self-loss and in a return to self closely resembling auto-affection. It remains the same Dasein, whose ontological lot is precisely that it cannot escape this ambiguity of its existence.

Assigning the reduction the same task does not make Husserl and Heidegger say the same thing (p. 9). The modes in which transcendental subject and Dasein appear differ fundamentally, and so does the analysis of the relation that binds and separates the two modes of that appearing. The comparison to follow is thus not a demonstration of identity; it establishes a shared structure in order to locate the real difference precisely.

### Husserl

Using a text written by Fink to show how the Husserlian reduction reveals a double transcendental life may surprise (pp. 9–10). The *Sixth Cartesian Meditation* was drafted in 1932 at Husserl's request, as a sequel to a reworked version of the *Cartesian Meditations*, when Fink was Husserl's private assistant and their collaboration at its closest. Husserl followed its genesis, reread it attentively several times, and annotated it copiously, especially where he wished to mark disagreement with Fink's presentation of his thought. Taking those critical remarks into account is what licenses treating the remainder as an authorized presentation of Husserl — a methodological warrant, not a claim that Fink and Husserl coincide.

Fink's text, titled "The idea of a transcendental doctrine of method", is devoted above all to bringing out the ontological import of the Husserlian reduction, which is meant to make appear the difference between the "being" of the world and the "pre-being" (*Vor-Sein*) of transcendental consciousness (p. 10). Since that consciousness is both the object appearing in the reduction and the subject performing it, the ontological difference extends into the transcendental subject itself. Fink even holds that the difference is deepest at the transcendental level: between constituting subject and constituted world there reigns an analogy of being, whereas constituting subject and phenomenological spectator are separated by a profound abyss, an opposition of being. The strongest formulation of the split is Fink's, and this is the point at which Husserl will demur.

#### The Being of the World and the Being of Constituting Consciousness

For Husserl and Fink alike, both the reduction and the non-worldly phenomenon it brings to appearance take their support from the world (p. 11). The reduction makes constituting consciousness appear outside the world, but it equally makes the world appear as constituted — the being of the world from its origin in the transcendental subject. The two beings are different but not indifferent to one another. Dislodging consciousness from the world alters nothing in the fact that this consciousness is toward-the-world: since its being consists in occupying itself with the world, it is naturally led to integrate itself into the world as a mundane or "empirical" subject. That fascination explains why in everyday or "natural" life the transcendental subject perceives itself as part of the world and forgets the ontological difference obtaining between itself and the world. The difference appears only through the artificial procedure of the reduction, which introduces a new mode of transcendental life in which the subject is no longer implicated in constituting. The "phenomenologizing spectator", though separated from the world and abstaining from any part in its constitution, still does the world honour by letting it appear as constituted — and can enter into action only on the basis of a constitution already accomplished, a world "pregiven" to it. The spectator is therefore parasitic on the constitution it observes, which forecloses any reading of it as a worldless first principle.

The *Sixth Meditation* asks at length what motivates a subject to effect so radical a change of life, and finds no motive compelling (pp. 11–12). No difficulty met with in natural life, no desire for clarification, no will to do scientific work has the power to extract a human being from immersion in the natural life accomplished within the world. As Husserl puts it in a marginal note, whoever tears himself from his fixation on the world, who transcends (*übersteigen*) "his natural self, his [empirical] being-human", performs a "leap" (*Sprung*). Even though phenomenology is a science, entry into phenomenology must therefore count as an act of faith devoid of all foresight (*Vor-Sicht*). The motivation problem is not solved but relocated: the reduction has no ground within natural life, which is why it appears as a discontinuity rather than a conclusion.

The leap is a leap into the reduction and out of the world (p. 12). The same transcendental subject is found on either side of it, yet in the course of the leap the subject splits into constituting subject and spectator subject. Leaping out of world-constitution, the subject installs itself in the position of the onlooker who observes constitution without taking part (*unbeteiligter Zuschauer*). By creating such a spectator, the reduction makes constituting subject and constituted world appear otherwise than in natural life, and makes their difference appear: it strips the constituting subject of its empirical apperception and exhibits the constituted world as the universal ground of natural life. Only by attaching to itself such a spectator as its "exponent" can constituting consciousness appear to itself as different from the world it constitutes. For the correlational unity proper to constitution and the difference of being between transcendental consciousness and world to appear at all, the transcendental subject must split in two and become both at once.

Husserl's claim that the reduction shows how, in the course of natural life, transcendental consciousness constitutes the world without noticing, looks very much like an act of reflection — as though the phenomenological phenomenon were simply a reflective view of the natural one (pp. 12–13). Closer inspection shows that the passage from natural to phenomenological phenomenon is the work of a reflection, or "thematization", of a quite particular kind. It neither sets the constituting subject before the gaze by separating it from the constituted object, nor makes constituting consciousness the object of a second constituting consciousness. It brings in the gaze of a spectator who supervises the work of constitution without participating in it. Two familiar models of reflection are thereby ruled out at once: the subject-object model and the higher-order-consciousness model.

The reduction is thus far more than a reflective doubling of consciousness, since it introduces a genuine scission (p. 13). The scission concerns not only the relation between constituting consciousness and constituted world but transcendental consciousness itself, as at once agent and spectator of world-constitution. What appears for the first time thanks to the spectator is the ontological difference between constituting consciousness and constituted world — the difference natural life misrecognizes and which the subject-world correlation covers over from within the labour of constitution. The reduction's aim is accordingly ontological. To "reflect" as a phenomenologist on the work of world-constitution is not merely to rouse transcendental consciousness from its worldly torpor; it is above all to make the ontological difference appear.

How then is the sense of this difference between the being of constituting consciousness and the being of the constituted world to be understood, in what horizon does it give itself, in what perspective must the spectator stand to perceive it? (pp. 13–14.) On this the *Sixth Meditation* is strangely mute. It contents itself with a purely conceptual answer, distinguishing the "being" (*Sein*) of the world from the "pre-being" (*Vor-Sein*) of transcendental consciousness. Even granting a certain "affinity" or even "analogy" between the two forms of being, the transcendental entity and the mundane entity do not fall under a single general concept of being. The complaint is that a distinction has been named rather than phenomenologically exhibited.

Where Fink disappoints on the difference between the being of the world and the being of constituting consciousness, he is far more explicit on the "opposition of being" (*Seinsgegensatz*) obtaining within the transcendental subject's pre-being and separating the phenomenological spectator from constituting consciousness (p. 14). Numerous marginal notes in Husserl's hand register his deep unease at the way Fink hardens the "division" (*Entzweiung*) and the "dualism of transcendental life" and accentuates the "abyss" (*Kluft*) between spectator and constituting consciousness. Without contesting a certain duality within transcendental life, Husserl doubts — with reason, on Bernet's view — that the spectator of world-constitution has lost all interest in the world and ceased entirely to refer to it. The same attention to the phenomenological elucidation of the world's being leads Husserl to stress a further ontological difference passed over in silence by Fink: that between the being of the things belonging to the world and the being of the world itself. This second difference will occupy Bernet's own chapter on the world (pp. 93–118).

#### The Being of Constituting Consciousness and the Being of the Phenomenological Spectator

The *Sixth Meditation* reaches its greatest speculative force in the analysis of transcendental consciousness's double life (pp. 14–15). The split (*Spaltung*) of the transcendental subject follows directly from the reduction: in the universal epoché the phenomenological spectator produces itself, separating itself from constituting consciousness in order to make the latter appear as its counterpart. But the spectator, self-produced, does not produce the constituting consciousness it brings to light: transcendental consciousness constitutes the world long before and independently of any reduction. Before the reduction it is too deeply buried in the world and too riveted to worldly things for its own constitutive activity to reveal itself to it; in natural life it lives in ignorance of itself, and only the spectator's intervention ends that blindness. There is, by contrast, no phenomenological spectator that would be ignorant of itself while working anonymously in natural life — the spectator does not exist before the reduction. Presiding over the radical change by which constituting consciousness comes to recognize itself, the spectator remains identical to itself, the impassive (*unbeteiligt*) onlooker of world-constitution. Its work is to extract transcendental consciousness from the world, to "strike out" its self-apperception as mundane subject, and to reveal the ontological difference between the pre-being of constituting consciousness and the being of the world it constitutes. The asymmetry established here is decisive: one term pre-exists and is blind, the other is born of the reduction and is lucid.

That the spectator is what makes constituting consciousness appear entails that the self-relation (*Selbstbezug*) of constituting consciousness is marked by heterogeneity or alterity (pp. 15–16). Its relation to itself passes through a different self — the spectator, another self of the same transcendental subject. Being wholly turned toward the world and mired in it prevents constituting consciousness from appearing to itself immediately. The spectator, by contrast, does possess an immediate self-consciousness, though this remains implicit as long as it goes on observing world-constitution instead of looking at itself. Hence self-recognition is accomplished in homogeneity for the spectator and requires a form of alterity for constituting consciousness. Constituting consciousness needs the spectator in order to appear to itself; the spectator depends on nothing but itself to reflect on its own activity as spectator. At the level of the spectator, that is, of the phenomenologist's own activity, reflection is iterable within homogeneity; between world-constituting consciousness and the spectator that brings it to self-recognition there is heterogeneity. This is where Bernet's later engagement with Derrida is prepared: self-presence is secured only for the spectator, and only by a detour through another self for the consciousness that matters.

How then are the "heterogeneity", "difference", "abyss" and "scission" within transcendental life to be understood, and what is the sense of the *Seinsgegensatz* between two modes of being belonging to the same transcendental subject? (p. 16.) Fink's answer, endlessly repeated, is that what distinguishes the spectator's life from every other form of life is above all the absence of any interest in the world. The spectator lacks the "tendency toward being" (*Seinstendenz*) and the "finality of a realization of the world and in the world" (*Finalität der Weltverwirklichung*) that inhabit every constituting consciousness, whether functioning (*fungierend*) in natural life or recognized as such through the reduction. It is carried instead by a wholly other tendency, directed not at the being of the world but at the pre-being of transcendental consciousness, and notably of the consciousness that constitutes the world. Husserl's correction is the more accurate formulation: the spectator is interested in the origin of the world in transcendental consciousness, rather than uninterested in the world. Disinterest is redescribed as a different interest, which preserves the spectator's relation to the world that Fink's abyss had severed.

It is the absence of any spontaneous urge to meddle in worldly affairs that allows the phenomenologist to exhibit the being of the world in its dependence on the pre-being of constituting consciousness (pp. 16–17). But the spectator's illuminating intervention changes nothing in the fact that constituting consciousness will go on following its tendency toward being. Even under the spectator's gaze it does not renounce its work of constituting the world. Phenomenological self-elucidation (*Selbsterhellung*) of its own constitutive activity will not prevent constituting consciousness from plunging back into the world and into the self-blinding that follows. The reduction is therefore not a conversion that abolishes what it illuminates, which is why the double life is permanent rather than a stage to be passed through.

Inhabited by different interests, constituting consciousness and the spectator realize themselves in different works (p. 17). The one constitutes, orders and enriches the world; the other develops a phenomenological analysis of that uninterrupted labour of constitution. The spectator works in solitude, whereas constituting subjects naturally associate and collaborate in shaping a world common to them. Fink's text is quoted to this effect: the reduction leads the phenomenologizing subject out of the situation of an intersubjectively common relatedness (*Bezogenheit*) to intraworldly beings accessible to anyone, and exposes it to the solitude of its transcendental-egological existence; the "others" exist transcendentally as constituting monads with which the ego stands in a community of constitution, but not in a community of transcendental self-knowledge (*Selbsterkenntnis*).

There is, then, a solipsism of the transcendental subject in Husserl, but it does not concern the subject that constitutes the world, as a hasty reading of the *Cartesian Meditations* might suggest (p. 17). Constitution of the world is communal work and the being of the constituted world is an intersubjectively common being. What is solitary is the evidence: this common being of the constituted world is revealed only in the solitary evidence of a transcendental subject that, by way of the phenomenological spectator, has become conscious of its individual contribution to the communal work of constitution. The standard charge of solipsism is thus relocated from constitution to self-knowledge, where it is not a defect but a structural feature.

The divergence of interests and works must not obscure that this opposition arises within one and the same transcendental subject (pp. 17–18). Hence "the unity of transcendental life" is an identity in difference, an opposition within what remains equal to itself (*Sichselbstgleichbleiben*). The transcendental subject put into play by the reduction is therefore never merely constituting consciousness nor merely phenomenologizing spectator. To recognize itself as constituting, the consciousness engaged in constituting the world needs the spectator; but the spectator's "non-participation" (*Unbeteiligtheit*) is possible only if what it does not participate in — world-constitution — takes place. Being neither one alone, it is necessarily both. It emerges from the world to appear under the phenomenologist's gaze while remaining immersed in that world. Its identity is a composite unity: it is itself only by being itself in several ways; when it knows itself it misrecognizes itself at the same time; when it appears it appears under only one of its two faces; in appearing, it simultaneously disappears. This last formula states the book's thesis in its most compressed form and will be reclaimed at the end of the Introduction for Dasein as well.

### Heidegger

Husserl reproached existential phenomenology, and Heidegger above all, with having abandoned the reduction for want of understanding it, and most interpreters of both accepted and repeated the verdict (pp. 18–19). Their astonishment in 1975 is therefore understandable, when a course delivered by Heidegger in the very year *Sein und Zeit* appeared presented "the phenomenological reduction" as a fundamental piece of phenomenological method. What is piquant is that the avowal comes with an immediate reproach. For Husserl, as Heidegger reports him, the reduction leads the phenomenological gaze back from the natural attitude of the human being who lives unquestioningly among things and persons to the life of transcendental consciousness and its noetic-noematic lived experiences, in which objects are constituted as correlates of consciousness. For Heidegger, it leads the gaze back from any grasp whatever of a being to the understanding of the being of that being, projected upon the mode of its unconcealment (*Unverborgenheit*).

Bernet's aim is not only to show that such a reduction is indeed at work in *Sein und Zeit* but that it does not differ so greatly from the Husserlian presentation — at least not from the one found in the *Sixth Meditation* (p. 19). For Husserl and Fink the reduction has the task of bringing to light the "transcendental" correlation between constituting consciousness and constituted world, and exhibiting that correlation includes showing that it was already at work in the anonymity of "natural" life without natural life noticing. That gives no warrant for Heidegger's tireless claim, stated flatly in the 1925 course, that Husserl's primary question is not that of the being-character of consciousness. On the contrary: by revealing the correlation between constituting consciousness and constituted world, the reduction makes precisely the (pre-)being of that consciousness and the being of that world appear, along with the difference between the two forms of being.

For Heidegger, natural or everyday life takes above all the form of the circumspective concern of a Dasein turned toward the practical use of the tools (*Zeuge*) belonging to a familiar surrounding world (*vertraute Umwelt*) (pp. 19–20). In everyday life Dasein attends neither to the way tools refer to the world nor to its own concerned existence and the way that existence inserts itself into the world by ordering it. Only a first reduction reveals, through a certain malfunction of natural life, the correlation between Dasein's (inauthentic) existence and the familiar world to which it relates. Exactly as in Husserl and Fink, this first reduction, by making the intraworldly being of tools appear in its relation to the being-in-the-world of concerned Dasein, reveals the hidden being of the world and of Dasein, their difference and their bond. Insisting on the fundamentally practical, or rather poietic, character of Dasein's existence and on the entanglement of Dasein and world, Heidegger nonetheless avoids speaking any longer of an activity of "constitution" on the part of a "transcendental" subject. The vocabulary changes; the structure does not.

Husserl and Fink had taken a second step in clarifying the being of the transcendental subject by holding that its life is not confined to constitutive activity (p. 20). Making the hidden work of world-constitution appear, the reduction thereby introduced a scission within that subject, which was at once constituting subject and observer of its own constituting. Unlike the constituting subject, the spectator did not pre-exist in natural life at all: it was a new transcendental subject existing only in and through the reduction. Unlike constituting consciousness it manifested no particular interest in the world, and unlike constituting consciousness, which often operates within natural life without noticing, the spectator always knows very well what it is doing.

In what may be called a second reduction, Heidegger reaches similar conclusions (pp. 20–21). In the experience of anxiety Dasein becomes aware of how derisory and futile its incessant concern with intraworldly things can be. In anxiety and above all in the call of conscience, Dasein discovers itself divided between its flight into the current affairs of worldly life — what Pascal called *divertissement* — and the care it keeps for its own potentiality-for-being. Anxiety cannot abolish Dasein's being-in-the-world or efface the world as such, yet the concerns by which Dasein habitually inserts itself into a familiar, anonymous and common world are bracketed. Like the reduction in Husserl and Fink, anxiety brings it about that Dasein, finding itself face to face and alone with itself, encounters for the first time not only a new self but the phenomenon of its own being.

The differences between the two conceptions are nonetheless real, if less radical than Husserl and Heidegger believed (pp. 21–22). In the *Sixth Meditation* the phenomenological questioning of the phenomenon of being is far more advanced than in *Ideen I*, the text at which Heidegger's critique — most fully developed in the 1925 course — was directed. But even this late text of Husserl and Fink, though it does interrogate the being of transcendental consciousness, still assumes that this being appears in the form of an intentional object, however higher-order. To appear is to appear before an intentional consciousness: whether it is the constituting consciousness that appears and disappears, and in appearing makes its difference from both world and spectator appear, or the spectator that makes constituting consciousness appear and needs nothing but itself in order to appear in turn. When Heidegger speaks of the "transcendence" characterizing Dasein's "being-in-the-world" he does much more than rename Husserlian intentionality: he inaugurates a new interrogation of the phenomenality of the phenomenon. Bernet defers his own treatment of that critique to the first chapter of Part I (pp. 53–59).

#### The Being of the Ready-to-Hand and the Being of Concerned Dasein (Inauthentic Existence)

What Heidegger says of everyday life corresponds fairly well to what Husserl meant by the "natural attitude" (p. 22). For Husserl this natural life is entirely turned toward things whose sense is above all practical and which refer the subject endlessly from one thing to the next. Other men and women appear in terms of their influence on things, without being confused with things. If things bear a sense answering to the natural subject's practical aspirations, then natural relations between persons take the form of interaction or collaboration: in natural life subjects meet and quarrel over particular things against the background of a "life-world" (*Lebenswelt*) that is either common or foreign.

*Sein und Zeit* develops and illustrates this Husserlian conception of natural life without adding much that is genuinely new (pp. 22–23). The distinction between things appearing as ready-to-hand (*zuhanden*) and as present-at-hand (*vorhanden*) was already sketched in *Ideen II*, whose manuscript Heidegger consulted from 1925; his contribution is to reinforce the practical character of natural life by presenting presence-at-hand (*Vorhandenheit*) as a "deficient" form of readiness-to-hand (*Zuhandenheit*). What Husserl called, in a visual metaphor, "horizon", Heidegger calls straightaway "world", more precisely "surrounding world" (*Umwelt*) — itself not free of visual connotation, as the paired term "circumspection" (*Umsicht*) attests. Like Husserl's horizon, this surrounding world is never taken into account for its own sake in everyday life, even though it underlies and makes possible circumspective concern with things; it is the frame of the manifold references (*Verweisungen*) woven among intraworldly things. As the condition of the appearing and understanding of the practical sense of things, it differs radically from intraworldly things and even from any collection of them. Husserl too had allowed for the difference between things and world before *Sein und Zeit* appeared, though he did not at once recognize its importance — the topic of Bernet's third chapter (pp. 93–118).

These similarities would deserve fuller specification, but they suffice to show that Heidegger's chief innovation does not lie on the side of the description of natural, everyday and ordinary life (p. 23). Should the original contribution then be sought in his having been the first to interrogate the being of intraworldly things from the being of concerned Dasein, stressing the difference between the two modes of being? Heidegger certainly wanted us to believe so. But in Husserl and Fink as well, the phenomenological examination of the constitutive correlation between transcendental subject and intraworldly things issued in a similar result. The question of Heidegger's originality is thus pushed one stage further back, and answered in the next unit.

The radical difference concerns less the ontological concern to understand the difference and the bond between the sense of being of intraworldly things and the sense of being of Dasein than the phenomenological *access* to the revelation of these modes of being — that is, the concept of the reduction and the manner of its performance (p. 24). For Husserl and Fink the access is a "leap" (*Sprung*), but a leap attempted and risked by a subject that wants to know more, wants to settle the matter, wants to pronounce in the name of scientific evidence. Nothing of the sort in Heidegger, where the revelation of being is a gift offered in an extraordinary givenness (*Gegebenheit*). Concerned Dasein receives the revelation of the readiness-to-hand of tools; Dasein is overwhelmed by the sudden illumination of its own being. If there are two reductions in Heidegger, one performed within inauthentic existence and the other giving access to an authentic understanding of Dasein's being, both are the result of an event that imposes itself on Dasein unexpectedly and in spite of it. In both cases, too, being appears against a ground of absence. Will against undergoing, and evidence against event: this is the difference the comparison was designed to isolate.

The first reduction concerns the appearing of the readiness-to-hand of tools within the circumspective concerns characterizing Dasein's everyday existence (pp. 24–25). In *Sein und Zeit* — cited throughout by the German pagination — the movement of such a reduction is begun at least three times: at the referential assignment (*Verweisung*) constitutive of the being of tools (§§16–17); at the spatiality of a tool occupying a determinate place within a region (*Gegend*) (§22); and at being-with (*Mitsein*), which for concerned Dasein takes the form of an interaction among subjects conforming to the implicit norms of the anonymous community Heidegger calls the "they" (*das Man*) (§§26–27). All three moments make the being of tools and the being of concerned Dasein appear against a ground of absence: the being of a thing is revealed when the thing fails, and being-with others appears most clearly when others go missing. Only by absenting themselves do beings manifest their being.

The sense of being of what is ready-to-hand is not exhausted in serving an occasional user in the realization of some particular work; the tool's serviceability (*Dienlichkeit*) draws its sense first of all from a whole complex network of a priori or essential references, whose dimensions Heidegger patiently dismantles (pp. 25–26). All these references fit into a totality of references Heidegger calls "world", more precisely "surrounding world". The world is not the sum of the references but their frame — what allows concerned Dasein to let itself be referred from one thing to another without ever getting lost. Dasein can understand a thing's practical significance, what it is involved in (*Bewandtnis*), and can let itself be guided by it (*Bewendenlassen*), only on condition of having first become familiar with the significance (*Bedeutsamkeit*) of the world to which the thing belongs. Familiarity (*Vertrautheit*) with the world, often purely implicit, is the major presupposition of the good use of the referential network that constitutes the being of ready-to-hand things. Good use consists in letting oneself be carried along by the references without stopping at them, so that forgetting the references, forgetting the world, and finally forgetting the being of the ready-to-hand is the best guarantee of their functioning. Familiarity with an environment and effective understanding of a thing's sense consist precisely in going without saying, unnoticed. One stops only insofar as things, and more precisely their references and their sense, no longer go without saying — insofar as they suspend, in an epoché, the natural course of Dasein's concerns.

§16 distinguishes three modalities of such a "disturbance of reference" (p. 26). A thing may prove "unusable" (*unverwendbar*), so that its un-readiness-to-hand (*Unzuhandenheit*) forces itself on concerned Dasein's attention as conspicuousness (*Auffälligkeit*). A thing may also be missing at just the moment it is needed to bring a work to completion, imposing itself as obtrusiveness (*Aufdringlichkeit*). And a thing may obstruct a concern and thwart Dasein obstinately (*Aufsässigkeit*). In all three cases circumspection strikes emptiness and only then sees what the missing item was ready-to-hand for and with; the surrounding world announces itself anew. In all three, too, the being of the tool is revealed precisely because a tool escapes concerned Dasein's mastery. To appear in the truth of its being, the tool needs to take distance from a subject that wants to take it in hand, and the field of that revelation is one of *atopy*, a place at the margin of the places of the familiar world. The first reduction thus makes the being of tools and of the surrounding world appear by withdrawing them from the grip of the subject of natural life. Making the being of things appear is at the same time restoring their freedom.

No tool is safe from such malfunction, and concerned Dasein remains permanently at the mercy of a disturbance that nonetheless has the merit of making it attentive to the being of the being that fails it, namely its readiness-to-hand (pp. 26–27). Heidegger also mentions the singular case of a tool whose normal function is precisely to draw concerned Dasein's attention to the references that constitute the being of the things of practical life: the sign (§17), aptly called a "showing-tool" (*Zeigzeug*). Rather than referring to other tools, this exceptional tool excludes itself from the world of ordinary tools and refers to the crossed references with which those tools are invested. Where normal tools follow the references in order to show other tools, the sign is an abnormal tool that shows how ordinary tools refer, how their readiness-to-hand is inseparable from their referring. It is a tool that shows what a tool is, a being that shows the appearing of ready-to-hand beings, and by that fact it offers the phenomenologist an "ontological clue" for grasping the readiness-to-hand of those particular beings that tools are. The sign is thus the ready-to-hand analogue of the phenomenologizing spectator: an element of the field that exhibits the field's own structure.

Heidegger's illustration, illuminating if now obsolete, is the movable red arrow that indicates to all road users entering the same intersection the direction a car is about to take (p. 27). This direction indicator orients the conduct of a group of persons by revealing a total equipmental complex made up of means of locomotion and traffic regulations. The arrow can perform this function of showing a traffic situation involving a set of vehicles only insofar as it presents itself as a sign and not as a tool or a mere component of the vehicle-tool to which it is fixed. Arrow and wheel may both be considered detachable parts of a car, yet the sense of being of the arrow differs radically from that of a wheel. The sign shows insofar as it strikes attention, and it strikes attention insofar as it emerges from the set of utilitarian references binding wheel to vehicle, vehicle to driver, driver to other drivers, cyclists and pedestrians.

The sign therefore works like a tool that fails (pp. 27–28). It shows the references woven among things within the surrounding world because it excludes itself from that normal function, because it does not integrate itself into that world. It strikes by departing from the ordinary and shows by making itself forgotten; it presents the readiness-to-hand of things by absenting itself from their utilitarian context. Hence I can point at something with my finger only to someone who, unlike my dog, does not lapse into stupid contemplation of my index finger. For a tool to become a sign capable of showing the mode of being of the totality of the ready-to-hand within a surrounding world and its proper worldhood, it must be heard and understood by a Dasein able to give sense to absence. Absence, in short, does not disclose by itself: it discloses only for a being that can read it.

The same reasoning is repeated for the spatiality of tools (§22) and for being-with others (p. 28). A tool's spatial being — the "place" it occupies within a "region" (*Gegend*) of the world — appears most clearly to Dasein when it does not find the tool in its habitual place. One is tempted to say that the place in which the tool's place manifests itself is a non-place, an *a-topos*; in any case this revelation of the tool's spatiality is accompanied by a momentary disorientation of Dasein within its familiar world. A tool has its place in the surrounding world by virtue of its reference to other places, all situated within one region. Without familiarity with that region, concerned Dasein would be wholly lost in its everyday activities: it would never find anything, for want of knowing where to look. In ordinary life Dasein finds things without having to seek them, and if it is driven to seek its tools it is forced to interrupt its work. This existential inconvenience, damaging to the efficient management of daily concerns, is rich in ontological instruction: in seeking, Dasein discovers for the first time what it had already found without having had to seek it and without having stopped at it. Heidegger's formulation is quoted — the prior readiness-to-hand of each region possesses, in a still more original sense than the being of the ready-to-hand, the character of an inconspicuous familiarity (*unauffällige Vertrautheit*); the region's readiness-to-hand becomes visible in the mode of conspicuousness (*Auffallen*) only in a circumspective discovering of what is ready-to-hand, and more precisely in the deficient modes of concern; it is often because something is not found in its place that the region of the place first becomes expressly accessible as such.

Within this region, tools occupy places assigned to them by concerned Dasein in virtue of what Heidegger calls de-severance (*Entfernung*) and directionality (*Ausrichtung*) (§23) (p. 29). Even though the spatiality of the surrounding world thus seems concentric and polarized by a singular Dasein, this does not prevent the surrounding world from being intersubjective from the outset. The world to which the works of concerned Dasein refer is a common world rather than a shared one: a world prior to any division and any pooling of mine and yours, the world of everyone and of no one, the world of an anonymous Dasein Heidegger calls the "they" (*das Man*). The they is not a trans-individual subject but a mode of Dasein's existence dominated by conformism, commonplaces and received ideas. Even when entirely alone, concerned Dasein still acts in the mode of the they. Solitude does not necessarily challenge what Heidegger calls the "dictatorship" of the they, yet the absence of others has the peculiarity of making Dasein attentive to the fact that its existence always has the form of a being-with (*Mitsein*). Heidegger is quoted accordingly: being-with determines Dasein existentially even when no other is in fact present-at-hand or perceived; even Dasein's being-alone is being-with in the world; the other can be missing only in and for a being-with; being-alone is a deficient mode of being-with, and its possibility is the proof of being-with.

Here again it falls to an absence to make Dasein attentive to what underlies its everyday existence unnoticed (pp. 29–30). The solitude of concerned Dasein belongs among those existential inconveniences Dasein would gladly do without, but which enlighten it about the being of things and about its own being far more than a life without incident. Seeking a tool that is not in its place, Dasein finds what it neither sought nor wanted: the manifestation of the readiness-to-hand of tools and of their insertion into the space of the world. Finding itself alone, it discovers that throughout its concerned existence it had been acting, without knowing it, in terms of its being-with others. These experiences do have the value of a phenomenological reduction, but of a reduction undergone in the discomfort of disturbance. Far from willing this reduction and its train of revelations, Dasein would rather avoid it, and hastens to repair the "disturbance" (*Störung*) to which it owes the discovery of the being of the familiar world and of its entanglement with Dasein's concerned existence. This avoidance is explained by the price of the ontological revelation: the loss of the usability of tools and of the smooth functioning of that Dasein's concerns.

#### The Being of the World as World and Dasein's Being-in-the-World (Authentic Existence)

The first reduction shows the worldly mode of being of the tool and the mode of being of concerned Dasein's communal existence to a Dasein that continues to exist in the form of concern, even disturbed concern (pp. 30–31). The second reduction, accomplished in anxiety (§40), owes nothing either to concern or to its disturbance: the opening onto being effected by anxiety leaves the natural frame of everyday life altogether. Courtine was among the first to establish that the analysis of anxiety in *Sein und Zeit* constitutes the "repetition" of the Husserlian problematic of the epoché and the transcendental-phenomenological reduction, and Bernet takes up that identification here. This does not mean that in anxiety Dasein is deprived of all relation to everyday life and to the ontological lessons of its disturbance. If in becoming anxious Dasein undergoes a revelation of its own being, it is no less true that in this revelation its concerned and inauthentic existence also appears in a new light — as flight before the truth. The revealed futility of everyday existence goes together with the collapse of the familiarity of the surrounding world in which that inauthentic existence is accomplished. Anxiety thereby reveals to Dasein what a world is that is no longer the familiar world of ready-to-hand things and from which that familiar world nevertheless receives its sense. The second reduction takes up the ontological lessons of the first and deepens them: confronting Dasein no longer merely with a tool out of its place but with a world in which it no longer feels "at home" (*Un-zuhause*), it deepens the sense of that atopy, that non-place where being can manifest itself in its truth. It institutes a new gaze and a new "care" (*Sorge*) that bring Dasein "before itself" and measure the being of things, of the familiar world, and of Dasein's concerned existence against the authentic revelation of Dasein's own being.

The appearing of Dasein's own being and of the world in anxiety is still owed to a confrontation of Dasein with absence (p. 31). This time it is no longer the absence of a tool but the absence of that very readiness-to-hand which the tool's absence had already made appear. Anxiety is thus the experience of a redoubled absence, the revelation of the radical nothing Heidegger calls "the nothing of readiness-to-hand" (*das Nichts von Zuhandenheit*). In anxiety one witnesses the foundering of the intraworldly being to which Dasein proximally and for the most part clings (*klammert*). Whereas in the disturbance of use only tools lose their involvement (*Bewandtnis*) and so become unusable (*unverwendbar*), in anxiety the entire significance (*Bedeutsamkeit*) of the familiar world collapses, and the world takes on the character of a total in-significance (*Unbedeutsamkeit*).

How could things and world come to this, and how can it happen that all at once they "say nothing more" to Dasein? (pp. 31–32.) For Heidegger this loss of interest in the world and the estrangement that follows are the effect of Dasein's nagging, obsessive encounter with its own being. Faced with the unfathomable facticity of its existence and with its being-toward-death, Dasein loses its taste for the things of the world; under the blow of anxiety, circumspective concern for what is ready-to-hand appears futile. Tearing Dasein from its habitual concerns within the world, anxiety also expels it from the undifferentiated community of the they and abolishes the security of what Lacan will call an "imaginary" identification with others. Becoming anxious about itself and bearing that anxiety without lapsing into phobic fear, Dasein finds itself alone before its ipseity, *solus ipse*. It is in the solitude of anxiety that the sense of its own being appears to it — the sense that, in conformity with the design of *Sein und Zeit*, must serve as the ground for questioning the sense of being in general. And it is in that solitude, listening to the silent voice of conscience (*Gewissen*), that Dasein hears the call of its "vocation" for ontological questioning.

What then is the sense of the solitude accompanying authentic existence, and what does the solipsism to which anxiety, functioning as reduction, constrains Dasein amount to? (pp. 32–33.) The anxious Dasein's disinterest in the world irresistibly recalls the impartiality of the phenomenologizing spectator (*unbeteiligter Zuschauer*) of Husserl and Fink, who was likewise alone, chiefly because one sees well only by oneself. Appearances notwithstanding, the two cases differ. Dasein is indeed anxious about itself, about its own self, but it does not thereby deliver itself as an object to its own gaze. Dasein undergoes itself in that particular mode of feeling Heidegger calls disposedness (*Befindlichkeit*): anxiety is an auto-affection in which Dasein undergoes its individuality in what is incomparable and irreplaceable about it. In anxious care for its own being Dasein pays scant attention to other Dasein, but this does not prevent its solitude from remaining a mode of being-with. And unlike the phenomenologizing spectator, Dasein, while undergoing the revelation of its own being, is far from having cut all bridges to the world: even if the truth of its own being appears in the solitude and estrangement of anxiety, it belongs to Dasein's being to be-with other Dasein and to be-in-the-world (*In-der-Welt-sein*). Through the in-significance of the world experienced in anxiety, "the world as world" announces itself for the first time; in anxiety Dasein is individualized (*vereinzelt*), but as being-in-the-world. Self-objectification and worldlessness, the two features that made the Husserlian spectator vulnerable, are both refused here.

Heidegger's opposition of authentic to inauthentic existence works somewhat as Fink and Husserl opposed, within one and the same transcendental subject, the phenomenologizing spectator to constituting consciousness (pp. 33–34). The opposition in principle does not prevent the cohabitation of the two forms of existence within one Dasein, and this "double life" appears most explicitly in what Heidegger calls "the call of conscience" (*Ruf des Gewissens*) (§§54–60). The call comes from me and yet it comes over me (*aus mir und doch über mich*): from me, since on Heidegger's account no one can care about my own being as much as I can; over me, because I have always already lost myself in my worldly concerns. The call expressed in silence and the return to myself that answers it — the whole scene of vocation and conversion — takes place between me and me. While doing me violence, the call finally asks nothing but that I take up (*Ergreifen*) my existence, that I choose myself rather than let myself be carried along by the conformist attitudes and received ideas of the they. It brings it about that Dasein authentically fetches itself back from its lostness in the they, gathering the self that had been "dispersed" (*zerstreut*) in it. This self-recognition again recalls the way the phenomenologizing spectator extracts constituting consciousness from its immersion in the world in order to reveal it to itself.

The authentic existence to which I am called nevertheless retains the status of a "modification" of inauthentic existence, and so cannot claim to be an autonomous style of life (p. 34). The irresoluteness of the they remains in power; the only difference is that it can no longer touch resolute existence. Thus in the second reduction — the one accomplished by anxiety and the call of conscience, revealing the being of Dasein's own self — the trace of the double life Dasein leads still appears: disclosed in its "there", Dasein stands equiprimordially in truth and in untruth.

In making Dasein's being appear, the reduction makes a double phenomenon appear, each side of it with its own mode of appearing (p. 34). By making inauthentic and authentic existence appear differently, it reveals at the same stroke the difference and the rift traversing the being of that singular being which is Dasein. For if the "being" of Dasein is its "existence", and if Dasein exists "equiprimordially" in an authentic and an inauthentic manner, then Dasein's being cannot be kept safe from contamination by this double life. The reduction that makes Dasein's being appear shows it torn between truth and untruth. There is accordingly no Dasein whose being would reduce to authentic existence, any more than there was for Husserl and Fink a pure phenomenologizing spectator. The life of Dasein, like the life of the transcendental subject, is a life in the authentic and in the inauthentic, in care for self and in concern for the world. "To be" means for both: to be still otherwise, to be always different from oneself, never to coincide with oneself. And if difference is thus lodged within the very being of the transcendental subject and of Dasein, then the reduction called upon to make that being appear will forever be incapable of exhibiting a "pure" phenomenon.

Agreement that the reduction exhibits the divided being of transcendental subject and Dasein leaves Husserl and Heidegger with quite different ideas of how that being *appears* (p. 35). For Husserl, the appearing of being means appearing before the inquisitorial gaze of an "impartial spectator". Appearing as the intentional object of a vision gorged with evidence, the being of transcendental consciousness still appears in the manner of a being (*étant*). Whether it is the being of constituting consciousness, the being of the phenomenological spectator, or the difference between these two modes of being of the transcendental subject, their appearing always consists in presenting themselves before the intentional gaze of a "supervisor". Nothing escapes the phenomenologist's gaze, not even the internal division of a two-faced transcendental subject that cannot appear on one side without disappearing on the other. In the end phenomenological appearing is distinguished from ordinary forms of appearing only by the quality of the phenomenologist's gaze, which requires that the object appearing be given in person and entire (*Selbstgegebenheit*), keeping nothing back. For Heidegger, by contrast, Dasein's being is never given in the manner of an object spread out whole before a spectator. Marion is right to oppose Husserl's "flat phenomenon" to the Heideggerian conception of a phenomenon whose "enigmatic" depth is to be sounded.

A first aspect of that depth is that Dasein is far from being able to command the phenomenon of being (pp. 35–36). Whether it is the being of the tool, of the world, or of Dasein, the appearing of being is inseparable from the way Dasein's existence is actually accomplished — most often against Dasein's will. It is therefore no accident that Dasein's own being is not presented as the object of an intuition, but that Dasein receives its revelation in being anxious, that is, in being touched in its existence and more particularly in its disposedness (*Befindlichkeit*). Being can thus appear without betraying its invisible character. A second aspect is bound to Dasein's double life: the appearing of Dasein's authentic being is inseparable from its covering-over by inauthentic being, so that untruth is an integral part of the way the truth of Dasein's being appears. To sound as a phenomenologist the depth of the phenomenon of Dasein's being is, in Marion's phrase, to render apparent not only the unapparent but the play of the apparent with the unapparent within the appearing. A closing note flags a third aspect Bernet sets aside: the question that presses on Heidegger with increasing urgency, no longer only of the being of beings but of the sense of being, or of being without beings — a deepening of the question of the phenomenon of being, understood as the phenomenality of the phenomenon, that leads toward the paradoxical phenomenon of the appearing "of nothing".

## I. Intentionality and Intersubjectivity

### 1. Intentionality and Transcendence (Husserl and Heidegger)

"Where has intentionality gone in Heidegger's phenomenology?" — a question Bernet has often heard put, naive in formulation but a good question (pp. 39–40). Those who ask it are usually prompted by some footnote in which Heidegger pays emphatic homage to Husserl's work; one credits his sincerity but has difficulty verifying the homage against the main text, that of *Sein und Zeit* for instance. Only the recent publication of the Marburg courses ended this embarrassment. Those texts not only make *Sein und Zeit* more profitable to read, they finally inform us about the sources of fundamental ontology — and it emerges that a very attentive reading of Husserl's Sixth *Logical Investigation* played an important part in the genesis of the young Heidegger's phenomenological ontology. Bernet's demonstration rests chiefly on the 1925 course *Prolegomena zur Geschichte des Zeitbegriffs*, which contains the longest and most searching discussion Heidegger ever devoted to Husserl, placed in the course's preparatory part under the heading "Sense and task of phenomenological research". Intentionality in Husserl's sense thus plays a central role in elaborating what for Heidegger is in the process of becoming "the thing itself" of phenomenology. Throughout the chapter, bare page and paragraph references are to the *Prolegomena*; other volumes of the *Gesamtausgabe* are cited by volume number and date of composition.

#### The Intentionality of Sensible and Categorial Acts

What is most striking in Heidegger's exposition of Husserl's analysis of intentionality is its systematic character (pp. 40–41). He begins from a first exhibition (*Aufweis*) of the phenomenon, proceeds to an analysis of the fundamental constitution of intentionality as such, and finally attacks a full morphology of intentional acts, both simple and categorial. The dry exposition is also vigilant to the point of intransigence toward thinkers who precede Husserlian orthodoxy on intentionality or depart from it. Heidegger appears to efface himself entirely before the doctrine of the *Logical Investigations*, and treats *Ideen I* — a work for which he notoriously had no sympathy — with searching and unfailing competence. But the explicit allegiance goes together with more covert work that clarifies and even corrects certain Husserlian analyses, and one should not be taken in: under the appearance of serving Husserl, Heidegger uses him, and serves only himself. He uses Husserl to demonstrate the necessity of resting phenomenological ontology on new foundations, those of the existential analytic. That the discussion of intentionality functions as an introduction to the problematic of fundamental ontology is already evident from the 1925 course's overall plan, from its critical part, and negatively from the omissions and slippages an attentive reader will find within the exposition itself. Whether this propaedeutic function is not a second piece of Heideggerian staging is the question to be pressed: is there really continuity between Husserl and Heidegger, in the sense of a deepening of what makes intentionality possible, or rather rupture and abyss between the intentional relation to beings and a transcendence open to the world and to the question of the being of beings? On that answer depend the further questions of a supposed continuity in the understanding of the subject, of truth, and of time.

Treating the fundamental structures of intentionality, Heidegger defines it as a comportment in which an *intentio* "directs itself toward" (*sich-richten-auf*) an *intentum* (pp. 41–42). The vague Latin terms are not accidental: they let him draw a provisional veil over everything separating him from Husserl's "noesis" and "noema". He likewise avoids speaking of intentional "acts", preferring intentional comportments (*Verhalten*) — a terminological shift that already betrays the concern to tear intentionality out of the narrow frame of a theory of signification and above all of knowledge in which Husserl had confined it. Yet Heidegger joins Husserl without reservation in holding that the transitive relation of a directing-toward is far from a satisfying determination of intentionality. The enigma is not transitivity but what Husserl calls the correlation of *intentio* and *intentum*, which Heidegger in turn calls their co-belonging (*Mitzugehörigkeit*). He thereby defends what would later be called a noematic phenomenology, approaching the mysterious intentional co-belonging by way of a close interrogation of the *intentum*, and he argues from the naturalness of a procedure that lets itself be carried by the movement of the intentional relation toward its object. Arguing from naturalness is rare among philosophers and usually hides ulterior motives. Noematicians such as Gurwitsch, Sartre and Føllesdal openly intend by this route to bypass the transcendental I or exclude it outright from the analysis of intentionality. Heidegger's reasons must be different, since his new determination of "the subjectivity of the subject" does not exclude its egological character. What guides his choice is the project of a phenomenological ontology, and the description of the *intentum* offers the first occasion for questioning intentionality's ontological significance.

Heidegger distinguishes two principal senses of *intentum*: the being in itself (*das Seiende an ihm selbst*), and the being taken in the how of its being-intended (*das Wie des Intendiertseins*) (pp. 42–43). The distinction goes back to Husserl, sketched in a single passage of the Fifth *Investigation* — where the object as it is intended is distinguished from the object which is intended — and amply developed in *Ideen I*'s analysis of the noema. Heidegger follows Husserl in holding that only the second, the thing as and such as it shows itself in a concrete intentional act, gives a properly phenomenological determination of the intentional object: a being whose mode of being and appearing is determined from a particular, concrete and individual intentional comportment. Which makes it surprising that the best of Heidegger's effort should go not to that strictly phenomenological determination but to the investigation of the non-phenomenological concept of the *intentum*, the being in itself. The puzzle is posed here and answered two units below.

The being in itself carries three senses in Heidegger: thing of the surrounding world (*Umweltding*), thing of nature (*Naturding*), and thinghood (*Dinglichkeit*) (pp. 43–44). Attentive reading calls up a crowd of associations. The distinction between thing of nature and thing of the surrounding world is hard not to refer back to Aristotle's distinction between things by nature and things by craft. Things of the surrounding world are made by human hands, are thereby at human disposal, and belong to a world shaped by human beings; things of nature are determined independently of human action, understood in terms of their physical constituents and their place in an objective natural order that gives or imposes itself. In this opposition it is easy to recognize the sketch of *Sein und Zeit*'s distinction between the ready-to-hand (*zuhanden*) and the present-at-hand (*vorhanden*). Already in 1925 Heidegger says clearly not only that the same being lends itself to being understood in both modes of being, but that on closer inspection things of nature present themselves "proximally" within the context of the things and characters of the surrounding world. A brief remark whose richness he may not yet measure adds that this surrounding world, the human milieu of life, has a narrative structure: a world in which one recounts (*erzählen*) nothing but the precise if banal history (*Geschichte*) of the chair, a history in which the chair presents itself here continually, day after day. The third sense, thinghood, is no longer a concrete being but the general determinations or structures things have in common — materiality, extension, coloration, movability. Thinghood is what belongs to the material content (*Sachhaltigkeiten*) of every thing, what Husserl calls a sensible essence or a materially rather than formally determined essence; investigating its general traits is the business of a material ontology (*materiale Ontologie*) of the thing.

The three senses of "the being in itself" reflect the preoccupations of traditional ontology from Aristotle to Husserl, and there is no doubt that the being taken in the how of its being-intended is for Heidegger the promising anchorage for a new phenomenological ontology (pp. 44–45). The earlier puzzle is thereby answered: Heidegger's interest in the non-phenomenological determination of the *intentum* is an interest in traditional ontology, whose articulations serve as guiding thread for elaborating a properly phenomenological one. Even though the distinction between the two principal senses of *intentum* goes back to Husserl, Heidegger gives it an entirely new sense. In Husserl the relation between the object simply intended and the object as it is intended concerns the correspondence between the reality of the empirical thing and its givenness as noematic phenomenon; that correspondence is the theme of an epistemological interrogation baptized "phenomenology of reason", which is nothing but a phenomenology of the transcendental constitution of objective reality. In Heidegger the same relation becomes an ontological interrogation. What is at stake beyond the relation of traditional to phenomenological ontology is the phenomenological investigation of the different sorts of beings with respect to their mode of being: the being taken in the how of its being-intended is not other than the being in itself, but that same being interrogated as to its mode of being on the basis of its intentional appearing — which Bernet writes *appar-être*, splicing "appearing" and "being" to mark that in this appearing the being of what appears is what is at issue. This shift from an epistemological to an ontological reading of the *intentum* governs everything that follows, and in particular Heidegger's account of categorial intuition. A note records that *Die Grundprobleme der Phänomenologie* takes up the same analysis apropos of position and perception in Kant, with the difference that there the perceivedness of the perceived, the presence-at-hand of the present-at-hand, is shown to be disclosed only from Dasein's understanding of being — a disclosedness of being (*Erschlossenheit des Seins*) effected by Dasein's existential transcendence.

Heidegger gives an extremely clear and nuanced summary of what is doubtless one of the major contributions of Husserl's analysis of intentionality: the distinction between different sorts of intentional acts, sensible and categorial, and the relations of interdependence or founding that unite them (p. 46). Bernet passes over it in order to reach the distinction between two great classes of categorial acts, those of synthesis and those of ideation. But that requires first fixing the more general distinction between simple acts and categorial or multi-levelled acts (*gestufte Akte*), which turns on how the unity of an intentional object is apprehended: directly and immediately, or indirectly and in several stages.

Neither Husserl nor Heidegger worries much about the circularity of this distinction, which is surprising, since the simplicity of an act cannot be defined strictly without appeal to its not being categorial, and a categorial act cannot be defined without appeal to its being composed of, or more precisely founded on, a set of simple acts (pp. 46–47). Phenomenology has nonetheless importantly clarified and renewed the traditional distinction between simple, so-called sensible representations and complex acts involving a synthetic activity of understanding or reasoning. Far from expressing an autonomous and unlimited formative power, or the active arrangement of a passively received material, categorial acts are deeply rooted in perceptual life. And that perception is not an abstract pure seeing but a fabric to which the several senses have contributed, an articulated relation to the world. Categorial acts merely make explicit the articulations of the world of perceptual life; they unbind, as it were, the threads perception had welded together in a vital bond to the world. The phenomenological innovation goes further still: it widens the concept of sensibility to include certain concepts, called "sensible concepts", so that sensibility becomes the domain of everything apprehended in its material content (*materielle Sachhaltigkeit*). It is by this openness to the material content of things that sensibility is distinguished from a properly categorial concern directed at empty formal objects. The categorial distinct from the sensible is then only the explicit articulation of forms already entering into the composition of sensible experience. This way of taking the distinction owes as much to Aristotle as to Husserl — an Aristotelian reading of Husserl from which Heidegger also draws a considerable secondary benefit in his polemic with neo-Kantianism.

Despite their rootedness in sensible perception, categorial acts never reduce purely and simply to the simple acts that compose them or found them (pp. 47–48). What is given to be seen in a categorial intuition is at once a sensible being and its beyond, its surplus, its "excess" (*Überschuss*). This categorial excess is exemplary in the case of the perceptual judgments (*Wahrnehmungsurteile*) to which Husserl devotes his attention from the opening of the Sixth *Investigation*, and which Heidegger prefers to call perceptual statements (*Wahrnehmungsaussagen*). Such a statement does nothing but express a perception, and yet in doing so it is inexorably driven beyond perception, since it contains grammatical and syntactic forms with no direct counterpart in any moment of sensible perception. Hence the need for a categorial intuition to justify the truth of a perceptual statement, that is, to secure what Husserl calls its intuitive fulfilment. Such categorial intuition is only very exceptionally the intuition of a pure formal category; most often it is an act explicitly establishing relations already implied in perception — a synthetic relation among sensible objects, or an ideative relation between the sensible object and its concept. Husserl and Heidegger agree, too, that far from being derived from language these categorial intuitions constitute its intuitive foundation. Within this large fidelity a new Heideggerian slippage deserves attention: understanding language as an expressive power does not at all exclude that language should in fact precede and guide sensible perception, a thought Heidegger compresses into a shock formula — we do not state what we see, but conversely we see what is stated about the thing. This hermeneutic conception of language, this openness to language's facticity, rests in truth on an ontological understanding of the categorial still to be specified; and against the ontological stakes commanding Heidegger's attention here, the question of language still carries little weight.

The presence of a categorial excess within the experience of simple beings announces itself as soon as one compares things, gathers them into a set, or orders them in any way (pp. 48–49). As soon as a relation is explicitly posited, whether by means of language or not, a categorial act "of synthesis" is performed and a categorial object is given. Such acts are governed by the same logic of whole and parts that forms the real weave of the *Logical Investigations*. Described more phenomenologically, acts of synthesis are intentional acts that emerge from sensible perception by bringing to it an explicit articulation — a lifting out, a raising, an articulating — of relations implicitly assumed. The type case is the predicative judgment establishing and affirming a relation of unity or identity among sensible data. Heidegger dwells on it willingly, less from sympathy for an atomistic conception of predication than from interest in the copula "is". Here he has a first example of a categorial object which under his hands quickly turns into the thing itself. Take a perceptual statement: my dog is bigger than yours. Everything is already in the perception — the dogs, their look, the contrast between a pot-bellied and a slender dog — everything except that "is" which is the pivot of the statement.
