Taken separately that "is" does not mean much, which is precisely why it counts as a categorial object whose intuitive apprehension is founded on, but not reducible to, sensible perception. Heidegger refines the analysis by noting that this synthetic categorial act establishes an ideal relation ("is") one of whose relata is itself a real relation grasped in perception ("bigger than"): A is (bigger than) B. The perceptual relation concerning the dogs' bulk is thus set into the predicative relation established by the statement.

The second type of categorial intuition, the acts of ideation, differs from acts of synthesis precisely in excluding its sensible foundation rather than including it (pp. 49–50). Acts of ideation are categorial intuitions apprehending general or universal objects, what are commonly called ideas or essences, and they require that their sensible foundation be submitted to an ideating abstraction (*ideierende Abstraktion*) — in truth nothing other than Husserl's famous seeing of essences. Despite extreme concision, Heidegger's exposition specifies and innovates on Husserlian doctrine, notably about the sorts of ideas and the mode of their apprehension. His interest again falls on the sensible ideas contributing to the articulation of perception. When I see that this round thing is a dog, I make perceptual use of what taken in itself exceeds sensible perception, namely the idea of the dog. Long before being explicitly apprehended in a categorial intuition, ideas guide the gaze and determine as what (*Als-was*) a sensible object is perceived. Playing on the sense of *eidos* as the look of something (*das Aussehen von etwas*), Heidegger calls ideas the whereupon of the regard (*das Worauf der Hinsicht*). Rather than analyse the difference between the apprehension of sensible and of categorial objects, he sets out to show their original bond: far from having a separate existence, ideas are that in view of which a sensible object is perceived. This unmistakably involves a further departure from Husserlian orthodoxy.

Rather than a slippage it should be called a tightrope walk (pp. 50–51). Categorial intuition leads to a phenomenological ontology only on condition of effacing at once the empiricist and the idealist elements in Husserl's doctrine. On the empiricist and immanentist side, it is striking that the term representative content (*repräsentierender Inhalt*) is never mentioned by Heidegger, neither for sensible perception nor for categorial intuition. There is no trace of sensations deprived of intentionality on which perception would have to lean, none either of that psychic bond which the Sixth *Investigation* made into a representative content for every categorial intuition. Expelled from psychic interiority, categorial intuition can find no refuge on the side of a Platonizing logic either: large chapters of Husserl's work and the whole inheritance from Lotze, Bolzano and Meinong are simply erased. Far from being objects of a higher order belonging to a separate world of thought governed by immutable laws, categorial objects for Heidegger are hardly genuine "objects" at all. He also carefully avoids alluding to any constructive or constituting activity of a spontaneous intelligence of which categorial objects would be the product — as one passage makes plain: that categorial acts constitute a new objecthood does not mean that they make things first come to be somewhere; constituting does not mean producing in the sense of making and manufacturing, but letting the being be seen in its objecthood (*Gegenständlichkeit*). Guarding himself thus against both empiricism and idealism, Heidegger is naturally brought up against a Kantian and still more a neo-Kantian reading of categorial objects: are the categories then mental forms allowing objects of experience to be constituted in their necessary unity? Here as elsewhere he criticizes the neo-Kantians all the more readily because it lets him dispose with impunity of everything in Husserl that obstructs fundamental ontology. He draws the force of this polemic from the solid support of Aristotle: his ontological reading of categorial intuition in Husserl descends in a straight line from the discovery of the phenomenological character of Aristotle's ontology.

Aristotle is what leads Heidegger to stress the incarnation of the categories in the sensible and to attend to the bond between sensible and categorial objects rather than to what separates them (pp. 51–52). That bond is no longer strictly a bond of constitution but a bond between what shows itself straightaway and what makes that showing possible. In categorial intuition it is this very possibility of an object's showing that is revealed, the objecthood of objects that gives itself to be seen. Husserl said nothing else, and made the investigation of this a priori of objects the task of a logic at once formal and transcendental. For Heidegger, however, the original sense of the a priori is not logical but ontological, and every logic is an ontology unaware of itself: the a priori understood phenomenologically is a title of being. It is also a title of time, or more exactly the title of the temporal sense of being. Heidegger resumes the polemic by stressing what he calls the a priori's specific indifference toward subjectivity: the a priori of the object, which he also calls the being of beings, belongs neither to a subject nor to an object. Rather than say it belongs to nothing, he prefers to say it belongs, in a manner still obscure, to intentionality. Whereupon the question rebounds: what is the being of intentionality? For Heidegger this fundamental question can be approached only by interrogating the being of the intentional, which leads him, logically enough, to examine later in the course what Husserl calls the phenomenological reduction. The question of the a priori of objects thus leads to the question of the a priori of the subject, of what Heidegger elsewhere calls the subjectivity of the subject. Husserl is credited with having found in categorial intuition a fruitful approach to the objectivity of present-at-hand objects; the transcendental reduction will be reproached with having deprived phenomenology of access to the question of the subjectivity of the subject. The change in verdict goes with a change of scene: Heidegger moves from the *Logical Investigations* to *Ideen I*.

#### The Being of Intentional Consciousness

The stake of this second confrontation is a genuinely phenomenological determination of the relation between the being of intentionality and the being of the intentional subject (pp. 52–53). In the Marburg courses Heidegger repeatedly stresses the paradoxical character of that relation. On one side, any abusive subjectivation of intentionality must be avoided: every determination of the subjectivity of the subject must be preceded by a phenomenological analysis of the intentional relation, so that the subject is understood from intentionality rather than intentionality from preconceived ideas about the subject. On the other side, the intentional relation can be fully understood only from its foundation in a new understanding of the subject. In sum: the phenomenological investigation of intentionality issues in the discovery of Dasein's transcendence, which in turn proves to be the ontological foundation of intentionality. The circle is not vicious but ordered: intentionality gives the access, transcendence gives the ground.

What is at stake in Heidegger's discussion of *Ideen I* is that a preconceived idea of the subject blocks phenomenological investigation both of the being of the intentional relation and of the being of the intentional subject, of what Husserl calls intentional "consciousness" (p. 53). Every reader of *Ideen I* knows that the structures of noetic-noematic intentionality are developed only *after* the fundamental phenomenological consideration, which is devoted above all to setting out the reduction and determining pure consciousness and the pure I. Husserl's analysis of intentionality is therefore inscribed within the epistemological project of exploring the contours of the region of pure consciousness and its constituting power. The 1925 course sets out to show not only that such a procedure prevents Husserl from taking the full measure of what intentionality offers a phenomenological ontology, but that it employs ontological concepts wholly without phenomenological foundation. If the transcendental and eidetic reduction allowed Husserl to avoid the objectivation of intentionality, it pushes him toward a greater subjectivation of it.

Heidegger patiently dismantles the several ontological significations with which pure consciousness is invested by the reduction (pp. 53–54). *Ideen I* wants to establish phenomenology as an absolute science of pure consciousness, so Husserl's first concern is to explore a givenness of consciousness in which consciousness reveals its whole essence and nothing but its essence, and which can therefore secure for phenomenology an autonomous field of investigation. The concern to show that pure consciousness is not only an object *sui generis* but an object independent of every other runs through the fundamental consideration from end to end, and explains why Husserl insists so heavily on separating pure consciousness from its natural place of insertion in the world, from the psycho-physical structure of the human being. The bond between consciousness and body must be shown to be empirical, and one must turn from it to grasp the pure, independent essence of consciousness. For a phenomenologist this implies that consciousness gives itself to be seen otherwise and better than in the form of the empirical existence of a human being, and this more original and purer givenness is its reflective givenness. In Husserl's eyes reflective givenness has decisive advantages: reflection spares the detour through the empirical human being; the act of reflection is attuned to the mental nature of consciousness and so permits immediate access and fully intuitive apprehension. Reflection wakes the being of consciousness from its alienation in empirical alterity and reveals it to itself in the rapture of an immediate and indubitable evidence. The being of consciousness is thus not only an independent being but a being apprehended absolutely.

The consequences of this reflective determination for the understanding of intentionality are worth pausing over (pp. 54–55). Reflection is an intentional act owing its apodictic evidence to being accomplished within the subject's interiority: in reflection pure consciousness is at once the subject of intentionality and the intentional object. This privileged intentionality of reflection then serves as the measure for determining intentional acts endowed with lesser evidence, such as the perception of things in the external world. Intentional consciousness is not shut up in the subject's immanence, but that is nonetheless where it resides: it is from the subject's interiority that it goes out toward the world of transcendent objects, and into the subject's interiority that one must go to interrogate the being of intentionality. For Heidegger this implies not only an abusive subjectivation of intentionality but a relapse of phenomenology into a metaphysics of Cartesian type. Instead of asking phenomenologically after the being of intentional consciousness, Husserl would demote it to a present-at-hand object apprehended with evidence; instead of interrogating pure consciousness as the a priori of intentionality, he would treat it as an intentional object of a particular type. The questions of the being of the intentional and of the being of intentionality, and the search for a phenomenological approach to them, would remain unpaid.

Subjectivation acquires a new dimension in *Ideen I* when pure consciousness is endowed with a constituting power (pp. 55–56). The idea of a transcendental constitution of objects by consciousness results from interpreting intentionality within an idealist ontology. That transcendental idealism is not without phenomenological foundation, but the foundation is no longer interrogated within a phenomenological ontology. Its basis is the observation that whatever is, is determined at least partly by its possible relation to being-present-for-consciousness: everything that is must be able to present itself in some way to consciousness, and the presentifying comportment of intentional consciousness is a perhaps partial and insufficient but legitimate approach to the being of beings. On Heidegger's account Husserlian idealism goes much further, in four theses. First, it reduces the sense of the being of every being to its manner of presenting itself to a present consciousness. Second, it interprets the sense of being of every being in terms of the distinction between immanent and transcendent objects, so that to be is to be either an immanent or a transcendent object for a present consciousness. Third, the sense of being of a transcendent object is said to depend on the sense of being of an immanent one, namely constituting consciousness, whereas the sense of being of an immanent object depends only on another immanent object: a transcendent object depends in its being on a being other than itself, an immanent object on an object like itself. Fourth, the sense of being of every being is identified with its reality, its *actualitas*. Whence the *actualitas* of a transcendent object depends on that of an immanent object, while an immanent object needs no other thing in order to exist — and whence the relative, presumptive existence of transcendent objects and the absolute, necessary existence of a transcendental consciousness that would survive an annihilation of the world without difficulty.

This deduction of the ontological presuppositions of *Ideen I*'s idealism is not found as such in Heidegger's text, but each of the four theses about being is abundantly interrogated and criticized by him, and the whole critical discussion can be reduced to three main stakes (p. 56). First, Heidegger contests that the being of any being should be nothing but its capacity to be apprehended by a present consciousness in the form of an immanent or a transcendent object; to think so is to misrecognize the ontological difference and its rootedness in temporality, and the misrecognition bears principally on the ontological a priori of the *intentum* of intentional comportments. Second, he contests that intentionality is a mental activity assigned to reside in the subject's sphere of immanence: far from constituting a self-enclosed and absolute domain of being, as Husserl would have it, the subject of intentionality is a being whose being is openness, transcendence — so Husserl would misrecognize the true sense of the being of the intention and above all of the being of the intentional subject. Third, he contests that the being of that subject can be unveiled only by turning away from the facticity of human existence; for Heidegger the contrary is true, and a phenomenological approach to the being of the intentional must begin by attending to the intentional manner in which a human being in concrete daily life actually comports itself toward things and world.

Conceptions borrowed from the existential analytic and fundamental ontology are thus at work in this critique (p. 57). Does that mean one understanding of intentionality is criticized in the name of another — Husserl's pure consciousness in the name of Dasein's existence, intentional knowledge of objects in the name of being-in-the-world and transcendence? In a certain way yes, since Heidegger's chief reproach is that Husserl did not carry his analysis of intentionality through to its ontological foundation in Dasein's transcendence. But it does not follow that the critique remains inoperative for anyone who has not adopted the standpoint of fundamental ontology. The point matters for the chapter's argument: the criticisms are to be tested on Husserl's own ground, not simply asserted from Heidegger's.

Whatever one makes of the general idea that a theory of knowledge is only a deficient ontology unaware of itself, the theory of knowledge proposed in *Ideen I* undoubtedly works with major ontological presuppositions (pp. 57–58). Heidegger insists particularly on the Cartesian character of this hidden ontology. In his eyes the reduction of *Ideen I* is an operation of separation, diametrically opposed to the being of intentionality, which is the being of a relation, a bond. It separates the human being from consciousness, the immanent *intentio* from the transcendent *intentum*, constituting from constituted being, absolute consciousness from world — and Bernet notes in support that Husserl himself, in a course slightly earlier than *Ideen I*, designated what he then called the *distinctio phaenomenologica* as an essential moment of the reduction. The movement of separation continues in the eidetic reduction. For Husserl the eidetic reduction permits the passage from an empirical to an a priori phenomenology of pure consciousness, the passage from the analysis of a factual intentional comportment to its analysis by right, in its necessary and general constituents. For Heidegger this analysis of essential constituents is something quite other than a phenomenological analysis of intentionality's a priori: the *eidos* of an act is an atemporal and separate structure, whereas the a priori of an intentional comportment has a sense at once temporal and inseparable from the actual individual accomplishing of that comportment. Husserl's eidetics would trade phenomenology for a "noumenology", abandoning the individual facticity of the phenomenon for an absolute and immutable knowledge of the *logos*. The whole long series of separations has its root in Husserl's idea of phenomenological science: *Ideen I* seeks to establish phenomenology as an absolutely certain science with an absolutely autonomous field, and the being of intentionality and of the intentional are worked over until they satisfy that requirement.

Against this Cartesianism Heidegger makes himself the defender of a more natural and also more phenomenological approach to intentionality (pp. 58–59). He insists that the sense of the reduction is not to separate *intentio* and *intentum* but on the contrary to make the being of their co-belonging appear — a reading supported by his own gloss on phenomenological bracketing (*Einklammerung*) in the 1925 course, where the bracketing of beings and the switching (*Umschaltung*) of the gaze have precisely the sense of making present the being-character of beings. Granted that this requires a human being to tear itself from its miring in things, it does not follow that it must fly off into the transparency of a transcendental subjectivity. Granted that being human and existing do not suffice for understanding the sense of being, it does not follow that one must turn away from the human being and the facticity of its existence toward an absolute and separate transcendental consciousness. Nor is there any properly phenomenological reason to turn away from the individuation of a concrete intentional comportment when it is the a priori *logos* of that particular phenomenon that is in question. Heidegger holds on the contrary that there are good reasons to interrogate the a priori of intentionality and the being of the intentional from the individual and concrete enactment of an intentional comportment — a conviction traceable at least to his 1919–21 notes on Jaspers, where the *intentio* as relational character and the *intentum* as content-character are already set in relation to the character of enactment (*Vollzugscharakter*). For Heidegger it is in the intentional discovering of an object that the pre-understanding of world it presupposes is revealed; that pre-understanding informs us about the being of the intentional as existential openness and transcendence; and transcendence with its horizon introduces us to the ecstatic-horizonal nature of temporality.

#### Intentionality and "Transcendence"

The question whether there is continuity or discontinuity, deepening or rupture, between the Husserlian and the Heideggerian understanding of intentionality has still not been answered, and put so generally it could not be answered satisfactorily in a modest article (pp. 59–60). Doing so would require pushing the investigation of intentionality in Husserl well beyond the *Logical Investigations* and *Ideen I*, and analysing in particular the modifications a phenomenology of time and history brings to it. It would require scrutinizing the difficult notions that impose themselves on Husserl almost against his will: the non-objectivating intentionality of absolute, atemporal consciousness; the idea of an indefinite intentional process, the idea of the infinite Husserl calls an "Idea in the Kantian sense"; the intentional horizon within which genetic phenomenology's regressive questioning is carried out. On Heidegger's side one would have to follow the development of intentionality step by step to the point where it merges with Dasein's transcendence and ecstatic temporality. All that exceeds present ambition; what remains possible is to attend to Heidegger's remarks on the early Husserl's intentionality and its relation to Dasein's transcendence, and by way of conclusion to gauge the importance of the distinction between intentionality and transcendence by sketching its effects on the understanding of the subject, of truth and of time — a conclusion that thus anticipates work carried out in the chapters to come.

The Husserlian understanding of intentionality as Heidegger presents it in 1925 comes to this (pp. 60–61). On one side, Husserl is credited with a first approach to the ontological difference within the intentionality of categorial intuitions: categorial intuition is an intentional comportment that unveils the ontological a priori of the present-at-hand being, the being of that being, or that being according to its mode of being. On the other, he is criticized for not having exploited the discovery for an ontological approach to the being of intentionality and of the intentional subject: the intentional relation, of perception for instance, would be demoted to a relation between immanent and transcendent object, and the intentional subject would become a pure, separate consciousness. The criticism means two things. First, that the Husserlian understanding of intentionality, epistemological though it is, rests on prejudices inherited from a Cartesian ontology contrary to the phenomena. Second, that for Heidegger intentionality acquires its true sense only by contributing to a new phenomenological ontology: intentionality, and more precisely its concrete enactment, must serve as guiding thread for the phenomenological interrogation of the being of intentional objects and of the being of the intentional subject. Husserl's categorial intuition would permit a first phenomenological approach to the determinations of the being of present-at-hand objects that tradition called the categories; the approach to the being of the subject through what Heidegger calls the existentials would be cruelly lacking. For Heidegger the two ontological questions are necessarily bound together, in virtue of the intentionality of being-in-the-world, which permits at once the understanding of the being of things and the understanding of the being of the self (*Selbst*); and that intentionality of being-in-the-world is more properly called transcendence.

It was therefore not quite right to say that the investigation of intentionality's ontological significance was merely partial in Husserl, touching the being of things and neglecting the being of the self (p. 61). Understanding being, whatever being is in question, is a matter of transcendence, and transcendence is a form of intentionality preceding the distinction between subject and object, interiority and exteriority. If transcendence is the common root of the understanding of the being of things and of the being of the self, and if Husserlian intentionality is essentially different from that transcendence, then Husserlian intentionality is incapable of contributing directly to the understanding of the being of any being whatever. The concession made a moment ago is thereby withdrawn, and the stakes raised: on this syllogism Husserl's categorial intuition would not be a partial achievement but no ontological achievement at all.

The syllogism, and beyond it the whole debate about continuity, turns on the relation between intentionality and transcendence (pp. 61–62). That Heideggerian transcendence is not Husserlian intentionality is clear; that the two are not unrelated is equally clear. Heidegger himself, depending on context, sometimes accentuates the continuity and sometimes the discontinuity. But everywhere he envisages the relation as one of founding: intentionality is an ontic comportment, an "ontic transcendence", finding its ontological foundation in the primal transcendence (*Urtranszendenz*) of being-in-the-world. In intentionality one discovers (*entdecken*) a present-at-hand being whose being has already been disclosed (*erschliessen*); one appropriates what, on the basis of transcendence, has already been overleapt (*übersprungen*), which is to say unveiled (*enthüllt*).

A properly phenomenological understanding of a founding relation requires that ground and grounded be given to be seen in their co-belonging and not separated (pp. 62–63). There is no pure transcendence: intentionality is one of the ways transcendence is ordinarily accomplished. There are doubtless better ways of living transcendence, but intentionality seems to be the first because the most everyday and the most natural; it is in ontic comportment toward present-at-hand things and in concern (*Besorgen*) that a human being first experiences transcendence. Hence Heidegger's slightly scholastic formula for their mutual relation: intentionality is the *ratio cognoscendi* of transcendence, and transcendence the *ratio essendi* of intentionality in its various modes. In more Heideggerian language, the relation must be understood according to a mode of founding still to be discovered, which in the same course he already calls the ontological difference. Whoever, like Husserl, stops at ontic intentionality is therefore guilty not merely of not having pushed the analysis far enough, to the ground; he is in addition in a state of misrecognition of the ontological difference. Consequently Heidegger's chief reproach to the Husserlian understanding of intentionality is that it thought the being of the intentional and the being of intentionality badly, by making them into beings of a particular type — and this forgetting of being, a positive and not merely privative forgetting, is itself a manifestation of Dasein's being. Bernet notes that the scholastic formula echoes Kant's remark at the opening of the second *Critique*, that freedom is the *ratio essendi* of the moral law and the moral law the *ratio cognoscendi* of freedom; the verbal association is neither wholly fortuitous nor insignificant, since transcendence is a freedom that first imposes itself on Dasein as a constraint, and the constraints of the intentional relation to beings follow from a freely chosen relation to being.

The ontological difference, as it announces itself in the difference between intentionality and transcendence, is also what makes the difference between a Husserlian and a Heideggerian understanding of the subject, of truth and of time (pp. 63–64). Heidegger's severe critique of pure consciousness already indicates how differently the two conceive the subjectivity of the subject and its relation to facticity: for Husserl the subject is a supratemporal unity or essence that temporalizes itself only secondarily, by entering intentional life; for Heidegger the subject is a burst, disseminated unity (*Streuung*, *Zerstreuung*), since Dasein's being-in-the-world is an intentionality without reserve, that is, transcendence. This is what explains the notes with which Heidegger peppered Husserl's *Encyclopaedia Britannica* article — among them the objection that a world must be said to belong to the essence of the pure ego. The difference implies a difference in the conception of truth as well. Husserl and Heidegger share a notion of truth based on the intentional relation to things and therefore rid of the problem of a "bridge" between knowing subject and real object, but they envisage that relation differently: for Husserl truth is a matter of evidence, of a synthesis of intuitive fulfilment between intentional acts, and the task is to justify an assertion or a presumption; Heidegger's notion is hermeneutic, a matter of discovery and unveiling rather than justification, its ground being nothing but the being received by Dasein's openness and anticipated by its transcendence — so that logic must issue in ontology. Matters are less clear-cut with time. On one side Husserl's analysis attaches above all to the mode of temporality Heidegger calls within-timeness and amputates it from its existential foundation. On the other it is no less true that Husserl pushed the metaphysics of presence to its bursting point: his time-manuscripts teem with unorthodox notions such as non-objectivating intentionality and the flux preceding all ontification. It is doubtless in his phenomenology of time and temporalization that Husserl came closest to a conception of intentionality as transcendence — the thesis Bernet reserves for the chapter on the origin of time (pp. 189–214), and which sets the trajectory of the book's third part.

### 2. The Concept of the Noema (Husserl)

Few of Husserl's coinages have met with such universal interest or raised such hopes as the noema, and few have provoked as much controversy (pp. 65–66). It has been seen as his most original contribution to completing Brentano's doctrine of intentionality; as the confirmation and phenomenological grounding of Fregean semantics; and as the porch of a theory of phenomena standing this side of the split between subject and object. It has equally been understood at once as ideal sense and as appearance, as what I hold in my hand and as the hand in the sense of the bones and muscles composing it — Frege's own image for a confusion — and finally, understandably enough, as a bastard and absurd concept. The division keeps reproducing itself between opponents of the concept, from Adorno through Sartre to Tugendhat, and its defenders; and the defenders are themselves divided, as the polemics between Gurwitsch and Føllesdal, and between Sokolowski and Føllesdal–Mohanty or Smith–McIntyre, sufficiently attest.

The most recent document of these debates, from October 1987, once more opposes Sokolowski and McIntyre with all desirable clarity, and its crux is what was already the crux between Gurwitsch and Føllesdal: the identity of the noema and its dependence on consciousness (pp. 66–67). The exchange is symptomatic of a state of discussion in which the dispute bears on the correct interpretation of Husserl's texts while what is really aimed at is carrying his ideas further than he carried them himself. For Sokolowski, phenomenology must set itself the search for the condition of possibility of being-given in all its forms; this phenomenological ontology must break with the idealist-mentalist misreading of modes of givenness as mental contents, and equally avoid the realist identification of being with the objective being of objects-in-themselves. For McIntyre, phenomenology's task and its opportunity lie in freeing Fregean semantics from its too one-sided alignment on the separation of ideal and real objects: phenomenology studies ideal objects in their connection with intentional consciousness and real objects in the context of their insertion into possible worlds.

The debate about phenomenology's future could just as well be conducted without immediate reference to the interpretation of Husserl's texts (p. 67). But if its outcome is made to depend on the correct interpretation of the noema, then three questions must be decided, and it is the answers to them that divide Sokolowski and McIntyre. Does noematic sense play a mediating role in the relation of the concept to the object, and is it what confers intentionality on the act? Is reflectively given noematic sense an absolute product of reflection, or a content of consciousness already implicitly given in advance — and what of the ideality of that sense and its relation to the object? To what extent can noematic sense also be characterized as a phenomenologically reduced object, and does not every distinction between sense and object thereby lapse?

Recent publication of Husserl's lecture courses of winter semester 1906–07 and summer semester 1908, with their appendices, decisively favours the treatment of these questions (pp. 67–68). Working on those texts settles much of the Sokolowski–McIntyre controversy and, more surprisingly, shows that their divergent conceptions of phenomenology were already prefigured in Husserl's own texts. Husserl himself deployed the noema on the one hand within a phenomenological *theory of knowledge* and on the other within the elaboration of a phenomenological *theory of signification*, and noematic sense and its relation to the intentional object are determined differently in the two contexts. Only in *Ideen I* do the two treatments cross in a way that renders them largely indistinguishable. Since the whole discussion of the noema has, Sokolowski excepted, relied almost exclusively on *Ideen I*, the failure to unify viewpoints is unsurprising. This is the chapter's thesis, and its method: what looks like a single ambiguous concept is two concepts with distinct provenances, and the archival record separates them.

#### The Noema as Phenomenologically Reduced "Object"

The first and strongest motive for introducing the noema comes from the problematic of a phenomenological theory of knowledge (pp. 68–69). The question of the possibility of valid knowledge is predominantly understood in Husserl's work as the question of the aptness (*Triftigkeit*) of acts of knowing — whether the intentional aim of the act reaches its target, whether it hits (*trifft*) the actual determinations of things or misses them. Two ideas guide Husserl's handling of this question of a possible *adaequatio* between the subjective knowledge-claim and the objective state of affairs.

First, an explication of the possibility of knowledge in general must not presuppose any particular knowledge as unquestionedly valid (p. 69). Knowledge is free of presuppositions only when its statements do not depart by an inch from the intuitive givenness of the things they concern. The requirement is not a scruple but a constraint on method: it forces the theory of knowledge to work only with what shows itself.

Second, if what is at stake is aptness, that is, the adequation of the act of knowing and the object known, then one must begin from exemplary knowings in which the adequation in question is beyond doubt (p. 69). The two requirements pull in different directions — the first toward presuppositionlessness, the second toward paradigm cases — and the noema will be what satisfies both at once.

The first requirement leads, along the Cartesian way, to a phenomenological theory of knowledge that treats intentional acts as evident data of reflection and strips them of the presuppositions burdening them, the apperception of consciousness in the empirical-psychological sense among others (p. 69). It submits acts of knowing to the phenomenological reduction, treating them as phenomena given in person in evidence, as "pure" phenomena. The second requirement implies that these pure phenomena must at the same time be cases of valid knowledge, cases in which the subjective claim is shown apt by the corresponding intuitive givenness of the claimed object itself. The phenomenological analysis of the constitution of objects on the basis of their intuitive givenness is the concrete execution of this programme.

Both presuppositions govern the text where the noema is first introduced, the theory-of-knowledge portion of the 1906–07 course (pp. 69–70). Phenomenological inquiry into the aptness of knowledge cannot content itself with the pure, evident givenness of acts of knowing. Once adequation is at issue, and therefore the correlation of act and object, the object too must be included among phenomenologically evident data. The object of a perception of a thing is likewise a pure phenomenon — at least so long as one does not judge its reality as an object of nature, and takes the appearing thing as the correlative objecthood of the act of perception, that is, exactly insofar as it is given in a consciousness of that type.

The noema here is thus the phenomenologically reduced object, or better, the object exactly insofar as it is in each case intuitively given and intended in a phenomenologically reduced act (p. 70). As correlate of a punctual act, as the *cogitatum* of a punctual *cogitatio*, this noema is an "ontic phenomenon", not to be confused with the object that constitutes itself as a unity across a manifold of phenomena. The noema as phenomenologically reduced object can therefore designate on one side a pure constituting phenomenon and on the other an object-in-quotation-marks constituted unitarily in the regulated chaining of such phenomena. This amphiboly first appears in the summer 1907 course and will occupy the rest of the chapter. It is the first of the two confusions Bernet undertakes to disentangle, and it is internal to the theory-of-knowledge use of the term.

Restricting attention first to the noema as punctual ontic phenomenon, a whole series of questions arises from its first mention in December 1906 that will keep occupying Husserl for years, into *Ideen I* itself (pp. 70–71). Ontic phenomena are correlates of reduced acts of consciousness — objects of consciousness as such, including their eventual reality or unreality, their mode of being or not being. As objects of consciousness they are not autonomous objects of nature but inseparably bound to noetic phenomena of consciousness: the correlate of consciousness is inseparable from consciousness without being really contained (*reell*) in it; the lived experience of perception taken in itself contains in its essence "the perceived tree as such", the full noema. The greatest difficulty is then precisely to understand what still distinguishes noematic correlates, given only exactly insofar as one is conscious of them in the corresponding noetic acts, from those acts themselves. From the first mention it is clear that a punctual correlate can be claimed by the phenomenologist only insofar as it satisfies the same requirements as noetic acts, that is, insofar as it is an evident datum of phenomenological reflection. Determined as objecthood in the how of its being-meant, or as *cogitatum qua cogitatum*, it gives no ground for doubting its adequate givenness in reflection.

There remains the difficulty of determining an objecthood to be understood neither as a real (*reell*) component of the act nor as an object belonging to empirical nature (pp. 71–72). Husserl most often speaks of the *ideell* being of the noematic correlate when stressing what separates it from *reell* immanence, and of its *ideal* being when what is at issue is its difference from real things — a terminology maintained into *Ideen I* and the source of many confusions. Is the ideality of the "meant as such" the ideality of an essential generality? Husserl firmly rejected that reading, even for the "judged as such" of a punctual, individual act of judging. It is nonetheless natural to understand the judged as such as the signification of the judgment and to attribute to it a supratemporal validity that stays the same. But what of the meant as such — correlatively, the appearing as such — of a punctual perception of a thing? There seems little sense in determining this noematic appearance as something identical and supratemporal, even holding to its ideality or, as Husserl will later say less equivocally, its irreality. On closer inspection the situation in the correlation of judgment and judged is exactly the same as in the correlation of appearance and appearing: what an act judges in each case is temporally individualized exactly as the act of judging is. But when one reflects on the judged of a judgment one generally pays no attention to that temporal determination: what interests us is the content of the judgment, what has been said, not when it was said or whether it was said slowly or quickly. Interest in the said orients itself on the signification, on its identity and its adequation to actuality — which is precisely why the temporal individuation of the noematic correlate goes unnoticed.

Take it as established, then, that the noematic correlate of a punctual intentional act, in its full concreteness, is temporally individualized exactly as the act is (p. 72). This throws no light at all on its mode of being; on the contrary it raises a new question, whether this temporally individualized correlate does not after all belong to the act as a real (*reell*) component. Husserl's own formulation of the worry: confine ourselves to what we have in mind when we judge, say, that gold is yellow — can that be given otherwise than by the judging? Is it not a real component of the act of judging? For one is inclined to say that when I judge, this is present, "gold is yellow", and that is the phenomenon I possess; and it does indeed have its temporality, extending longer or shorter in phenomenological time according as I judge slowly or quickly.

Husserl's terminology eloquently attests the difficulty of holding at all costs to the perilous distinction between the mode of being of *cogitationes* and that of *cogitata* (pp. 72–73). To the *reell* immanence of acts is opposed the intentional immanence of their correlates; to *reell* inclusion, *ideell* belonging; to phenomenological, "phansiological" or "phansic" givenness, "ontic" phenomena; to the intentional act, the "intentional" in the objective sense; to perception, the "perceptional"; to the categorial act, the "categorial"; and from 1912, to the noesis, the noema — the term itself first appearing, so far as Bernet can tell, in the pencil manuscript of *Ideen I*, dated by Schuhmann to October 1912, whereas "noetics" had been in use since the *Logical Investigations*. Husserl also sometimes marks the peculiarity of the noematic correlate's givenness by calling it the object of an original reflection, and a 1910 manuscript goes further: attending to the profoundly different ways in which acts and lived experiences become objects, and in which intentionals or appearantials do, one is tempted to criticize the unitary term "reflection". This emphasis on the peculiarity of noematic givenness loosens the bond between noesis and correlate. Husserl still maintains in *Ideen I* that the correlate of consciousness is inseparable from consciousness and must therefore be characterized as an object wholly lacking autonomy — but he adds at once that despite this lack of autonomy the noema can be considered in itself, a statement in sharp contrast with the first mention of the noematic correlate, where dependence on the noesis was stressed so hard that Husserl had to conclude there is no separate ontic phenomenology.

All these declarations testify to Husserl's effort to maintain a clean distinction between the mode of givenness of the act and that of its correlate, but they open no perspective on the deep reasons for it (pp. 73–74). In the Husserlian literature — Gurwitsch, Føllesdal, Dreyfus, Smith and McIntyre — that distinction has almost without exception been legitimated by appeal to Husserl's own characterization of the perceptual noema as the sense of the perception: the perceptual noema would not be a real content of consciousness because, as an ideally identical sense, it is transcendent to each act of perception. But it has just been established that the perceived, precisely as implicitly given in an act of perception individualized temporally in each case, is in no way yet an ideally identical object of reflection such as the signification of a logical judgment would be. The standard defence of the noema's non-mental status therefore rests on assimilating two things Husserl himself distinguishes.

Although *Ideen I* handles the concept of sense with notorious offhandedness and takes little care to distinguish the forms of its ideality, Husserl already notes, at the very first introduction of the noema, that noematic correlates such as the perceived as such or the remembered as such — exactly as it is intended in remembering and comes to consciousness — can be called "sense" only in a much widened signification of the word (p. 74). The sense of the perception is the perceived as such, as it resides immanently in the lived experience of perceiving, as that lived experience offers it to us when we interrogate the experience purely in itself.

It seems impossible to determine the perceived, precisely as it is in that lived experience, otherwise than as an object of consciousness, an object in the mode of consciousness, and therefore as a content (pp. 74–75). As meant in the how of the meaning, it differs from every act of meaning both in determination and in mode of givenness, but it is nothing transcendent to consciousness. Even the noematic appearance — the object as it is itself intuitively given in each act of perception — is still a content of consciousness and as such belongs to the sphere of the mental. One easily understands why interpreters like Sokolowski refuse to call the object in the how of its punctual appearing something subjective or even mental: they appeal to the fact that modes of appearing concern the modes of being of the *perceived object*, not of perceiving consciousness, and argue that Husserl's characterization of appearance as the intuitive givenness of the object *itself* must not be confused with a characterization of appearance as a conscious being; the concept of self-givenness would break definitively with the old doctrine of the representation of the perceived thing in perceiving consciousness. That conception is certainly right for the object-in-quotation-marks constituted unitarily in the continuum of appearance: the thing constituting itself in a continuous perceiving is a phenomenological noematic datum transcendent to consciousness. It does not hold for the punctual noematic appearance. The latter's mode of givenness is a species of intuitive being-meant, and as such inseparable from the way each punctual act of perception intuitively lays claim to the object. So long as the intuitive givenness of the object in the punctual noematic appearance is determined — as it constantly is in Husserl — as a claim made by each punctual intuitive intentional act, that intuitive givenness cannot be anything but a mental content.

Considered for its philosophical content, this Husserlian conception raises many questions (p. 75). If in reflecting afterwards one orients oneself precisely on what one has seen and on its perspectival modes of appearing, one is interested in the object and not in mental processes — interested in the object's modes of givenness, or in Heideggerian idiom in its being as being-given. Naturally the same object can have different ways of being given, and naturally these depend on various subjective attitudes; but that dependence does not compel us to identify, as Husserl does, being-given with a particular mode of being-meant. This is Bernet's own objection, and it concedes the substance of Sokolowski's motive while denying that Husserl's text supports it: the exegesis goes against Sokolowski, the philosophy with him.

For Husserl the punctual noematic appearance that gives the thing itself is a content of consciousness and thereby a mental representative of the real (*real*) thing (pp. 75–77). But then, if the thing constituted unitarily across a plurality of appearances is likewise defined as a form of noematic givenness, the question arises whether it too must be defined — exactly as the punctual appearance was — as a content which, though not really immanent to consciousness, is nonetheless a conscious content. What is clear in any case is that the thing as constituted is essentially dependent on constituting consciousness both in its mode of givenness and in its mode of being. Experience itself "contains" the thing; the thing is nothing but a unity to be drawn from experience in order to be seen and determined from it, something immanent to experience in a widened sense, nothing beside or outside actual and possible experience. As object of conscious experience it is not really included in it but included as a unity of validity, and a unity of validity is what it is only in relation to the concatenation in which the validity is made. If the stream of consciousness is in its own way, then whatever else is or can be, is; nothing more is needed. And whatever we call a being but not consciousness is in the stream of consciousness, whence it must be drawn as a unity buried and rooted in it — which authorizes calling the source-consciousness absolute by contrast with relative being, which is being only in relation to consciousness. This 1908 manuscript stands in for innumerable passages to the same effect down to *Ideen I*; but its sequel says more clearly than the others that the essential dependence of the constituted thing on constituting consciousness changes nothing in its determination as an object of nature transcendent to consciousness. It would be fundamentally false to say that only absolute consciousness exists, as though everything else were illusion, appearance without reality, pure fiction: objects of nature are genuine objects, their being a genuine being, and it is fundamentally false to apply to that being any measure other than the one it demands in accordance with its category.

Many interpreters, and sometimes Husserl himself, yield to the temptation of this fundamentally false Humean idealism or phenomenalism (p. 77). For if, as is constant in Husserl, the being of the object is identified with its being-given and the latter with constituting consciousness, then nothing apparently justifies speaking any longer of the being of the constituted thing as a real (*real*), spatial being transcendent to consciousness. The thing *qua constituta* designates the object exactly insofar as it is given in a manifold of appearances; and those appearances are, as has been shown, conscious contents, noematic appearances included. Must the constituted thing not then be characterized as a conscious object too?

The answer is no, for two different reasons (p. 77). The two reasons are what keep the constituted thing on the far side of the line the punctual appearance falls on.

First: the constituted thing is entirely determined by the synthetic chaining of fulfilment across the manifold appearances, yet it is one with none of them (p. 77). It is rather the unity forming itself in such a synthetic chaining of appearing — an object determined ever further and ever more sharply in the course of the appearances, given in each and contained in none. As a noematic unity-structure of that type the constituted object, unlike the manifold noetic-noematic appearances, is no longer a datum temporally individualized by the stream of consciousness.

Second: as a real thing the object must be distinguished further from the synthetic unity of the various appearances just explained (pp. 77–78). The real thing is the unity of an infinite series of synthetically chained appearances, a series that cannot in principle be closed — what Husserl calls an Idea in the Kantian sense, the formally conceived idea of an adequate givenness of the thing, an idea regulative of the lawful course of the appearing manifold but realizable by no finite manifold of appearances. The real thing therefore transcends not only the conscious appearances taken one by one but also the noematic unity intuitively offered in a manifold of them.

The idea of a phenomenological constitution of the thing thus does not endanger the thing's transcendence to consciousness (p. 78). On the contrary, the unitary thing constitutes itself in the manifold chaining of appearance precisely as a being transcendent to consciousness. If the idealism of Husserl's theory of knowledge is so hard to understand, it is above all because on one side it identifies objective being with being-given to consciousness, and on the other determines the thing as a being that cannot be adequately given to consciousness. So too with the difference between immanence and transcendence, where the paths of Husserl's thinking are often too tangled to follow: he begins from the realist distinction current in the natural attitude, then attempts in idealist style to reduce transcendence to immanence, and finds himself forced to extend that immanence to the domain of noematic data, before finally returning to the transcendental-phenomenological finding of an irreducible difference between the transcendence of the spatial object and its immanent noetic-noematic modes of givenness.

The determination of the noema as phenomenologically reduced object is not free of equivocations either, though on closer inspection these do not affect the concept's coherence (pp. 78–79). The first source of confusion is Husserl's characterization of the punctual noematic correlate as "sense"; in any case the punctual correlate of each intentional act, though endowed with a certain form of ideality, possesses neither identity nor essential generality. Far more dangerous is the equivocation between this punctual correlate, individualized by consciousness, and the noematic givenness of the constituted unitary object. The constituted thing is indeed a form of noematic givenness, but the givenness of an object of nature, spatial and transcendent; the punctual correlate by contrast is a form of conscious being-intended with no spatial or physical property. The tree simply as such, the thing in nature, is in no way identical with the perceived-tree-as-such which as sense of the perception belongs to the perception and is inseparable from it: the tree simply as such can burn up and be resolved into its chemical elements, whereas the sense of this perception cannot burn, has no chemical elements, no force, no real (*real*) properties — everything distinctive of the lived experience in purely immanent, reduced form is separated by an abyss from the whole order of nature and physics, and no less from that of psychology. Interpreting this passage himself in the *Krisis* more than twenty years later, Husserl maintains that the punctual noematic correlate of perception, the perceived-tree-as-such, does not burn: to say it does is to speak nonsense, since one would be demanding of a component of a pure perception, thinkable only as a moment of the proper essentiality of an egoic subject, something that can only make sense for a body of wood. Unfortunately Husserl in both passages omits to say expressly that the noema conceived as constituted object can perfectly well burn, and that its capacity to burn must be understood as a real-causal, phenomenologically constituted property of the thing.

More than that: punctual correlate and constituted thing differ not only as two forms of noematic givenness of the phenomenologically reduced object but in playing essentially different roles in the process of the thing's intuitive constitution within a continuously unitary perception (p. 80). Punctual noematic correlates are constituting manifolds, things are constituted unities. And although in his early analyses of the constitution of the thing's noematic unity Husserl most often works with noetically conceived appearances, a purely noematic analysis of the constitution of the thing remains perfectly thinkable and is begun in the later work. The process is correctly described by saying that the thing constitutes itself "through" the noetic or noematic appearances, coming to self-givenness in phenomenological purity "through" or "in" the synthetic chaining of the manifold.

Matters look here as they do with the statement, which on Husserl's account attaches to its object "through" the signification (p. 80). Noematic appearance and noematic signification are both consciousness-dependent noematic data that refer beyond themselves to the unity of the object. But they differ in the form of ideality belonging to them, and above all in the form of their relation to the object. The noematic appearance is an incomplete self-givenness of the object, but an intuitive one, which phenomenologically determines and justifies the object's real being — which constitutes it. The noematic signification of a judgment, by contrast, characterizes the object by a predication but does not necessarily contribute to the phenomenological justification of that object's reality as referent. This asymmetry is what the second section will develop, and what *Ideen I* obscures.

#### The Noema as Ideal Signification of a Judgment

Just this clear determination of the difference between noematic appearance and noematic signification is what *Ideen I* lacks (p. 81). Three essentially different concepts of the noema keep crossing and even merging there: the noema as punctual correlate of an act, that is, as punctual noematic appearance; the noema as ideally identical sense or signification; and the noema as constituted unitary object. Exactly as with the distinction between the first and third, the *Nachlass* texts shortly preceding *Ideen I* are of great help with the distinction between the first and second. Many are now available in Husserliana XXVI, which contains the 1908 lectures on the theory of signification, where the noematic concept of the signification of a judgment is first systematically introduced.

The systematic context from which that concept comes must be seen clearly (pp. 81–82). The 1908 theory of signification differs essentially from the 1906–07 theory of knowledge, and reflecting on their difference yields a better understanding of what distinguishes the two concepts of the noema first expounded in those courses. In the theory-of-knowledge part of 1906–07 the noema appears in immediate connection with the phenomenological epoché, as the phenomenologically pure objective correlate, given in evidence, of a punctual intuitive act of knowing. In the 1908 course on signification there is scarcely any question of explaining the truth of knowledge and so of justifying the reality of its object, and consequently scarcely any question of the phenomenological reduction either.

The 1908 theory of signification is a phenomenological investigation of pure formal logic, in particular of its first task, the doctrine of the forms of the signification-categories implicit in analytic thinking (p. 82). What is discussed there are the modes of givenness of signification-forms in formal logic, especially the predicative signification of the judgment; and on the same plane the phenomenologist investigates how each act of judging is bound to the signification of the judgment and to the object it is about. The interest is in the distinction between act of judging and signification of the judgment, and in the relation, mediated by signification, of the judgment to the object. The object in question is the object as posited in the judgment, not the real state of affairs that may correspond to it; studying that correspondence is no longer the business of a phenomenological theory of signification but of a transcendental phenomenological theory of truth.

In the *Logical Investigations* the ideal signification of a judgment is determined as an act-essence individualized in the intentional matter of each act of judging, a determination Husserl will later criticize on the ground that ideal signification can be neither an identical noetic mode of intending a state of affairs nor, generally, an essence (pp. 82–83). The 1908 course takes over the noetic concept of signification unchanged but introduces as its correlate the ontic, noematic concept. Noematic signification is determined as the said as such of a statement, and as an objecthood given in reflection it is distinguished from the object simply as such: if in contrast with the object simply as such we speak of the object in the way it is signified or thought, then we see a mode of the thought as such, and out of that comes a new concept of signification, the categorial objecthood of the statement considered, as distinct from the objecthood simply signified. In the course of the lectures this categorial objecthood is also abbreviated as "the categorial" or "the propositional". The categorial is a reflective datum: Husserl speaks of a categorial reflection, grammatically determined as a nominalization. Noematic signification is therefore, exactly as Sokolowski describes it, the object of an after-the-fact propositional reflection that reflects critically on what has been said. But as an object of reflection the signification is not brought to life for the first time by that afterthought, as Sokolowski claims; as a datum implicit until then it is merely thematized — the categorial being determined as an objecthood of which we have, so to speak, an implicit consciousness, and which becomes the object spoken about thanks to a new orientation of the gaze, categorial reflection, and the nominalization resulting from it.

Noematic signification thus seems to be nothing other than the noematic correlate of a punctual act of judging, temporally individualized in the stream of consciousness (pp. 83–84). It is, exactly like the perceived as such, the remembered as such and every other form of punctual noematic correlate, *ideal* because not real (*real*) and *ideell* because not really belonging to the act of consciousness. Unlike the perceived as such, however, noematic signification is not a self-givenness of the actual object: one can understand the sense of a statement without holding it true. Every judgment, even false, even absurd, even if no truth and no possibility corresponds to it, has a content, has its signification. The noematic judged as such is consequently more ideal than the perceived as such, because not essentially tied to an actuality posited as real; and it is also more *ideell*, because more independent of the real content of the punctual act of consciousness — the partiality of the noematic appearance depends essentially on the punctual orientation of the perceiving subject, whereas the said as such can be understood independently of any relation to the punctual intention of the speaker. Consciousness is something temporal and flowing, which the *quid* is not, being a unity taken exactly as one is conscious of it in the world of significations.

Does this privileged form of ideality not also imply that the noematic signification of a punctual statement is an *identical* objecthood? (pp. 84–85.) Can a statement not be repeated without a new signification arising from each repetition; can different statements not have one and the same sense? Husserl's answer is of course yes, but the precise phenomenological determination of this ideal identity gives him much trouble. In the 1908 course he still partly conceives the identity of noematic signification on the analogy of the *Logical Investigations*' theory of signification as act-essence, so that grasping the ideally identical signification would require an ideating generalization of the noematic correlate of the punctual act of judging. Husserl paraphrases this idealizing seizure of signification from the individual categorial as follows: if we judge repeatedly that gold is yellow, we have several judgments but several judgments of the same logical content, and one can in any case, for logical purposes, understand the proposition so that it phenomenologically signifies something ontically identical, something to be taken up as an intentional objecthood, exactly like the categorial and its essence. But this noematic essence is not the only concept of noematic signification worked out in the 1908 course, and there is hardly any common ground between this noematic essence of the proposition, elaborated for logical purposes, and the said as such of a punctual judgment about an intramundane, non-purely-logical state of affairs. It is also doubtful that in categorial reflection on the said the identity of the said is expressly given from the outset; and it is finally implausible that every reflective consciousness of a noematic signification implies an act of ideation and thereby relates to the essence of the signification.

For all these reasons Husserl from 1910 begins to doubt the determination of signification's ideal identity as a noematic essence (pp. 85–86). The 1908 course already contains what Bernet takes to be the decisive idea: consciousness of an identity presupposes a synthesis of identification. Certainly in understanding what someone says, and especially in reflecting on what he has said, a certain consciousness of possible repetition, even of a new formulation of the said, is implied. But the ideality of noematic signification is explicitly given only by repetition, or by comparison of the different modes of givenness of that signification: when we judge that gold is yellow, we have a priori a consciousness in which the state of affairs so intended — better, the being-intended of that state of affairs — is given to us consciously in such a way that a new similar consciousness will be conscious of the same thing, the possibility of a genuine identification. The same possibility naturally exists for the perceived as such, but unlike reflection on the said it is certainly not implied *eo ipso* in reflection on the noematic appearance of a thing. The sense of a judgment is therefore not only more ideal and more *ideell* than the perceived as such but also more identical, that is, anticipated a priori as a possible identical datum. Husserl's calling the perceived as such the sense of the perception covers over this essential difference between the noematic appearance of a thing and the noematic signification of a statement; it is a terminological misstep — and no ground for praising him as a precursor of current developments in cognitive psychology, as Dreyfus and his disciples do.

Husserl's determination of the relation of the noematic signification of a statement to the object it is about has also occasioned polemics (p. 86). What is certain is that Husserl never contented himself, contrary to what Sokolowski seems to suppose, with identifying the object the statement is about with the object simply as such given prereflectively. Since signification is constantly determined by Husserl as an ideal moment of the intentional aiming at an object — every noema has a content, namely its sense, and by it the noema relates to its object — the after-the-fact reflection on signification must additionally bring out signification's relation to the intentional object. On the other hand Sokolowski is right, against Smith and McIntyre, that the object as given in reflection on noematic signification cannot in any case be the actual object, the object simply as such given prereflectively. The aporia is easily resolved by studying the 1908 course; the same solution is in *Ideen I* too, though less clearly articulated because embedded in theory-of-knowledge discussions aiming at a phenomenology of reason.

The 1908 lectures formulate the problem with all desirable clarity (pp. 86–88). If we perform an act of signifying, we intend the objecthood — which means that in one sense we intend the object simply as such and in another we intend the signification. Is this a kind of double vision? Naturally not: in an act of signifying, objects are not something *beside* significations; we can address ourselves to objects only by addressing them as determined in this or that way, as grasped significatively in consciousness. It is as significatively grasped, as determined thus and so, that the object belongs to the signification. But as the identical object of various noematic significations, that is, as object of various significative determinations, it at the same time transcends signification. The signified object is therefore in the same position as the identical signification itself: implicit in the punctual noematic correlate, but requiring a synthesis of identification to be grasped explicitly. The object the statement is about is the one determined as such and such by the said; a different said, and so different noematic significations, can still relate to the same object of the statement under different determinations. But the identity of the object common to the various significations can be expressly given only in a predication of identity linking the diversity of the said — a predication that first brings the sense of the relation to the object to expression. If we say that A is the same thing, the same object, as B, two representations and so two noematic significations are unified in a complete propositional act which intends and states that the object A is the same as the object B; thereby the sense of the relation of representation to object emerges for the first time, since the reflective discourse pronouncing the identity of the relation of the representations to the objects A and B receives its sense from the identity-statement into which the representations themselves enter, and since this discourse about the relation to the object necessarily refers back, in elucidation, to the identifications in which one and the same objectivity is given to consciousness as the same. The object as signified and as grasped in reflection on noematic signification is thus the identical unity-point, the identical to which the manifold determinations relate within a determining judgment-context: to judge is to produce such and such a statement about such and such things, in which the things behave as bearers of determinations, as identities.

From about 1911 Husserl calls these identical unity-points, these bearers of the manifold significative determinations, "X" (pp. 88–89). In *Ideen I* this X is named, entirely in accord with the 1908 course, the innermost moment of the noema — the unifying centre, the junction point or support of the predicates, necessarily to be distinguished from them though it cannot be set apart and separated from them. Within the reflectively grasped said, that is, within the noematic signification, the noematic proposition, the nominalized state of affairs, the object of the statement as indeterminate support or subject can be distinguished from the significative determinations or predicates. The much-cited and much-discussed sentence of *Ideen I* — every noema has a content, namely its sense, and by it the noema relates to its object — must therefore be understood as follows: within the said as such of each act of statement, given in a reflective nominalization, one can further distinguish and make explicit, by synthetic comparison with other noematic judgment-correlates, the ideally identical sense and the object as bearer of significative determinations. In short, sense and object are bound to the noema inseparably, but the punctual noematic correlate is not simply *identical with* the ideally identical sense — it only implies it, it "has" it; and the ideally identical sense is not the X, but relates, through its predicative determinations, to its bearer, its object. A 1921 manuscript puts it thus: the intended object is a moment of sense belonging to the proposition itself, nothing transcendent to it; in the categorial proposition we have a unity of sense in which the intended object has the character the intended predicates attribute to it, being the object intended *in* those predicates and intended in a way determined by them.

If the object intended "through" sense is characterized as a moment of sense, then the "through" — the mediation of sense — links the various ideal moments of the noema to one another, and not the ideal being of the signification to the real (*real*) being of the object (p. 89). What remains unclear is the exact determination of the ideality of the object = X, and the relation between the object = X and the actual state of affairs belonging to the empirical world. These two obscurities structure the rest of the section.

Exactly like noematic signification, the X is on one side a non-autonomous moment, implicitly given, of each said-as-such, and on the other transcends the givenness of the latter when explicitly grasped, in a subsequent synthesis of identification, as an identical unity-point (p. 89). Express consciousness of the X's identity is the product of the synthetic chaining of a diversity of ideally identical senses, which is why Husserl in the winter 1920–21 logic course ventures to characterize the X as a second-order ideal. Against that characterization stands the fact that the passage from the ideal identity of the sense to the ideal identity of the X has, logically speaking, the form not of a generalization but of a formalization. Nor would it be intelligible phenomenologically to say that the X is more identical than the sense: if the X is a moment of sense belonging to the proposition itself, nothing transcendent to it, then the identity of sense and the identity of the X cannot be distinguished by degree of generality.

As for the second difficulty, it is clear a priori that the X determined as a moment of sense can in no case be the object simply as such given prereflectively (pp. 89–90). The givenness of the X as an ideally identical moment of sense prejudges nothing about the truth of that sense, and so nothing about the actuality of a state of affairs corresponding to it. The identity-judgment that brings the X's identity to expression does not rest on establishing an equivalence of validity, since within the theory of signification the question is not whether the stated objecthood is in truth or not. In other words, the X as moment of sense cannot yet be the actual state of affairs that phenomenological analysis of constitution brings out: the intended of the judgment, the proposition, can be true or false, and only when it is true, when a judgment asserting that content is correct, do we say that a state of affairs corresponding to the judgment actually exists — and the actual state of affairs is not the true judgment, or better, is not the true proposition itself. Demonstrating the actuality of the state of affairs is no longer the business of a phenomenological theory of signification but of a phenomenological theory of knowledge, which attends to the intuitiveness of the saying and the said and grasps the actual state of affairs as the unity of various intuitive significations of the statement that mutually confirm one another in positing and determining the object.

The difficulty of distinguishing the X as moment of sense from the actual state of affairs does not arise only because the phenomenological theory of knowledge relates the sense studied by the theory of signification, and so the X, to the actual state of affairs (pp. 90–91). What creates difficulty above all is that in treating the theory of knowledge Husserl introduces a *new* concept of X: the X determined as the unity-pole of the actual state of affairs. "X" thus signifies on one side the identity-pole of noematic sense, intuitive or not, and on the other the actual state of affairs itself, to which the various rationally grounded positings of being relate. Since this actual object, as phenomenologically constituted object, that is, as phenomenologically reduced object-in-quotation-marks, is still characterized as "sense", confusion between the two concepts of X becomes inevitable. The intelligibility of *Ideen I* is gravely compromised by this amphibolic determination, though one passage makes the difference perfectly clear: "object" is everywhere a title for eidetic connections of consciousness, presenting itself first as the noematic X, the subject of the sense, implicated in different eidetic types of sense and proposition, and further under the title of actual object, where it is a title for certain rational connections, eidetically considered, in which the X that introduces unity in terms of sense receives a positing conformable to reason.

The difference in the treatment of the noema's relation to the object, according as one stands within a theory of signification or a theory of knowledge, does not amount to an insurmountable opposition (p. 91). The transcendental phenomenological theory of truth is rather a higher stratum of phenomenological considerations, presupposing the phenomenological theory of signification and completing it by studying the correspondence between the said and the actual state of affairs. The distinction between the two theories nonetheless keeps its legitimacy and value: it protects against confusing the moment of sense that stands out in reflection on noematic signification and bears the name object = X with a central moment of the fully determined state of affairs whose actuality is demonstrated by phenomenological constitution. And it elucidates the difference between the relation of noematic sense, through its predicative determinations, to the X belonging to it, and the relation of the statement to the actual state of affairs through true senses.

The permanent value of the distinction between theory of signification and logic of truth lies above all in its permitting the analysis of meaningful speech without concern for questions of truth (pp. 91–92). In such a phenomenological attitude the sense of speech is determined independently of whether an extra-linguistic reality corresponds to it. The objects one speaks about belong to the sphere of the said, of discourse, and owe their unity and identity to the context of everything said or that has been said about them. The relation to those objects is therefore mediated not only by the predicative determinations attributed to them but by the community of speakers and by the cultural tradition of their language. Husserl's noematic theory of signification, conceived as the stepping-stone to a transcendental-phenomenological elucidation of pure logic, turns out at the same time to be the fruitful first instalment of a hermeneutic understanding of ordinary language — a result that answers the chapter's opening question about Husserl and Frege by relocating it: the noematic signification is not a Fregean sense floating free of consciousness, but neither is it a mental content, and its identity is an achievement of repetition within a speaking community.

### 3. The World (Husserl)
