Husserl and the whole phenomenological movement after him never stopped interrogating the significance of the phenomenon of world, as the roster of world-compounds among phenomenology's basic concepts suffices to show: spiritual world (*geistige Welt*), life-world (*Lebenswelt*), surrounding world (*Umwelt*), home world (*Heimwelt*), world of normality, world of particular interests (*Interessenwelt*), universal world (*Allwelt*), annihilation of the world (*Weltvernichtung*), mundanization (*Verweltlichung*), worldhood of the world, flesh of the world (p. 93). The list is an argument in itself: a term that combines this freely has not been fixed as a concept.

The term's growing success has hardly served its clarification or its adoption as a philosophical problem (pp. 93–94). The fundamental questions of the world's appearing and of how the subject is implicated in it have too often been obliterated by the project of an exhaustive description of the life-world. Wanting to fit philosophical thought to the least details of our ordinary daily life has produced a phenomenology that says everything about the world and keeps silent about the essential. The essential is to know from where, from what standpoint, the subject attends the world's revelation — precisely what is at stake in the phenomenological reduction. The reduction can seem disconcerting in engaging the subject in a movement that turns it at once away from the world and toward the world; but a better understanding of the world's appearing is worth that price, and a phenomenology devoted to it cannot dispense with the reduction. By transcending the things of the world the reduction reveals at one stroke the world as world and the subject as subject. Taking the question of the world together with the question of the reduction makes it possible to rethink what a leading-back of the phenomenon to the subject means, and to grasp better how the subject contributes to the appearing of a plurality of worlds.

If what is at stake in the reduction is revealing the bond between the diversity of worlds and the subject's modes of life, and the persisting difference between the subject and "its world", then the reduction must be something other and far more than an outmoded methodological exercise (p. 94). Transgressing the closed world of our habits, witnessing the collapse of a cultural world, being excluded from a particular world that claims universality are ordinary existential situations, yet they offer a purchase on the phenomenon of world and therefore belong to the reduction. The reduction thus issues in the essential philosophical task of safeguarding the idea of a common and indefinitely open world, which is what human rationality is playing for. The reduction is here removed from methodology and given an ethical and political charge — a move that governs the chapter's third part.

In Husserl the analysis of the phenomenon of world and the doctrine of the reduction share the same fate, and nothing is more revealing of the shifts in his conception of the reduction than his growing attention to the world (pp. 94–95). He never renounced the conviction that a phenomenological philosophy is instituted only in a movement of thought that in some way turns away from the world; it is no less true that this withdrawal from the world will show itself ever more clearly to be a necessary detour on the way to discovering the world. That discovery signals no abandonment but the necessary outcome of the programme of the reduction. The point is not to stop thinking in order at last to begin living, but on the contrary to think the world as phenomenon. The phenomenological view of the life-world already belongs to another mode of life: the phenomenologist has woken from the dogmatic slumber of a life curled up in the world like a dog in its basket. Such life in the world forgets the world because the gaze sinks into the things of the world, which speak so loudly to our interests that they leave us no respite. Something must break this enchainment to things, this incessant *divertissement* as Pascal called it; the gaze must wander idle among things for the world to be able to appear. One and the same movement takes us out of the world of our preoccupations and reveals to us the order of the world.

#### I

Husserl compares the reduction to the poet's attitude in his letter of 12 January 1907 to Hofmannsthal, but the most powerful motive for exiling oneself from the world of things remains for him theoretical: understanding how the subject relates to a world (pp. 95–96). That is conceivable only if the subject is not itself a thing and the order of things is not foreign to the subject's life. What most radically distinguishes subject from thing in Husserl's eyes is intentionality — which is nothing other than the subject's relation to things. Intentionality therefore marks at once the difference and the bond between subject and things, and it reveals the world as the order of things, since that order appears only for a subject that relates to things without sinking into them. For Husserl this means that the order of things appears to the subject as an instituted order, shaped by it. The slide from a world *for* the subject to a world *by* the subject is made under cover of the reduction understood as withdrawal from things and openness to their appearing. It may be possible to think the being of things independently of the subject; it is not possible for their being-given. As phenomena, things offer themselves to a subject, their *logos* is unveiled to a subject, and the phenomenologist will be tempted to make that subject the holder, the instigator, even the founder of the *logos* of phenomena. On this way of thinking the world ends up appearing as the significant order of things as instituted by a transcendental subject that posits it as its counterpart. Instead of showing the co-belonging of the being of the world and the being of the subject, the reduction then risks demonstrating a world subjected to the subject — Bernet writes *dé-montrer* and *as-sujetti*, hyphenating to make "demonstrate" read as "un-show" and to hear the subject inside "subjected": the more thoroughly the world is exhibited as the constituting subject's own, the less it is shown.

This logic of subjection dominates Husserl's earliest reflections on the world, notably world-view (*Weltanschauung*) and annihilation of the world (*Weltvernichtung*) (pp. 96–97). If world-view testifies to the subject's subjection to the world, annihilation of the world consecrates the world's subjection to the subject. In a world-view the subject remains riveted to the empirical world and to the practical interests, urgent problems and vital preoccupations composing it. In the actual functioning of a world-view the world is not taken into account for itself; it appears as a set of things it is urgent to do, reasonable to hope, wise to believe. The world of the world-view appears as such only in a second moment, in a theoretical reflection usually animated by the project of comparing different world-views and their possible common ground. For Husserl this seen world is hardly better than the believed world, since the subject loses itself there by committing body and soul to a particular, provisional, self-enclosed world-view.

In the annihilation of the world of *Ideen I* the subject on the contrary disengages radically from the world by positing it as its counterpart (p. 97). The world of things becomes for the subject a pure object of contemplation, but under conditions such that this perfect visibility of the world also implies the possible disappearance of the world seen. The argument is familiar: if the reduction amounts to making every *esse* a *percipi*, then the world's being-seen depends on a seeing by the subject, whereas the subject, which can see itself without the world's help, depends only on itself. The subject's being therefore has a higher dignity than the world's, since it would survive the world's abolition while the world could not subsist independently of the subject's life. Yet the constituting subject's life does not suffice to secure the world's existence absolutely: the world is at the mercy of the harmony among the subject's lived experiences, and that harmony at the mercy of an always possible rupture. The effect is that of a maddening conjuring trick in which the pigeon pulled from the magician's hat turns into a magician who makes the hat and its unfortunate owner fly away. The pigeon of the affair is the subject, which after extracting itself from the world with great difficulty in order to get a good view of it looks on helplessly at the collapse of the world it so wanted to see.

When twenty years later Husserl vigorously criticizes the hypothesis of the world's annihilation, the immediate consequence is to raise world-view to the dignity of an original project of transcendental phenomenology, which has the power to actualize the correlation between the possibility of experience and the possibility of the world in the form of a *Weltanschauung* (pp. 97–98). The fault of the annihilation hypothesis had been precisely to sever that correlation between the seeing of the world and the world seen. The hypothesis runs contrary to the sense of intentionality, which ceaselessly carries the subject toward the things of the world and their appearing. It rests on the Cartesian-type ontological dualism between the evident being of immanent consciousness and the dubitable being of the transcendent world, a dualism that at bottom obstructs any understanding of what transcendental constitution of the world by the subject means. The constituted world is neither purely interior nor purely exterior to the constituting subject: if the sense of its being is said to depend on its being-given-for-the-subject, that does not mean the world's appearing is nothing but an immanent lived experience of the subject. The paradox of the annihilation hypothesis is that it ends by levelling the very difference between subject and world it set out to bring into relief — the world's existence is treated as though it were the reality of a single thing, given, confirmed or "exploding" in the course of a particular and limited experience, while the subject shows up in the deficient, indeterminate form of an immanent residue of the transcendent world's annihilation, that is, as a little scrap of the world (*Endchen der Welt*).

The annihilation hypothesis thus answers in no way to the programme of the reduction: it exhibits neither the phenomenon of the world, nor the transcendental being of the subject, nor the correlation and co-belonging of subject and world (pp. 98–99). It does not even deserve its name, since the world as exceeding the real existence of things is never taken into consideration. Where the world understood as the excess over the perception of things does announce itself in *Ideen I*, it is rather under the term halo (*Hof*) or, most often, horizon. The notion of horizon takes its sense from what Husserl calls horizonal intentionality (*Horizontintentionalität*), which at least in its beginnings remains marked by the concerns of a psychology of attention: everything given to me without my noticing it, without my granting it full attention, belongs to the horizon of the actual perception. Horizonal intentionality has multiple modes — the retentional and protentional horizon surrounding the present grasp of an object, active and passive motivation pushing me to perform further acts, to realize one possibility of experience rather than another, to widen or narrow my field of vision. It is thus responsible for the continuity of the subject's life: it anticipates future possibilities, preserves the memory of past acts, secures the passage from potentiality to realization, and integrates all acts into the continuous unity of a life, into the stream of consciousness. An essentially subjective principle, it nonetheless has its objective or noematic correlate — a field of vision in which some things are nearer than others, in which the appeal of merely co-perceived things grows more pressing, and in which the future courses of my gaze are faintly traced.

One hesitates to follow Husserl when he calls this noematic horizon a surrounding world (pp. 99–100). The horizon is more a potentiality of constituting life than an order of constituted objects. Rather than an indefinitely open frame into which things insert themselves to take on sense and come to meet us, the horizon is the particular and changing figure, the *Gestalt*, taken by the present, future and past data of my experience. To become a world the horizon lacks not only objectivity and permanence but the possibility of being held in common. A world, even the solipsistic world Husserl introduces for methodological reasons, lends itself to being shared with other subjects. A horizon cannot be shared, since it is nothing but the motivation carrying the constituting subject from one lived experience to another and correlatively from one object to another. The criterion Bernet applies here — shareability — is what will govern the rest of the chapter, and it is why the horizon cannot be the last word on world.

The most fruitful approach to the phenomenon of world is in the passages where *Ideen I* opposes to the objective reality of the natural sciences a world of appearances (*Erscheinungswelt*) or world of experience (*Erfahrungswelt*) (p. 100). This proto-life-world is the correlate of the open totality of possible experiences of an intersubjective community. One and the same movement of the reduction reveals the a priori conditions of possible experience and the a priori conditions of the possible world, since it reveals their correlation. Yet the possible world of experience still depends on the possibility of experience: it is experience that gives it its sense and its reality, the subject's experience that constitutes the world. The possible reality of this constituted world must nonetheless not be confused with the being of a *res*, a single thing; the being of the world is the being of every possible thing. If the being of the possible world thus transcends the possibility of particular things, then in strict consequence the being of the world has transcendental standing. The world prepares and receives the coming-to-being and the manifestation of things — but this gift and this office of the world were conferred on it by the transcendental ego.

The *logos* ordering this possible world is therefore of subjective essence (pp. 100–101). The lines of force running through and ordering the world are still understood by Husserl as motivational in nature: it is the bonds of anticipation between various possible experiences that govern this order of possible things which is the world. *Ideen II*'s analyses of the constitution of the spiritual world confirm it. That description is preceded there by an analysis of the constitution of nature, comprising physical objects and mental states so far as these remain conditioned by physical causality — a presentation that has served Husserl's design badly, since it suggests that beside the world of spiritual or ideal objects there is a world of physical or real ones. Nothing of the kind: for Husserl the totality of objects of nature does not deserve the name world, and natural objects are in no way excluded from the spiritual world. The determination of the spiritual world owes less to a metaphysical division between nature and spirit than to the phenomenon of *expression*, which precisely reunites them. Things belong to the spiritual world not by virtue of a purely objective essence but by virtue of a way the subject, or more precisely the person, has of approaching them — what Husserl calls the personalistic attitude. The spiritual world exists only as a world constituted by a community of persons; it is the common surrounding world of their exchanges and interactions, and in it things refer to one another not through causal links but through the place they occupy in the motivational fabric of personal life.

The characterization of the surrounding world in *Ideen II* remains unchanged in the writings of the twenties and thirties (pp. 101–102). The surrounding world is a limited world carved out of a broader and more universal one, and the carving is done according to the theoretical, practical and axiological interests of a person or a community of persons. It is the familiar world in which everything I meet already has a sense for me and for others. It is a significant world because it bears a spiritual sense in itself, but a significant world that remains open because it is forever capable of receiving new sense of that kind. Its significance (*Bedeutsamkeit*) is owed to the activity of a community of persons who take an interest in things not only to know them but to appraise their ethical or aesthetic value and to use them in practical production.

The signification of the things of the world is thus closely conjugated with the various modes in which the subject approaches them (p. 102). To knowing and in particular to perceiving, things give themselves as there-before (*vorhanden*); in acts of valuing, the object is given as having a value, as agreeable, beautiful; in practical acts, objects can be apprehended as serviceable (*dienlich*) to the satisfaction of such and such needs in virtue of such and such a property, and are then apprehended as means of subsistence or as objects of use of some kind — heating materials, axes, hammers. I see the coal as heating material, know it as serviceable and serving for heating, as suited and determined to produce heat. Husserl does not hesitate to stress that in the relation of persons to their surrounding world it is the practical dimension that has primacy: in the broadest sense the personal attitude can also be called a practical attitude. The passages deserve citing if only for their deep resemblance to *Sein und Zeit*'s analyses of the presence-at-hand and readiness-to-hand of the things of the world — the resemblance being, as the first chapter argued, a matter of priority rather than coincidence.

*Ideen II*'s analysis resembles *Sein und Zeit* further in that the surrounding world comprises both familiar things and the persons who occupy themselves with them (pp. 102–103). My surrounding world contains not only the real objects I actually experience and all the things in the world not yet known to me but experienceable, but also fellow human beings, animals, and the human being I designate as myself, the one bearing such a name and made in such a way — as a human being I am an integral part of the real surrounding world of the pure ego. If the surrounding world as the world of the significance of things is constituted by a pure ego, then this constitution of the world goes together with a mundanization (*Verweltlichung*) of the constituting subject itself. Or, in the rigorous formulation of the *Cartesian Meditations*: by the fact that I, this ego, have constituted and continue to constitute this world existing for me as phenomenon or correlate, I have also accomplished a mundanizing self-apperception making me an I in the ordinary sense of a human person situated within the totality of the constituted world. In its ordinary or, as Husserl says, anonymous life, the constituting subject is toward-the-world; it discovers its power to constitute the world only after having already been constituted as a mundane subject, a human person. The transcendental ego's mundanization of itself has always already taken place anonymously, and the world it constitutes has always already been there as pregiven.

*Ideen II*'s personalistic description of the surrounding world opens to phenomenology the rich domain of what Husserl will later call an intentional anthropology, to which many texts in Husserliana XIV and XV are devoted (pp. 103–104). This phenomenological anthropology studies the life of human beings in the world, and is called intentional because it is by way of intentional lived experiences and the tendencies or motivations attaching to them that people occupy themselves with things, meet one another, and relate together to the common frame of life that fully deserves the name life-world. That frame is not independent of the intentional experiences carried out within it, but neither does it reduce to them. All intentional relations of people to things and to other people are therefore mediated by consciousness of the world: in this world I know how to move in order to reach a distant thing and how to behave in order to collaborate with a colleague on a common work. Every comportment inscribed within this familiar world therefore relates intentionally to things and persons and at the same time exceeds them by understanding them in advance, a priori, from the world to which they belong. Intramundane comportments are thus doubly constituting: their intentionality carries them toward things and persons, and their transcendence reveals and shapes the world.

Within the intentional relation to intramundane beings, the relation to things and the relation to persons must nonetheless be clearly distinguished — a distinction, as with the difference between the intentional relation to intramundane beings and the transcendent relation to the world itself, *de jure* and not *de facto* (p. 104). In fact the human being who understands the things of the world and produces works enriches the world, which cannot fail to rebound on the sense of being-with others. If, as Husserl stresses from *Ideen II* on, the relation to the things of the world is first of all practical, then this being-with others takes first and foremost the form of interaction. In the facticity of common life within the world, interaction and the sense of being-one-with-another (*Miteinandersein*) precede empathy (*Einfühlung*). Empathy is therefore not at the origin of community; it is rather the symptom of community's collapse, the sign of a failure of understanding among intramundane subjects and the mark of a confrontation with the foreign. This inverts the order of the *Cartesian Meditations*, where empathy founds the intersubjective world, and it is what allows the next section to treat the encounter with the alien as world-disclosing rather than as a derivative complication.

#### II

In a text dated 10 January 1931, clearly marked by his reading of *Sein und Zeit*, Husserl describes being-in-connection-with-others as a way of living into the same world (*Hineinleben*) and caring into the same world (*Hineinsorgen*), constantly with one another, in the same world that becomes the world of our interests (*Interessenwelt*), the surrounding world of our practical life (pp. 104–105). In this way of living, I and the others belong ourselves permanently to the constituted world of objects, while this "we" is at the same time the subject of the world — a we that experiences the world, knows it, acts in it and produces results enriching its objective content. In virtue of the historicity of human Dasein, Husserl continues, this we must be understood as a historical community living in a surrounding world that is itself historical. That historical surrounding world bears a sense reaching this side and beyond the actual community of the we: it belongs to a historical world, the world of humanity as a whole, which is a persisting reality of a higher order maintaining itself through the change of persons, through their entering the world by being born into it and leaving it by dying out of it.

Through the constitution of intramundane objects — among which Husserl counts the communal we and the social and political institutions in which it is embodied — the world maintains itself and is maintained (pp. 105–106). If human beings contribute to that maintenance, it is as much by enriching the world as by entrusting it to new generations: one shapes a world received in keeping and transmits it to future humanity. If maintaining the human world is for human beings a task and even a duty, if the world is a willed *ethos* for which one feels responsible, then that responsibility concerns the future of a world one has received, or as Husserl says, that has been pregiven (*vorgegeben*) to one. The human being who exceeds itself in its care for the world exceeds itself toward what gives sense to its existence: exceeding itself, it opens onto the world, receives it in inheritance, becomes its ephemeral guardian. Its existence in the world thereafter resembles in nothing the presence of the things of the world, and the givenness of the world can no longer be confused with the presence of a thing made into an object of knowledge and property. This openness also radically changes relations with other human beings and with humanity as a whole: caring for the world that exceeds it, a community of men and women receives from it a new sense, inscribing itself in the indefinite chain running through the generations. The units of life bounded by birth and death are traversed by the stretch of the unity of humanity's life, the unity of all-human experience and, based on it, of tradition. So I understand humanity as historical, and understand how world-time reaches beyond my lifetime and that of my contemporaries.

The familiar surrounding world shared by contemporaries is not yet the historical world of humanity as a whole; yet the surrounding world can appear as such only by already gesturing toward the revelation of the world of humanity (pp. 106–107). Since the surrounding world transcends everything belonging to it, it can manifest itself only to a subject capable of transcending its commerce with the intramundane beings belonging to it — a subject for whom intramundane beings, things and persons, have lost their tranquil security, their purely immanent self-evidence; a subject at the edge of the familiar world, neither quite inside nor quite outside. Husserl gives this limit-situation an existential sense in speaking of entry into the world by birth and exit from the world by death. In birth the world welcomes the human being; the world is given as that gift Husserl calls pregivenness. During life one is generally too avid to seize the world's riches and too eager to impose oneself on those with whom they must be shared to pay much attention to that gift. Only in situations of crisis where the ground seems to give way underfoot does one experience all one owes the world. Yet one cannot fail to stand before the world at the moment of having to leave it in death — whether clinging to it desperately or handing on its gift to future generations.

Birth and death are events in which the familiar and the unknown confront one another in a shock, and so is the encounter with foreign cultures (pp. 107–108). Travelling in a foreign country, we do not only perceive things whose sense escapes us; we also recognize things familiar to us which here manifestly bear a different sense. Here again the familiar surrounding world, the home world (*Heimwelt*), reveals itself to a subject transported to its limit, to the point of intersection between its own home world and the foreign one. The home world is a symbolic system that makes the surrounding world appear in its contingency but also in its necessity, in a singular facticity that is nonetheless not unique, in an arbitrary yet unavoidable transcendence. In the home world relativity is raised to the rank of the absolute, and the arbitrariness of a symbolic order imposes itself as an unconditional law. Husserl notes that it is nonetheless possible to translate the foreign world into the idiom of my own, and even to raise oneself to the understanding of a single total world encompassing all these singular worlds. Still, that translation will not make the foreign surrounding world a home world for me, and even if a universal world of humanity exists, I could never quite make my dwelling in it. But if the encounter with a foreign world does not take me entirely out of my own, it is no less an invitation to take an interest in a more general world, that of humanity as a whole. Intercultural shock has this in common with death: it reveals at once the familiar world of a person or community and the universal world of humanity.

For Husserl the surrounding world and the universal world reveal themselves as forms of transcendence exceeding one's being-in-the-world, from which one nonetheless receives the full understanding of one's existence-in-the-world (p. 108). The transcendent relation to the world therefore deeply determines the sense of the being of the human being, and Husserl characterizes the life of this being-toward-the-world as a constituting activity that shapes and appropriates the world rather than genuinely producing it. If the world appears as such, and if the sense of the world is to be constituted by human beings, then it must be able to appear *as constituted* — which sets the phenomenologist the task of describing how and in what type of human comportment the world reveals itself as constituted. Husserl's many analyses of normality and abnormality in the apperception of the world answer precisely to that concern. Once again it would be in the relation to the abnormal, that is, to what is strange and foreign, that the sense of the being of the world would be revealed.

The abnormals to which Husserl attends are above all the mentally ill, children and animals (pp. 108–109). They belong to the same world as normal subjects and behave in it as subjects having a certain apperception of that common world, so they can in no case be considered mere intramundane things. Their abnormality comes from the fact that normal subjects cannot understand either how the common world reveals itself to them or how they contribute to shaping it. Their place in the world is obscure because they are recognized as subjects participating in the constitution of our world while the sense of that participation remains enigmatic. They are not subjects "like me" (*meinesgleichen*) with whom I can come to an understanding about the sense and destiny of the historical world we live in together, and their incomprehensible contribution has the effect that part of the sense of that world remains for me indeterminate and unknown. It is this patch of strangeness within my familiar world that by contrast makes the world whose sense I understand appear as *my* world, a world whose sense I can appropriate through constituting activity. The experience of this void-of-sense functions as a call to sense: the limited, self-enclosed and therefore inaccessible world of the abnormal reveals by contrast the normal world as infinitely open, as a work to be perfected, as the stake of an adventurous quest for sense. Rather than being the residue of the world's order, the disorder of abnormality provokes the active search for and constitution of an ordered world — above all the intelligible world presiding over rational exchange among human beings.

This constituted world of rational subjects then serves as the standard for measuring the deviation of abnormal comportments (pp. 109–110). A child's comportment is called abnormal because the child does not yet contribute to constituting the rational sense of the human world; maturation will be precisely the process of appropriating or reconstituting the sense of the world that will make the child my equal. Animals are a different case: they are in principle excluded from the rational world of human beings, since they constitute their own world varying with their species. Yet because the world of certain domestic animals integrates advantageously into the human surrounding world, Husserl envisages a partial animal contribution to the constitution of the human world: if animals are understood as relating to the world, the same world as ours, they may sometimes behave in a way that co-constitutes the world as world — when we understand the dog scenting game, it teaches us something we did not yet know and enlarges the world of our experience. This possible understanding of the animal's contribution to constituting the surrounding world, which is of course not yet the rational world of humanity as a whole, depends on a similarity between the animal and the normal human subject, and Husserl locates the appearance of that analogy, exactly as with another human subject, at the level of embodiment (*Leiblichkeit*).

On the mad Husserl is hardly prolix, though their case, and that of all comportments belonging to the unconscious, is particularly interesting (pp. 110–111). My parapraxes certainly contribute to constituting my world even if I do not recognize myself in them. Psychotic delusion, whose logic remains foreign to me, is nonetheless cut from the same cloth as my rational apperception of the world and is therefore capable of enlightening me about the sense of my world. One must resign oneself to admitting that there are human comportments whose sense I cannot appropriate although they appear to me as participating in the constitution of the human world. The unconscious and delusion constitute an improper world that is nonetheless neither beneath, nor behind, nor beside the proper world of rational subjects — Bernet's own study of delusion and reality in psychosis develops the point. This improper world erupts and unfolds in the very middle of the proper world. The proper world does not thereby become improper, but it appears as a world holed through by strangeness, as a haunted dwelling, as mined ground. The proper world of rationality is never an accomplished fact; its fragility is what gives it its worth.

#### III

The appearing of the human world — whether the familiar home world of a social group, the universal world of humanity, or the disquieting world of madness — is a phenomenon resembling no other (p. 111). If it is hospitable, its hospitality is revealed above all when one is deprived of it by anxiety or death; the same holds for its rationality, which appears only when threatened. Must one then conclude to a dramatic essence of the world, or to a separation between the human being and the world of things it shares with others? That is what Heidegger seems to do at the time of *Sein und Zeit* when he opposes the phenomenon of the surrounding world to the phenomenon of the world.

For Heidegger the surrounding world is the world of Dasein's everyday life, what Husserl calls the life-world (pp. 111–112). In ordinary life Dasein is absorbed by the things of the world, and its incessant preoccupations leave it no leisure to ask after the sense of the world as such. Its relations with other human Dasein fall within those preoccupations and issue in associations of collaboration. If in the course of its occupations Dasein raises its gaze toward the surrounding world, that too is to assure itself of the arrangement of things and persons with a view to the efficacy of its future action. The surrounding world is taken in view in circumspection (*Umsicht*), and this circumspection is a proprietor's round confirming that nothing the familiar world contains is missing from the roll-call. Among the things of that world are Dasein itself and other human Dasein: without confusing things and persons, the busy circumspection of concern nonetheless embraces them in one and the same glance as being in the world. So Dasein does see the world and not only intramundane beings, yet it remains too riveted to its worldly preoccupations to consider the world as such. To make the world appear as such, the movement of daily life must be suspended; one must leave the surrounding world or at least transport oneself to its limit.

*Sein und Zeit* analyses this exit from the surrounding world that makes the world appear as world — the worldhood of the world — by way of a phenomenological description of anxiety: anxiousness discloses the world as world originarily and directly (p. 112). In anxiety the familiar surrounding world collapses and the world imposes itself on Dasein in all its strangeness. Anxiety brings Dasein back from its fallen absorption in the "world"; everyday familiarity collapses; Dasein is individualized, but as being-in-the-world; being-in takes on the existential mode of the not-at-home (*Un-zuhause*), and the expression uncanniness (*Unheimlichkeit*) means nothing else.

This analysis instructs us exemplarily about the ambiguities of an exit from the world that brings the world to manifest itself as world (pp. 112–113). First, the world one exits and the world that manifests itself as world thanks to that exit are different, a difference registered even terminologically: one exits the surrounding world and finds oneself before the world. Second, the world as revealed in anxiety concerns only the being of Dasein itself; exchanges with intramundane beings, things or persons, are suspended, and Dasein's understanding of the surrounding world and of the being of other beings no longer holds — the world and Dasein's being-in-the-world are the nothing of readiness-to-hand. Third, Dasein's world as manifested in anxiety isolates Dasein from everything it is not, even from another Dasein: the strangeness of this world is a proper and not a foreign strangeness. Heidegger does insist on the gift-character of the world, on its imposing itself on Dasein by affecting it, in that eminent disposedness (*Befindlichkeit*) or mood (*Stimmung*) which is anxiety. Still, the isolation in which Dasein accedes to the proper understanding of its being makes anxiety an auto-affection: if Dasein receives the revelation of the world as world rather than constituting the world, it can nonetheless receive the gift of the given world only from itself. The point is the same one made against Husserl — a world revealed only by the subject to the subject is not yet a world given — and it prepares Bernet's own thesis at the chapter's end.

There is a striking analogy between *Sein und Zeit*'s description of the world's revelation and Husserl's analysis (pp. 113–114). The world appears as such only when its familiar presence becomes questionable or threatens to slip away, and it appears only to a subject that has distanced itself from it or to whom it begins to be lacking. Heidegger's anxiety therefore does accomplish a kind of phenomenological reduction or even an annihilation of the world, as Courtine established. The revelation of the world depends on a subject transcending its immediate environment and its being-in-the-world, and this essential subjective contribution allows the world to be conceived as constituted, or as formed or figured (*gebildet*) by the subject — Heidegger's own formula being that the human being is world-forming. The world reveals itself only in favour of a specific attitude or comportment of the subject, and reveals itself as having already been marked by the subject. These analogies must not disguise notable differences in how Husserl and Heidegger envisage the revelation and formation of the world by the subject, and the nature of the bond uniting subject and world — differences on which their collaboration over the *Encyclopaedia Britannica* article foundered, Heidegger's question to Husserl summing up the dispute: does a world in general not belong to the essence of the pure ego?

On how the world's appearing depends on a specific attitude of the subject, *Sein und Zeit*'s position is further from Husserl's than it first seems (p. 114). For Husserl the world's manifestation depends on a subjective decision: taking the world into account as such is also a taking charge that appeals to the subject's will and responsibility. For the author of *Sein und Zeit* too the revelation of the world requires that the subject exist authentically, but authenticity is no longer a matter of voluntary commitment or deliberate effort. In anxiety the subject undergoes the manifestation of the world's worldhood; it is literally submerged by it. The world's appearing paralyses the subject rather than inspiring in it a detached attitude of studious curiosity. No better illustration of the dread seizing the subject at the world's appearance can be found than Levinas's description of insomnia: in the heart of the night's silence, in the darkness depriving it of the presence of familiar things, the extraordinary but impersonal evidence of the "there is" imposes itself on the child, of being that is ceaselessly, imperturbably. Unbearable lightness of being perhaps; cruel anonymity of the world certainly.

But the world also gives itself in less hostile forms, and the subject can receive its gift with gratitude and happiness (pp. 114–115). Patočka echoed this sublime gift of the world from his earliest writings and stayed faithful to the intuition when everything in the world seemed to have conspired against him. If the world's manifestation demands movements tending toward the limits, that does not necessarily mean the subject is thereby crushed and isolated. Open comportments such as acts of devotion, the artist's activities or the thinker's lead back behind everything that exists, in order to draw from there a possibility of being human other than the everyday; what is thus effected is a change of world and in no way of its content. These movements are characterized by the fact that one can dwell in them, that there is in them a halt or rather a sojourn — the philosopher in astonishment, the artist in admiration, others in hatred, some in love. In love, for instance, it is possible for a human being to become, for another thrown claim, a hearth of warmth transforming the anxiety of the uninhabitable into a possibility of acceptance: without acceptance there is no human existence, since the human being is not only thrown into reality but also accepted.

The human being accepted by the world receives that world as a gift (pp. 115–116). There is no gift without giving and without a giver, no given world without the encounter with other human beings and their works. But it is no less true that a work of art which genuinely opens a world gives far more than what the artist meant it to say. So too the given world is rich with a signification going well beyond what a person, or even humanity as a whole, has lent it. Husserl was not insensible to the extraordinary phenomenon of the pregiven world; but for him this world given to us as having already been there, which gives itself after having already received and accepted us, is envisaged only as a world whose sense has been constituted by subjects preceding us in history. The world manifesting itself as pregiven offers us a sense that other subjects, known or unknown, explicitly or anonymously, have conferred on it. The pregiven world must accordingly not be confused with the pregiven hylē: the latter is a formless sensible datum preceding all acts of constitution, whereas the former is the sedimentation of earlier acts of formation and constitution. Which is precisely the limitation — pregivenness in Husserl is always someone's prior achievement, never a gift from elsewhere.

Husserl's notion of constitution does not permit the full measure of the world's transcendence to be taken (p. 116). It leads him to exaggerate the importance of what the subject does for the world's manifestation and at the same time to underestimate what the world's manifestation does for the appearing of the subject's being. New discoveries such as noematic phenomena, passive and anonymous constitution, and horizonal intentionality have hardly shaken his idea of constitution as a subjective accomplishment (*Leistung*) issuing in a constituted taken as its objective result. Husserl's intentional anthropology does seem to move away from this frame governed by the dual opposition of subject and object, describing forms of the human person's being-in-the-world that exhibit an indissoluble interlacing of subject and world; but the sense of that analysis depends on a transcendental resumption which puts to work a pure ego that constitutes the world and at the same time constitutes itself as a person in the world by mundanizing itself. The being of the world, the subject's being-in-the-world and their interlacing then appear as phenomena placed before the impartial spectator that is the transcendental ego, which op-poses to itself a world as the objective correlate of its subjective positing. Having lent everything to the world, the subject only recovers its own in understanding the world's sense; the pregiven sense of the world is a sense returned to the heirs of the subjects who constituted it, and to understand that sense is to take it back. Disposing of the world's sense, the transcendental subject nonetheless knows the poverty of those whose wealth makes them incapable of receiving an unexpected gift. The concept of a constituted and appropriated world thus screens off the revelation of a world that is foreign, different from the subject and yet infinitely close.

If the revelation of the world cannot be reduced to understanding a historically constituted sense, Husserl nonetheless provides a valuable analysis of the taking-charge of the historical world (pp. 116–117). For him a correct understanding of the world's sense issues inevitably in practices that preserve and enrich the given sense of the world in order to transmit it to future generations, making the world more human and more rational. Nor is he wrong to present openness to the idea of a universal world as a task engaging the philosopher's social responsibility. In opening the familiar world onto the world of humanity as a whole, Husserl avoids the extreme solutions of reducing the plurality of home worlds to the unity of a universal world and of simply abandoning the rational idea of human equality for cultural relativism. This philosophical idea of a universal world has of course nothing in common with applying a one-dimensional technological rationality to every country: making everyone a citizen of the world in no way implies uniformizing the styles and contexts of human life. On Husserl's view the philosopher's most precious contribution to the common work of constituting the world is to open the march of history onto the *telos* of a world rational through and through. But there are many other ways of participating in constituting one's world by modifying, enriching, preserving, widening and improving its sense, and all of them issue in a revelation of the world accompanied by satisfaction and gratitude for what humanity has brought to the world over its history. Must one conclude that everything revealed in the world's manifestation is revealed as a sense constituted by human beings? And where the world does manifest itself so, is that enough to warrant saying I can wholly appropriate its sense? Is every sense of the world a sense constituted by human beings, and is every constituted sense the result of a deliberate action lending itself to reactivation with perfect fidelity?

The world revealed in birth and death, in anxiety, insomnia, devotion and admiration, in hatred and love, cannot be reduced to the world civilized and tamed by human beings (pp. 117–118). The world often appears to us in an inhuman form, fundamentally strange or savage. That does not mean it does not concern us or that it reveals itself as wholly resistant to any attempt at humanization. On the contrary, the appearance of an inhuman world calls for human work, but it circumscribes that work's power in advance. The forming and constituting of the world are preceded and guided by a manifestation of the world that reveals the essential finitude of human capacity. Rather than revealing itself as the work of human beings, the world reveals to them the finitude of their transcendence. The appearance of a world never entirely human reveals the limits of what one can do for the rationalization of the world and of what one can do to become oneself a fully rational being.

The appearing of the world breaks the identification of human beings with their familiar world (p. 118). The revelation of the world is always the revelation of the difference between subject and world. Difference does not mean separation: the subject that receives the revelation of the world exists neither outside nor independently of the world. But neither does it belong entirely to the world, else it would be a thing and not a subject for whom the world appears. The difference between world and subject excludes neither their interdependence nor even their interlacing. Thinking difference and interlacing together means thinking an interdependence without symmetry and without reciprocity. The world reveals itself at once as inhuman and foreign and as in need of humanization and appropriation by the human subject. The subject, discovering itself as subject, discovers itself different from the things of the world while living in the world; it discovers itself different from the world without being able to withdraw from its grip. Whoever works to form and transform the world is inhabited by the conviction that he cannot do otherwise without ceasing to be human — and it is precisely in so acting that he takes the full measure of the world's irreducible strangeness and savagery. It belongs to the nature of the care that carries one to take charge of the world's sense never to be entirely reassured about the world's fate.

## II. Perception

### 1. Finitude and Teleology of Perception (Husserl)

Husserl's phenomenology remains dominated by teleological motifs, in the philosophy of animate nature as much as in the philosophy of spirit or culture (p. 121). Animal life and human life — the life of the transcendental subject — tend toward an end. For Husserl the development of living organisms, the life of human communities, and the history of philosophical thought are unintelligible apart from the teleological anticipation of a final order of sense representing an ideal of perfection.

Such a way of thinking does not enjoy much favour among our contemporaries (p. 121). Husserl's attachment to teleology appears to them either as the sign of an outmoded rationalism or as the testimony of a touching but fundamentally naive optimism. More subtly: teleology seems either the symptom of a metaphysics of presence misrecognizing human finitude, or the expression of a philosophical faith that shatters the frame of a phenomenological theory of knowledge. The second, subtler pair is the one Bernet takes seriously, and the chapter is built to answer it.

Bernet proposes to meet these criticisms on their own ground by examining the role of teleology within the phenomenological analysis of knowledge, and more precisely of the sensible perception of a spatial object (pp. 121–122). If teleology has its place in traditional discourse on the philosophy of history or of ethical life, its presence within a theory of knowledge is surprising. Analysing the teleological motif in Husserl's gnoseology will at the same time prove that the appeal to teleology is far from a late speculative addition to his work. The choice of terrain is thus strategic: perception is where teleology should be least at home, and where its necessity, if shown, is hardest to dismiss.

The teleological anticipation of an ideal of knowledge is governed by attachment to the idea of an adequate presence of the object known — but this in no way excludes an acute consciousness of the finitude of human knowledge (p. 122). Husserl's epistemology is traversed throughout by the concern to do justice at once to the desire for absolute knowledge and to the limited possibilities of intuitive understanding in the human being. That double concern is expressed most clearly in the determination of the teleological anticipation of an *ideal* of human knowledge. Far from representing a denial of the paradoxical structure of human knowledge, this ideal refers on the contrary to a praxis of theoretical knowing whose source is an ethical will. The theoretical ideal of knowledge thus remains tributary to a practical end bearing on personal responsibility, which is defined by the will to take upon oneself and give account of the tension between the desire for unlimited knowledge and the limited means available for attaining it. The responsibility of the finite knowing subject consists for Husserl in facing that tension and thereby taking on the accomplishment of an infinite task.

At the level of sensible perception this tension is bound to the phenomenon of perspectivity, the contrast between the totality of a fully determined thing-in-itself given without remainder and its partial, provisional appearance or adumbration (pp. 122–123). In treating this relation between the thing itself and the way it appears for a finite, embodied subject riveted to a particular point of view, Bernet makes no claim to exhaust Husserl's analyses of visual perception, its insertion into the complex structure of a synaesthesia, or its dependence on the kinaestheses of a carnal subject. His procedure is at once more limited and more formal, and has three stages.

First, to determine the precise sense of the ideal toward which perceptual knowledge of a spatial object tends (p. 123). It must be shown how such knowledge can be oriented toward the adequate originary givenness of its object when it belongs to the phenomenological essence of that object to render such a mode of givenness impossible.

Second, to illustrate the teleological function of this ideal within the visual perception of a spatial thing (p. 123). It must be shown how the teleological anticipation of the thing-in-itself effectively functions as the fundamental, dynamic principle of the living organization of the process of sensible knowledge.

Third, to make explicit the practical sense of this ideal of knowledge (p. 123). It must be shown that the sense of an unrealizable end, or of an indefinite progress of the effort of knowing, is intelligible only if one takes into account the human subject's concern to give its life a form answering to the norm of a final responsibility.

#### I

The first step already presents a difficulty that may appear insurmountable (pp. 123–124). On one side, inadequate knowledge or the partial intuitive givenness of a transcendent object seems to presuppose a total understanding of that object which would therefore escape the finitude of its givenness. On the other, belief in the existence of such a fully determined transcendent object or thing-in-itself must be bracketed by the reduction, since it constitutes a dogmatic presupposition of the natural attitude. Phenomenology puts an end to the Kantian conception of a noumenon as the hidden and therefore non-phenomenal cause of appearances. If the concept of a thing-in-itself keeps a valid and even indispensable sense for transcendental phenomenology, that thing-in-itself must manifest itself at the level of phenomena rather than being opposed to them. The *Logical Investigations* and *Ideen I* both strove to elaborate such a purely phenomenological determination of the thing-in-itself toward which the various partial appearances converge, but their solutions differ. By turning away from epistemological realism, *Ideen I* overcomes the lingering traces of an explanation of knowledge of reality by interposed images — the image-theory (*Bildertheorie*) — that survive in the *Investigations*. But this progress toward a better phenomenological formulation of the ideal presiding over knowledge of transcendent objects is paid for by a certain sublimation of that ideal, which passes directly from the status of a material object to that of a subjective aspiration. For *Ideen I* the final end of knowledge consists less in the perfect givenness of the object than in the indefinite progress of knowledge itself.

The *Logical Investigations* analyse the relation between appearance and appearing within the concept of representation (*Repräsentation*), which owes much of its phenomenological significance to Husserl's critique of the image-theory (pp. 124–125). Among Husserl's contemporaries it was above all Twardowski who, under Brentano's influence, defended such a theory, otherwise well known since the scholastics; Twardowski hoped it would help solve the much-debated problem of objectless representations. On that theory each act of consciousness or representation would have an internal intentional content to which a real object of the external world might correspond, so that the intentional object would be a content inherent in consciousness playing the role of representative or image of the transcendent object. Husserl's incisive criticism is familiar: image-consciousness presupposes that the original depicted by the image can itself be perceived, and the doubling of the intentional object into mental content and external object leads to an infinite regress. Intuitive representation or perception must therefore not be confused with a substitutive representation in which the object belonging to reality-in-itself would be present in consciousness as a perfectly resembling image. Husserl never tires of repeating that the intentional object of intuitive representation is nothing other than the real object, and that the reduction — while determining the reality of that object in terms of the intuitive character of the representation or intentional consciousness relating to it — takes nothing away from its external transcendence.

Where a single appearance or intuitive representation suffices to secure the originary givenness of a fully determined intentional object, the representation is *adequate*: the intentional act enjoys such intuitive fullness that appearance and appearing, representing and represented, coincide totally (pp. 125–126). An inadequate intuitive representation such as the perception of a spatial thing can claim no such purely or totally intuitive givenness. The intuitive appearance of a spatial thing always remains partial: given originarily and in the flesh (*leibhafte Selbstgegebenheit*), the thing is not fully given. Its intuitive givenness remains surrounded by an "empty" givenness, by intentions devoid of intuitive content. In the *Investigations* this empty component of intuitive representation is called the *signitive* content of the perception of a thing, so that the appearance of a thing puts to work a mixed form of representation: some parts of the intentional object are represented intuitively, others only emptily — more precisely, signitively and imaginatively.

In determining the signitive component of the intuitive perception of a spatial thing, Husserl is manifestly inspired by the way the sense of an expression is represented by a linguistic sign (p. 126). This leads him to assume that the relation between the intuitive and the signitive components of inadequate perception is as external as that between signified and signifier, so that what unites the two components — the intuitive and the non-intuitive datum — would be a relation of mere contiguity. This introduction of the sign, or at least of its mode of functioning, into perception plainly raises more questions than it settles. It goes without saying that the relation between the intuitive and the empty component within an intentional act of perception has nothing external or arbitrary about it as the relation of signified to signifier does. It is equally implausible that the perceptual apprehension of a thing's invisible face should have the form of a signitive representation. What I see of the thing without really seeing it is the object of a possible seeing: what is in question is the anticipation of an actual perception and a possible originary givenness, not the interpretation of a sign — of which one cannot see how it could have slipped into a perception that Husserl elsewhere says has direct access to the thing itself.

For want of having recognized in the empty component of perception the structure of the anticipation of a possible perception, the *Investigations* end by succumbing to the demons of an image-theory they believed definitively exorcized (pp. 126–127). This appears most clearly where Husserl speaks of a relation of resemblance or analogy between the perceptual appearance of a thing and the thing itself. For there to be resemblance there must be the possibility of comparison; but the possibility of comparing the partial intuitive appearance with the fully determined thing or thing-in-itself is explicitly suspended by the reduction. The reduction consists precisely in the wager of trusting entirely to phenomena, to appearing, in determining the sense and being of things, and this presupposes that phenomena are at least partially identical with things and that things are themselves present in phenomena. A partial identity between phenomenon and thing, such as characterizes the perception of a spatial thing, must not be confused with a relation of mere resemblance. To say that the partial appearance or inadequate perception resembles the thing presupposes a knowledge of the thing-in-itself no longer dependent on its partial givenness — a dogmatic knowledge whose currency the reduction has precisely suspended.

Bernet does not intend to expound in detail the aporias to which this conception of the perception of a spatial thing as a mixed representation leads, but he does want to understand its reason (pp. 127–128). Two possibilities: either a concept of the thing-in-itself is borrowed from the natural attitude and its dogmatic presuppositions, or appeal is made to a phenomenological and therefore critical concept of the thing-in-itself which nonetheless does not suit the inadequate perception of a spatial thing. There is good reason to believe the second hypothesis — the more honourable for Husserl — is correct.

The analysis of inadequate perception in terms of mixed representation seems to follow from a conception of adequate representation drawn from *internal* perception (p. 128). Inadequate representation would thus be measured against the yardstick of an adequate representation in which representing and represented are not merely identical but coincide totally. Husserl was not unaware that in the case of a spatial thing such a coincidence between perception and perceived is not only unrealizable in fact but excluded by right, even for a divine subject. Yet this seems not to have prevented him, at least in the *Investigations*, from resorting to the model of adequate givenness to determine the ideal toward which the perception of a spatial thing would tend. Since such adequate representation is realized only in the internal perception of consciousness by itself, the inadequacy of external perception would then be its failure to be an internal perception. Seduced by the advantages of consciousness's reflection on itself and by the use phenomenology can make of it, Husserl seems for a moment to have forgotten that it is absurd to suppose external perception could be guided by an ideal belonging to the form of internal perception. The concept of the true being of the spatial thing — the ideal datum toward which the process of external perception tends — cannot be borrowed from a domain of being situated outside space.

How then is the phenomenological sense of the thing-in-itself of an external perception to be understood, and how is the ideal of an adequate givenness of the spatial thing to be kept from flagrant contradiction with its eidetic determination? (pp. 128–129.) What will be *Ideen I*'s solution is already outlined in the aporias of the *Investigations*. Their failure comes from the fact that their concept of the thing-in-itself of external perception contradicts the necessity of a partial or perspectival givenness of the spatial thing. This incompleteness or finitude of givenness must therefore be inscribed within the very concept of the thing-in-itself as anticipated in the process of external perception. One must account for that thing-in-itself while guarding against untying it completely from the ontological condition of what gives itself through partial adumbrations, and equally without identifying it with such a partial adumbration.

*Ideen I*'s solution is prepared by passages showing explicitly that no perception whatever can grasp the whole of the thing and the whole spatial thing (p. 129). The same conviction already emerges in the 1907 course, where Husserl demonstrates that adequate knowledge is impossible even where perception addresses only the fully visible face of a spatial thing, and that the idea of an *intuitus originarius* of a spatial thing is therefore wholly unacceptable to the phenomenologist. A second premise of *Ideen I*'s solution is that this text understands the finitude of the perception of a spatial thing less in terms of the lack of a total givenness than in terms of the unlimited character of the multitude of appearances relating to the same thing. A perception or appearance is called inadequate because it is inseparable from an empty intentional horizon containing the anticipation of other possible and complementary appearances of the same object. One can therefore prolong perception as long as one likes, progressing from appearance to complementary appearance and thereby confirming the thing's determination and existence; this does not prevent the emergence of a contradictory appearance, which could go so far as to cross out the result of the whole preceding process, from always remaining possible.

The inadequacy proper to all knowledge of a transcendent object is thus itself a phenomenological datum, manifesting itself as the impossibility of closing off, of bringing to a definitive term, the progress of that knowledge (pp. 129–130). The phenomenological concept of the thing-in-itself that measures the advance of the perception of a spatial thing must therefore necessarily include a reference to the fact that this perception is essentially open onto an unlimited progress.

That is more easily said than done (p. 130). For if the idea of the thing-in-itself is inseparable from the accomplishing of an indefinite experience, then it seems it can never be adequately given; and if the adequate givenness of the idea of the thing-in-itself is indefinitely deferred, how can it guide the perceptual process by serving as its *telos*? Husserl's ingenious answer is elaborated in two moves. First, he insists that the thing-in-itself must not be confused with the thing as it progressively constitutes itself in an unlimited perceptual process. Second, he specifies that the thing-in-itself is a mere idea whose paradoxical status consists in regulating the perception of a spatial thing without ever being given in the manner of a spatial thing. In this regulative idea serving as *telos* to a process of experience without that process being able to appropriate it, one recognizes the structure of what Husserl himself explicitly calls an Idea in the Kantian sense. The adequate givenness of a spatial thing that orients the perceptual process teleologically is therefore an Idea and not an object, as the *Investigations* still supposed.

What does this mean? (pp. 130–131.) Above all it permits the reconciliation of what first appeared irreconcilable: the indefinite character of the perceptual process and the adequate givenness of the thing-in-itself. The thing-in-itself is an idea lending itself to adequate intuition, but this idea is the idea of an indefinite progress of perception. That adequate givenness of the idea of an indefinite progress of perception is what must make intelligible how the thing-in-itself is the dynamic principle regulating the perceptual process without ever letting itself be encompassed within it. The trick is a displacement of the object of adequacy: what is adequately given is not the thing but the necessity of the endlessness of its givenness.

#### II

It is certainly appearance (*Erscheinung*) that represents the key concept of a phenomenological analysis of perception (p. 131). For phenomenology this appearance is neither a mental image nor a sign whose real object would be its natural cause. As the originary self-givenness of the thing itself, the appearance *is* the spatial thing that appears; but as a partial givenness it is not, or at least it is never quite identical with the thing. Husserl tries to account for this double determination by holding that every appearance of a spatial thing contains a proper component (*eigentliche Erscheinung*) and an improper one (*uneigentliche Erscheinung*). In noetic formulation this means the appearance is an intentional act composed of a purely perceptual content and an apperceptive surplus lacking intuitive fulfilment. The same can be expressed noematically by saying that the appearing is an intuitive datum surrounded by an empty horizon anticipating the possibility of other intuitive data in which the same spatial object would come to appear (pp. 131–132). The noematic formulation seems to have the advantage of standing closer to the phenomena and of dispensing with a terminology — apperceptive interpretation of a hyletic content — whose application to sensible experience is problematic.

In truth the difference between the two components of the appearance of a spatial thing emerges only in the course of a continuous perceptual process involving multiple appearances and unifying them synthetically (p. 132). This continuous process is a temporal flux of intentional consciousness; its duration is composed of a succession of present moments with ever new and possibly different appearances linked by a continuous synthesis. That synthesis is a sensible synthesis of fusion (*Verschmelzung*) or covering (*Deckung*) unifying the manifold appearances in terms of the unity of the same thing giving itself in them. It is made possible by the fact that an appearance never suffices to itself but necessarily implies the anticipation and retention of other appearances of the same spatial thing. The emergence of a new appearance is therefore motivated, prepared and foreseen by the preceding ones, and this motivation obeys certain rules — one being that an appearance calls forth another in virtue of links of proximity or complementarity. The most important force leading from one appearance to the next is nonetheless, on Husserl's account, the interest of the perceiving subject, who always wants to know more of the thing. Taking that interest and the teleological movement it imprints on the process into account, we discover that the continuous synthesis of appearances has the form of a process of intuitive fulfilment.

The laws of association therefore do not suffice to explain how perception passes from one appearance to another and why, among all possible appearances, just this one and not another occurs (p. 133). What determines the passage is — over and above compatibility and complementarity — the increase or progression (*Steigerung*) of the intuitive character of the perceptual datum. To know the thing better is to perceive more of it, to dispose of a more complete intuitive givenness. This interest in knowledge, and the continuous synthesis of intuitive fulfilment operating within the progression of the perceptual process, are guided by the teleological anticipation of the ideal of an adequate givenness of the thing. In a perceptual process animated by an interest in knowledge it is therefore this ideal, or more precisely the approach to it, that guides the progression from one appearance to another. Since the ideal is inaccessible, the process of fulfilment animating the continuous synthesis from within is an indefinite progression: each appearance retains an empty horizon of possible appearances. The synthesis of fulfilment is therefore at the same time a synthesis of emptying (*Entleerung*) or unfulfilment (*Entfüllung*), since what is gained in intuitiveness by one appearance is lost by another. It thus always remains possible for a new appearance to destroy the fine harmony of a long process of perceptual fulfilment by contradicting or disappointing (*Enttäuschung*) everything one had expected of it.

This unlimited process of perceptual fulfilment is also a process of transcendental constitution of the thing that appears (pp. 133–134). For the phenomenologist the progress of knowledge realized by the perceptual process does not consist in ever better discovering a thing that would exist independently of consciousness and be fully determined in itself. The perceptual process is indeed a progressive unveiling of the thing, but the phenomenological sense of that thing's reality is progressively built up in the measure of that unveiling. Following the sense of being instituted by the transcendental reduction, the thing is nothing other than the synthetic unity of its multiple appearances. Essentially bound to the temporality of the perceptual process, it is always in movement; but its movement is not identical with the movement of the appearances, since the thing is the unity of those appearances. Depending in its being and its determinations on the flux of intentional consciousness, it does not belong to consciousness. Depending on constituting consciousness without coinciding with it, the thing is a transcendence within immanence: the spatial thing is an object *for* perceptual consciousness, not an object *in* consciousness or a moment of consciousness. Here the book's governing formula surfaces in its narrowest technical application, and it will be extended to the subject itself in the Conclusion.

Does this constituting consciousness necessarily have an egological form? (p. 134.) Is perceiving an activity whose source is the freedom of a pure I? Does the noematic concept of appearance not open the possibility of a purely noematic description of the perceptual process — and how could such a phenomenology account for the synthesis of intuitive fulfilment that is the motor of the process? Husserl's unpublished writings contain some indications of what such an analysis might be, and after Husserl, Gurwitsch used the same concept of a field of appearances or field of transcendental consciousness to elaborate a purely noematic phenomenology of perception.

The key concept of this new description is the horizon of possible appearances surrounding each noematic appearing (pp. 134–135). Following Husserl and Gurwitsch, this horizon has the form of a quasi-monadological system of possible appearances all referring to one another, so that the actualization of one possible appearance entails a modification of the whole system. Husserl proposes to understand that actualization as the translation of a possible appearance from the periphery toward the centre of the field of consciousness, a movement triggered by an appeal or attraction of associative order issuing from the centre and going in search of a complementary intuitive datum. The actualization of a possible appearance would thus be the waking of a dormant appearance in accordance with the actual demands of the field of forces that perceptual consciousness constitutes. The interest in knowledge animating the perceiving subject would then finally be a matter of forces of attraction or even anonymous drives rather than the expression of an ego's will. Instead of being the result of deliberate action and progressive construction of the thing, perception would happen all by itself within a passive life of the subject.

In his noematic phenomenology Gurwitsch goes further than Husserl, making virtuoso use of the lessons of Gestalt psychology (p. 135). For him the field of appearances is organized in the manner of a *Gestalt*, and the relation between the proper appearance and its empty horizon of possible appearances obeys the same laws. This allows him not only to go beyond an egological and intellectualist conception of perception but, in the first place, to dispense with the noetic concept of appearance and the form–matter dualism it presupposes. His identification of the thing-in-itself with the totality of all appearances relating to it is by contrast less convincing.

One can hardly speak of such a totality, since the number of appearances of the same spatial thing is necessarily unlimited (p. 136). The spatial thing constituting itself in the course of the perceptual process — and *a fortiori* the thing-in-itself — is a unity which, while revealing itself in the incessant ballet of appearances, never coincides with any of them. Identifying the thing-in-itself with the totality of its appearances thus carries the danger of falling back into the analysis of the ideal of adequacy for external perception found in the *Investigations*, whose idea of a coincidence between the appearances and the appearing spatial thing was shown to be contradictory. Gurwitsch would thereby lose the benefit of *Ideen I*'s analysis, on which the adequate givenness of the thing-in-itself is an Idea in the Kantian sense — with the risk of denaturing the transcendental phenomenology of the appearing of a transcendent thing and folding it back into a form of empiricist phenomenalism.

#### III

The phenomenological conception of an adequate givenness of the spatial thing whose teleological anticipation regulates the perceptual process leads to an aporia: such adequate givenness is essentially unrealizable, yet it is constantly present in perception as a teleological anticipation (pp. 136–137). Husserl attempts to surmount the aporia by treating the adequate givenness of a spatial thing as an Idea in the Kantian sense. This implies, first, that the thing-in-itself is given not as a real object but as an idea; second, that this idea is the idea of an indefinite progress of perception — and if the object of such unlimited experience cannot by essence be adequately given, that does not hold for the idea of the necessity of the unlimited progress of that experience, which can reveal itself to a finite subject without betraying its infinite character and without the subject forgetting its finitude, so that one escapes the contradiction of supposing a spatial thing could be given in the manner of consciousness and its intentional life; and third, that this idea of an indefinite progress plays the role of a regulative principle of the perceptual process.

What is difficult in this fine construction is above all that perception would thus attempt to reach an end the subject knows to be inaccessible (p. 137). The finitude of the perceiving subject would consist in the desire to push the limit of its knowledge ever further while knowing the process to be unlimited; the presence of the thing-in-itself would be deferred in the very measure in which one approaches it. By making the thing-in-itself an idea lending itself to adequate intuition, and by saying that the object of that intuition is the unlimited character of perception, Husserl attributes to the knowing subject a paradoxical knowledge of its own finitude. On this conception the perceiving subject would know its finitude perfectly, and this would in no way prevent it from wanting to exceed it. Far from leading to denial or flight, the subject's genuine finitude would consist in facing its own finitude without resigning itself to it; the subject would properly assume its finitude precisely insofar as the knowledge of that finitude had no purchase on its desire to exceed it. Its finitude would finally be nothing but its being torn between an ineffective knowledge of its limits and an unshakeable will to push them back infinitely.
