In a different idiom Husserl finally says nothing else (pp. 137–138). By understanding the thing-in-itself no longer as a reality but as the idea of an indefinite progress of knowledge, the interest of knowledge tips over onto the side of knowledge itself: the final end is not an inaccessible object but the accomplishing of an infinite task of knowing. But how could one take upon oneself a task one knows can never be brought to term? To understand this, one must suppose that the will to exceed oneself by harnessing oneself to an unlimited task draws on sources other than the knowledge of one's own finitude. If one takes on a task one knows to be endless, this will to know must draw its strength elsewhere than in theoretical knowledge — which is precisely what Husserl says when he makes theoretical knowledge a matter of practical and ethical choice.

The chief advance of *Ideen I* over the *Investigations* is to have made us understand that what counted in knowledge was not mastering the object in contempt of human finitude but bearing the burden of that finitude by committing oneself to an endless quest (p. 138). Taking on the destiny of finitude while seeking to push back its limits is for Husserl a matter of will and responsibility, so that the will to know is subject to ethical constraints. To comport oneself as a responsible knowing subject is to give account of one's judgments and conceptions by pushing the interrogation of the things themselves to its extreme limit. This life of knowledge subject to absolute ethical demands is for Husserl at the same time a style of life — one style among others, but also the highest and best. One must always conduct oneself as a responsible person and account for one's positions by leaning on intuitive data, but no one does so better than the philosopher, who alone assumes all the demands of theoretical knowledge and is by that fact more exposed to the experience of the finitude of human knowing. To ask how one can take on a task one knows to be unlimited is therefore precisely to pose the question of the philosopher's desire. For Husserl the philosopher's desire is to push human responsibility to its limit (*Letztverantwortung*), not to retreat before the limit of human finitude. Responsible for himself (*Selbstverantwortung*), the philosopher is thus also responsible before humanity and responsible for humanity's ethical essence.

### 2. Perception and Hermeneutics (Husserl, Cassirer and Heidegger)

Hermeneutics was not born in the twentieth century, but the idea of a hermeneutic philosophy is now most familiar to us in the form Heidegger, Gadamer and Ricœur gave it (pp. 139–140). The new hermeneutics is inseparable from the great stakes of twentieth-century philosophy: the definitive overcoming of a sensualist or rationalist theory of knowledge; the taking into account of the many forms of a symbolic mediation of the intentional relation between subject and object; the reevaluation of the link between theoretical and practical philosophy; and finally a new determination of philosophical knowledge itself in the face of the growing rift between the natural sciences and the human sciences. These stakes are not new, and merely listing them recalls the problematic of Kant's three *Critiques*. The impression is confirmed by the inquiry to follow, which concentrates on the decisive period of the 1920s: it is through a new reading of Kant, henceforth inseparable from its Hegelian shadow, that a hermeneutics of objective spirit (Cassirer) and a hermeneutics of the finitude of Dasein (Heidegger) assert their ambitions and move rapidly toward a conflict still being discussed.

Heidegger's hermeneutics was born in a historical context marked essentially by the rivalry between neo-Kantianism and phenomenology (p. 140). Everyone knows how Heidegger and soon Gadamer were caught up in the debate agitating philosophical circles at Freiburg and Marburg alike. But perhaps insufficient attention has been paid to the fact that this struggle of the phenomenologists with the neo-Kantians is not unconnected with the fact that the phenomenological movement had itself already split into a cognitivist, essentialist and transcendentalist tendency on one side and a practical — more precisely poietic — existentialist and hermeneutic tendency on the other. It was by fighting on both fronts, against the first generation of phenomenologists and against neo-Kantians of every stripe, that Heidegger developed his hermeneutic approach to fundamental ontology. Even granting that Heidegger's cause was philosophically important and right, one cannot help thinking that he often mistook his adversary. How could he consider Husserl's first philosophy the expression of a mere theory of knowledge based on a dualist ontology? And how could he go so far astray as to take Cassirer's thought for the type of an epistemology essentially preoccupied with the status of the natural sciences?

Heidegger's remarks on Cassirer's *Philosophy of Symbolic Forms* are by no means limited to a summary assimilation to neo-Kantianism, and the Davos discussions do not bear only on the right way to read Kant (pp. 140–141). The disagreement about the finitude of the subject, about the relation between activity and passivity in its comportment, about productive imagination and language, about philosophy in its relation to ethics and religion, refers above all to the problematic of a philosophy that wants to be hermeneutic. The reader quickly sees that Heidegger's criticisms do not dispense him from attending closely to Cassirer's still timely work; giving Cassirer the same attention as Heidegger, he will nonetheless find it impossible to ignore Heidegger's remarks or to treat them all as null and void. Once one steps out of a simplistic polemic, one realizes that in the discussion between Heidegger and Cassirer two conceptions of hermeneutics confront one another, between which it is still hard to choose. And can one choose between two types of hermeneutics without first reflecting on what separates them from a phenomenology to which both continue to refer? Is it not this common appeal to Husserl that best measures the distance between them? The chapter's method is thereby fixed: Husserl is the third term by which the Davos quarrel becomes legible.

Bernet will try to show how Cassirer attempts to escape the alleged necessity of choosing between Husserl and Heidegger (p. 141). Hence the inquiry into Cassirer's hermeneutics begins with his conception of symbolic perception, before turning to broader questions: the relation between a hermeneutics of subjective experience and a hermeneutics of the works of objective spirit; the plurality of symbolic forms and the possibility of their unification; and finally the evaluation of the status of hermeneutic knowledge itself.

#### A Hermeneutic Conception of Perception

What place does the analysis of perception occupy within the systematic and rigorous — though sometimes ambiguous in detail — construction of the *Philosophy of Symbolic Forms*? (pp. 141–142.) The first volume treats language, the second myth, the third the symbolic function common to all symbolic forms together with that particular form which is science; elsewhere Cassirer analyses the symbolic forms of religion, art and technology more deeply. These forms result from a bestowal of sense (*Sinngebung*) or a symbolic comportment (*Verhalten*) of the subject or of consciousness, which in turn has several modes of functioning. Cassirer distinguishes three types: perception (*Wahrnehmung*), intuition (*Anschauung*) and knowledge (*Erkenntnis*). Each is governed by a different symbolic functioning: one perceives according to the symbolic function of expression (*Ausdruck*), intuition is accomplished in the symbolic register of representation (*Repräsentation* or *Darstellung*), and knowledge obeys the rules of pure signification (*reine Bedeutung*). To these subjective modes correspond objective correlates that are nothing other than the symbolic forms already mentioned: Cassirer parallels expressive perception with myth, representational intuition with language, and knowledge articulated in significations with science.

This correlation between the types of symbolic comportment of the subject and the symbolic forms composing the realm of objective spirit has the effect of committing the hermeneutic analysis of the *Philosophy of Symbolic Forms* to a circular procedure (pp. 142–143). Travelling indefinitely between the *terminus a quo* and the *terminus ad quem* of the symbolic life of spirit, Cassirer's hermeneutics denies itself all immediate access to the subjective origin of objective spirit or of the world of culture. The path of this hermeneutics of the symbolic function is thus a living testimony to the originary character of the symbolic mediation it strives to bring to light. The circularity is not a defect but the doctrine enacting itself, and it is what Heidegger will attack as an evasion of the *terminus a quo*.

Within this general frame, perception appears as a subjective comportment determined by a specific symbolic function and accessible only through the detour of a hermeneutic analysis of a particular symbolic form of objective spirit (p. 143). Strikingly, Cassirer distinguishes perception in the narrow sense (*Wahrnehmung*) from perception in the broad sense (*Anschauung*). The first is expressive in nature and is met above all in a form of existence bound to the symbolic form of myth. The second is more evolved, since it uses the articulations proper to the function of representation, and it maintains the closest relations with the symbolic form of language. It is this second form of perception whose hermeneutic character the following analysis will bring out. A note adds that Merleau-Ponty's *Phenomenology of Perception* should be reread in the light of this distinction between an expressive, mythical or animist perception and a quasi-linguistic perception requiring a structuralist analysis of the system of differential traits and symbolic references it implies — which would not only take the true measure of that book's immense debt to Cassirer but might clarify passages whose obscurity seems due precisely to the absence of a clear distinction between the two modes of perception.

Exactly like Merleau-Ponty after him, Cassirer proposes to open a middle way between an empiricist or sensualist and a rationalist or intellectualist analysis of perception (pp. 143–144). Rereading Kant's first *Critique* in the light of the third, he holds that in perception content and form constitute an indivisible whole. A datum can be apprehended through different forms, but it is no less true that the form is itself a datum — a form in the sense of Gestalt psychology: at once sensible figure and function of differential articulation. What appears in perception is a phenomenon that makes sense from the outset and appears according to its sense. The perception of an object is possible only if accompanied or even prepared by that work of a schema-producing imagination Cassirer calls symbolic activity or function.

Always eager to enter into dialogue with other thinkers, Cassirer wanted to bring out what separated his conception of the sensible phenomenon from Husserl's and Heidegger's (p. 144). As one would expect, the Husserlian conception of *hylē* and *morphē* and their possible synthesis finds no favour in his eyes: a datum devoid of noetic apprehension is a nonsense for Cassirer. The confrontation with Heidegger is more interesting, since both share the conviction that seeing is seeing according to a mode of vision (*Sicht*) and both appeal to Kantian schematism. But their conceptions of transcendental imagination and schematism differ on essential points. For Heidegger, Kantian schematism corresponds to the elaboration of an a priori knowledge of ontological type, deeply marked by the finitude of Dasein. Cassirer proposes a more classical interpretation, insisting that the chapter on schematism must not be isolated from the chapters on the *Grundsätze* and on the distinction between phenomena and noumena, and that it bears on what he calls a phenomenology of the object. Cassirer is thus led to contest that the symbolic function of productive imagination has an exclusively temporal sense, that it concerns a sense of being irreducible to objects, and that it is marked by an insurmountable finitude.

This discussion has important bearings on the problematic of perception (p. 145). For Heidegger the unity of perception and imagination is the sign of a fundamental dependence of the understanding's formative activity on sensibility; for Cassirer it represents on the contrary the possibility of its emancipation. For Heidegger the sense of the sensible phenomenon is *given* to the subject — he speaks of a fundamental experience of being referred to givenness; for Cassirer this primitive givenness must be prolonged by an activity of symbolic formation conferring on the phenomenon an objective sense. Precisely because Cassirer understands perception as a symbolic comportment whose nature is to exceed itself toward a more objective determination of the world, Heidegger's insistence on finitude and passivity appears to him as the sign of an enclosure of subjective life within a primary — mythical and exclusively practical — mode of comportment. What Heidegger and Cassirer debate at Davos is therefore situated within a hermeneutic analysis of perception and concerns directly the mode of givenness of the sense apprehended and interpreted in perception. For Cassirer the significant articulation of the world presents itself as an objective and universal symbolic form resulting from a symbolic function that is pure spontaneity (*Geist*). For Heidegger the world is a significant web in which Dasein is itself caught, which it may transform but never form in the sense of a transcendental constitution. This does not mean Heidegger is less subjectivist than Cassirer: on the contrary, he never stops reproaching Cassirer with having left too much in shadow the question of the subjective — existentiell and existential — foundation of that system of objective spirit which the symbolic forms constitute.

Cassirer's own stakes in a "true phenomenology of perception" are revealing of what Bernet has called his hermeneutics (p. 146). Like Heidegger, but apparently before having read him — the third volume was completed by 1927 and all its references to *Sein und Zeit* are in notes probably added after the first draft — Cassirer repeats tirelessly that perception in the sense of representational intuition is a seeing (*Sehen*) made possible by a symbolic vision (*Sicht*). He is quoted to the effect that there is for us neither sight nor anything visible that does not fall under some mode of spiritual vision; a seeing and a being-seen outside that vision, a "mere" sensation exterior and prior to any kind of formation, are hollow abstractions; the given must always already be envisaged and grasped under a determinate point of view, which alone confers on it its sense — a sense to be understood not as a conceptual or associative accessory but as the naive sense of primitive intuition itself.

As in Heidegger, this vision is brought close to the Platonic idea (*eidos*); it is a frame permitting an interpretation (*Deutung*) of what one sees, and it determines the direction and goal toward which the apprehension of the sense of a perceptual datum tends (p. 146). Thanks to it perception is able to *read* the sense of what appears rather than *spell out* scattered and isolated data. It permits perceiving, acting and speaking together, as empirical research on aphasia, apraxia and agnosia shows — each of these bringing to light a deficit of this *Sicht* and notably of the subject's capacity to pass from one *Sicht* to another.

Bernet cannot enter into the detail of Cassirer's analyses, extremely well documented, rich in historical insight and critical discussion, and testifying to a simply astonishing erudition and openness of mind; he wants rather to deepen the sense of that symbolic function of the *Sicht* which makes seeing a hermeneutic experience (p. 147). For Cassirer this *Sicht* is a symbolic function or order which, without issuing from the sensible data, nonetheless adheres to them to the point of forming a whole with them — a phenomenon impregnated with its sense. To characterize this whole of the sensible and of sense, of the visible and of light, Cassirer introduces the term symbolic pregnance (*symbolische Prägnanz*), defined as the way in which a lived experience of perception, as a sensible experience, contains at the same time a certain non-intuitive sense that it brings to immediate and concrete representation.

The appearing of the phenomenon is thus ineffaceably marked or impregnated by a symbolic order that gives it its sense (p. 147). In other words: only what is significant can appear, and significant in a certain respect or according to a certain order of sense. If the diversity of orders of sense can be examined as such by a philosophy of symbolic forms, these forms are nonetheless inseparable from the sensible data, at least at the level of the symbolic function called representation. The forms impregnate the datum and the datum represents (*Darstellung*) the form. Changing form or *Sicht* therefore requires a familiarity with analogy and metaphor; the subject is bound to an effort of transposition, transformation or translation of the phenomenon, which in appearing according to another sense appears otherwise and becomes another phenomenon.

In his conception of symbolic pregnance Cassirer situates himself once more fairly precisely midway between Husserl and Heidegger — between the doctrine of categorial intuition and the analysis of understanding as an existential mode of being of an individual Dasein, though all three appeal to Plato (pp. 147–148). Even if Heidegger wanted to pull Husserl's categorial intuition toward his own conception of transcendence and the ontological difference, it is incontestable that for Husserl it is an intuitive intentional act aiming at an ideal object. That ideal object is a non-empirical object exceeding the power of sensible intuition, but this in no way means for Husserl that categorial intuition is the condition of possibility of sensible perception. Being an intuition *sui generis* of an ideal object, categorial intuition is also not that sensible perception impregnated with a non-intuitive sense of which Cassirer speaks. Husserl thus differs from both Heidegger and Cassirer in that his interest in the appearing of a non-sensible object in no way leads him to doubt that sensible perception suffices by itself to give a sense to what appears to it. On the other hand, Husserl shares with Cassirer the conviction that the categorial form is something objective whose validity is general and therefore irreducible to the lived experience of an individual subject. It is precisely here that Heidegger separates from both Husserl and Cassirer by treating the *Sicht* as an a priori of sensible perception that has nothing objective about it but on the contrary characterizes the mode of being of an individual subject. The a priori of sensible perception, the light that makes visible what appears, is therefore for Heidegger neither the intentional lived experience of a transcendental subject (Husserl) nor a universal form of objective spirit (Cassirer), but the world of existence of an individual subject marked by its facticity.

To summarize: Cassirer innovates on Husserl by stressing that sensible perception is inseparable from what Husserl analyses as categorial intuition and Cassirer calls *Sicht*, and on this point he agrees with Heidegger against Husserl (pp. 148–149). But Cassirer also agrees with Husserl against Heidegger in understanding symbolic forms as ideal objects, and in reproaching Heidegger with having given a purely anthropological description of the *Sicht*. The three-cornered mapping is now complete, and it is what the rest of the chapter tests.

Cassirer's theory of symbolic pregnance, which makes sensible perception a hermeneutic experience, is a theory of the unity of the sensible datum and its sense, bringing out a reciprocal dependence between the sensible phenomenon and the symbolic order conferring its visibility (p. 149). To see is to see and understand *according to*, *via*, *by the detour of*, and therefore implies what Heidegger calls the hermeneutic "as". But one can truly speak of hermeneutics only insofar as a datum can be interpreted according to different points of view or different *Sichten*. Cassirer accordingly specifies that in a perception deserving to be called hermeneutic, the unity of sensible datum and symbolic form is not a unity of confusion or expression but a unity of distinction and determination he calls a unity of representation (*Darstellung*). As an experience shaped by the symbolic function of representation, hermeneutic perception is thus situated midway between an expressive or mythical mode of perception delivered over to the occult powers of the thing and its metamorphoses, and a scientific mode depending only on a formal, autonomous symbolic system and thereby freed from all dependence on a pregiven reality. In hermeneutic perception sense no longer merges with a concrete sensible, but neither does it detach from it to the point of attaining the logical autonomy of a formal system of pure signifiers.

What then does representation mean for Cassirer? (pp. 149–150.) Among the many definitions in the *Philosophy of Symbolic Forms*, the reader familiar with Husserl, Heidegger and above all Derrida will be most struck by the formulation that no content of consciousness is in itself either purely present or purely representation; every actual lived experience contains both moments in an indissoluble unity, every present functioning in the sense of a re-presentation just as every re-presentation demands its attachment to something present to consciousness.

A hermeneutic or representational perception thus addresses a datum insofar as that datum is caught in a network of references (p. 150). These deserve to be called intentional even if they are not really of subjective order. Nothing is given as simply present; the event of the presence of a datum triggers a vibration (*Schwingung*) that propagates and wakes new data announcing themselves in the distance. Perception crosses and at the same time hollows out the distance separating present from absent. The symbolic function of representation mediates the relation of present and absent, and representation using the linguistic sign is only a particular — though, as will appear, highly significant — case of the function of representation operating within all perception. This function, common to perception, image-consciousness and the way the sign refers to signification, thus effaces the opposition between presentation (*Gegenwärtigung*) and re-presentation (*Vergegenwärtigung*) on which Husserl's entire theory of perception rested. Drawing largely on Gestalt psychology, Cassirer takes the reciprocal relation of whole and part as the paradigmatic case of this representation. Perception becomes representational because animated by a hunger for form (*Gestaltenhunger*) pushing it toward a dynamic unification of the visible and the invisible under the guidance of a centre of interest or a symbolic form permitting a globalization of phenomena. Hermeneutic perception thus moves in a field or horizon where anything can become a representative of anything and of the form of the whole itself. The presence of the phenomenon is therefore a function of a network of representations where lateral and vertical references cross; it depends on the way the phenomenon represents other phenomena and the symbolic form common to them, which determines the general type of their sense — for, as Cassirer puts it, phenomena thereby lose all character of absolute isolation, each element taken with others in such a common movement containing its law and the overall form it is capable of representing to consciousness.

Thus the colour of a wall can be perceived in terms of the material it was built with or the light bathing it; it represents an architectural style, an epoch, or a city; it recalls Vermeer's little patch of yellow wall sung by Proust, or the Wailing Wall (p. 151). Hermeneutic perception is representational because analogical. The example is Bernet's, not Cassirer's, and the Proust reference will return in the chapter on memory.

Cassirer does not content himself with describing how one phenomenon refers to others; what interests him above all is exploring the different symbolic dimensions in which this function of representation is cast (p. 151). He devotes an entire chapter to each of its forms — space, time, and the unity of the thing and its properties — showing how the presence of a here represents a there, how the presence of a now is indissolubly bound to the representation of past and future, how multiple changing properties are gathered in a representation of the unity of the subsisting thing. Cassirer each time tries to show that the multiplicity and changeability of representational relations, and the adherence of these symbolic forms to the sensible data, in no way compromise the stability of the symbolic order of space, time and the subsistence of things. That stability is on the contrary for him the result of the non-coincidence of a place, an instant and a thing with themselves; understood as representation, this non-coincidence creates distinctions and differences of determining value (pp. 151–152). Rather than threatening the constancy and general validity of the sense of the sensible order, difference and the multiplicity of representational relations are on the contrary a necessary condition of the emergence of unity and identity in the field of phenomena. Before the concept and otherwise than the concept, representation institutes the unity of recognition within sensible perception.

Such a description of hermeneutic perception will nonetheless remain incomplete until the close link uniting this perception to language is clarified — in Heidegger's terms, the analysis of the hermeneutic "as" calls for an analysis of the apophantic "as" (p. 152). Here again Cassirer stands midway between Husserl, who holds that language is only an unproductive reflection of intentional lived experiences, and Heidegger, who declares that we see what one says about the thing. For Cassirer the statement comes neither before nor after hermeneutic perception, since statement and perception are both rooted in the same symbolic function of representation. He is quoted to the effect that asking whether the articulation of the world of intuition precedes or follows the birth of articulate language, whether the first is the cause or the effect of the second, is in that form a badly posed question: neither is born of the other in a purely chronological sense; they resemble rather two trunks springing from the same spiritual root (pp. 152–153). And further: the more our investigation of the particular paths taken by the general function of representation and recognition progresses, the more clearly the essence and specific unity of that function appears, and the better we see that it is finally thanks to one and the same fundamental operation that spirit raises itself to the creation of language as to that of the image of the world.

What more precisely is the nature of this language that is hermeneutic perception's twin? (p. 153.) Like perception it must stand midway between the reign of myth and that of science: a language neither mimetic nor purely symbolic. By its articulation it exceeds the mimetic or expressive language of primitive peoples and children, but because of its adherence to sensible phenomena it cannot claim the universal range of the symbolic language of science. It is therefore rather an analogical language functioning as representational perception does. The hermeneutic nature of this perception and this language would thus be bound to their analogical character, to their way of seeking a unity of sense without renouncing the multiplicity of references; it would consist in their way of exploring an intermediate space between the sensible and the logical, and would mean the wager of profiting from the play between signifier and signified rather than folding one onto the other, as myth (or schizophrenia) and science each do in their own way.

#### Method and Status of a Hermeneutic Philosophy

Is exhibiting a hermeneutic mode of perception in Cassirer enough to make the *Philosophy of Symbolic Forms* pass for a hermeneutic philosophy? (p. 153.) What would be the field of investigation of such a hermeneutics, how would its method differ from both Husserlian phenomenology and Heidegger's hermeneutics of the facticity of Dasein, and to what extent would it announce a hermeneutic epoch of philosophy itself, a new age of hermeneutic reason? The three questions are answered in turn, and the last one negatively.

On the method Cassirer follows, Heidegger's criticism — expressed as early as his 1928 review of the second volume and reaffirmed forcefully at Davos in 1929 — is revealing (p. 154). On that criticism, Cassirer would have concerned himself too exclusively with the analysis of the symbolic forms of objective spirit while totally neglecting the rootedness of their significance in the existence of an individual subject. Cassirer's reply is no less interesting: he says he is attached to the objective expressions of subjective activity because they make communication among human beings possible and are thereby the guarantee of an enlightened rationalism and a responsible humanism. For Heidegger the learned analysis of the symbolic forms governing our culture is the work of a lazy philosophy creating nothing new and retreating before the facticity of human existence. Cassirer for his part says he is preoccupied by the ethical and political consequences of what appeared to him as the exacerbated form of an irrationalism and individualism closer to religion than to the great philosophical tradition. We know he was not entirely wrong; but to understand what is at stake one must beware of the too simple and slyly ideological images found among the protagonists themselves as much as among their faithful — images on which the affair would be a struggle between a philosophy anchored in the *Aufklärung* and a militant, mystical, anti-humanist fundamentalism, with stakes comparable to what divided Erasmus and Luther, Goethe and Hölderlin, Kant and Jacobi.

On Cassirer's hermeneutic method, Heidegger is certainly not wrong to remark that Cassirer's characterization of the subject of the formative activity producing the symbolic universes of language, myth, art and science is singularly lacking in precision — Heidegger asks what the mode of being is of what is called now consciousness, now life, now spirit, now reason (p. 155). But nothing obliges us at first sight to follow Heidegger further when he claims this imprecision follows directly from the absence of a radical ontology of Dasein in the light of the question of being in general. Cassirer offers an explanation at once simpler and more illuminating by referring to the reconstructive method of Natorp's psychology, which he summarizes: we can never lay bare the immediate being and life of consciousness in a pure state, but it is a pertinent task to draw from the process of objectivation, which as such cannot be suppressed, a new aspect and sense by traversing it in both directions, from the *terminus a quo* to the *terminus ad quem* and back; only in such a constant coming-and-going, in this double methodological procedure, can the object of psychology in general become visible as such. The quotation is a direct answer to Heidegger's objection at Davos that Cassirer's *terminus ad quem* is the whole of a philosophy of culture while his *terminus a quo* is completely problematic, whereas Heidegger's position is the reverse.

Natorp had greatly appreciated the introduction of the noema in Husserl's *Ideen I*, and it is again to the correlation of noesis and noema that Cassirer refers when he pays homage to Husserl's phenomenology (pp. 155–156). To this could be added the importance of the problematic of the guiding thread (*Leitfaden*) from the *Ideen* on, and of the regressive question (*Rückfrage*) in Husserl's later work. Husserl therefore shared with the neo-Kantians, but not with Heidegger, the conviction that access to constituting transcendental subjectivity is won only by a detour through the constituted. The examination of transcendental life cannot do without the support of the regional ontologies, even if it must exceed them. The origin reveals itself only afterwards and in an incessant zigzag of the gaze between beginning and end — or, in Cassirer's and Heidegger's idiom, between *terminus a quo* and *terminus ad quem*. Husserl is thereby placed on Cassirer's side of the methodological quarrel, which is the pivot of Bernet's defence.

Heidegger's criticism might therefore, rather than denouncing a fundamental failing in Cassirer's philosophy, bring to light precisely its hermeneutic character (p. 156). Nor would it be abusive to use this hermeneutics of the coming-and-going between subject and culture to cast a critical eye on the project of fundamental ontology. What is certain in any case is that Cassirer, before Ricœur, already insisted on the necessity of approaching the subject's life by trusting the testimony of its works. That detour is inevitable and indispensable once one stresses, as Cassirer does with fine constancy, the symbolic nature of reason. It follows that the subject's relation to itself is always already mediated by culture and notably by the language it shares with other subjects: the subject is immersed in the work of symbolic imagination long before the sense-laden data with which it finds itself gratified make a sign to it and permit it to recognize itself in them.

Cassirer's hermeneutics has the further great merit of bringing to light that objective spirit fragments into a multiplicity of symbolic forms or universes (p. 157). If the analysis of each of these symbolic orders gives rise to a possible return toward their subjective principle, that principle will appear differently each time. Consequently it is not enough to stress that the subject's life is governed by the same symbolic forms as the world it lives in; one must add that there are different worlds and therefore different ways of living. Apprehending itself through a plurality of symbolic forms, Cassirer's subject remains indefinitely in search of its own unity. Here too this hermeneutics open to dissemination runs up against Heidegger's criticism, which reproaches it precisely with its lack of unity and proposes to understand the multiplicity of symbolic forms from their foundation in Dasein's existence. Cassirer's reaction is not long in coming: he suspects the hermeneutics of Dasein of promoting a new metaphysical monism, noting that in referring and even reducing all cognitive faculties to the transcendental imagination Heidegger is left with a single plane of reference, that of temporal Dasein, whereas Kant nowhere maintains such a monism of the imagination. The project of grounding the various senses of being in the facticity and finitude of an individual subject's existence has all the less sense for Cassirer in that Heidegger seems content to examine a purely practical mode of that existence. The disagreement thus bears not only on the method of approaching the subjective principle of the world; it concerns more fundamentally the conception of the mode of being of world and subject, and it will entail a different understanding of the nature of philosophical knowledge.

In Cassirer the hermeneutic procedure of a coming-and-going between the multidimensionality of a world shaped by the symbolic forms and the corresponding forms of life (*Lebensformen*) in no way compromises the ideal objectivity of those forms (p. 158). Despite their multiplicity and difference, the symbolic forms represent forms of order whose validity is universal and whose subsistence is marked by no contingency or arbitrariness. They are ideal objectivities issuing from the self-objectivation of an equally ideal subjective principle called spirit (*Geist*). Cassirer's hermeneutics therefore seems to accommodate a conception of intentionality and constitution scarcely different from Husserl's transcendental phenomenology — and on closer inspection it would even lag behind Husserl by virtue of its narrowly Kantian conception of the transcendental subject. The *Geist* realizing itself in the symbolic forms is a subject whose determination remains quite formal: a principle functioning above all as the condition of possibility of those ideal objectivities which are the symbolic forms, and maintaining a fairly obscure relation with the actual experiences of a subject at once transcendental and individual. Husserl had noticed this before Heidegger, as his 1925 letter to Cassirer shows: while otherwise laudatory, it reproaches Cassirer with not having pursued his investigation of the various world-conceptions and their historical concatenation as far as the question of the sense of a genesis characterizing the transcendental subject itself, and with having thereby neglected to interrogate the transcendental significance of facticity.

Heidegger's criticism goes further still, but under cover of allying with Husserl against Cassirer in the name of a phenomenological conception of the subject, it will end by turning against Husserl as well (p. 159). The ultimate stake of Heidegger's critique of the *Philosophy of Symbolic Forms* is nothing other than the finitude of the subject. This problematic was already central to the disagreement about Kant. Reviewing both the first and the second *Critique*, Cassirer maintains against Heidegger that for Kant the human being is a citizen of both the *mundus sensibilis* and the *mundus intelligibilis*, and that Kantian reason represents precisely the call to a self-exceeding that immerses one in the realm of the universal. The symbolic forms, with their ideality and general validity, are for Cassirer the brilliant testimony of the possibility and sense of such an exceeding of the personal toward the trans-personal. Beyond this quarrel over the right way to read and extend Kant, what is at stake is therefore the status of philosophical knowledge itself. It is not only that Cassirer does not share Heidegger's taste for receptivity, anxiety, death and the nothing, but that he is incapable of recognizing in these figures of human finitude a genuine philosophical and more precisely ontological value. The hermeneutics of symbolic forms and the hermeneutics of Dasein thus differ not only in their object but in their philosophical status.

In putting to work a critique of culture, the *Philosophy of Symbolic Forms* incontestably effects a hermeneutic turn within philosophical thought inspired by neo-Kantianism (p. 160). The project of a philosophy of the natural sciences transforms itself into an analysis of the understanding of the world (*Weltverstehen*), which Cassirer takes care to distinguish from an explanation of the world (*Weltbegreifen*): the philosophy of symbolic forms does not consider exclusively or preferentially the explanation of the world characterizing pure exact science but is equally interested in all directions of world-understanding, seeking to grasp it in its multiform life, in the totality and internal diversity of its expressions. This philosophy of symbolic life within the world studies the forms of signification as they operate in the experience of the human world as much as of nature, and deciphers in them the reflection of that universal constituting principle it calls spirit or reason. It brings to light the diversity of symbolic orders as well as their reciprocal arrangement, and shows how all are traversed and articulated by the same basic symbolic functions. Commentators have noted that the link binding the different symbolic forms lends itself to a double reading, synchronic and logical or diachronic and genetic; they have nonetheless been astonished that the place of philosophy among these forms remains singularly obscure. That imprecision is no accident: it leads back to the capital question of the relation between hermeneutics and finitude.

Heidegger's hermeneutics of the finitude of Dasein leads necessarily — even if Heidegger did not realize it at once — not only to the recognition of the finitude of the manifestation of being but to the finitude of the thought solicited by that manifestation (pp. 160–161). If in order to interrogate the sense of being the philosopher must slip into the skin of a finite Dasein whose existence is marked precisely by its inability to escape the question of its own being, then one does not see how philosophy could escape the destiny of human finitude. Cassirer may be sceptical about the movement of recoil Heidegger claimed to detect in Kant's treatment of the transcendental imagination, but it seems incontestable that the *Philosophy of Symbolic Forms* sketches an analogous recoil before the prospect of making philosophy one symbolic form among others. Divided between Kant and Hegel, it seems finally to rally to the standpoint of absolute knowledge, describing the system of symbolic forms from a point of nowhere. This philosophy therefore remains hermeneutic so long as it occupies itself with perception, language or world-images, but ceases to be so as soon as it bends back on itself. It is a philosophical hermeneutics and not a hermeneutic philosophy. Unlike a philosophical hermeneutics, a hermeneutic philosophy is entrusted with the heavy task of thinking its own finitude. This does not mean, as Cassirer seemed to fear, that it must leave philosophy for religion. But it does mean that it can no longer evade the question of the range, value and validity of philosophical knowledge and of its place among the figures of objective spirit. Cassirer's recoil before a recognition of finitude that might issue in philosophical relativism must continue to put us in question: the credibility of hermeneutics is at that price.

### 3. Perception and Natural Life (Husserl and Merleau-Ponty)

For the centenary of Husserl's birth Merleau-Ponty wrote that tradition is forgetting of origins (p. 163). Precisely because we owe him so much, we are unable to see just what is his; toward a philosopher whose undertaking awoke so many echoes, apparently so far from the point where he himself stood, every commemoration is also a betrayal — whether we pay him the superfluous homage of our own thoughts, as though to find them a guarantor to which they have no right, or on the contrary, with a respect not without distance, reduce him too strictly to what he himself wanted and said. The epigraph is also the chapter's warning about its own procedure, and Bernet will be caught between the same two betrayals with respect to Merleau-Ponty.

More than thirty years after Merleau-Ponty's death the situation is different (pp. 163–164). For many of us it is hard to say what we owe him, so thoroughly was our reading of Husserl, Sartre, and even Heidegger and Derrida influenced by him. But it is no less true that for many, and for the philosophical community as a whole, the memory of what he himself wanted and said has faded too fast. The best homage this generation can pay a thinker who so decisively marked its first steps in philosophy is therefore to reread his writings — a procedure rewarded not only by a better understanding of what we owe him but of what phenomenology as a whole, down to its most contemporary questioning, has received and can still receive from him.

One cannot reread Merleau-Ponty without being seized by the desire to read Husserl's texts again in his company (p. 164). There is no more inspired interpreter, none more concerned to bring to light the most subterranean tendencies of that thought and thereby to secure it an unsuspected future. Advancing in the great philosopher's shadow, Merleau-Ponty nonetheless shows himself as he is in himself: attached to understanding unity from difference, insisting on the essentially unfinished character of every synthesis, tying subtle links between the visible and the invisible. Even if he never opposes Husserl as sharply as Heidegger does, he does not lack vigilance: while slipping into the movement of Husserl's thought, he hollows out within it a gap whose critical yield is very rich — witness his interest in an intentionality preceding objectivating representation, his critique of intellectualism and reflective philosophy, his fascination with the mystery of a *logos* inscribed in the flesh of the sensible world. But among those who today rediscover with enthusiasm the speculative promises of Husserl's transcendental phenomenology, not enough attention is perhaps paid to what Merleau-Ponty said about the necessary incompleteness of every phenomenological reduction. His cherished project of a genesis of the transcendental subject does put in question that subject's constituting power and the splendour of its isolation, which cuts it off from its roots in the perceptual faith of natural life. It is in showing how the natural attitude is already tormented by interrogation, and how all human life, however primitive, is already marked by division, loss and negativity, that Merleau-Ponty innovated most with respect to Husserl (pp. 164–165). That claim is the chapter's thesis, and it locates the innovation on the side of natural life rather than of the late ontology.

One may therefore justly be astonished that Merleau-Ponty's thought is scarcely met with in current writings on Husserl, still less in studies of Heidegger (p. 165). Yet he has much in common with the latter and never stopped reading and meditating his writings. It is true that the *Phenomenology of Perception* makes little of Heidegger and confines itself to an unoriginal reading of *Being and Time*. But this is not so of *The Visible and the Invisible*, which is like a single great meditation on the phenomenological sense of the Heideggerian concept of ontological truth. The lateral or indirect approach to being, the conception of the invisible as the reverse of the visible or as the manifestation of a withdrawal of the visible, earn that work, more than any other, the title Sartre somewhat usurped: essay in phenomenological ontology.

With Husserl and Heidegger, Sartre is doubtless the contemporary philosopher Merleau-Ponty most read and discussed, and the stake of the debate is from the outset ontological (p. 165). It is doubtless through recognizing the incapacity of the ontological dualism of being-in-itself and being-for-itself to account for the most ordinary phenomena of human existence that Merleau-Ponty converted himself into a thinker of the between, even of ambiguity. Rejecting a form of dialectic that does violence to phenomena by reducing them all to an opposition of contraries, and committing himself to a non-Hegelian thought of difference, he was also led to give a new sense to the traditional concepts of negativity and interrogation. It is this new thought of difference and negativity that permitted him an unprecedented analysis of that work of freedom which is history — a history that for him nonetheless remains rooted in a pregiven nature and returns to it in the form of a deposit Husserl had already called historical sedimentation.

Merleau-Ponty's absence from the admittedly restricted scene of contemporary phenomenology is therefore astonishing (p. 166). Derrida's silence about a philosopher with whom he has much in common intrigues even more than the hasty judgment of someone like Michel Henry — who asks what a thinker like Merleau-Ponty, for all his immense talent, truly brings that is new by comparison with Husserl, Heidegger and Scheler — Henry having always distanced himself from every thought of transcendence and of the ecstatic phenomenon, whether Merleau-Ponty's or Heidegger's. One is also struck that current work seems limited almost exclusively to deciphering the late or posthumous work, above all *The Visible and the Invisible*, so that the *Phenomenology of Perception* attracts little interest. This is understandable: the analysis of intersubjectivity and social life there is not very convincing, language is treated as a poor relation, the systematic and repetitive opposition of intellectualism and empiricism eventually wearies once its point is taken, and the contemporary reader sometimes has trouble caring about the long discussions of the psychological work of the day. Finally, Merleau-Ponty himself expressed serious reservations, writing in *The Visible and the Invisible* that the problems posed in the *Phenomenology of Perception* are insoluble because he there starts from the distinction between consciousness and object. Is it then worth undertaking the rereading proposed here, and what can it bring to contemporary thought?

First, in starting from the consciousness–object distinction the *Phenomenology of Perception* is far from confining itself to it (pp. 166–167). Second, it would remain to show which posed problems remain insoluble there and how the solution of *The Visible and the Invisible* actually differs from the earlier attempt. There seems to be agreement that the difficulties arise above all in the last part, concerning what might be called a philosophy of spirit treating of others and the human world, the tacit *cogito*, the temporality of a disincarnate consciousness, and freedom as a power of withdrawal attributed to a voluntary subject. That philosophy of spirit follows, quite logically, a philosophy of nature preoccupied above all with bodily existence, its dialogue with things, and its insertion into the world. That analysis of the natural life of the human body is not at all disowned in what *The Visible and the Invisible* says of the flesh, of brute being and of wild spirit; on the contrary, without recourse to the experiences patiently described in the earlier work those notions would remain enigmatic and poor in phenomenological content. Reading the *Phenomenology of Perception* remains useful and even gripping also because it makes us witnesses to the tormented birth of a problematic that is not only highly topical but seems much harder to circumscribe within *The Visible and the Invisible*: that of a *philosophy of nature*. It even seems that what in the first text was still called nature has suddenly become spirit in the second. It is not useless to ask after the reasons for that mutation, which seems to go together with the claim to a definitive overcoming of a philosophy of the metaphysical subject. The still current questioning about the status of the human subject has much to learn from this analysis of a natural life which on one hand owes nothing more to the spiritual life of man, and on the other meets the structure of the subject where one would least expect it: in the life of things within the world.

#### I

In the *Phenomenology of Perception* Merleau-Ponty tries to clarify the sense of the distinction between philosophy of nature and philosophy of spirit by starting from a phenomenological description of human existence (pp. 167–168). Human existence is steeped in both milieus, according as it is accomplished in the mode of an anonymous or prepersonal life on one side and a personal life on the other. As in Husserl, personal life is to be understood as that of the free acts of a subject who voluntarily decides what it does, who takes a position (*Stellungnahme*). But there must be something with respect to which a position is taken, something preceding and conditioning that act of freedom. In other words: freedom is situated, and the autonomous decisions of personal life have the character of a resumption or taking in hand of prepersonal existence. One can also speak of a relation between reflection and the unreflected, provided that reflection is not taken as an objectivation or thematization of the unreflected with a view to better theoretical understanding. Since prepersonal and personal acts both imply an existential engagement of the subject in the world, their relation can only be practical in essence.

In taking prepersonal life as the natural life of the human being, Merleau-Ponty appeals to a conception of nature giving it the sense of a substratum of the spiritual existence of personal life: there is another subject beneath me, for whom a world exists before I am there and who marked my place in it (p. 168). This natural basis of human spirit is of course something quite other than a matter without form. This nature is of living essence, and the life animating it is neither exclusively human nor wholly inhuman. It is, within the human subject, what comes to it from this side of itself; it is the space in which a subject can come to be, the site of the subject's birth. Summarizing his 1956–57 course on the concept of nature, Merleau-Ponty presents at the same time the great stakes of the philosophy of nature sketched from the *Phenomenology of Perception* on: nature is not only the object, the partner of consciousness in the face-to-face of knowledge (pp. 168–169). It is an object out of which we have arisen, in which our preliminaries were little by little laid down until the instant of knotting into an existence, and which continues to support that existence and furnish its materials. Whether it is a question of the individual fact of birth or of the birth of institutions and societies, the original relation of man and being is not that of the for-itself to the in-itself; and it continues in every man who perceives. However overloaded with historical significations his perception may be, it at least borrows from the primordial its way of presenting the thing and its ambiguous evidence. Nature always gives itself as already there before us, and yet as new under our gaze; this implication of the immemorial in the present, this appeal in it to the newest present, disorients reflective thought.

On closer inspection this nature is thus at once what presides over natural or prepersonal life and what precedes it — an ambiguity to be interrogated (p. 169). It is nonetheless clear that a nature out of which a properly human mode of life arises cannot be the nature-in-itself with which the natural sciences occupy themselves. Nature in Merleau-Ponty's sense is not foreign or opposed to man; it maintains with him a relation of essential complicity. It is, within human existence, what does not belong to it as its own: a ground (*Grund*) of its constituting power that is at the same time an abyss (*Abgrund*), a reason for that power escaping the power of constituting reason. If there is, as Merleau-Ponty never tires of repeating, an impossibility of complete reduction, it is precisely because of this natural ground of human existence. Husserl's reduction, bringing to appearance only the correlation between the constituting activity of the transcendental subject and its constituted work, is incapable of fishing up and giving to be seen what precedes and supports that constitution. To make nature appear as a dimension of human existence that calls for constitution without letting itself be enclosed in it requires a new form of reduction. Instead of being a reduction *of* natural life, the reduction in Merleau-Ponty's sense is rather a reduction *to* natural life: what in us resists phenomenology — natural being, the "barbarous" principle Schelling spoke of — cannot remain outside phenomenology and must have its place within it.

When the *Phenomenology of Perception* speaks of the anonymous character of a natural life governed by an indeterminate and general "One", these terms borrowed from Husserl and Heidegger take on a new sense (p. 170). Prepersonal life is neither the lived experience of a transcendental subject accomplishing its work of constituting objects without being conscious of it, nor the inauthentic existence of a Dasein fleeing personal responsibility by identifying with a silent majority. The operative intentionality (*fungierende Intentionalität*) animating natural life is not an objectivating intentionality lacking a reflective consciousness of itself; it is on the contrary an intentionality devoid of all representation of an object. The "One" of prepersonal life is likewise not a personal subject melting into the anonymity of the mass; it is on the contrary a subject remaining buried in the natural world because it lives only through its body. Natural life as Merleau-Ponty conceives it is a life that is bodily through and through.

Merleau-Ponty uses "body" (*corps*) to translate Husserl's *Leib*, and within the system of regional ontologies of *Ideen II* Husserl makes this *Leib* the principle of animal nature, situated between material nature and the spiritual world (p. 170). Does this mean that for Merleau-Ponty the bodily existence of man is what distinguishes us from inanimate nature while also preventing us from assimilating wholly to the spiritual world of symbolic significations? The position of the *Phenomenology of Perception* remains ambiguous: on one hand traces of a spiritualist subjectivism undeniably survive in it, on the other bodily existence implies and even expresses systems of symbolic significations of great richness and an advanced degree of articulation. If one insists on giving a phenomenological ancestor to this philosophy examining the natural life of man within a nature that precedes him, one will look toward Scheler rather than Husserl — despite Merleau-Ponty's own explicit declarations, notably in "The Philosopher and His Shadow".

Saying that the natural life of the living subject has its seat in the body excludes that the body could still be considered an object for a separate consciousness or a mere formless matter: the body is a natural self and as it were the subject of perception (p. 171). On closer inspection it is even at once the effective subject of natural life and the symbolic frame governing it. The body perceives, but it also deploys in advance the field in which a perception can occur. It is therefore, as Merleau-Ponty never tires of repeating, at the same time an empirical and a transcendental subject, a contingent fact and the possibility of that fact: the experience of the body makes us recognize an imposition of sense that is not that of a universal constituting consciousness, a sense adherent to certain contents; my body is that significant nucleus which behaves as a general function and which nonetheless exists and is accessible to illness. When the body perceives a thing it apprehends its sense, and that sense is tributary to a symbolism which is precisely that of the body's internal organization, its movements and its power of grip on the world. Bodily existence therefore has its own existentials, in Heidegger's sense, and these answer to the symbolic structure of the world: the body is that strange object which uses its own parts as a general symbolism of the world and through which we can therefore frequent that world, understand it and find a signification in it.

The *Phenomenology of Perception* examines the symbolic structure of the body and its effects at length and in detail (pp. 171–172). In all its aspects that structure proves to be that of a form or *Gestalt* uniting and encompassing very heterogeneous parts. The sensible experience of the same thing differs according as the thing is seen, touched, smelled; each sense deploys a different phenomenal field with its own sensible logic. The apprehension of the thing and its significations is thus rooted in a pregiven visual, tactile, olfactory field which Merleau-Ponty rightly calls a gift of nature. This fragmentation of sensible experience according to the different senses is another example of a natural or contingent fact with transcendental bearing.

Such difference among the senses does not prevent them from communicating and associating their respective powers of exploration to form a synaesthesia (p. 172). A pure sensible experience limited to a single sense, touch for instance, would be an exceptional case and the result of a sensible abstraction; conversely, when a blind man gains sight, the organization of his touch is profoundly modified. The link established between part and whole is a matter neither of subordination nor of inductive generalization: since the part already anticipates the whole and the whole is nothing without the parts, one should speak, with *The Visible and the Invisible*, of a logic of carnal reversibility. Each sense constitutes a different point of view on the world, and this world, far from being a totality, is the open set of the different points of view it arouses — it is neither contradictory nor impossible that each sense constitute a small world within the large one, and it is even by reason of its particularity that it is necessary to the whole and opens onto it.

It is unsurprising, then, that Merleau-Ponty understands the unity of the body and the unity of the world by analogy with this unity of the senses (p. 172). Just as the senses communicate, so the different parts of the body imply, envelop or encroach on one another to weave systems of practical equivalence: seated at my table wanting to reach the telephone, I can stay leaning back in the chair provided I extend my arm further, or lean forward, or even half rise. And just as the senses associate within a synaesthesia, the different organs of the body tend toward the realization of the same goal and form a synergy: my body is not a sum of juxtaposed organs but a synergic system all of whose functions are taken up and bound in the general movement of being-in-the-world.

This synergy plays an important role in the experience of the unity and reality of the thing, and it intervenes in a new way in the body's self-perception (p. 173). The hand touching a thing knows nothing of its power of grip: abandoning itself to exploring the thing's relief, it is unaware of itself as touching hand. The other hand must touch this hand lost in the world, the gaze must follow its movement, for it to wake to itself. A kind of sensible or carnal reflection is then established, a play among the body's organs in which the other hand or the gaze functions as the reflective consciousness of the touching hand. This sensible reflection therefore passes through exteriority and difference. There is synergy among the organs with a view to the body's self-perception, but this in no way abolishes their heterogeneity and separation: the body apprehends itself through the difference of its parts, as the place where sensible powers with nothing in common at the outset cross and bind. Returning upon itself through a fragmented external perception, the body perceives itself only partially, and in doing so loses its effective grip on the world.

The body that plays the role of subject in natural life is thus indeed a symbolic system in which each moment is defined by its difference from all the others (p. 173). It follows that the relations established among its parts are likewise symbolic — Merleau-Ponty insisting particularly on relations of equivalence and pregnance. The same symbolism finally governs the relation of parts to whole: by referring to all the others, each part of the body also functions as a symbolic, metaphorical representative of the whole.

This symbolism of the body, this synchronic and fixed system, is nonetheless nothing without the diachronic movement of bodily existence that realizes it and breathes life into it (pp. 173–174). But that does not mean the symbolic system is something an individual body constitutes as it goes along existing. The body is rather an existent whose natural existence evolves within a pregiven carnal symbolism. As the unconstituted ground of natural life, this symbolic system of the body deserves the name nature. But this nature is no longer merely the substratum of man's spiritual activities; it is rather the pregiven system of the structure of the human body into which every movement of carnal existence must necessarily insert itself. It is what is most natural within the natural, bodily life of man, and yet it is already a finely articulated symbolic system whose functioning resembles the way a given linguistic code governs concrete acts of speech. Far from representing the immediate, formless and inhuman within man, this nature is a symbolic system governing human existence without issuing from it. Nature thus appears as a primordial form of transcendence which from the origin accompanies and guides the movement of bodily existence. This is the chapter's key move: nature is not the pre-symbolic but an already-articulated symbolic order that no subject instituted.

Everything Merleau-Ponty says of the body's symbolic structure, of the synergy of its parts and of the way that synergy answers the solicitations of the world and the needs of unifying a thing, is gathered in his notion of the body schema (p. 174). With that notion it is not only the unity of the body that is described anew but, through it, the unity of the senses and the unity of the object. The body schema must be understood as an anticipative mobilization of the body's symbolic system in the face of a precise situation of perceptual life: the body knows in advance — with a carnal knowledge — what it must do and how. Its gestures, and the order in which it performs them, accord with what it wants and with what things demand of it. The body schema is therefore the resultant of a multitude of constraints: it must take account of a subject's particular project and its realization within that subject's carnal constitution, but must also answer demands emanating from a given constellation of things, from their sensible texture and the world's armature. The nature from which bodily conduct arises cannot therefore be confined within the limits of the symbolic system of the perceiving body (pp. 174–175). It also encompasses things and the world, and nothing permits saying it has its centre of gravity in the perceiving subject's body. If the body is only a part or aspect of that nature, and if this body is at the same time endowed with a subjective power, then one is tempted to attribute a subjective power to the other parts of the whole as well — to things and to the world. That is indeed the path the *Phenomenology of Perception* takes.

#### II

Every lived experience of perception is thus the result of a subtle balancing of different and sometimes antagonistic forces (p. 175). Merleau-Ponty insists especially that the influence the subject exercises on the course of the perceptual process is highly variable. The distinction between the concrete movement of grasping (*Greifen*) and the abstract movement of showing (*Zeigen*) illustrates this well: the latter requires an active and voluntary intervention on the subject's part, whereas the former is a simple bodily reflex triggered by a given situation. Concrete movements are animated by an anonymous motor intentionality inscribed in the structure and habits of the perceiving body, whereas abstract movements bring in a subjective form of intentionality — which Merleau-Ponty hastens to specify is nonetheless not an objectivating intentionality but still belongs to an existential attitude or project.

This analysis of two sorts of bodily movement takes up an earlier distinction between the habitual body and the actual body (pp. 175–176). The actual body is the body insofar as it serves a present and effective interest of the perceiving subject, whereas the comportment of the habitual body pursues old projects that have meanwhile sedimented in the subject's flesh. The habitual body thus testifies to an erosion of the subject's power over its body; conversely, abstract movement marks a refinement of bodily movement in the direction of greater subjective mastery over the body and the circumstances of perception. The frontier between the two remains very fluctuating, since the two forms of perceptual comportment relay one another and pass continuously into each other. One has the impression that their separation follows less from the phenomena than from the principles of a philosophy concerned to measure the degree of the subject's immersion in nature, or rather the degree of its emergence and spiritualization.

There are indeed traces of a spiritualist subjectivism even in what the *Phenomenology of Perception* says of bodily existence (p. 176). One should really speak of a psychological subjectivism seeking a better understanding of the relation of soul and body: one must understand how psychic determinants and physiological conditions mesh with one another. The answer is not long in coming: the body is the vehicle of being-in-the-world; its permanence near me gives me access to the physical presence of things, but it is not a permanence in the world, rather a permanence on my side. The body is thus considered here as the instrumental prolongation of the constituting subject; what differentiates it from the things of the world is its great proximity to the subject — a particular material object which constituting consciousness uses to operate and make itself known in the world.

Merleau-Ponty is here only repeating what Husserl already said in the second part of *Ideen II* in speaking of the body as organ of the will (pp. 176–177). Such a conception of one's own body not only presents grave drawbacks but is largely superseded by everything the *Phenomenology of Perception* has already taught about a body traversed, supported and guided by a natural life that infinitely exceeds it. Husserl's text shows well how such a vision, reducing the body to an instrument of consciousness, far from clarifying the meshing of psychic and physical, can lead no further than the description of a psycho-physical parallelism. A consciousness whose vital essence is not carnal can at most ally itself with a body, not inhabit it by incarnating itself in it. Merleau-Ponty's philosophical project cannot accommodate such a spiritualist understanding of the bodily subject, and it is in fact abandoned and surpassed in favour of analyses that all stress the expressive force of the body.

What the *Phenomenology of Perception* says of sexual experience is a first example of how the alleged parallelism of consciousness and body gives way before the analysis of the ambiguity of their relation, indeed of the impossibility of a clear distinction between sexuality and existence (p. 177). There is in human existence a principle of indetermination; existence is indeterminate in itself because of its fundamental structure; and this excludes delimiting what comes to us from nature and what from freedom. A further step toward abolishing the separation of consciousness and body is taken when the lived body is compared to a work of art whose ideal signification cannot be detached from the sensible texture of the unique and irreplaceable object to which it belongs: a novel, a poem, a painting, a piece of music are individuals, beings in which expression cannot be distinguished from the expressed, whose sense is accessible only through direct contact and which radiate their signification without leaving their temporal and spatial place — and it is in this sense that our body is comparable to a work of art.

The body is therefore an expressive sign of a wholly particular type, a sign without detachable signified (pp. 177–178). If the body expresses at each moment the modalities of existence, this is not as stripes signify rank or a number designates a house: here the sign does not merely indicate its signification, it is inhabited by it, it is in a certain way what it signifies. And further: so understood, the relation of expression to expressed or of sign to signification is not one-directional like that between an original text and a translation; neither the body nor existence can pass for the original of the human being, since each presupposes the other.

These passages show not only how the analysis of the expressive body leads Merleau-Ponty to shake off a certain spiritualist subjectivism inherited from Husserl, but also that the opposition of spirit and nature has gradually emptied of sense (p. 178). The human body is a nature charged with symbolic significations, contributing in no small way to the formation and transformation of the world of human culture. Yet this symbolism of nature inscribed in the body issues only very partially from human action and history: the life animating the human body owes as much to a prehuman nature as to a human culture. The body is therefore that place where spiritual interests and natural drives mingle to the point of merging — and if every place is situated either in nature or in culture, then the body is a non-place, an *a-topos*. The figure of atopy has now appeared three times in the book: the place where the tool's being shows itself, the world of anxiety, and now the body.

It is through his body that man builds a human world, and yet it is in that same body that he is dispossessed of it (p. 178). Instead of occupying the centre of the human world, the bodily subject is deported toward its periphery. He does not thereby fall into a barbarous, formless nature but rather draws closer to a life culture had made foreign to him: that of animals and things. Merleau-Ponty in fact speaks very little of animals but much more often of things. He shows that in abandoning his power over things and making himself attentive to what they have to say to him, man nonetheless finds himself at home: he discovers that things, despite the air of indifference they may assume, are scarcely different from him. It is by making himself a bodily subject open and exposed to the solicitations of the world — a thing among things — that man becomes conscious of the bodily subjectivity proper to those things. When the human subject surrenders to things, they return the favour by making themselves subjects in turn and inviting him to dialogue. The return to nature accomplished through the analysis of the natural life of the human body thus receives its ultimate signification: an immersion in the flesh of the world (pp. 178–179). Before *The Visible and the Invisible*, the *Phenomenology of Perception* already sketches the broad lines of this generalized incarnation by which body becomes flesh.

#### III

The theme of a bodily subjectivity of things announces itself very early in the *Phenomenology of Perception*, from the first pages on the body (p. 179). Each object is the mirror of all the others: looking at the lamp on my table I attribute to it not only the visible qualities available from my place but also those the chimney, the walls, the table can "see"; the back of my lamp is nothing but the face it "shows" to the chimney. I can see an object only insofar as objects form a system or a world, each disposing the others around it as spectators of its hidden aspects and guarantee of their permanence. Every vision of an object by me is instantly reiterated among all the objects of the world, which are grasped as coexisting because each is everything the others "see" of it.

There is thus not only a symbolic system of my body but a symbolic and carnal system of things to which my gaze must bend if it is to perceive a thing (p. 179). A thing can be perceived only through and according to the things surrounding it, which is why Merleau-Ponty says that the absolute positing of a single object is the death of perception. This referral of the gaze from one thing to another is not only symbolic and carnal but perceptual in essence: it is because the chimney sees the table that it can make me see it. The symbolic system of things, and already the system of each singular intersensory thing, thus reflects the structure of the symbolic system of the body. Must one conclude that this carnal and perceptual system of things, like that of the lived body, still needs to be animated and traversed by life to produce all its effects — and what would that life be, which actualizes the system of things by interrogating my gaze?

Merleau-Ponty's answer is clear: the life animating things is their expressive power (p. 180). The thing is the bearer of a current of sense — the sense of a thing inhabits that thing, the sense of the ashtray animates the ashtray, is incarnated in it with evidence; before others, the thing realizes this miracle of expression: an interior that reveals itself outside, a signification that descends into the world and begins to exist there, and which can be fully understood only by seeking it with the gaze in its place.

The sense inhabiting the thing does not fully belong to it: it comes from an expressive life that merely traverses it, joining it to the other things and to the gaze wandering among them (p. 180). Since this expressive life of perceptual sense belongs to no one as its own, one is tempted to call it anonymous or even asubjective. Merleau-Ponty on the contrary seems to consider it intersubjective: since the relations among things or among the aspects of things are always mediated by our body, all of nature is the staging of our own life or our interlocutor in a sort of dialogue.

Paraphrasing Benjamin on the work of art, one might say that every thing is invested with an aura captivating my gaze by a superabundance of sense (p. 180). The gaze is never done with the thing, because the thing goes on forever speaking to it and interrogating it. The long and remarkable analysis of hallucination functions as a proof *a contrario* of this interrogative power of the thing, establishing that what distinguishes the hallucinated from the real thing is precisely that absence of depth which makes the sense of the real thing a promise inviting exploration. Merleau-Ponty specifies that this unfathomable depth of sense and the interminable dialogue between gaze and thing that it institutes concern not only works of art or cultural objects like the ashtray but so-called natural objects too (pp. 180–181): contemplating the blue of the sky, I am not before it an acosmic subject, I do not deploy before it an idea of blue that would give me its secret; I abandon myself to it, I sink into this mystery, it thinks itself in me, I am the sky itself gathering itself together, collecting itself and beginning to exist for itself, my consciousness engorged by this limitless blue.

It is therefore true that in making itself a thing among things the perceiving body wakes things to their status as perceiving bodies and unites with them in the same mode of existence: perception and the perceived necessarily have the same existential modality (p. 181). Lived body and thing coexist in a union Merleau-Ponty calls by turns symbiosis, coupling, communion, communication. This unity goes well beyond a dialectical reunion of contraries — for-itself and in-itself — or an association based on the analogy of their functioning: it testifies to a genuine connaturality.
