If it is not only the body but also the thing that sees things, if my gaze sees what things are willing to show it, that is because body and things are cut from the same cloth, traversed by the same undivided life.

The *Phenomenology of Perception* hesitates to draw the ontological consequences that impose themselves, and in putting to work a naturalization of the subject it lets itself be drawn toward a subjectivation of nature: we find the real charged with anthropological predicates (pp. 181–182). One may suppose such a conception of nature owes much to Cassirer: is it not striking how far the analysis of this natural life enlacing body and things within a single world in becoming recalls the description of a primitive life dominated by the symbolic structure of myths in the *Philosophy of Symbolic Forms*? For Cassirer myth is already on the way toward science and philosophy, and the *Phenomenology of Perception* explicitly subscribes to that phenomenology of spirit or teleology of consciousness. If Merleau-Ponty's first great work, while anticipating an unprecedented philosophy of nature, never quite renounces the opposition of nature and spirit or the conception of natural life as the life of a primitive subject, that is doubtless explained as much by Cassirer's influence as by Husserl's. The previous chapter's suggestion — that the *Phenomenology of Perception* be reread through Cassirer's distinction between expressive and representational perception — is here made good.

The watchword of the *Phenomenology of Perception* is not yet the flesh common to body and things, but the function of flesh is largely anticipated in what the book says of the world (p. 182). Body and things interlace within a natural world animated by a cosmic life. If the body is the symbol of the unity of the world, it is hardly surprising that the analysis of that world proceeds as did the analysis of body and thing: the world too is a pregiven symbolic system producing its effects only insofar as it is animated by natural life, and this system is such that it makes a system with the system of things and the system of the body. The natural world is the horizon of all horizons, the style of all styles, guaranteeing my experiences a given and unwilled unity beneath all the ruptures of my personal and historical life, and whose correlate in me is the given, general and prepersonal existence of my sensory functions in which we found the definition of the body.

Like the thing, and yet otherwise than the thing, this world is an unfinished unity, a unity in becoming, an essentially temporal unity (pp. 182–183). What distinguishes the world from every thing is above all that there is an infinity of things but only one world: the world cannot receive its signification from its difference from other worlds. Yet there is no world without things and bodies. Does the world then receive its signification from those things and bodies? Was it not said on the contrary that the connaturality of things and bodies was founded on their common belonging to the world? Must one not conclude that the sense of the world belongs neither to the world, nor to things, nor to bodies — that sense belongs to no one, but is that natural life traversing world, things and bodies and attaching them to a single web? The sense of natural life would then be that universal and yet each time new reversibility which makes things refer to one another, makes things call to the gaze, and leaves the gaze, while letting itself be carried along by the referrals proper to things, nonetheless firmly moored to the world.

Without neglecting the reversibility between things and world, the *Phenomenology of Perception* insists above all on the unique and original character of the link attaching the perceiving body to the world: the lived body is in the world as the heart is in the organism, continually keeping the visible spectacle alive, animating and nourishing it inwardly, forming a system with it (p. 183). The citation must be completed by adding at once that the world in turn nourishes the body by offering it that spectacle, exciting its desire to see ever more. This mutual stimulation presupposes a visceral attachment of body to world: each perceptual act appears as taken from a global adhesion to the world. Through the spectacle of things that appear and depart, complete and contradict one another, the perceiving subject's faith in the world affirms and reinforces itself: beneath perceptions properly so called there is a deeper function underpinning them, the movement that carries us beyond subjectivity and installs us in the world by a sort of faith or primordial opinion.

Only on the basis of this co-belonging of lived body and world can the gaze meet the thing, make itself a thing with things, and listen to what they have to say to it (pp. 183–184). If the *Phenomenology of Perception* does bring to light a flesh common to world, things and body, it nonetheless continues to understand it within the horizon of bodily subjectivity — which is why the philosophy of nature there takes the path of a naturalization of the perceiving subject, which in turn entails a certain subjectivation of nature. Yet in going back this side of the opposition of nature and subject, Merleau-Ponty already commits himself to a genealogy of the subject which, far from grounding a reflective philosophy, gives birth on the contrary to a new conception both of the subject and of nature.

Emerging from among things within a common world and affirming its ipseity through its difference from things, the human subject is at once itself and other, one and multiple, present and absent, visible and invisible (p. 184). It is, within the universal intersubjectivity or intercorporeity of the world, that singular subject through which the world articulates itself into an open system of diacritical differences. This advent of difference is like an earthquake whose shock propagates through the whole world: everything is shaken by it, including the one who triggered this wave of universal vibration traversing the symbolic system of the flesh of the world. The human subject is indeed nothing without things and world, and yet distinguishes itself from them by imprinting on them the mark of its difference: it does but pass, and its passage leaves behind it a wake of the negative. The nature in which man is born and which nourishes him throughout his existence is therefore nothing but this universal life of sense, this pulsation — at each moment new — of a system of differences preceding all human constitution and to which every work of human constitution returns in the form of a sedimentation. This life of nature can equally well be called a life of spirit, provided that spirit is no longer referred to a spiritual subject, mortal or immortal. That is why in his late work Merleau-Ponty will readily speak of a wild spirit; and we now better understand how the *nature* put forward in the *Phenomenology of Perception* and the *wild spirit* introduced in *The Visible and the Invisible* — how, in other words, a natural symbolic system traversed by an untameable life and a spirit bound to the web of the universal intercorporeity of the flesh — are one and the same thing. The mutation of "nature" into "spirit" flagged at the chapter's opening is thereby explained as a change of name rather than of doctrine.

Might one not say too that Merleau-Ponty's whole work, far from resulting from a linear and continuous development, is itself traversed by the current of that same inexhaustible life of difference, resumption and sedimentation? (p. 185.) Is not the fascination it continues to exercise on the contemporary reader due precisely to the fact that, in the fluidity of its style, it is the most eloquent testimony of that fugitive and multiform life of wild spirit whose analysis, interrupted by the accident of a premature death, could by its essence only remain unfinished?

## III. Temporality, Historicity, Language

### 1. Origin of Time and Originary Time (Husserl and Heidegger)

Husserl and Heidegger never confronted or even met one another at the level of their respective understandings of time (p. 189). Husserl did read and copiously annotate *Sein und Zeit*, but he scarcely grasped the novelty of an ontological interpretation of time; his remarks on the second division testify above all to his irritation at seeing Heidegger abandon the consecrated terminology of phenomenology, and attest his lack of clear-sightedness about fundamental differences, especially where these are masked by a common vocabulary. On Heidegger's side the refusal of confrontation or dialogue is still more marked. Hardly had he finished editing Husserl's *Lectures on the Phenomenology of Internal Time-Consciousness* than he declared to his Marburg audience that as regards the problem of time everything in Husserl remains fundamentally as it was.

He could not have said more clearly that Husserl had in fact taught him nothing and that there was nothing to be got from him for an ontological understanding of time (p. 190). He repeated the same version of things to the end of his life, saying in 1969 that his own question of time advanced in a direction that always remained foreign to Husserl's researches on internal time-consciousness. In Heidegger's eyes, then, a confrontation with the Husserlian analysis of time simply could not take place, for want of common ground.

Bernet's own procedure, which tries to exhibit deep analogies between the two analyses of time, is therefore perilous in many respects (p. 190). It will be suspected of disrespect and infidelity toward Husserl, since it proposes to reread him as a precursor of Heidegger. It will still more surely irritate those readers of Heidegger who do not permit one to go looking where their master said there was nothing to find. Bernet will have to report not only extensive and striking resemblances but the fact that in presenting Husserl's *Lectures* Heidegger regularly omits what in Husserl comes closest to his own views. Some will conclude that Heidegger was indeed influenced by the Husserlian analysis and sought to forget it or have it forgotten. Bernet will not take that step, since in the current state of knowledge nothing permits it: there is no document showing that Heidegger had read the *Lectures* before Husserl entrusted them to him in April 1926 for publication. By then *Sein und Zeit* was already at press and Heidegger had been developing his own understanding of time for at least two years (pp. 190–191). He could rightly believe Husserl had nothing more to teach him — which also makes it easier to understand why he contented himself with editing the *Lectures* in the form Edith Stein had given them in 1917, and discharged the task with an irritation he readily vented on his Marburg students.

If nothing permits saying Husserl actually influenced the formation of Heidegger's conception of time, what authorizes speaking of striking resemblances and so contradicting Heidegger? (p. 191.) Nothing but an attentive reading of the texts. It falls to Bernet first to circumscribe a terrain on which the two analyses can meet and even converse in good understanding, and the working hypothesis is that this terrain — and therefore the key to the resemblances — is the question of the *phenomenological origin of time*, common to both. The logic of that question leads both to distinguish different levels of the experience of time and to bind those levels by a relation of founding or derivation. This common frame will also permit a better situating of what continues to make the difference between them; hence particular attention to the sense each gives the word "origin" and to the way each ties the question of the origin of time to the phenomenological description of an originary time.

The project has advantages and drawbacks (pp. 191–192). Among the advantages: the systematic character of the question spares Bernet the trouble of reviewing the entire published and unpublished work of both thinkers. For Husserl he limits himself to the *Lectures* published by Heidegger; for Heidegger, to *Sein und Zeit* and the Marburg courses of 1925–28. The question of the origin of time plays a determining role in all these texts and could have given rise, at that period more than any other, to a fruitful dialogue. The drawback is that foregrounding the question of origin limits the discussion to relatively formal considerations about how the two envisage and articulate the levels of temporality; the very concrete phenomenological analyses to which both texts owe much of their fascination — of death, of memory, of the measurement of time — will receive only summary attention. But it is also true that the multiplicity and diversity of those analyses makes it desirable to recall the general frame within which they are set.

This confrontation of frames is in any case a first step the posthumous dialogue cannot do without, and it immediately engages the ultimate questions (p. 192). What does it mean to interrogate an origin of time? Is this founding origin approached with the same intentions by both? In what sense can this founding procedure be said to amount, for both phenomenologists, to a regression from objective to subjective time? What does subjective time mean, and what does subjectivity mean that constitutes itself thanks to temporality? Is there a self-constitution of subjectivity independent of the relation to the things of the world? Is this temporal relation to self and to the things of the world still of the order of objectivating intentionality?

The reflection follows a very simple scheme: first the analysis of temporality in Husserl and in particular of what he calls the flux of absolute, time-constituting consciousness; then Heidegger and his conception of ecstatic temporality as accomplished in the authenticity of care (*Sorge*) and the inauthenticity of concern (*Besorgen*); third, the two set face to face, insisting notably on the similarity between Husserl's analysis of the unity of primal impression, retention and protention within absolute consciousness and Heidegger's analysis of the ecstatic-horizonal unity of presentifying, retaining and being-tensed-toward (*behaltend-gewärtigendes Gegenwärtigen*) (pp. 192–193). This will not prevent concluding on a series of deep disagreements concerning the ontological signification of time and its relation to the historial being of the subject, and the signification of a phenomenological founding of objective time in an originary time which for Heidegger is also, in a wholly new sense, "world-time".

#### I

Husserl characterizes his phenomenological approach thus: the question of the essence of time also leads back to the question of the "origin" of time, but this question of origin is oriented toward the primitive formations of time-consciousness (p. 193). The research deserves the name phenomenological in that it interrogates lived experiences of time as to their eidetic structure and so contributes to clarifying the a priori of time. This approach requires a bracketing of objective time — the "world-time" in which the natural sciences locate both physical nature and the psychic. The phenomenological question of the origin of time thus distinguishes itself clearly from the natural sciences and in particular from the causal structures put to work by a genetic psychology. First, it concerns itself neither with the existence of a world-time nor with the existence of the spatio-temporal objects located in it (pp. 193–194). Second, phenomenology undoes all the links uniting time-consciousness to the world, turning away from the single, objective, infinite time in which every thing and event — bodies with their physical properties, souls with their psychic states — has its temporal place, determinate and determinable by chronometers. The time-consciousness Husserl investigates is pure time of lived experiences: appearing time, the immanent time of the course of consciousness.

What concretely is this immanent or internal time-consciousness? (p. 194.) In the first place it is an intentional lived experience directed toward the temporal determinations of an immanent object. In virtue of that intentional correlation, each temporal determination of an object must be given in its own intentional act: a present object is an object of perception, a past object gives itself in an act of remembering, a future object is anticipated in an act of expectation. These three sorts of correlation are not independent of one another: what now belongs to the past was earlier present; what is presently remembered was earlier perceived. So too for the future object of a present expectation, which will later be apprehended as the present object of a perception. What is remarkable is not only the interlacing among the temporal determinations and among the corresponding acts, but that what binds and knots these different threads of time into a continuous web is always the *present*. This privilege is explained partly by the fact that memory relates to what has been present and expectation awaits the advent of a new present, and partly by the fact that all intentional acts, whether or not they relate to the present, are always and necessarily accomplished in the present of consciousness (pp. 194–195). What is past is presently remembered as having been present, what belongs to the future is presently awaited as a present to come.

What then of the present perception of a present object — is it perceived as an object earlier awaited as to come and later remembered as belonging to the past? (p. 195.) Here Husserl differs from Heidegger in conceding that the present of perception *can* in fact so spill over onto future and past, but need not by right do so. If there is no experience of past and future without experience of the present, there is indeed experience of the present purely and simply. The perceptual experience of the present — presentation (*Gegenwärtigung*) — is the foundation of all time-consciousness; memory and expectation derive from it as acts of re-presentation (*Vergegenwärtigung*) putting to work a doubling of the present. This concession marks a precise fault line: Heidegger will deny that any present can be had without the other two ecstases.

This autonomy of the present does not imply that Husserl, like Brentano and Meinong, wants to limit the present to a mathematical point, a pure instant (p. 195). Even if a punctual now could be isolated within the duration of a perception, that now would still be the apprehension of a duration of the perceived object. In the course of a perception each moment implies a retentional and protentional consciousness: it keeps present the elapsed duration of the temporal object and anticipates the continuation of its duration. Retention and protention must not be confused with remembering and expectation; they are not autonomous intentional acts but non-independent moments of every intentional act accomplished in the present. It is because at each instant of perception a duration of the temporal object is already given that the course of perception permits following the temporal object in its continuous and living deployment.

This description of the duration of perception forms in truth only the first level of the phenomenology of internal time-consciousness (pp. 195–196). It shows how the temporal determinations of immanent objects have their origin in intentional acts of perception and, secondarily, of remembering and expectation. In truth the question of the origin of time has thereby only receded a notch: if the temporal determinations of objects are founded on the temporality of intentional acts, it remains to understand whence those acts draw their own temporal determinations. The logic inherent in the question of origin leads Husserl to found the temporality of intentional lived experiences on an ultimate stratum of consciousness he calls the flux of absolute, time-constituting consciousness. At first Husserl understood this absolute consciousness as the consciousness of the pre-intentional sensations implied in the intentional acts of perceiving a temporal object. When in a second phase he abandoned the analysis of the perception of an immanent temporal object in terms of an intentional apprehension of a pre-intentional apprehension-content, absolute consciousness became quite simply the consciousness of every immanent temporal proto-object, whether intentional act or immanent object such as a sound.

This absolute consciousness in which the temporality of intentional acts is constituted is no longer, properly speaking, an intentional consciousness, a perception (p. 196). A perception is an intentional act relating to an object in the form of a spontaneous positing; the absolute consciousness of an intentional act, on the contrary, is a passive consciousness resulting from an affection — what it is conscious of has imposed itself on it rather than being posited by it. A second difference: intentional acts flow *in* time, whereas absolute consciousness is an apprehension of time which is not itself in time (pp. 196–197). Husserl says explicitly that strictly speaking this absolute consciousness should not even be called a "flux of absolute, time-constituting consciousness", since flux is still a temporal notion applying properly only to what is constituted by it: the flux is something we so name after what is constituted, and we can call what constitutes a flux only metaphorically and because for this absolute consciousness names fail us. If temporality is a determination of what is contained in time, then absolute consciousness is not temporal. Husserl clearly assumes the consequence, affirming that temporal predicates such as "now", "before", "successively", "simultaneously" no longer apply to absolute consciousness itself but only to immanent temporal objects such as the intentional lived experiences of perception. The constitutive relation binding immanent temporal objects to absolute consciousness therefore does not abolish the fundamental difference between the temporality of intentional acts and the non-temporality of absolute consciousness. This non-temporality is in truth an *other*-temporality: the temporality of a consciousness this side of intentional lived experiences, the very form of the subject's life, absolute in the sense of being irreducible to that subject's intentional experiences. The same difference will be found in Heidegger as the difference between the derived temporality of acts of perception and remembering and originary ecstatic temporality.

What makes for confusion in Husserl is that his analysis of absolute consciousness uses the same terms already used for the temporality of intentional acts (pp. 197–198). The "flux" that is not a flux has "phases" that are not temporal, and each phase, present but not "now", comprises non-independent moments Husserl again calls primal impression, retention and protention. Through primal impression, absolute consciousness relates to the present moment in the temporal flowing of an intentional act. This primal impression is indissolubly bound to retentions permitting absolute consciousness to keep present the already elapsed course of that same act, and likewise to protentions by which the present absolute consciousness anticipates its future course. In Heideggerian terms: the present of absolute consciousness at once presentifies (*Gegenwärtigen*), retains (*Behalten*) and anticipates (*Gewärtigen*) the flowing of intratemporal (*innerzeitig*) objects.

Husserl's analysis attaches above all to absolute consciousness in its retentional form, and it is here that his phenomenology of time innovates most radically and runs up against that intentionality of a singular kind which Heidegger will call transcendence (p. 198). As a non-independent moment of the present of absolute consciousness, retention is not an intentional act of reproduction like remembering but a retrospective and prolonged familiarity with the elapsed duration of an immanent temporal proto-object. On closer inspection this retention of the elapsed duration proves to be at the same time retention of the elapsed or rather having-been (*gewesen*) primal impressions by which absolute consciousness apprehended the proto-temporal object in the course of its living deployment. Consequently the present of absolute consciousness is at once retention of the past of an immanent temporal proto-object and retention of the elapsed phases of the flux of absolute consciousness itself: absolute consciousness retains and retains itself — it retains past objects in the present and retains itself in what it already is no longer. Husserl calls the retention of the proto-object's past duration transverse intentionality (*Querintentionalität*) and the retention of the elapsed flux longitudinal intentionality (*Längsintentionalität*).

This longitudinal intentionality puts to work a self-consciousness of a wholly new and unexpected type in Husserl, since it is a pre-reflective and non-objectivating consciousness in which the self gives itself belatedly (*nachträglich*) (pp. 198–199). Bernet cannot dwell on it, having developed the point elsewhere, but insists instead that for Husserl the longitudinal and transverse intentionalities of retention form an indissoluble unity, requiring one another as two sides of one and the same thing, interlaced with one another. For Husserl there is no prolonged familiarity of the subject with what it has itself been independently of a familiarity with the past duration of proto-objects; and inversely, for a proto-object to be able to appear as belonging to the past, the subject must apprehend itself as the outcome of what it has been.

Among the many consequences of this description there is at least one Husserl refused to envisage seriously: that absolute consciousness is not absolute (p. 199). If within absolute consciousness there is no longitudinal without transverse intentionality, and if the transverse intentionality of the flux can be described only by leaning on the temporality of the immanent proto-objects it constitutes, then it is not possible to affirm that the flux of consciousness *nulla re indiget ad existendum* — the Cartesian formula for substance with which *Ideen I* characterizes the absolute being of constituting consciousness. One would have to say on the contrary that the constitutive relation between the so-called absolute flux and the temporality of intratemporal objects is, to use a term of Merleau-Ponty's, made of reversibility. What prevented Husserl — and will also prevent Heidegger — from envisaging a founding relation of time that is not one-directional was finally nothing but the question of the *origin* of time (pp. 199–200). An origin cannot depend on what it gives birth to; it must be autonomous, absolute. Heidegger will go still further along this path in maintaining that an origin, in the very measure in which it generates, is also degeneration. Husserl too wants us to believe that the dependence chaining the other-temporality of the so-called absolute consciousness to the intratemporality of immanent objects is a mere matter of terminology or a purely methodological difficulty in no way calling into question the absolute character of constituting consciousness. As an honest phenomenologist, however, Husserl pays his tribute to the phenomena by installing a reversible founding relation at the very heart of originary time: the origin of time is the flux of absolute consciousness, a flux in which longitudinal and transverse intentionality — that is, the temporality of the relation to self and of the relation to things — are interlaced with one another, requiring one another as two sides of one and the same thing.

#### II

Heidegger pronounced several times on the content and value of the Husserlian texts on time whose first publication he had undertaken (p. 200). The most significant observation is found not — and this too is significant — in his editor's preliminary remark of April 1928 but in the last Marburg course of that same year. There he declares that in his phenomenology of internal time-consciousness Husserl occupies himself much with intentional acts such as remembering, re-presentation, reproductive re-presentation, expectation; but that what for Husserl constitutes primary and originary time-consciousness is rather the knowing concerning a simple datum of sensation such as the temporal flowing of a sound (pp. 200–201). Heidegger thus clearly noticed that Husserl's analysis comprised two different levels of time-consciousness: the level of intentional acts flowing in immanent time, and the level of the originary time-consciousness Husserl calls the flux of absolute, time-constituting consciousness.

In his appreciation of what Husserl accomplished, Heidegger — as is his unfortunate habit — blows hot and cold at once (p. 201). On one hand he congratulates Husserl on having been the first to discover the relation between time and intentionality; on the other he remarks superbly that as regards the problem of time everything in Husserl remains fundamentally unchanged. In other words: for anyone who has read Aristotle and Augustine, Husserl is not worth the detour. More unexpected than this customary ambivalence is that Heidegger purely and simply spirits away the searching analyses Husserl devotes to absolute time-consciousness: neither the flux, nor its double intentionality, nor even the phenomenon of retention is mentioned in a single word. Heidegger proceeds as if for Husserl consciousness were not itself temporal — and temporal in a sense different from intratemporality — but simply a consciousness relating to time. Without accusing Heidegger of anything, one must nonetheless remark that what is thus passed over in silence is also that in which Husserl is closest to Heidegger. Heidegger's own contrary formulation is quoted: what Husserl still calls time-consciousness, that is, consciousness of time, is precisely in the originary sense time itself.

There are in truth quite striking similarities and correspondences between the two analyses of time and temporalization (pp. 201–202). Like Husserl, Heidegger lets himself be guided by the question of the phenomenological origin of objective time. As in Husserl, the logic of that question leads him to distinguish levels of temporalization: first, the time of enunciation and of concern (*ausgesprochene Zeit*, *besorgte Zeit*) and its levelling down into the "vulgar" time of the natural sciences; second, the temporalization of the a priori possibility of that time by the ecstatic unity of a presentifying that retains and is tensed-toward; third, the originary temporalization of ecstatic-horizonal temporality in the authentic existence of Dasein, which in anticipatory resoluteness comes back upon itself and faces the demands of the present situation. As in Husserl, these three levels derive from one another by a founding relation. It is not hard to recognize in the first level what Husserl treats as the immanent temporality of intentional acts, nor to parallel the second with the flux of absolute consciousness. It is harder to find in Husserl an equivalent of the third, the temporality of Dasein's authentic existence.

Heidegger's analysis of a temporalization at once ecstatic and horizonal also recalls the central place intentionality occupies in Husserl's understanding of time (p. 202). The correspondence goes further still, since ecstatic temporality and the flux of absolute consciousness have in common that they are at once and indissolubly a relation of a subject to itself and a relation to other subjects and to things. The analyses of the double intentionality of the flux and of the temporality of the transcendence of being-in-the-world thus show important analogies. They also show differences, since the new signification Heidegger gives "world-time" indicates clearly that he turns radically away from a temporality temporalizing itself in the immanence of a consciousness. For Heidegger the being of the subject constitutes itself through the bursting-forth of the ecstatic temporality of being-in-the-world, whereas for Husserl the identity of the pure I remains unaffected by the temporal flux of consciousness.

Such comparisons have little interest so long as one does not consider what is proper to Heidegger's understanding of time (p. 203). Everything he says about time and its levels is anchored in his conception of the ecstatic-horizonal character of temporality and its relation to Dasein's existence. Temporality is called *ecstatic* because it is an impulse tearing everything that is out of itself, making it be as a movement without respite or rest. This vital impulse is accomplished in different guises, which have in common that they gather diverse moments each of which is already an exit from self, and gather them all into a unity that is itself a wrenching, an opening, an exile. Hence Heidegger speaks of temporality as the ecstatic unity of ecstatic moments or ecstases.

There are three ecstases from which derive the dimensions tradition has always attributed to time — future, past and present — and these dimensions are ecstatic in a threefold sense (p. 203). *First*, they do not belong to beings: rather than being the determination of a thing as future, past or present, the ecstases open the horizon permitting a thing to appear as future, past or present; they concern a temporal understanding of being guiding commerce with intratemporal beings. *Second*, each of the dimensions loses itself in the other two: the ecstasis of coming back upon the having-been (*Gewesenheit*) necessarily implies the anticipation (*Vorlaufen*) of the to-come and the presentation of presence. This unity of coming-back-upon, anticipating and presentifying must not be understood as the immutable essence of temporal change; it is itself a movement of temporalization, of provisional and indefinitely deferred ecstatic unification. Whence the *third* characteristic: their ecstatic unity is accomplished temporally and always in view of the particular horizon opened by one of them (pp. 203–204). There are therefore three originary forms of the temporalization of temporality, differentiated according as the ecstatic unification is accomplished in view of the horizon of coming-back-upon, of anticipating, or of presentifying. Heidegger's whole phenomenology of time stands within this formal frame, which is why at each step of his exploration he discovers new modes of tripartition: originary time, historicity, intratemporality; originary temporality of understanding, of disposedness and of falling; originary temporality of authentic understanding temporalizing itself in the unity of anticipation, repetition and the moment of vision (*Augenblick*).

This formal play of the figures of tripartition remains devoid of phenomenological signification so long as the question of how, where, and with respect to what being temporality unveils itself most originarily is left unaddressed (p. 204). If that privileged being is Dasein, then a phenomenology of the guises in which the essence of temporality reveals itself must lean on an analysis of Dasein's modes of existence. This intimate relation introduces a new complication: from the double possibility of an authentic or inauthentic existence there results a doubling of the tripartite structure of originary ecstatic temporalization. Take the existential "understanding": the ecstatic unity put to work by the authentic mode has the form of anticipation, repetition and the moment of vision; in the inauthentic mode, ecstatic temporalization is accomplished in the unity of being-tensed-toward (*Gewärtigen*), retaining (*Behalten*) and presentifying (*Gegenwärtigen*).

The different existentials, in their authentic and inauthentic modes of accomplishment, are distinguished from one another in a way that is still temporal — as a function of the particular ecstasis presiding over the ecstatic unification (pp. 204–205). Concretely: in authentic understanding it is the ecstasis of anticipation that presides, in inauthentic understanding that of being-tensed-toward; in authentic disposedness it is repetition, in inauthentic disposedness retaining; in falling and discourse it is presentifying. As is his habit, Heidegger is not content with this architecture of a wholly baroque rigour but tries at the same time to make us understand it as the differentiated repetition of one and the same structure, one and the same fundamental form of Dasein's existence: care (*Sorge*), which is not only the common denominator of all modes of existence but the structure unveiling Dasein's existence in its totality. The temporal interpretation of care in §65 of *Sein und Zeit* is therefore of capital importance, since it unveils the fundamental structure both of Dasein's temporal existence and of the ecstatic-horizonal temporalization of temporality, standing thus at the point of intersection between a temporal understanding of Dasein and a temporal understanding of being.

The distinction between authentic and inauthentic existence is often explained as resulting from the difference between a mode in which Dasein cares about its own being and another in which it is exclusively preoccupied with things (p. 205). This is not so, and the point matters when one recalls that for Husserl too the relation to self and the relation to things do not go one without the other. Heidegger says of authentic existence that through authentic self-understanding being-in-the-world is understood, and that thereby certain determinate possibilities of being-with-others and of commerce with intraworldly beings are already sketched out. Likewise inauthentic existence is not pure self-loss: in being-tensed-toward some event or process, our own Dasein is always co-tensed-toward; and in retaining and forgetting, Dasein is itself co-retained. The distinction does nonetheless concern the pre-eminence either of the relation to self or of the relation to things (pp. 205–206). In authentic existence Dasein expressly seizes the possibility of its own potentiality-for-being and grants it absolute priority; in inauthentic existence it turns away from that extreme confrontation with its own possibility — which is in truth impossibility and nullity — and apprehends itself within the reassuring frame of accomplishing the tasks of daily life.

The temporal sense of inauthentic existence makes us attentive to the fact that it can take two different forms, to which two distinct forms of temporality correspond (p. 206). The ecstatic unity of being-tensed-toward, retaining and presentifying can on one hand remain attentive to the temporal horizon it deploys — a horizon that is not the temporal determination of a being but its a priori condition of possibility. In this first form of inauthentic existence there is a commerce with things which, rather than sinking wholly into the things of objective time, remains attentive to the temporal horizon giving those things their temporal characters. On the other hand, inauthentic commerce with things can effectively proceed as if temporal determinations like "now", "then", "before" were objective properties of given things. In the first form Dasein turns away from its ownmost potentiality-for-being, is irresolute, but does not thereby close itself to the ecstatic horizon giving the being of things its temporal sense. In the second it turns a deaf ear to the question of the origin of the temporal determinations of objects and simply busies itself perceiving present beings, remembering past ones and expecting future ones (pp. 206–207). This second form reduces time to a succession of punctual nows and characterizes the "vulgar" conception, whereas the first puts to work the understanding of the essential structures of what Heidegger calls intratemporality (*Innerzeitigkeit*). Datability (*Datierbarkeit*) is one of these essential structures and as such makes possible the dating, measuring and counting of the successive moments of vulgar time. If vulgar time thus derives from intratemporality, the latter results from the ecstatic unity of presentifying, being-tensed-toward and retaining; and that ecstatic unity, from which inauthentic existence receives its temporal sense, refers in turn to the temporal sense of authentic existence.

This founding of the ecstatic horizon of intratemporality on a more originary ecstatic temporality put to work by Dasein's authentic existence has no equivalent in Husserl and remains difficult to understand (p. 207). It implies that there are two levels of understanding the temporal sense of the being of beings, and that these two levels divide according to two different modes of Dasein's existence. For the origin of time to unveil itself to the Dasein that interrogates it, that Dasein must adopt a certain style of life Heidegger calls authentic existence. This view commands the whole Heideggerian analysis of time, since it lies at the heart of the project of a fundamental ontology, which implies that the temporal sense of the presence-at-hand of things and the readiness-to-hand of tools can unveil itself only to a being whose essence is to exist and whose existence is the questioning of the sense of being. Making the analysis of Dasein's existence play the role of a fundamental ontology is not unlike the way Husserl founds the range of regional ontologies on a phenomenology of the transcendental subject. What by contrast remains inconceivable for Husserl, and constitutes a genuine *tour de force* on Heidegger's part, is that this fundamental ontology and the sense of being it aims at are said to depend on a factical mode of life of Dasein.

This first astonishment quickly yields to perplexity when Heidegger introduces the authentic mode on which his whole edifice of levels of temporality rests (pp. 207–208). Anticipatory resoluteness (*vorlaufende Entschlossenheit*) plays a decisive role there: a factical and concrete mode of living one's life by anticipating one's death and taking over one's own nullity. It is far from evident why just this mode of existence and no other reveals the temporal sense of Dasein's being. Heidegger's conviction seems to rest on the fact that it is in anticipatory resoluteness that the finitude of Dasein's temporal existence and the individuation of the individual reveal themselves most authentically. It nonetheless seems difficult to deny that in proceeding thus Heidegger makes the understanding of the most originary mode of temporalization depend on a relatively arbitrary and contestable idea he forms of the true sense of human life — the classic objection, reformulated forcefully in Ricœur's presentation of the analysis of temporality in *Time and Narrative*.

The temporality of Dasein's authentic existence is determined primarily by the ecstasis of the to-come, characterized at once as resolute anticipation of one's own death and as letting one's own life come toward oneself (p. 208). Life and death, and still more the doggedness of resolution and the grace of abandon, are inseparable and make authentic existence a procedure full of pitfalls. In inauthentic existence the ecstasis of the present dominates: in the circumspective concern of daily affairs Dasein interests itself in the presence or absence of things or tools that are suitable or unsuitable. But the temporality of concern is not limited to the ecstasis of presenting presence; that ecstasis is surrounded by those of being-tensed-toward the to-come and retaining the having-been (pp. 208–209). What is there "now" is at the same time apprehended and understood as what was present "before" and will still be present "after". The temporality of concern, which is also enunciated time in the form of the now, the before and the after, has as its own the capacity to be dated. Besides datability, Heidegger mentions extension (*Gespanntheit*), significance (*Bedeutsamkeit*) or destination (*Bewandtnis*), and publicness (*Öffentlichkeit*) as further essential structures of the time of concern and enunciation. It is in this temporal horizon that the things of daily concern appear and have their dated localization, which is why Heidegger also calls the time of concern intratemporality.

Heidegger undertakes to demonstrate that each of the essential determinations of intratemporality owes its sense to the ecstatic unity of being-tensed-toward, retaining and presentifying (p. 209). It is this ecstatic temporality that opens the horizon of intratemporality, which in turn permits dating, measuring and calculating the duration of things. In everyday life, when one reckons with time, one most often forgets the ecstatic horizon to which that time owes its sense: one counts in days and hours without a thought that day and hour owe their sense as usual measures of time to a mode of life in which the position of the sun and daylight played a determining role. With the replacement of sundials by artificial clocks permitting a measurement at once more precise and more objective, the last traces of the provenance (*Herkunft*) of everyday time from the ecstatic temporality of circumspective concern disappeared. So too disappeared the significant referral of the "now" to the "before" and the "after". In the levelling constitutive of vulgar time, temporality is brought back to a succession of punctual nows enunciated in the form "now-now".

#### III

What Heidegger calls vulgar time corresponds exactly to Husserl's objective time, determined and determinable by chronometers (p. 210). Likewise, what Heidegger writes about the measurement of time as a supposedly objective property of time recalls what the *Crisis* says of the forgetting of the subjective origin of the geometrical measurement of space. Finally, the founding relation between intratemporality and the ecstatic temporality of concern can without difficulty be paralleled with what Husserl says of the relation between objects contained in immanent time and the flux of absolute, time-constituting consciousness. That flux is an ecstatic unity in which a primal impression refers to retentions and protentions. Primal impression, retention and protention are genuinely ecstases, for they temporalize by going outside themselves, each referring to the others and together deploying the horizon in which a temporal object can be given to be seen as "now", "before", "after". Husserl shows clearly that these ecstatic unities are no longer intratemporal objects and that the predicates of the latter cannot be attributed to them without absurdity: it can make no sense to say of them that they are in the now and were before, that they are successive or simultaneous with respect to one another.

Likewise the double intentionality of the flux is singular, being a non-objectivating ecstatic intentionality and therefore more properly transcendence (pp. 210–211). In the transverse intentionality of retention what is aimed at is the continuous modification of the horizon of the having-been of immanent temporal objects; in the longitudinal intentionality it is the flowing of the flux itself that is, in Heidegger's language, co-retained. Exactly like the ecstatic temporality of concern, the flux of absolute consciousness makes possible the appearing of intratemporal objects while at the same time making the originary temporality of the flux itself appear, at least implicitly. In virtue of the ecstatic unity of primal impression, retention and protention, there is for Husserl no more than for Heidegger — and contrary to Brentano and Meinong — any purely instantaneous presence of an intratemporal object. The intratemporal present is a temporally extended, stretched field of presence (*Präsenzfeld*). Only the objective, naturalistic conception Heidegger calls vulgar puts to work the idealizing fiction of a time understood as a succession of mathematical points.

Alongside these similarities there are also disparities and even deep differences (p. 211). First, in *Sein und Zeit* the founding relation between ecstatic temporality and intratemporality is mediated by the analysis of historicity (*Geschichtlichkeit*). Historicity attaches on one side to the temporality of authentic existence, whose dimension of selfhood it develops; on the other it refers to intratemporality in that it is the condition of possibility of historiography, unthinkable without the dating of intratemporal events. A note records Ricœur's contrary reading, on which Heidegger cannot determine historicity without the results acquired in his analysis of the essential structures of enunciated or intratemporal time, so that the founding relation would be reversible and historicity and intratemporality equi-originary — a reading following from Ricœur's master thought that an analysis of originary time either as the time of subjective life or as the time of the objective world is equally partial and insufficient, the opposition between subjective time and world-time being surmountable precisely by a new meditation on historicity.

In the Husserlian analysis an explicit meditation on historicity is lacking for the same reason that the temporality of authentic existence is lacking (pp. 211–212). So long as the temporal ecstases are understood as moments of a pure and absolute consciousness, one hardly sees what they would have in common with the factical stretching (*Erstreckung*) of Dasein's life between birth and death. This absence of a historial understanding of time points toward a second fundamental difference concerning the subject's relation to time. For Heidegger it is temporality, or rather the originary impulse (*Schwung*) of temporalization, that makes Dasein's transcendence come about. For Husserl on the contrary there is a subjectivity this side of the originary temporality of the flux: if there is no living consciousness that is not temporal, there is nonetheless for Husserl an atemporal substrate of the subject, namely the bare identity of the pure I.

For Husserl the phenomenology of time belongs to an epistemological programme aiming to validate the natural sciences by a phenomenological science of the essential structures and constitutive functions of pure consciousness (p. 212). Husserl has no reason to follow Heidegger when the latter speaks of vulgarity, levelling and covering-over apropos of objective time. For Husserl a phenomenological founding of objective time casts no shadow or suspicion on the conception of time put to work by the natural sciences — as his marginal note on *Sein und Zeit* records: as if the "vulgar" conception of time did not have its own originary right, a right in no way diminished by constitutive analysis. For Heidegger this founding or derivation amounts in no way to a validation of objective time but rather to a destruction of it. The founding of the objective time of the sciences by originary existential time is in truth the narrative of a falling, of a degeneration of the origin of time (pp. 212–213). This falling, in which Dasein succumbs to the charms of the objective sciences and technology, is nonetheless not only, as an existentialist interpretation would have us believe, a matter of flight before death: what disquiets Heidegger in this decline is much more the forgetting of the question of being and of its temporal sense.

It is in the question of the sense of being and of the ontological difference that the deep reason for the third disagreement must be sought (p. 213). For Husserl's phenomenology, originary temporality belongs to the domain of pure consciousness, the supreme region among all regions of beings. Husserl is certainly led to exceed that frame when he discovers the singular intentionality or transcendence at work in the flux of absolute consciousness — a note recalls that he approached a transcendence beyond all intentionality by another route too, far more decisive in Heidegger's eyes, namely the analysis of categorial intuition in the Sixth *Logical Investigation*, where Heidegger says he first found the foundation of a properly phenomenological understanding of being. Husserl's notions of knowledge and truth do not, however, permit him fully to exploit the ontological signification of the temporal sense of that transcendence. If the ecstases of the flux permit a first approach to the temporal sense of the being of present-at-hand beings, the temporal sense of the being of absolute consciousness itself and of its transcendence remains largely unexplored in Husserl.

The distance separating the two phenomenologies of time reveals itself most clearly in the different signification each gives the concept of world-time (*Weltzeit*) (pp. 213–214). For Husserl world-time means objective time, the time of natural and causal reality. Heidegger by contrast understands world-time as the unity of the ecstematic horizons of temporality. The encompassing horizon of world-time schematizes itself according to the form taken by the ecstatic unity of the temporal ecstases: thus to presentifying, retaining and being-tensed-toward corresponds the world-time of the things of concern. Those things are not contained in that time; world-time is rather the horizon permitting an understanding of their temporal being — the temporal being of the ready-to-hand things of concern being called *Praesenz*. But world-time permits understanding not only the being of intratemporal beings; it is also what gives sense to the being of Dasein: the world is a determination of the being of Dasein, and Dasein's transcendence means understanding itself from the world. It follows that world-time plays an essential role in the way Dasein understands its own being. For Heidegger, then, world-time is far from being the objective and derived result of an originary, subjective, constitutive temporality as Husserl would have it. As the temporal condition of possibility of transcendence, world-time determines the temporal (*temporal*) sense of the being of all beings, whether Dasein, present-at-hand things, or ready-to-hand things. This difficult concept is the virtual focus of the whole Heideggerian analysis of ecstatic-horizonal temporality, and it is doubtless through it that Heidegger most radically distanced himself from Husserl's phenomenology of time. One should doubtless also say that through this concept of world-time a new thought of time and being announces itself, in which Heidegger takes leave equally of the questions concerning an origin of time, concerning an originary time bound to Dasein's existence, and concerning a temporal founding of the understanding of the sense of being from Dasein. The chapter thus ends by dissolving its own comparative frame: the terrain of encounter Bernet constructed is one Heidegger himself will abandon.

### 2. The Presence of the Past (Husserl)

Heidegger's way of interrogating the work of the great thinkers of the past has been decisive not only for what is called the history of philosophy but for the understanding of what the procedure of thought itself is (p. 215). For if the possible future of philosophy necessarily rests on a questioning backwards — the Husserlian *Rückfrage* — that means the sense of a philosophical work is to be understood as the opening of an inexhaustible questioning. There is no final resumption and no ultimate step back, no deconstruction that would exhaust a work's unthought. For this unthought does not refer only to zones left unexplored, to shadow-zones cast by the lighting of privileged questions; it concerns more fundamentally the light itself in which those questions stand out. That light resists thought not only because it lights and hides at once but because, like the Heraclitean *logos*, it is never identical with itself.

Truth and being are events demanding an understanding capable of inscribing itself in the horizon of time and history (pp. 215–216). Conversely, interrogating what time and history are also engages one to question being and truth. If we propose to study what the past is and how it remains and becomes present again, we find ourselves necessarily confronted with the questions of the sense of being and of truth — first questions of philosophy which nonetheless let themselves be approached only through this interrogation of the present past of the tradition of philosophical thought.

Husserl's phenomenology is a high place of that tradition and notably of that particular epoch of thought called the philosophy of transcendental subjectivity (p. 216). His analysis of time-consciousness leads the investigation of the transcendental subject to its extreme point, signifying at once its accomplishment and its bursting, or rather its rending. It is in Husserl's phenomenology that this rending occurs in exemplary fashion, because Husserl carried and sustained the tension between the thought and the unthought of a philosophy of consciousness to its breaking point. In phenomenology this tension is no longer that of the gap between thinking substance and extended substance, nor that between subject and object, but rather that between the presence and the absence of what appears. The crucial question becomes that of the phenomenalization of absence. For Husserl the fundamental figure of this presence of absence is not the horizon surrounding the manifestation of a *spatial* object but the temporal absence arising at the very heart of the transcendental subject's presence to itself. The subject can apprehend itself as constituting subject only if its presence exceeds the present and spills over onto the past present and the present-to-come. In virtue of an anticipative decision, or rather a prejudice whose weight will be felt throughout his analysis, it is in the presence of the *past* that Husserl sees the fundamental mode of this presence of absence. Hence the examination will concentrate on retention and re-memoration (*Wiedererinnerung*) — how the retention of the indelible trace of the elapsed present and the re-presentation of the past in remembering permit widening the frame of a phenomenology of presence (pp. 216–217). Naming the priority of past over future a *prejudice* is Bernet's own judgment, and it is the wedge by which the chapter will later question Husserl's whole metaphysics of presence.

This privileged attention accorded to presence and the present is not proper to phenomenology (p. 217). According to Heidegger it affects the way being and time, thought and truth have been treated throughout the metaphysical tradition. Bernet need not ask whether it is true that the history of philosophy has been nothing but the more or less faithful variation of a single scheme of thought, nor above all interrogate the possibility of a philosophical thought that would attempt to break with that tradition and "overcome" it. But nothing prevents expounding the Husserlian analyses in order to confront them subsidiarily with what Heidegger says are the classical positions of metaphysical thought. Three are retained: the privilege accorded to presence (*Präsenz*) as a horizon of givenness centred in the punctuality of the now; the perceptual approach to the given, apprehended as a subsisting being (*Vorhandenes*); and the accentuation of the analogy between time and space, which leads to considering time as an order in which temporal events are situated with respect to one another (pp. 217–218). Secondarily Bernet will pause over the conception of truth that follows, instituting a logic dominated by the subjective functions of identification and representation. It will be seen that there is not one of these great metaphysical options that is not interrogated, displaced and modified by the Husserlian analysis of the presence of the past. A long footnote records the more radical reading Bernet has attempted elsewhere and will resume in the Derrida chapter: that the analysis of the differential iterability of the retentional past may on the contrary contest the very notion of the presence of the present, the present retained belatedly being not a secondary modification of the originary present of the instant but that difference without which there is neither instant nor time.

#### I

Re-memoration of the past as Husserl understands it is an intentional act in which a past event is presently given as having been present (p. 218). If Husserl during his lecture remembers the illuminated façade of the theatre, that view is presently given to him in the mode of an object belonging to the past. In this memory, present and past are indissociably interlaced: the past is present as past, and this past is a past present. The presentification of the past thus links two types of present — the actual now of the lecture and of the perhaps importunate memory, and the past now in which the same person saw with his own eyes the illuminated theatre standing out from the darkness of the narrow streets of a sleeping town. Since everything past seems to have begun by being present, this notion of the actual present must be interrogated first.

It is significant that Husserl's first meditations on the presence of the present lean on the philosophical analysis of the perception of a thing extended in space (pp. 218–219). On that analysis, whoever perceives a building disposes of a raw given of impressions or sensations which, traversed or animated by an intentional act of apprehension (*Auffassung*), is interpreted as a partial appearance of the object relative to a particular point of view. Like the understanding of an image or a linguistic sign, perception would thus be made of interpretations of a significant given — with the capital difference that this impressional given is not a spatial object and is interpreted neither as a conventional sign nor as an analogical image but as the thing itself, originarily but only partially given. One can dispense with closer examination of this analysis, which at first sight seems to bring off the feat of combining the characteristic prejudices of an empiricist and of an intellectualist theory of perception at once. What matters exclusively is that Husserl begins by applying this theory of spatial perception without hesitation to the constitution of objective time, qualified for good reasons as "spatiotemporal".

The present instant of a railway journey, the objective now of the referee's whistle, owe their temporal qualification to the apprehension of a sensation-content endowed with the temporal form of actuality — that is, to a primal impression (*Urimpression*) apprehended at an individual point of the present instant (p. 219). It seems, then, that this primal impression is to be understood by analogy with those sense-data representing the localization of an object of perception in space. An object of perception would thus give itself to consciousness through the apprehension of three types of sensation whose interdependence would remain to be specified: qualitative sensations representing the material determination of an object, and sensations relating to that object's localization in space and in time. The most embarrassing question is not whether this primal impression belongs to an autonomous type of sensation or concerns only the temporal qualification of the present content of perceptual apprehension. There is another question no reader will fail to raise, and which is implied in the later development of Husserl's own analyses: how can the primal impression be defined as sensation of the present instant when the present instant itself is to be defined by originary sensation? (pp. 219–220.) This definition of the present is circular, and it does not tell us what makes the primal impression lend itself to being apprehended as the immanent representative of the objective instant.

Is it because it is situated in the present instant of the subjective flux of consciousness? (p. 220.) But reasoning thus, one must also ask whether the now of the primal impression must not in turn be founded in a still more primitive present. To say that the whistle is situated at the present instant of objective time, and that this objective present is constituted by the present instant of immanent time, forces us to admit a still more primitive time at whose centre the present instant of immanent time is constituted — and so on. This movement of infinite regress can be defused only if its deep causes are unveiled. If there is no end to the founding of the present, is it because this procedure of regressive founding is inscribed within a thought of the constitution of objects, or because time is there thought in the form of an encompassing container in which the present instant is found, or quite simply because there is no genuine origin of time? The three hypotheses are left open here and answered by the chapter's end in favour of the third.

Before pursuing this debate one must understand that consciousness of the present is not a simple instantaneous consciousness of a punctual now of objective time (p. 220). If the present lived experience were only a perception of an instant of objective time, there would be no time and in any case no perception of the duration of a temporally extended object such as a melody. It is to this last conclusion that the work of Brentano and Meinong led. Brentano affirms — at least in the early analyses of 1885/86 reported by Husserl — that there is perception only of what is given in the present instant, that only present objects of perception are real, and that a past object is therefore an unreal object, given at most in the manner of an image, a representation of an imaginary object associated with a perception and which as imaginary representation would somehow create the qualification of its object as past (pp. 220–221). The same limitation of perception to the grasp of a present instant, together with the analysis of objects essentially distributed across the points of a temporal succession in terms of objects of higher order, leads Meinong to deny that there is perception of a melody. A melody could be represented only once completed, by a simultaneous and retrospective grasp of all its phases and by means of the assumption (*Annahme*) of a logical form constitutive of the encompassing unity of its successive moments.

The nerve of Husserl's critique of Brentano and Meinong is aimed at the reduction of the present to the mathematical abstraction of an instantaneous point (p. 221). This is a dogmatic fixation of the analysis on the limit-concept of the geometrical point which, as the case may be, leads either to condemning the present-to-come and the present-having-arrived to non-being or to elevating temporal duration into the heaven of the constructions of categorial understanding. It is significant that Husserl contests these positions — inspired respectively by an outrageously realist metaphysics and by an ontological concern to inventory all beings through the grid of a hierarchical system of object-types — by leaning on psychological data, namely the necessity of a temporal extension of perception itself, which Stern called *Präsenzzeit* (pp. 221–222). Following Stern, Husserl considers that an act of perception is a continuous process traversed by a bond of consciousness, by an act of apprehension having a unity, and that already in a present phase of this process the consciousness of preceding and following phases is necessarily implied. A present phase of perception therefore relates to a succession of phases of the perceived object. The argument that prevented Meinong from admitting the possibility of perceiving a melody — that a succession of instantaneous perceptions never constitutes a present perception of a succession of objective instants — is thereby foiled.

Husserl's conception of a phase of immanent time, of the enlarged present of an act of perception, deserves attention, for it is implicated in the most important upheaval his analysis of temporality underwent (p. 222). Schematically analysed, the present phase of a perceptual process — often designated, in virtue of the diagram representing the constitutive modalities of the temporal flux, a transverse section (*Querschnitt*) — is composed of the intentional aim relating to a now, the aim relating to a series of instants just grasped in the now and which in receding have become past, and the aim relating to a series of future instants, that is, incessantly to come in the originary now of consciousness. Since the concern here is principally with the now becoming past and the past presentified in the now, and since Husserl analyses the consciousness of instants to come (protention) as a simple inversion (*umgestülpt*) of the structure of the consciousness of past instants (retention), the protentions can be set aside. If the present transverse section is to be understood as a non-independent phase of the perceptual process, it follows *a fortiori* that the now-point is only an evanescent moment, an ideal limit of the present phase (pp. 222–223).

In a first approach Husserl analyses this unity encompassing the consciousness of the now — of a musical chord, say — and the retentional consciousness of the preceding musical phase as an ensemble composed of intentional apprehensions interpreting a corresponding ensemble of real (*reell*) hyletic contents of consciousness as representing the actual instant and the past instants of the musical phrase (p. 223). These apprehension-contents being supposed temporally neutral — that is, not themselves relating to the temporal succession of the melody — it is hard to understand what makes some of them lend themselves to being interpreted as representing past phases and another as representing its present instant. The model of the perceptual process from which Husserl draws his inspiration leads him quite naturally to an empiricist solution consisting in imputing this difference of apprehensions to a difference at the level of the sensations functioning as apprehended contents. On this theory the aim at a now would be understood as the apprehension of an impression, whereas the retentional aim at the past would be an apprehension of a phantasm.

From 1908–09 the contradictory presuppositions of this construction and its principled incapacity to account for the retentional past are unmasked (pp. 223–224). The centre of this critique is the phenomenological observation that a real (*reell*) moment of the flux of immanent temporality cannot be held temporally neutral — and this holds for apprehension-contents as well as for the apprehensions relating to them. Every originary apprehension of objective temporality, whether its intentional aim relates to a present or a past instant of the melody, is effected in the now of consciousness. And every apprehension effected in the now can be apprehension only of a sense-datum equally present in that same now. Supposing the content apprehended by retention to be a phantasm instead of an impression therefore does not explain how an apprehension of a phantasm effected in the present could intuitively grasp anything past. The immanent now composed of present apprehensions of present contents can grasp nothing beyond the objective now of the melody: a series of primary contents coexisting in the now can never lead to the intuition of an objective succession, for the now cannot be present as non-now and the non-now cannot be given as now. That the non-now cannot be given in the now means that the past apprehended in the present can never be a constitutive moment, a real (*reell*) content of an act of perception effected in the now — the sound that has just elapsed, insofar as it is grasped in present time, is still grasped, but not as though it were truly sensed in the sense of a real content of consciousness. Is this to say that the past can never be given in the now? Certainly not, since that would amount to denying at once the possibility of perceiving a temporal succession such as a melody and the possibility of remembering that melody once perceived.

If the re-memoration of a melody is conditioned by the possibility of its perception, memory cannot be understood before an acceptable description of the extended present of the perception of a melody has been furnished (p. 224). More precisely, it must be explained, first, how a series of past objects can be intuitively given in the now of perception, and second, how the temporal character of this now of the act of perception and of the succession of phases of that perceptual consciousness is constituted. In a certain way, then, one is still seeking a phenomenological grounding for Stern's supposed solution to Meinong's problem: that the objective succession of the sounds of a melody can be perceived only through the subjective succession of the phases of the perceptual process (pp. 224–225).

In the abstractly isolated phase of the continuous act of perception, the grasp of the now of the perceived object is surrounded by a retentional and a protentional consciousness relating respectively to that object's past and future (p. 225). It is by leaning on this psychological datum that a Swiss mathematician, Andreas Speiser, could declare to his amazed students that whoever hears the first bars of a sonata and does not at the same time grasp its complete development is an idiot — and Valéry, who was no idiot, could complain that composers do not show themselves equal to what those retained first bars gave one to hope for: music bores him after a little while, since it comes to hamper what it has just engendered; rare is the music that does not cease to be what it was, that does not spoil what it has just brought into the world. The retention of what music has just brought into the world concerns at once the musical part already perceived and, at least implicitly, its past perception. It is therefore present retention of a past now of perceptual consciousness and of a past now of the perceived object.

Before considering the consciousness of perceptual consciousness, a last look at the relation between the subjective time of perception and the objective time of the perceived object (pp. 225–226). It has been shown why the retentional perceptual consciousness relating to the past phases of the melody could not be understood through the apprehension–content scheme. The arguments developed there do not, however, forbid still using that scheme to explain how actual perception relates to the objective now of the perceived melody. So long as one does not exceed the frame of a representation of an objective now by a subjective or immanent now, there is no logical contradiction in saying that it is the instantaneous apprehension of a primal impression that permits perceiving a musical chord as present in the sense of the objective now. The logical consistency of this description nonetheless rests on a series of natural, that is pre-phenomenological, presuppositions concerning objective time. Up to here it has constantly been assumed that the objectivity of the time of a succession of sounds is that of a spatiotemporal object represented in consciousness exactly as a thing extended in space is — whence the conclusion that the perception of a sound or a series of sounds is a perspectival grasp resting on sensations that are the immanent representatives of the transcendent object, playing the role of intermediaries. And, second major presupposition, it has constantly been assumed that the existence and properties of that thing itself are autonomous, in no way dependent on a perception coming to grasp them.

The first presupposition can be dismantled only by attacking the second first, but only the discussion of the first will introduce us to the properly phenomenological dimension of the problem (p. 226). The second concerned the natural belief in the autonomous reality of objective time, and holds the immanent time of lived experiences to be a derived representation reflecting objective time as a mirror does. This permitted concluding to the simultaneity of the objective and the subjective now, while supposing that the now of a perceptual act is situated in immanent time in the same way as the now of the perceived transcendent object is situated in objective time. This objective time represented in immanent time is "time" as Bergson distinguished it from "duration": a system of temporal ordering projected onto space and serving to localize objectively — once and for all and for everyone — the individual identity of an event with respect to other events (pp. 226–227).

Husserl's phenomenological procedure consists in contesting neither the nature nor the existence of this spatiotemporal or "cosmic" time but in analysing how it constitutes itself thanks to a naturalization or objectivation of the phenomenon of immanent time (p. 227). To understand the perception of a succession of sounds phenomenologically rather than naturally, a new analysis of the phenomenon of immanent time is therefore needed, and notably of the consciousness constituting that immanent temporality. Bernet notes parenthetically that he can dispense with treating in depth the constitution of objective time from the phenomenological given of immanent time — he will return to it briefly apropos of re-memoration, in which the reproduction of the past plays a preponderant role — but does not wish to conceal that the project of a grounding and constitution of objective time in the sense of contemporary physics by a phenomenology of the transcendental subject does not seem to have found a convincing outcome in Husserl's work.

A properly phenomenological analysis of how intentional consciousness apprehends the duration of a single sound or the succession of different sounds must bracket every naturalizing apperception of these objects (pp. 227–228). The being of the sound is thus reduced to its appearing in immanent time; the sound becomes an immanent moment of consciousness, an immanent object in the manner of the intentional lived experience of an act of perception. The conscious grasp of the continuous duration of a single sound or of a succession of different sounds must therefore be structured in the same way as the conscious grasp of the duration of an intentional lived experience that begins, endures, and is followed by another. It is unsurprising, then, that the new analysis of the perception of the temporality of sounds emerges in Husserl's writings only at the moment when the problem concerning the consciousness of the temporality of intentional acts found a satisfying solution, namely in 1909–11. This description of how the temporal duration of an act of intentional consciousness constitutes itself in so-called absolute consciousness will also give the lie to the first of the presuppositions mentioned — that the present perception of the now of a sound is to be understood through the scheme of the apprehension of an apprehension-content.

#### II

What Husserl calls an act or intentional lived experience is a given, a phenomenon, in which consciousness refers to an object (p. 228). The consciousness of an object is implicitly also the consciousness of the act. An act of perception is a perceived perceiving, a sensation is a sensed sensing. Perceived or sensed by whom? By the non-reflective or absolute consciousness necessarily accompanying every perceptual consciousness of an object and every sensation.
