In a phenomenological approach to time as Husserl understands it, everything follows from the analysis of this absolute consciousness of the temporality of acts of consciousness. Bernet can give only a fragmentary overview attaching above all: first, to understanding the present consciousness of a past act, the retentional grasp of the duration of an act; second, to interrogating the consciousness of the flux of absolute consciousness relating to the duration of an act; and third, to asking in what way Husserl's sketch of absolute consciousness accomplishes a decisive step toward an overcoming of a metaphysics of presence.

An analysis of the absolute consciousness of the duration of an act can partly draw on what was said about the perception of an object enduring in objective time (p. 229). It remains true that the conscious grasp of a succession presupposes a succession or at least a continuity of that consciousness itself. The present of absolute consciousness relating to an act therefore necessarily comprises, besides the privileged moment constituting the now of that act, moments relating to its elapsed and to-come phases: a phase of the flux, a transverse section, is composed of one or several primal impressions surrounded by retentions and protentions. What was said about analysing retention by means of the apprehension–content scheme also remains true. Whether it is a matter of the retentional grasp of the elapsed moments of an objective or a subjective duration, of the past of a spatiotemporal event or of an act, changes nothing in the fact that a present apprehension is unable to apprehend anything but a present content. One will sooner see a camel pass through the eye of a needle than a present apprehension of a present content grasp anything but a present object. As regards the retention of the elapsed phases of an act by absolute consciousness, applying the scheme is doubly absurd: not only does one fail to explain what one wants to explain, namely the present consciousness of the past, but one also supposes that a conscious act is grasped by absolute consciousness in the manner in which a spatial thing is grasped by perceptual consciousness, that is, through the intermediary of representational contents. Whatever the case with that representation of space, it is manifestly absurd to suppose that the absolute consciousness of consciousness has to be accomplished through a representational intermediary which one sees less than ever what it could resemble. The argument holds equally for primal impressions and protentions: the grasp of the duration of an act by absolute consciousness takes place without intermediaries (pp. 229–230). This is true for the instantaneous present of the act as much as for its elapsed or to-come phases — for, as Husserl says elsewhere, consciousness consists through and through of consciousness.

A clear answer can therefore be given to the question raised earlier about the grasp of an objective now in subjective temporality (p. 230). The now of objective time constitutes itself through an objectivation of the now of subjective time (the now of the act), which in turn is constituted by the primal impression of absolute consciousness. It is excluded that the apprehension–content scheme could account for the constitution of the now of an act or a sensation, and the constitution of a now of objective time is a matter rather of retention and reproduction of the immanent now of a lived experience than of objectivating apprehension of that immanent given.

The present of a phase of absolute time thus grasps the present moment and the past (and future) moments of an act (p. 230). Thus a single phase of absolute time grasps a multitude of phases of immanent time. But on closer inspection one must also say that a single phase of immanent time is grasped through a multitude of phases of absolute time: the same phase of the act's duration is first grasped as to-come, then as being present now, then as having-been-now, then as having-been-grasped-now as having-been now, and so on. This structure of the now becoming a having-been-now, becoming a having-been-having-been-now — this modification of retentional modifications of a primal impression of absolute time — deserves closer inspection, for it contains the key to Husserl's answers to the first and second of the questions mentioned.

The iterative structure of retentional modifications accounts at once for the consciousness of the act's duration and for the consciousness of the "duration", or rather the flux, of absolute consciousness (pp. 230–231). Retention is a present intention indissolubly bound to a present primal impression and relating to the primal impressions preceding the one it is bound to. Schematically: the retention associated with primal impression-2 is consciousness of primal impression-1; and the retention associated with primal impression-3 is retention not only of primal impression-2 but of the retention associated with it (retention-2), which is retention of primal impression-1. Primal impression-1 is consciousness of the now of an immanent object, say phase-1′ of the duration of an act. Retention-3 is a moment of absolute consciousness whose intentionality bears at once on the retention of object-1′ and on the retentional modifications the consciousness of that object has just undergone. In other words, retention-3 is at once and indissolubly retention of object-1′ and retention-3 of [retention-2 of (retention-1)]. According as one considers the intentional function of retention as bearing on an immanent object or on the retention of retention, Husserl speaks of the transverse or the longitudinal intentionality of retention, the names referring to the diagram by which he tries to represent this process graphically. It matters above all to understand that retention is a moment of absolute consciousness, that each new retention modifies all preceding retentions, and that in virtue of the structure of this modification each retention carries in itself the inheritance of the whole preceding process. One might therefore be tempted to consider consciousness a sort of bulimic ruminant continually swallowing new primal impressions and at each new ingestion rechewing everything it has in its stomach (pp. 231–232).

Becoming-past is thus not only accumulation or Bergson's snowball, and not only linear pushing-back, as though every new phase of consciousness pushed the series of preceding phases back a notch (p. 232). The past is continually remodified from the present of retention, and it is only this present modification of the past that permits the experience of temporal duration. The consciousness of the duration of immanent time is a matter of transverse intentionality, which is retention of successive immanent objects but also retention of the succession of objects and retention of the form of that succession. At the same stroke, through longitudinal intentionality, retentional consciousness is also conscious of its own "duration", that is, of the flux of absolute consciousness: from retention, to retention of (retention), to retention of [retention of (retention)], absolute consciousness appears to itself presently in its elapsed past. But must the present and past character of this absolute consciousness not be constituted in a still "more absolute" consciousness, and so on? To avoid such uncontrolled inflation of foundational levels, the relation between the duration of immanent time and the flux of absolute consciousness must be specified.

*First*, one must understand that the consciousness of the duration of immanent time and the consciousness of the flux — that is, transverse and longitudinal intentionality — are two intentionalities forming an indissoluble unity in one and the same flux of consciousness (pp. 232–233). Absolute consciousness thus grasps the retentional modifications of immanent objects and grasps itself through those modifications, or rather *as* modification, as modifying: it is in one and the same flux of consciousness that both the immanent temporal unity of the sound and the unity of the flux itself are constituted. *Second*, one must avoid so far as possible considering the structure of the flux as an isomorphic and simultaneous image or representation of the duration of immanent time (p. 233). Absolute consciousness is not a container in which primal impression and retention would be found; these are not localized in the same now, are not simultaneous, because they are not individual objects of absolute consciousness. Nor is the flux itself to be understood in the manner of a process of change of an object having a speed of flow and extended phases of rest or non-change. The regress of the constitutive procedure is therefore arrested at the analysis of absolute consciousness, which is non-temporal, namely nothing that is found in immanent time. And inversely, nothing is found in this absolute consciousness: it does not *have* retention, it *is* retention or primal impression. This being of absolute consciousness can be called flux only metaphorically — the flux is something we so name after what is constituted; for all this, names fail us. Husserl's later researches on temporality are essentially nothing but a sustained effort, to the end of his work, to name the living actuality, the primally standing-streaming pre-present (*urtümlich stehend-strömende Vorgegenwart*) of absolute consciousness.

Absolute consciousness is thus living and constitutive consciousness of the duration of immanent time and of its own flux (pp. 233–234). One could ask with Husserl whether in this appearing of the flux to itself the constituting and the constituted coincide totally; one could ask for explanations of the non-temporal character of the quasi-temporal insertion of the phases of the flux, which always and necessarily possesses the flowing now; and how to understand the non-temporal temporality of an ensemble of primal impressions given "at once" or "fore-at-once" in the present of this flux. But rather than stressing the manifestly unfinished character of these analyses, Bernet returns to the third question: in what way does this phenomenological founding of time inaugurate a procedure that separates from the traditional metaphysics of presence?

Several points have already been underlined where Husserl sets himself at the margin of a representationalist interpretation of the relation between the duration of immanent time and the flux (p. 234). Between the two types of consciousness there is neither structural identity (isomorphism) nor simultaneity. But immanent time is indeed an object of representation, namely the object of the transverse intentionality of absolute consciousness; and it even seems difficult to renounce using the structure of that constituted object as a guiding thread for engaging the analysis of the consciousness constituting it. Yet this constituting relation is not effected through the apprehension of an intermediary datum that would represent the temporality of immanent objects for and in absolute consciousness. And this constitution, effected through modifications of retentional modifications, can no longer be called perceptual if by perception one means an immediate relation of an act to its object. If on the contrary perception means intuitive grasp of an object in the flesh (*leibhaft*), then it must be said that through transverse intentionality absolute consciousness perceives immanent temporality — though obviously not as a spatial thing is perceived. The duration of immanent time is perceived through the mediation of modifications of retentional modifications and not through an intermediary representative. And this perceived immanent time is distinguished from immanent time as reproduced in re-memoration in that it is not an objective order assigning once and for all the same temporal localization to every immanent object (pp. 234–235). While one must admit that immanent objects are in a certain way *in* immanent time, one cannot say that this immanent time is perceptually given in absolute consciousness.

With the necessary restrictions, then, one can speak of a perceptual representation of the duration of immanent time and its objects by absolute consciousness (p. 235). But these terms lose all sense when one passes to the way absolute consciousness appears to itself. If in longitudinal intentionality absolute consciousness perceived itself as an object, that perception would have to be effected by a consciousness of a still more absolute level. The notion of longitudinal intentionality, in which absolute consciousness grasps itself as retentional modification in act, was elaborated by Husserl precisely to avoid the infinite regress in which every reflective theory of absolute self-consciousness is embarked. In longitudinal intentionality absolute consciousness therefore does not apprehend itself as an object, but in transverse intentionality it does apprehend objects. Since the two functions are said to be inseparable, absolute consciousness is found at the source of the difference between subject and object. But this subject is a spontaneity never in full possession of itself, since it apprehends itself only belatedly, that is, as having been retentional modification. The presence of consciousness to itself is therefore made against a ground of absence: consciousness gives itself only as having been given as [having been given as (having been given as…)]. This description of consciousness as a being that *has been* is not contradicted by the wholly central place the primal impression occupies in Husserl's analysis. That primal impression in no way exhausts the present of consciousness, since it is necessarily accompanied there by retentions; moreover, if it is indeed the grasp of the now of an immanent object, it appears to itself as action only insofar as that action is retained. If the time of this self-givenness of absolute consciousness can still be called "present", then it must be said that the "present" is an encounter or difference between "present" and "past" (pp. 235–236). This originary present (*Urgegenwart*) is a "present"-becoming-"past". This is the chapter's central positive result, and it is what qualifies the metaphysics of presence from within rather than from outside.

#### III

The notion of the retentional presence of the "past" in the "present" of absolute consciousness commands Husserl's analysis of temporality in all its ramifications (p. 236). It explains not only the self-givenness of the flux but how the duration of an act and the duration of immanent temporality are constituted. What was said of the constitution of the duration of an act by the transverse intentionality of retentional modifications therefore applies without the least change to a properly phenomenological description of the perception of the duration of a sound or of the continuous succession of sounds. But if a satisfying solution has thereby been brought to the classical problem of the perception of a melody, the same does not hold for the question from which these reflections set out: the presentification of the past in re-memoration. It remains to show how this new form of intuitive givenness of the past in the present distinguishes itself from retention while presupposing it — and to close the circuit by asking in what way Husserl's analysis of present remembering of the past serves the interests of a conception of truth inspired by a metaphysics of presence.

Re-memoration is a present act relating to a past act: I remember now, in the middle of a lecture, the illuminated theatre I saw yesterday evening (pp. 236–237). As an intuitive act aiming at its object through another act, re-memoration is to be understood by analogy with the intentional structure of an act imagining (*reine Phantasie*) the perception of an object, or with the appresentation of another's intentional lived experiences in empathy. In memory the illuminated theatre is given to me presently and intuitively, but not in the manner of something I would perceive in the flesh. What I presently perceive is partly masked (*Verdeckung*) by the remembering. It is often an element of the perceived environment that makes me remember, that awakens the past in a certain associative way: perhaps an attentive student in a drowsy auditorium makes me think of the illuminated theatre in the middle of a sleeping town. Nothing prevents the place in which I remember and the place of what I remember from being one and the same. What is capital, however, is that the now in which I remember and the now I remember can never coincide: an act of memory never remembers itself. Second capital observation: I can remember only my own experiences and not what someone else might have seen in my place. The present act of memory and the past experience remembered belong to the same consciousness, the same immanent duration; through the difference of their temporal situations their belonging to a single consciousness is affirmed — which is precisely why memory is the privileged site of the constitution of the ego. Third essential characteristic: memory is the identical reproduction of a past experience, a repetition of the same straddling the temporal distance between lived experiences situated at different points of one and the same conscious duration. It is through this synthesis of the recognition of identity between a present and a past lived experience that the identity of a temporal object and the identity of that system of temporal localization which is objective time are constituted.

Re-memoration is a present act grasping what has been present insofar as it is present in the past present (pp. 237–238). This present consciousness of a consciousness that has been present must be distinguished from the way absolute consciousness is retentionally conscious of an act that has been present, and this for two reasons: first, because the act of memory and the act remembered are both objects of immanent temporality constituted by absolute consciousness; second, because this reproduction of a past act by a present act is possible only if the reproduced act is one that has been present for absolute consciousness and has become past while remaining retentionally present in the transverse intentionality of that consciousness. For both reasons, a reproduction of an act is always the reproduction of the absolute consciousness by which that act was constituted. Moreover, this reproduction is possible only insofar as the reproduced act has become past while remaining present in the infinite iteration of retentional modifications — which means that an act can be reproduced now only if it has been present and if the precise localization of that past now within the duration of immanent time can be recovered. That individual place constitutes itself precisely in the living present of the transverse intentionality of modifications of retentional modifications.

This does not mean, however, that an act can be reproduced in re-memoration only insofar as it is still intuitively grasped in the present of the retentions of retentions (p. 238). To the extent that the flux advances, that the series of modifications of modifications lengthens and swells, and that the retained act is ever more distant from the actual now, the intuitive character of this transverse intentionality progressively fades. Our culture has tried to compensate for this progressive forgetting of the past by recourse to the symbolic representation of lived experiences, notably by writing (pp. 238–239). In this sedimentation of life, texts, but also paintings, photographs and oral tradition are the present substitutes for a lost past; they serve as material support, as memorial, for the search for that lost time. The symbolic order is thus a means by which absolute consciousness seeks to supply its incapacity to keep intuitively present the whole duration it has engendered, and to surmount its incapacity to link intuitively the present now of memory to the past now of what is remembered. A footnote records Duval's objection that such a presentation of memory misses the fundamental dimension of the past, that of the irreversible absence of what has been forgotten.

There is therefore no presentification of the past without retention, for without retention there would be neither past present nor present past (p. 239). But the way re-memoration presentifies the past differs fundamentally from the presence of the past in retention. The retentional modification transforming the now into a past has the character of a continuous gradation; by contrast there is no question of a continuous passage from impression to reproduction. Every now of an immanent object, by the mere fact of being constituted in the flux, tips over into the retentional past — for it is quite evident that everything which is, by the mere fact of being, will become having-been, and is, by the mere fact of being, a having-been-to-come. By contrast the reproduction of an act is not an automatic consequence of its temporal character, for the presentifying act is in its turn temporally extended exactly like the earlier perceptual act; it reproduces it, makes it flow phase by phase. In other words, there is a doubling identification, an "once again" presupposing the constitution of the duration of the reproduced act. Re-memoration is the repetition of the same across the difference of temporal localization between the presentifying and the presentified act. It is because the re-memoration of a thing implies the reproduction of the past perception of that thing that the perceived and the reproduced thing cannot be confused. A theatre I perceive is a present theatre, grasped through the apprehension of real (*reell*) contents representing its spatial form and qualitative determinations; and by the now familiar argument, the present apprehension of a present content can never grasp anything past (pp. 239–240). The same argument prevents confusing re-memoration with the perception of an image: perceiving a present image is never perceiving a past original. Moreover, perceiving an image as an analogical representative of an object presupposes the at least ideal possibility of comparing image and object, and therefore of perceiving both at once; if re-memoration were the perception of an image of the past, that would presuppose the contradictory possibility of perceiving an object *as past*.

The presentification of the past is therefore a representation of a representation of an object, and Husserl is much preoccupied that this second representation be a faithful copy of the one it reproduces (p. 240). But how be certain that what I now remember happened formerly exactly as I remember it? Knowing the importance of re-memoration for attaining objective knowledge of nature as of spirit, one understands that the question tormented Husserl. With an honesty verging on masochism he catalogues the causes of error: confusion (*Vermengung*) between past and present experiences, repression (*Verdrängung*), condensation (*Verdichtung*), fabulation (*Ausmalung*), overpainting (*Übermalung*) and covering-over (*Verdeckung*). For him, far from being constitutive of the very nature of memory, these pitfalls must and can be avoided by repeating a memory, explicating the temporal horizon of the lived experience remembered, and advancing from present toward past with prudence and in small steps. It is therefore possible that an act of re-memoration not be mistaken about a past lived experience, provided one proceeds with precaution. A memory of a near past (*Naherinnerung*) can thus be apodictic, and in the limit it is even ideally possible that it be adequate (pp. 240–241). This possibility follows from the fact that in such a case the distance separating present and past can be crossed in a fully intuitive manner: through a continuous progression within the duration of the reproduced past one can rejoin the present of the act of reproduction. The list of error-sources is Husserl's own, and Bernet's point is that Husserl treats as avoidable accidents what another tradition will treat as memory's very structure.

The truth of the presentification of the past in memory therefore depends on the possibility of memory's being a faithful reproduction of the past (p. 241). That fidelity is one of correspondence, which one would wish adequate, between a present now and a past now; and this re-productive doubling of consciousness can be adequate only by surmounting the temporal distance between present and past memory. Which is to say that the concept of truth inspiring Husserl's analysis of remembering proceeds from the wish to neutralize temporal difference by a doubled presence of intentional consciousness to itself. The analysis is marked by a sort of epistemological obsession leading one to interrogate the truth of memory as correspondence, the being of consciousness as representation or reproduction, and the temporal absence of the past as a masked presence of consciousness to itself. We are therefore far from an understanding of historical truth in terms of the temporal truth of being (*Seinsgeschick*), and far from a truth of the past constituting itself through historical explanation or narrativity. And despite some striking similarities, Husserl's description of memory is the opposite of a psychoanalytic conception of the belated truth of the split subject of speech. A phenomenology to come, rather than attaching itself to the study of the presentification of absence, will have to interrogate further that movement of radical differentiation Husserl called the flux of absolute consciousness. The verdict is thus divided: Husserl's retention breaks the metaphysics of presence open, his re-memoration seals it shut again — and the chapters that follow take up the two halves separately, memory in the Proust and Barthes chapter, differentiation in the Derrida chapter.

### 3. The Framing of Memory (Husserl, Proust and Barthes)

The way the present calls up the past and the past integrates itself into the present raises many questions (pp. 243–244). How does something present become past? Where does the past take refuge in receding from the present, and how does one reach the distant place where it rests? How and why does one forget what one has known, can forgetting be avoided, and are all types of object forgotten and remembered in the same way? How be sure of the truth of what one believes one remembers? Is memory an act of will and intelligence? How are images and signs, and their material presence, implicated in the reproduction of a past experience? Does every form of the past lend itself to memory? Is there a common past one might remember together? In what sense can one speak of a past withdrawn from all memory, or even of a past that was never present? Must one remember, and must one remember everything?

A phenomenological description of memory understood as the encounter of a human subject with its personal past cannot wholly evade these questions (p. 244). In interrogating the "frame" that gathers the past into the present by giving it a sense, Bernet chooses to draw attention above all to the role played in memory by the suggestive force of images and the power of conceptual categorization. Does the past not often resemble what we expect of it? Is memory not necessarily partial and selective? But then what good is remembering if it is only a matter of assuring oneself of what one already knows? Is it not a form of invulnerability and even of immortality that we aim at in this crossing of time by memory? In briefly recapitulating the analyses of Husserl, Proust and Barthes, Bernet will try to show not only how these three thinkers struggle with the question of the framing of memory but that their conceptions of memory differ precisely in that each belongs to a different frame. All three nonetheless share the conviction that this frame is the result of the life-experience a subject has acquired or of the convictions a community of subjects has forged over the course of history. The remembered past being of subjective essence, memory neither can nor ought to lay claim to a perfectly objective truth. In passing from voluntary memory in Husserl to involuntary memory in Proust, and stopping at the way Barthes interrogates their difference and the nature of a memory that pierces the pre-established frame, the personal implication of the subject in memory will appear ever more clearly. Nothing, indeed, speaks more eloquently of a subject's style of life, its hopes and its anxieties, than its way of remembering and forgetting.

#### Epistemology of Re-memoration in Husserl

Husserl went to great trouble to furnish a precise description of the experience of the past he calls re-memoration (p. 245). This pronounced interest in the possibility of recovering a past lived experience so as to relive it unchanged is nonetheless accompanied in him by a great disquiet, explained notably by the fact that the possibility of repeating the past in the present is at the same time an essential condition of all scientific knowledge and consequently of the constitution of phenomenology as rigorous science. The object of science is characterized by its ideal identity, and that identity is established only in that encounter of present with past which Kant called a synthesis of recognition. Re-memoration confers on the ephemeral lived experience and its intentional object the stable form that alone permits the development of an eidetic science of constituting consciousness. The same re-memoration is also what permits the inscription of an immanent lived experience in the objective time of the world: if I could not remember my own past, I could not communicate it to another or consign it in a writing that in principle renders it accessible to all and forever. It is hardly astonishing, then, that Husserl's analyses of intersubjective exchange, of the conservation of sense (sedimentation) and of its transmission draw largely on the structure proper to the act of re-memoration. Husserl's interest is thus motivated above all by that structure's exemplary character, and the disquiet accompanying this search for lost time is due principally to epistemological preoccupations. The chapter's frame-thesis is thereby applied to Husserl first: his frame is epistemological, and his account of memory is what that frame permits.

The structure of re-memoration is common to the large class of intentional lived experiences Husserl calls acts of re-presentation or presentification (pp. 245–246). Every presentification is an act of doubled presentation, an intentional act unveiling a double presence: empathy opens at once onto the perceptual presence of another's body and the comprehending presence of his mental life. In an act of re-memoration I bend presently over a past lived experience which I resuscitate by rendering it present again without forgetting that it belongs to a past present. I relive now what I have already lived in the past, and so live simultaneously in two presents that nonetheless remain separated by a certain temporal distance.

Refusing to assimilate the remembered past to a simple mental image, Husserl maintains forcefully that this past is truly, that is, originarily, present in the present act of re-memoration (p. 246). Bernet illustrates with his own case: writing this text, he remembers his first visit to Hadrian's villa some years ago, remakes "once again" the walk through the ruins under a leaden sun, sits "anew" under the great trees of the restaurant near the car park and eats there "as the first time" with his wife and children while night falls and a little breeze rises. He does not merely pretend to be at Tivoli; he really is there while remaining seated at his desk. He is not merely making "a film" by projecting past events on some interior screen; he imagines nothing and relives everything. In remembering, one truly returns to a past experience while realizing that what one saw and felt formerly is no longer given in the flesh. This loss of direct contact is nonetheless often compensated by the new pleasure of recovering the past and by the possibility of lingering there at will. In remembering, one walks about in one's own life as much as, if not more than, in a distant site. More precisely, says Husserl, one rejoins a past object because one has succeeded in recovering the intentional lived experience in which it was formerly apprehended; it is this doubling of intentional consciousness that explains why the past object, though given anew in the present, is recognized as belonging to the past.

In considering re-memoration as an act of presentification, that is, as the experience of a double presence, Husserl does not seem to doubt for an instant the possibility of acceding to the past from the present (pp. 246–247). What would make difficulty would rather be understanding how the experience of a doubled present can be accompanied by the legitimate claim to give account of the experience of the *past*. It can be so only if the presence of the act of re-memoration and the presence of the lived experience remembered do not coincide. But whence does the present recovered in an act of re-presentation get its character of "past"? How does the past introduce itself into the permanence of the presence of consciousness to itself? Whence comes this gap, within present consciousness, between what is presented there as present and what is presented as past?

Husserl attempts, not without success, to answer all these questions by means of his analysis of retention (p. 247). Retention is presented as an intentionality of a particular type making each new lived experience drag with it the trace of earlier ones in the manner of a comet's tail. At each instant consciousness is conscious both of the present instant and of the past instants of the same flux. In straddling the distance between the now and the non-now, in associating them indissolubly, the present instant of consciousness is conscious of the time of consciousness, that is, of its uninterrupted change and its unitary flux. Present consciousness is the witness of its own renewal as much as of the dispossession of what was its own. It is astride present and past; it apprehends itself as being what at the same time it already is no longer.

If one may suppose there was a first instant of a duration, the second would be consciousness of a new now and at the same time retentional consciousness of the first elapsed instant (pp. 247–248). At the third instant, the apprehension of the new now would be accompanied by the retentional memory of the second instant, which in turn was already accompanied by the memory of the first. Ideally, at each instant consciousness would thus apprehend in a single act a new now together with its whole past as it unrolled from instant to instant. Re-memoration would then have only to draw on this unlimited reserve of retentional consciousness to make the past live again. It would be, in Husserl's terminology, a secondary memory resting on the primary memory of retention — the two forms differing notably in that retention merely registers a past which re-memoration re-presents by traversing it anew and explicitly.

What is to be retained from this presentation is above all that the past is there the object of an explicit and voluntary repetition whose fidelity is assured, in principle at least, by a retention maintaining the elapsed flux of lived experiences within the living present of that same consciousness (p. 248). Husserl is of course not unaware that retentional conservation has its limits, but he considers those limits due to empirical contingencies. For his theory, forgetting is only an accident. What one has forgotten is never truly lost, since a fortuitous association suffices to awaken its memory. For Husserl, forgetting in no way threatens the continuity between the present and the past of consciousness, and re-memoration is like the celebration of this imperturbable coherence in the historical development of consciousness. To remember is to unroll the carpet of one's life, to tread it again so as to assure oneself with satisfaction that it has stayed safe from moths and from the patchings of repression. The means Husserl recommends for guarding against the illusions and confusions that nonetheless lie in wait is entirely revealing: in case of doubt, one must pursue the course of the past until its remembering issues in the actual present of consciousness.

In Husserl, then, the re-presentifying act of re-memoration not only assures the identity of the object and the possibility of science but reassures the subject as to its personal identity (pp. 248–249). Re-memoration permits me to recover my past and thereby contributes to my finding my bearings in the changing course of my life. Retention holds the past at my disposal; it answers "present!" to the call of my re-memoration. If not all memory is voluntary, it remains that any object surging unexpectedly from my past would only awaken my consciousness to the recognition of itself. Everything I remember can therefore appear to me only in the form of the familiar or the "already seen". In such a conception, where nothing belonging to consciousness can be entirely lost for consciousness, a person's past remains forever accessible and requires no operation of deciphering or decryption to be recovered.

If consciousness conserves everything, and if re-memoration always moves on known terrain, then the past integrates itself quite naturally into the present of the one who remembers (p. 249). Re-memoration deploys a frame ready to welcome into the present any past whatever belonging to the same consciousness. The past being an elapsed present and the present the continuation of that past, there is no ground to fear that present consciousness will be perturbed by the memory of the past. In reanimating the past, consciousness only "revises" the experiences of its past life. If this studious operation does not engender pure boredom, it is because it permits the subject to widen the field of self-knowledge while preserving it from loss by forgetting. Re-memoration reassures the subject; it resembles those insurance contracts by which we guard against the possibility of a future loss of what we acquired in the past.

Everything in Husserl's analysis is thus commanded by the idea of a flawless continuity of the living present of consciousness (pp. 249–250). To remember is to keep the past present so as not to risk losing it in the future. Exaggerating a little, one could say that in Husserl there is no loss of self except what might occur in the future, and that this is perhaps what led him to envisage that future above all under the perspective of the faithful preservation of the past. Husserl's re-memoration thus cruelly lacks that plastic force (*plastische Kraft*) Nietzsche demanded of whoever wants to make good use of the past — a use "for life" which employs forgetting and the deformation of the past to assure the possibility of a future creative of new values. The use of memory in the service of a project of renewal of life in the future thus enters into competition with a use whose ambition is to serve unconditionally the truth of the re-presentation of the subject's past life. In wanting to remain too faithful to the past, one risks becoming unfaithful to it by cutting it off from every perspective of future.

On closer inspection, the absolutely faithful repetition of the past and the personal identity it is supposed to guarantee are at best a regulative idea and at worst a fantasy (p. 250). In most cases the re-presentation of the past rests not only on a retention immanent to consciousness but on a material inscription of that past in some significant object — a photograph, a written sign, a story told, or any other private or public memorial. This material support favours the possibility of re-memoration while compromising its purity, that is, the unconditional fidelity of the repetition. As Derrida showed apropos of Husserl's analysis of the constitution of the ideal object, repetition engenders at the same time both identity and difference. The project of an identical repetition of the past is quickly submerged and carried off by the movement of life, characterized by its differential iterability and its undecidability (pp. 250–251). It follows that a representation of the past in the form of a doubling of the presence of the past in the present of consciousness not only lacks perspectives as to the future but is quite simply impossible. It is again Derrida who, following Paul de Man, draws the conclusion that imposes itself: memory is always allegorical.

#### Aesthetics of Involuntary Memory in Proust

At first sight Proust's analysis of memory is at the antipodes of Husserl's (p. 251). Is Proust not the writer of a lost time recovered only *in extremis*, whereas Husserl is the more assured of recovering the past in that it was never truly lost? Is time in Proust not pure nothingness in which a few isolated memories float like islets, whereas Husserl's time is a continuous duration crammed with memories attached to one another and all firmly moored to the present instant? Is Proust not obsessed with the disappearance of the familiar world, the bursting of self-identity, in short with death, whereas Husserl's subject plunges back into its past "at will" to repeat it and thereby confer on it an ideal existence bordering on eternity? Is Proust not preoccupied with signs and secret metaphors rather than with a quasi-visual re-presentation of an elapsed present kept by retention in close contact with the self-presence of actual consciousness? Does Proust not hope everything from the unmerited grace of a melodious and affective accord among sensations rather than from a studious revision of the past?

Doubtless — but this in no way excludes that memory is for Proust also a matter of attentive study, elaboration and purification issuing in a generalization or even an idealization of the sensation of the past (pp. 251–252). In Proust as in Husserl, memory remains haunted by the desire for eternity. Proust also shares with Husserl the cult of the contemplation of the past and the quest for originary lived experiences. And if he insists on the affective force of involuntary memory by opposing it to the "frozen memory" of facts he calls voluntary memory or memory of the intelligence, it is not said that either really goes without the other.

The involuntary memory Proust celebrates and scrutinizes is an *aesthetic* memory (p. 252). It springs from the quasi-musical accord between sensations or impressions belonging to different times, is accompanied by the grace of an incomparable happiness, and issues in the effort of artistic creation. Each of these three moments is the object of attentive examination on Proust's part. One must not lose sight, however, that this systematic study is inserted within the frame of a literary work. One cannot analyse the long meditation on time and art in *Le temps retrouvé* without taking account of the fact that the narrator abandons himself to it in a particularly significant place, the library of the Guermantes mansion. Nor must one forget that at the moment the narrator recounts how this meditation issues in the decision to write, he has himself already practically completed his work. It is clear too that the hero trying to recover lost time by memory is a character imagined within a novel, that his memories are fictive and owe their existence to an act that is already an act of writing (pp. 252–253). Thus, in studying the affective impact certain very precise memories produce in a person whose history is unique, Proust never leaves the frame of artistic fiction and generalization — a point strongly underlined in Ricœur's reading, whose central thesis is precisely that the experience of time cannot do without its refiguration within a narrative frame that is often fictive: one must renounce reliving the past if lost time is to be recovered.

In writing "a fable about time", Proust would thus be moving rapidly away from the epistemological frame in which Husserl developed his analysis — unless that epistemological frame is itself, as Nietzsche still claimed, to be understood as a fable (p. 253). It is in any case evident that both forms of analysis belong to a pre-established frame, and Bernet will try to show that the same holds for every memory. Whether memory is voluntary or involuntary, whether it confirms the frame within which it inserts itself or perturbs it, it remains that it needs to be *framed*.

It has rightly been stressed how far Proust's involuntary memory stands out against a ground marked by those experiences of death which are the loss of self, the loss of the time of the first strong emotions, and the loss of the familiar world of a room (p. 253). Proust's encounter with death occurs above all in the fringes of sleep, in the moments of falling asleep and waking. But life itself, insofar as it is caught in the linear and continuous unrolling of time, also appears to him as a race toward death. Involuntary memory, on the contrary, breaks with the monotony of time's indifferent march; it gives back what one is on the point of losing or has already lost without hope of recovery. Such memory appears as a help from on high bringing the gift of life (pp. 253–254); involuntary memory is the experience of a resurrection. The surprise of seeing a lost past emerge from a cup of tea is thus doubled by the happy assurance of having truly lived before dying.

This double happiness nonetheless remains enigmatic, and in the library scene the narrator resolves to pierce its mystery at last (p. 254). The first step is unsurprising: Proust discovers that involuntary memory is the result of associative chains — the badly squared paving stone in the Guermantes courtyard awakens the memory of the two unequal flagstones of the baptistery of Saint Mark's in Venice. It is to be noted, however, that Proust will recover the memory of Venice only through his body, by repeating several times, at the risk of making the innumerable crowd of tram-drivers laugh, the same step with one foot on the higher stone and the other on the lower. In Proust, recovering the past therefore does not simply mean re-presenting it but plunging back into it physically while enjoying the accord between sensations some of which relate to the present and others to the past. To study the conditions of the felicity provoked by involuntary memory comes down to learning to decipher the signs or hieroglyphic characters that bear this happy accord. If the analysis of involuntary memory is rooted in a movement of bodily imitation, it nonetheless issues in an apprenticeship in signs which in turn prolongs itself in the exercise of writing.

The associative mechanism by which ancient and buried sensations are resuscitated is for Proust of affective nature (pp. 254–255). The shock of sensations produces something like an affective resonance opening a thousand sealed vessels each filled with things of an absolutely different colour, odour and temperature. And yet, says Proust, in this crowd of memories rising at the call of a spoon that a clumsy servant knocks against a plate, there reigns a harmony permitting one to enjoy the essence of things. Proust's crucial passage is then quoted at length: the cause of that felicity was guessed by comparing these diverse blissful impressions, which had in common that he experienced them at once in the actual moment and in a distant moment, to the point of making the past encroach on the present and making him hesitate as to which of the two he was in; the being that tasted this impression in him tasted it in what it had in common between a former day and now, in what it had of the extra-temporal, a being appearing only when, through one of these identities between present and past, it could find itself in the only milieu where it could live and enjoy the essence of things, that is, outside time — which explained that his anxieties on the subject of his death had ceased at the moment when he had unconsciously recognized the taste of the little madeleine.

This capital text shows clearly that the identity between present and past must not be understood as an identity of repetition but rather as an identity of *confusion* setting in motion a process of idealization of the sensation (p. 255). Proust's involuntary memory does not stop at the comparison between present and past sensation; it scarcely troubles to localize an event in past time but rather leaps outside time. In the consonance of present and past sensations a new sense is produced whose blazing intensity and truth have no equivalent either in present or in past time. This is why it is quite useless and even inadvisable to return to Balbec.

But a new disquiet rises in the mind of the solitary thinker in the Guermantes library: this contemplation, though of eternity, was fugitive (pp. 255–256). Being now decided to attach himself to this contemplation of the essence of things, to fix it — but how, by what means? It is again Swann who puts the narrator on the right track: no longer by ringing at the door to visit his parents, but by having missed the artist's vocation which the happiness felt in listening to the little phrase of Vinteuil's sonata had awakened in him. The narrator draws the lesson that imposes itself: he will be a writer and will set to work at once, seeking to prolong the instants of happiness of involuntary memory, their gripping perspective on the essence of things, their evasion outside time, by submitting them to the work of writing that will make them accede to eternity.

The library meditation thus leads, in its rigorous logic, from a treatise on the art of memory to a treatise on art *tout court*, that is, to Proust's *art poétique* (p. 256). This is carried by the conviction that literary creation rests on a spiritualization of the impression closely resembling what Husserl called eidetic variation. Poulet dismantles its subtle mechanism by appeal to the famous passages on the hawthorns. In a first movement, the narrator seeks to surmount the distance and alterity separating him from the spectacle of the shrub's flowers by trying to mime within himself the gesture of their efflorescence. He accomplishes a second step toward the essence of things when the grandfather, noticing the child lost in contemplation of the white flowers, shows him other hawthorns a little further off whose colour is no longer white but pink. This double effort of idealizing identification issues at last in the creation of a metaphor, a condensation of a series of different yet interlinked impressions; and it is thanks to this metaphor that the narrator arrives at the recognition of the extra-temporal essence of the hawthorns and of the impressions of happiness their contemplation procures (pp. 256–257).

Even if this assimilation of the essence of things to a metaphor puts us at the heart of literary creation, Proust insists repeatedly that the work of writing gains by being sustained by a work of intelligence and thought: one had to try to interpret sensations as the signs of so many laws and ideas, trying to think, that is, to bring out of the penumbra what one had felt, to convert it into a spiritual equivalent (p. 257). It is true, however, that the passage continues: and this means, which seemed to him the only one, what was it but to make a work of art?

The interiorization of the impression by imitation, and the comparison that extracts its common essence by subsuming it under general laws, thus lead necessarily to art and particularly to literature (p. 257). Writing translates life, extracts its essence by subtracting the impressions of present and past from the contingencies of time, and by the rings of a beautiful style creates its metaphorical equivalent. Proust is quoted: one can make the objects figuring in a described place succeed one another indefinitely; truth will begin only at the moment when the writer takes two different objects, posits their relation — analogous in the world of art to what the unique relation of causal law is in the world of science — and encloses them in the necessary rings of a beautiful style; even, as with life, when in bringing together a quality common to two sensations he releases their common essence by uniting them so as to subtract them from the contingencies of time, in a metaphor.

Proust thus remains faithful to the idea so dear to the philosophers of German Idealism, that the work of art succeeds in translating and transfiguring what is absolutely singular to the point of rendering it significant for all (pp. 257–258). His literary work therefore consists in extracting the generality of our sorrow, in writing of it. Only when it is transposed into a work of art does the life of an individual subject acquire a genuine signification. For Proust all the innumerable disappointments and sufferings he has incurred are justified after the fact, from the moment they become the matter of a book. To write is thus not only to recover the lost past; it is also to find a necessary sense in having wasted one's time by postponing from day to day the moment when one would finally set about writing "the book". In inserting the art of memory within his *art poétique*, Proust slides from the analysis of involuntary memory resuscitating the lost past toward an enterprise in which the past is legitimated to the point of rendering its future loss unthinkable. Beyond lost time and beyond recovered time opens the extra-temporal reign of artistic creation.

The last pages of the *Recherche* thus lead to a resolution of the very tensions on which the whole book fed (p. 258). Tension between the utterly intimate interiority of impressions and the exteriority of their literary exploitation; between the anguish of an existence subject to time's inexorable course and the happiness of an instant prefiguring eternity; between lost past and recovered past, between the memory of intelligence and involuntary memory; between life's sufferings and their artistic utility; between the affective lived experience of involuntary memory and its methodical analysis. In short, tension between life and work, between naked truth and transfiguring fiction. These tensions are surmounted by according an absolute pre-eminence to the work over life, to the general over the particular, to useful experience over crushing suffering. It is probable that the reader who has patiently followed Proust's narrative to the last pages is a little disappointed by this final "good news". Bernet's disappointment is philosophical, not aesthetic: the frame has closed over the punctures that opened it.

Turning now to Barthes, one meets a far more explicit approach to the tensions characterizing our relation to the past (pp. 258–259). In insisting on the insurmountable contrast between the *studium* and the *punctum* of an image or a memory, and equally on their complementarity, Barthes nonetheless returns to the best of Proustian inspiration. Is the reader of the *Recherche* not particularly struck by those moments when, thanks to some insignificant detail of the narrative, his own life seems to come to meet him by leaping the margins of the page he is reading?

#### "Studium" and "Punctum" of Memory in Barthes

With Barthes the frame of the analysis of memory changes once again (p. 259). This time memory is no longer a voluntary re-presentation contributing to constitute the truth of an object of science, nor that involuntary memory which a work of art tears from time. The frame in which Barthes examines memory is neither science nor art but *photography*. The memories that fascinate him are in particular those released by the few photographs he has left of his recently deceased mother, going back to her childhood. But beyond this change of frame, Barthes is also the first of these authors to make the frame of memory the subject of his meditation: the framing of memory, which was already the vanishing point of the preceding reflections, thus becomes the object of an explicit analysis.

Photography is a paradoxical object (pp. 259–260). On one hand it lends itself to an indefinite reproduction such that the various copies engendered by a purely mechanical process can all be considered originals. On the other hand the photograph is an emanation of the referent, the absolute Particular, sovereign Contingency. The mere fact of looking at a photo and not at its subject demands of us an unaccustomed effort. Barthes evokes at length the effect produced on him by photographs that are portraits of himself, analysing finely the effect of the pose leading to an imaginary identity and betraying itself insidiously in the fact that he never stops imitating himself. He is astonished too before the contrast between the almost immortal solidity of the image and the fragility of a self that through its incessant change scarcely recognizes itself.

In fixing a fleeting moment in an immobile image, photography is a death sentence solemnly proclaiming: *that has been* (p. 260). Paradoxically, it is precisely the hyperpresence of the referent in the photograph that arouses the impression of its inaccessible absence. The photograph can thus be understood as an invitation to mourn the reality it withdraws in the very act of giving it. But it can also impose itself on us as the return of a past that, in the manner of a spectre, rises from its tomb to haunt our present life. In interrupting the duration of the present and fixing the past in its mute absence, photography leads us toward a beyond of time and history which is nonetheless not the eternity of the work of art but rather the death of all life. Barthes is quoted: photography does not re-memorate the past — there is nothing Proustian in a photo; the effect it produces is not to restore what is abolished by time or distance but to attest that what I see has indeed been.

Does this disenchanted finding hold for *every* photo? (pp. 260–261.) It seems not, since a certain photo makes a mark in him: this photo happens to him, that one does not. It is in trying to understand the reasons for this enigmatic fascination that Barthes introduces the distinction between *studium* and *punctum*. The studium resembles the voluntary memory of the intelligence; it considers the photo with a sort of general investment, eager certainly, but without particular acuity. The photo is looked at through a grid of reading, or rather a frame, which is that of knowledge or of the a priori belonging to a certain cultural field. What one sees, though particular, is immediately subsumed under the generality of a known photographer's style, a genre of photography, or quite simply a certain idea one forms of reality. The punctum by contrast is a detail of the photo inflicting something like a wound or a sting: this time it is not I who go looking for it; it departs from the scene like an arrow and comes to pierce me. The punctum is what in a photo strikes to the point of resisting every habit of categorization; it provokes in me an affective trouble that is precisely the symptom of the failure of the frame of a knowledge.

It is therefore clear that the punctum "points" me only insofar as it perturbs the studium of the photo (p. 261). Its arrow reaches me the more deeply the less my studious contemplation prepared me for it. A detail can strike only by departing from a frame, and to one who does not attentively study a photo nothing ever *happens*. Which is to say that punctum and studium do not go one without the other. The photographer must therefore guard against wanting to deposit the punctum himself in the image he composes, lest it cease to be that at once inevitable and gracious supplement and be in turn demoted to the status of a mere studium of the gaze. This gracious supplement is nonetheless, in Derrida's consecrated expression, an originary supplement producing belatedly that to which it is said to add itself. If there is no punctum without a pre-given studium, the punctum nonetheless overturns the conditions of vision to the point of calling for a new studium — which may in turn be perturbed or distracted by the unexpected emergence of a new punctum, and so on indefinitely.

The punctum belongs among those superfluous details which, far from being ineffective, produce on the contrary an incontestable reality-effect (pp. 261–262). It is the abundant accumulation of details and their gratuitous distribution — that is, without recognizable intention — through the narrative that contribute most effectively to giving fiction the appearance of reality. This holds not only for imagined reality but more generally for every experience of reality, that is, for reality as *phenomenon*. Is the impression of having to do with the real not largely tributary to the resistance certain details oppose to every attempt at integration into a pre-established frame? Is the real not always what withdraws from categorization? The real is what is and remains outside-the-signifier, in Lacan's phrase.

One sees clearly what a study of memory can draw from this distinction (p. 262). It permits, in the first place, understanding that Husserl's voluntary memory and Proust's involuntary memory are in truth not so far from one another as one wanted to believe. Would the emotion of involuntary memory happen to someone who in his life had never applied himself to reliving his past? And why struggle to recover one's past if one had not already felt the happiness of having been plunged back into it as if by enchantment? The dialectic of punctum and studium contributes not only to clarifying the sense of the distinction between voluntary and involuntary memory but the functioning of involuntary memory itself. Recall the celebrated passage where the small gesture of taking off one's shoes awakens in the narrator the memory of the tender, preoccupied and disappointed face of his grandmother, whose death had nonetheless left him indifferent. Through the fleeting apparition of this detail (punctum), the conception the narrator formed of his grandmother (studium) changes radically: there comes to him a mad desire to throw himself into her arms; it is as if for the first time he recovered the living reality of his true grandmother; and yet it is at the same instant, and again as for the first time, that he had just learned that she was dead (pp. 262–263). In this moving passage the resurrection of the grandmother is inseparable from the lucid and painful consciousness of her death. The narrator there accomplishes that work of mourning which, far from introjecting the dead by identifying with them as in melancholia, gives them on the contrary a life beyond death: the life of memory.

The studium of memory is the frame welcoming the past into the present and permitting the one who remembers to assign that past a precise place in the course of his life and to give it sense (p. 263). The evocation of the past thus leads to reassuring oneself about the continuity of one's life and the permanence of one's personal identity. This security and this purity of self-consciousness nonetheless exact a heavy tribute: to enter a single frame, all memories must bend to the same demands, and this fatally effaces their difference and their possible incommensurability. Rather than an idea or a concept in the Hegelian sense, the frame is a *fantasy* — a schematic and illusory image one has forged of one's past and one's life. The past is condemned either to confirm this image or to disappear from consciousness; it no longer opens any perspective on the future, no longer holds any surprise. Memory risks asphyxiating in a sterile repetition of the identical, in a regression toward an originary past whose sense is only too familiar and which nothing, however, will ever give us back. Memory thus slides toward nostalgia, which forgets forgetting and forgets at the same time that the sense of the past remains inexhaustible because subject to incessant refiguration. In nostalgia one is doubly mistaken: one believes one knows the past and one believes that nothing will give it back (pp. 263–264). One claims to dispose of a past one declares at the same time to be inaccessible. The desire for the past thus becomes an incurable suffering by miring itself in the contradiction of celebrating a loss one has nonetheless never truly undergone or accepted. A footnote adds a personal note — Bernet, an expatriate Swiss, cannot resist recalling Kant's passage in the *Anthropology* on Swiss homesickness, a longing for the places of simple youthful joys in which returning visitors find themselves disappointed and thereby cured, believing everything there has changed but in truth because they cannot spend their youth there again.

Even in the extreme case of nostalgia, then, the frame or studium of memory cannot be understood independently of the wound or punctum that the loss of mastery over our own past can inflict (p. 264). The frame's function of favouring categorization and therefore comprehension of the past cannot be separated from its defensive role of protecting the subject from the encounter with a past whose sudden emergence would wound it. If this frame is imaginary and therefore always more or less illusory, it must be considered a necessary or, more exactly, a vital illusion. The frame serves to neutralize the effect produced on the subject by the return of a traumatic past, a past that would introduce itself into our personal life in the manner of a foreign body. Far from lulling itself in a tranquil illusion knowing nothing of the punctum, the subject on the contrary uses the studium so as not to be submerged by a memory that would make it lose its anchorage in the present. What is at stake is thus less the preservation of a supposed purity of self-consciousness than the warding off of the risk of the contamination of our life by the unbearable. This is the chapter's answer to its opening question: the frame is not a failure of fidelity to be corrected but a condition of survival.

Every memory therefore evolves between the extreme possibilities of a studium stifling the plastic force of the relation to the past and a punctum risking the annihilation of the subject (pp. 264–265). Nostalgia and trauma are thus the threatening poles of that magnetic field, that tension, in which the subject's desiring relation to its own past is inscribed. In remembering, the subject calls and anticipates the past (studium) and is at the same time interpellated and touched by it (punctum). It is in search of the truth of its past and yet could not do without the emotion that past provokes in it. Seeking that emotion in the encounter with its own past, it wants to know that past; but to know a past that says nothing to us is without interest. Affect and representation, the evocative force of the detail and the quasi-presence of the past in re-presentation, do not go one without the other. The subject desires the presence of the past, but at the same time willingly contents itself with a sign or a metaphor helping to hold that past at a distance. In its desiring relation to the past, the subject seeks at once what fills an expectation and what arouses another.

In memory the subject is in search of a living past, that is, a past rich in promises for the future (p. 265). Paradoxically, it is precisely what threatens the possibility of recovering the past that is the source of memory's fecundity and happiness. There are no reunions without separation and loss; there is no memory without forgetting. Happiness is to remember something one had not noticed or had omitted to engrave in one's memory. Whoever remembers discovers with wonder and as for the first time the richness of his life, and lives more intensely because he is conscious of braving death. Thanks to memory the "that has been" of my past life is pushed back toward the limits of an immemorial past. My distant death, sketched by the limits of my capacity to remember, is at the same time what brings me closest to a life that infinitely exceeds me: the life of other men and women, past and to come; cosmic life; the life that creates without having been created.

### 4. His Master's Voice (Husserl and Derrida)

Derrida's *La voix et le phénomène* has had a singular destiny (pp. 267–268). Hailed on its publication in 1967 as a remarkable work, its influence nonetheless suffered from the simultaneous appearance of two other books of more varied composition and doubtless easier access, *De la grammatologie* and *L'écriture et la différence*. Whereas the earlier long and profound Introduction to Husserl's *Origin of Geometry* (1962) was scarcely noticed beyond a restricted circle of initiates, *La voix et le phénomène* contributed much to establishing Derrida's international renown — a renown that has grown, sustained by publications succeeding one another at an ever more accelerated rhythm, to the point of becoming, in the United States at least, a cultural and media phenomenon. This is not the place to interrogate the complex links binding the joyous deconstructionist horde to a thinker working in solitude whose voice grows ever more anxious. This new intellectual wave concerns us, however, in that it has distorted or even prevented, with rare exceptions, a reading of *La voix et le phénomène* equal to the richness and ambitions of the text. Bernet evokes two types of approach which, while opposed, share a total misrecognition of the book's key question: how is one to listen to the voice of one's master?

The first type of reading drowns the fish in the admittedly abundant flood of what is called "Derrida's thought" (p. 268). One brings this "Introduction to the problem of the sign in Husserl's phenomenology" alongside the contemporary texts on Saussure, Freud, Hegel and so on, and uses those comparisons to read in it anticipations of Derrida's later work. *La voix et le phénomène* is presented as the first milestone in the evolution of an autonomous thought which used Husserl's philosophy only the better to adjust its aim on the whole metaphysical tradition, guilty of a common attachment to presence. In proceeding thus one professes an interest in the postmodern reading of philosophical texts inaugurated by Derrida while at the same time ignoring *La voix et le phénomène* as a text to be deciphered. This is how "the" thought of dissemination, undecidability and "difference" is overtaken by a hasty systematization carting along all the prejudices of traditional history of philosophy while renouncing its rigour.

The second type is that proposed by certain Husserlians alarmed as much by Derrida's style as by his thought (pp. 268–269). Often they set about reading the book very late, on the basis of insistent and disquieting rumours about an author become famous thanks to other writings deemed more frivolous. To judge Derrida on the evidence comes down for them to verifying whether his listening to Husserl's voice has been faithful or not. Consequently *La voix et le phénomène* is read and appreciated as a commentary or interpretation of the first chapter of the First *Logical Investigation*, "The essential distinctions". This way of reading accords little attention to Derrida's preoccupations as they manifest themselves in his other texts, and above all it ignores the author's explicit warning that the book proposes a reading of Husserl which can be simply neither that of commentary nor that of interpretation. Thus one interests oneself in the deconstruction of the Husserlian distinction between expression and indication without realizing that it also implies the impossibility of an absolutely faithful expression and comprehension of Husserl's thought. And one ends by criticizing the book in the name of an immanent understanding of the sense of a work which Derrida had shown with much justice to be inhabited by the infinite return of exteriority, contaminated at its heart by the emergence of an irreducible alterity, and confessing the finitude of understanding by promoting phenomenology as an indefinite task. The chapter's title is thus its problem: to read Derrida on Husserl is to face the question of fidelity that Derrida's own argument renders unanswerable.

#### I

Bernet's approach wants to avoid the reef of treating this text on the basis of prejudices the text itself proposes to shake, but also to facilitate the reading of a work famed for its difficulty (pp. 269–270). That difficulty holds in large part to the fact that Derrida pursues very diverse objectives within a hundred-odd pages: he dismantles minutely the Husserlian distinction between expressive and indicative signs, and uses this microphenomenology of the sign as a springboard to leap with both feet into the interrogation of fundamental concepts such as the phenomenological reduction, transcendental consciousness, constitution, and the language of phenomenology. The rigour and ambition of these analyses must not deceive us as to the fact that they serve principally to open a much more general debate concerning the whole philosophical tradition, and notably the repression of the exteriority of the written sign, of the alterity of a non-originary present, of the heteronomy of a subject caught in the unforeseeable web of intersubjectivity, and of the finitude of a life constantly confronted with death. This issues in a philosophy of *différance* that upsets many received ideas and established values of traditional philosophy while confirming their importance and even their primacy. Bernet wants to show that these diverse preoccupations hold together and integrate themselves into a structure or order that is certainly not axiomatic but in which each element echoes all the others, and in which Derrida's voice amplifies and clarifies itself by dint of being reflected by ever new representations.

What this voice proclaims, or rather what its multiple echoes repeat, is precisely that there is no voice without representation, no origin without the supplement of repetition, no life without death, no presence without absence (p. 270). Sense springs from the gap, the *différance*, between the interiority of thought and the exteriority of the sign, between the presence of an identical sense and its changing representation, between the intimate and unsayable self-presence of the subject and its alienation in the language of all. The thought of *différance* contests the philosophy of presence not in the name of absence but in the name of the indissoluble interlacing (*enchevêtrement*) of presence and absence, essence and fact, time and space, spirit and flesh, sense and sign, perception and imagination, speech and writing. The precise analysis of each of these figures confirms that *différance* does not let itself be reduced to the form of a dual relation of opposition which would in turn lend itself to a reduction deriving one term from the other. For Derrida *différance* is irreducible: there is no philosophical or phenomenological reduction leading to the primitive unity of a Same identical with itself which would give itself an Other only in addition and by force of a negation of itself.

It is by following the trace of such an irreducible or undecidable *différance* that Derrida approaches each of Husserl's essential distinctions: between the expressive sign that gives sense and the indicative sign that lacks it; between the intuitive presentation of an ideal object and its imaginary or linguistic representation; between an immediate self-presence and the loss of self in discourse; between the now and its *après-coup*; between the interiority of intuitive thought and the exteriority of its inscription in language (pp. 270–271). Each stage of the demonstration counts as a defence and illustration of the same argument: none of these distinctions can be understood as concerning two separate and autonomous essences, or as one derivable from the other. If this double movement of an essential distinction and its subordination under the guiding idea of a primitive unity is supposed to represent the operation of the phenomenological reduction, one understands why Derrida wants to show the impossibility of such a reduction. But one must not lose sight of the fact that a philosophy of *différance* cannot claim to substitute itself purely and simply for a philosophy of essential distinctions, since *différance* manifests itself only through the failure, or rather the indefinitely deferred outcome, of the phenomenological reduction to presence. This qualification is Bernet's, and it will govern his defence of Husserl later in the chapter.

The essential distinction on which the book chiefly insists is the one Husserl introduces at the start of the First *Logical Investigation*, between expression and indication (p. 271). These are two sorts of sign whose difference is defined as a function of the presence or absence of the sense represented by them. An expressive sign gives its sense to be seen and forms with it an intimate unity comparable to that between soul and flesh, between seeing and the eye, between listening and the ear, between touching and the hand: the expressive sign is the *organ* rather than the mere representation of sense. The indicative sign, by contrast, gives more to believe than to see; it puts one on the track of a sense rather than capturing it, calls rather than gives. Roughly, the expressive sign is the linguistic sign that says what it means and effaces itself before its sense, whereas the indicative sign is a relatively autonomous object of perception which in addition makes one believe, recalls or warns. Indicative signs are sometimes natural objects such as fossils making us believe in the existence of prehistoric animals; but most often they are wholly fabricated objects such as the road sign warning of an unexpected bend, or the knot in the handkerchief Husserl says he uses to recall his decision to become a better man (pp. 271–272). It is unnecessary here to enter further into the detail of Husserl's semiotics, which is nonetheless not without merits.

What interests Derrida above all is, first, the criterion in whose name this essential distinction operates and, second, the particular case of those speech acts where, on Husserl's own admission, one and the same linguistic sign functions at once as expression and as indication (p. 272). While letting himself be guided step by step by Husserl's analyses, Derrida pursues a wholly different objective. For *Husserl*, what distinguishes expression from indication is its proximity to the thought of a subject who knows what he means and says what he knows; the interlacing (*Verflechtung*) of expressive and indicative function in the same sign is for him only an accidental contamination to be got rid of as fast as possible when one wants to preserve the purity of a wholly expressive language. *Derrida*, on the contrary, holds this interlacing to be essential because it gives to be seen how every sign is worked by *différance*. It is in the name of that essential or originary *différance* that he will deconstruct the idea of presence serving as foundation for the distinction.
