He will thus push Husserl's text to the point of making it confess the impossibility of a pure expression, and conclude that the condition of possibility of the showing of a thing, and so of the phenomenon, is not to be sought on the side of the consciousness of a subject present to itself but precisely on the side of the interlacing, the play of *différance* between expressive and indicative sign. The phenomenon does not depend on an interior voice in which the subject addresses itself in the secret of consciousness; on the contrary it finds its condition of possibility in the sign understood as *trace* or as *originary supplement*.

The first step of the argument, which decides all that follows, concerns the foundation of the distinction (p. 273). For Husserl, expression is endowed with a sense, a *Bedeutung*, that indication lacks. Indication cannot however be devoid of all sense, since it is a sign (*Zeichen*) and every sign signifies, that is, refers (*zeigen*) to something beyond itself. The *Bedeutung* of expression is therefore a sense of a particular type: its particularity consists in the fact that its referring depends on an intentional mental activity, and that the expressive sign is nothing but the carnal envelope of that *Bedeutung*. In expressive speech the pronounced sounds are infinitely close to their *Bedeutung*, render it present and form with it an indissoluble phenomenal unity. The gestures with which I involuntarily accompany my words, by contrast, are far from coinciding with the content of my thought, and Husserl consequently holds them to be indicative signs. Derrida is therefore right to say that what distinguishes expression from indication is its proximity to the pre-expressive layer of thought, together with its voluntary and explicit character. The *Bedeutung* manifesting itself in the expressive sign is a *vouloir-dire*, and the expressive sign is the effective saying of a sense preceding language.

According to Derrida this definition rests on a double idea of presence: self-presence of the thinking subject, and presence of the intentional object to that thought (pp. 273–274). The expressive sign aligns itself on the demands of this double presence: it approaches as far as possible the *vouloir-dire* of the thought it expresses, and it anticipates the intuitive presence of that thought's intentional object. The idealization and spiritualization of the expressive sign must be understood in the light of this proximity to the ideal *Bedeutung*: the concrete presence of the sign in empirical reality (token) is only a particular representation of the ideal and immutable form of the sign (type); the spiritualization of the sign's stuff is the reason for the prevalence of voice over writing. In its ideal form the sonorous sign is infinitely close to the ideality of the *Bedeutung*: the latter makes itself heard in the double sense of *entendre*. It is true that this *Bedeutung* must not be confused with the object or state of affairs to which the expression refers in virtue of its intentionality, but it is no less true that to *entendre* an expression well is also to grasp the reality it aims at. Even if false speech is not devoid of *Bedeutung*, there is no doubt that for Husserl the goal of speaking is to say and to make heard the truth of the real. On this precise point Derrida marks himself off vigorously by maintaining that there is no true speech that does not run the risk of tipping into the false, and no expression whose fidelity to the *Bedeutung* could exclude all misunderstanding. Once pronounced, the sense of the expression detaches from the speaking subject; it escapes him as does the object it speaks of, whose originary presence will henceforth be supplanted by its linguistic representation, that is, by what has been said of it. There is thus no presence without representation and without the threat of loss that representation makes hover over originary presence — and this holds for the presence of the (ideal) object as much as for the self-presence of the subject or the instantaneous presence of the present now.

The play of *différance* appears most clearly in Husserl's text in the form of the interlacing between the expressive and the indicative function of the same linguistic sign (p. 274). Husserl admits this occurs *in fact* whenever my expressions serve to communicate my thought to another: the same utterance I use to express my thought is apprehended by my interlocutor as a mere indication of what I mean. If for me what I say and what I mean are in principle identical, it is not so for the other, who hears what I say without my thought being originarily given to him: what is for me an expression of my thought is for the other only an indicative manifestation (*Kundgabe*) of what I mean. Husserl is not unduly disquieted by this *de facto* interlacing, since on his view it does not threaten the essential, *de jure* distinction between the nature of the expressive and of the indicative sign (pp. 274–275). He thinks that the interlacing characterizing communication changes nothing in the fact that for the one who speaks and hears his own voice, that voice remains a pure expression of his *vouloir-dire*. Soliloquy therefore seems to be an exemplary phenomenon, capable of exhibiting the nature of expressive signs in all its purity.

It is at this precise point of Husserl's reasoning — before the critical examination of soliloquy — that the polemical aim of Derrida's interpretation first surfaces (p. 275). *First*, Derrida seems to defend the view that an essential distinction not found as such at the level of concrete phenomena would be contrary to the principles of phenomenology and therefore inapplicable and condemnable: Husserl would in a way betray the phenomena of *de facto* interlacing in favour of *de jure* distinctions whose principles would follow from the prejudices of a metaphysics of presence. But is it not also true that a phenomenology confining itself to a simple description of phenomena as they present themselves *in fact* would quickly exhaust itself and in any case never manage to account for the whole richness of concrete data? Is there really occasion to choose between phenomenology and a science of essences when one knows that it is precisely essences that give the analysis of phenomena its orientation and thus prevent the dispersion of the phenomenological gaze? Even if there were in fact no case of a pure expression, the essential distinction would remain valid and would constitute a precious means for analysing the multiple forms of their phenomenal interlacing. Essential distinctions therefore remain an indispensable presupposition of a phenomenology of *différance* — and it would be astonishing if this had escaped Derrida. This is Bernet's first substantive objection, and it turns Derrida's own dependence on Husserl against the claim to have dissolved him.

To understand better this putting in question of the essential distinctions, one must appeal to a second moment of Derrida's critical interpretation, concerning no longer the *eidetic* but the *phenomenological* reduction (pp. 275–276). Derrida presents the latter as a change of gaze leading the phenomenologist to turn away from the empirical existence of things in the world in order to devote himself, in the form of an interior reflection, to the examination of the life of transcendental consciousness that gives sense to those things, that constitutes them. His interpretation owes its great originality to treating the reduction in superimposition with the essential distinction between indication and expression: the stake is to give the reduction a linguistic form, or rather to show its rootedness in language, or in a certain idea of language. In drawing too exclusively on the essential distinctions of the first paragraphs of the First *Investigation*, Derrida scarcely gives himself the means to carry this enterprise through. The result is doubly problematic: on one hand he rejects the phenomenological value of the distinction between expression and indication because he makes it bear the whole weight of his grievances against the reduction; on the other, by understanding the reduction in too close a relation with Husserl's preference for expression over indication, he skews its founding idea and ends by deconstructing a reduction more phantasmal than phenomenological. If the reduction is made solidary with the search for a case of pure expression, and if soliloquy seems the only case realizing such purity, it follows that the reduction to transcendental consciousness would be a reduction to solipsism or to a "voice that keeps silent". Every reader of Husserl knows, however — as the well-informed reader Derrida is could not ignore — that the transcendental consciousness unveiled by the reduction is in no way cut off from the world and from commerce with other subjects.

If Derrida's conclusions on the nature of the reduction are certainly too hasty and even abusive with respect to Husserl's work as a whole, the same does not hold for the premises of his interpretation (pp. 276–277). It is incontestable that Husserl privileges expression over indication, and no less true that expression distinguishes itself from indication in virtue of its proximity to intentional consciousness. Derrida is right too to stress that indication rests on the empirical reality of the sign and on a signifying relation operating by means of a causality belonging to empirical psychology. Nothing of the sort on the side of pure expression, which in the case of soliloquy dispenses with the physical existence of the sign, effects a spiritualization and idealization of it, and refers to its object in virtue of an intentional consciousness that knows it is thinking and knows what it thinks well before entrusting itself to a linguistic expression. The passage from indication to expression is therefore indeed a figure of the return to pure consciousness, and it is not abusive to speak of a "proto-phenomenological reduction". Even if the thought presiding over expression is not yet transcendental consciousness, it testifies to the same fascination for a consciousness totally present to itself. It follows that if a pure expression proved impossible because of the impossibility of a pure and immediate self-presence of the speaking subject, that would also affect the project of the reduction to transcendental consciousness — in any case in the Cartesian formulation of that project. Derrida is perhaps not wholly convincing when he wants to persuade us of the impossibility of the reduction, but he is certainly not wrong to accentuate the phenomenon of soliloquy and to make it at once the pivot of the argument about expression and indication and a fundamental stake revealing a difficulty affecting the whole phenomenological project.

#### II

Derrida summarizes Husserl's argumentation in favour of soliloquy as the very type of a pure expression by reducing it to two distinct convictions (pp. 277–278): in soliloquy the expressive sign rises as it were automatically to the status of an ideal object, an identical object maintaining no essential link with the world of empirical things; and soliloquy is expression of a presence of the subject to itself, a presence that not only precedes expression but can also be realized without its concourse — the subject experiences its life and knows its thought well before signifying them to itself by means of an expression. The two arguments can also be summarized thus: in soliloquy the subject has no need of the physical existence of the expressive sign, contenting itself most often with imagining that sign; and soliloquy, the voice that hears itself speak, is only an unproductive doubling of the life of a subject having an immediate reflective consciousness of its intentional acts. Derrida submits both to rude trial: he will seek, first, to demonstrate that the ideal identity of the expressive sign acquired thanks to imagination and assured through repetition remains precarious, because always at the mercy of a compromising with empirical reality; and second, that the subject never disposes of a full and immediate consciousness of itself, because the representation the subject gives itself of itself in "the voice", far from being a pure auto-affection, necessarily implies an *alienation*. Both series question the possibility of a pure presence — a fully intuitive and ideal presence — on the basis of its inevitable contamination by an impure representation. It follows that every presence remains tributary to an empirical exteriority belonging to indication or to "the trace".

The first argument takes up the long analyses Derrida had devoted to the presence of the ideal object in the Introduction to the *Origin of Geometry* and applies them to the case of the expression of thought by means either of an effectively pronounced or of an imagined word (pp. 278–279). For Derrida the effective, physical presence of the word in the empirical world never occurs without a contamination of the spirituality of its sense and without damage to the ideal identity, the univocity and the intuitive presence of its *vouloir-dire*. The expressive sign, once pronounced or written, is irreversibly drawn toward the troubled ways of indication. What Husserl mentions as a simple fact — that in solitary discourse we normally content ourselves with represented rather than real words — is understood by Derrida as a necessity: whoever wants to preserve the purity of expression, that is, to prevent its slide toward a compromise with indication, must abstain from speaking effectively. Expression is defined by the fact that in it sound makes itself the transparent representation of the presence of sense. Pure expression therefore lends itself to indefinite repetition without the clarity of its sense suffering, without its sense modifying or being lost. It is only as an ideal object that expression acquires this immutable purity, for everything empirical is impure, changing, torn apart by space and damaged by wear. The representation of words by means of imagination, with which Husserl says one can content oneself in solitary discourse, is interpreted by Derrida as an essential means of the purification of the sign, that is, of the constitution of the word as ideal object: the apprehension of an ideal object such as the identical sonorous form (type) of the word "lion" is most easily realized by means of an imaginary variation — I imagine different persons pronouncing this word with variable accents, intonations and intensities so as to make the common type of these multiple tokens appear.

The Introduction to the *Origin of Geometry* had already remarked that what contributes decisively to assuring the immutable presence of an object such as the sense of a geometrical theorem is at the same time what risks its loss (pp. 279–280). For the sedimentation of ideal sense in writing this reversal is particularly striking: once fixed in writing, sense remains constantly to hand, but it risks at the same time congealing and being lost in the blind repetition of a single formula indefinitely recopied. It is precisely in this context that Derrida had for the first time spoken of *difference*. If there is no repetition of the same without alteration, then there is no ideal object whose identity would maintain itself unchanged through its multiple representations. One must conclude that the presence of the ideal object is indefinitely deferred by the representations it nonetheless cannot do without: the ideality of the ideal object does not lend itself to an achieved presence; it is an Idea in the Kantian sense. Instead of assuring the maintenance of the same, repetition introduces difference and thus contributes to deferring infinitely the immutable presence of the same. A repetition is always impure because it hollows out the separation between the presence of sense and its re-presentation rather than surmounting it. The representative repetition of the ideal object remains essentially tributary to a real object such as the written sign.

If Derrida takes up this analysis apropos of a solitary discourse where the speaker contents himself with imagined words, it is above all to show that this appeal to imagination does not suffice to guarantee that we have here the rare case of a pure expression (p. 280). A pure expression is an ideal expression lending itself to infinite repetition without the presence of its *vouloir-dire* suffering. Whether this repetition takes the form of an effective or an imagined discourse is however unimportant with regard to the ideality of the expression: in both cases it is a singular representation (token) of the same ideal expression (type). The argument must therefore be sharpened by insisting that what matters is not so much the difference between representing the same type by a perceived or an imagined token as the *necessity of representing a type by a token at all*. The presence of the ideal form of the sign is thus far from autonomous, since it depends on its representation by tokens, that is, by concrete and variable occurrences. The phenomenon of the sign implies a criss-crossing between presence and representation and will therefore never let itself be reduced to a pure presence (pp. 280–281). Derrida understands this criss-crossing as a supplementary example of the indissoluble interlacing of expression and indication. That Husserl appeals to soliloquy as an example of pure expression is supposed to confirm the demonstration: if, despite everything just said, a purely expressive, univocal and objective language remained possible, it would annihilate itself, since it could be realized only in the form of a monologue or a "voice that keeps silent".

The reader does well, however, to reserve judgment before subscribing too promptly (p. 281). Does Derrida, in his crusade against a philosophy of presence, not gather too quickly under the same banner such different forms of representation as imagination, repetition, the concrete occurrence of a generality, and representation by means of a sign? And is the identification of the search for a pure expression in soliloquy with the search for a perfectly univocal and immutable language not too hasty? These reservations seem justified, but Derrida has not yet said his last word: there remains his principal argument, the deconstruction of the idea of an immediate and fully intuitive self-presence of the subject. For Derrida it is this self-presence that furnishes the ideal of a purely expressive language its ultimate basis and thus explains its propensity to monologue. To speak of indication apropos of monologue makes no sense, says Husserl, since in that case words and their *vouloir-dire* are lived by the subject "in the same instant", rendering useless (*zwecklos*) any representation of the subject by way of indices. If Derrida holds a pure expression to be impossible, it therefore falls to him to refute two arguments: that there exists an instantaneous presence of the present, and that the subject is present to itself without needing to appeal to an indicative representation informing it about itself as if from outside.

The analysis of the first argument leads Derrida to examine Husserl's *Lectures on Internal Time-Consciousness* more closely (pp. 281–282). For Husserl the time of the things of the world must be understood from the time of the consciousness in which those things are apprehended. That consciousness is a temporal flux composed of intentional lived experiences that last a certain time and succeed one another; on closer inspection consciousness doubles again into temporal flux and "internal consciousness" of that flux. One of Husserl's great finds is precisely that the present of a lived experience is not limited to an instantaneous point and that its duration is something other than a simple addition of separate instants: the apprehension of the present now is always completed by the apprehension of its elapsed and its to-come present — the apprehension of the present extends beyond the primal impression of a punctual now to encompass retention and protention. For Husserl this retention must nonetheless be clearly distinguished from a repetition or re-memoration of the past present: in retention the past is apprehended as an integral part of the present (Husserl compares it to a comet's tail), whereas re-memoration makes a past live again in the present without integrating it into the present act of memory. The retained past belongs to the perception of the present, that is, to an act of presentation; re-memoration by contrast is a reproduction, a re-presentation. Far from broaching the possibility of the direct apprehension of a present now, retention extends the field of this immediate grasp of the present beyond the now. Husserl's internal time-consciousness is thus indeed a figure of the presence of consciousness to itself.

Derrida's interpretation destabilizes this fine construction, first by insisting on the link of dependence uniting primal impression to retention, and second by sliding retention over to the side of re-presentation, of the indicative sign, of the trace and of *différance* (pp. 282–283). Far from prolonging the ravishment of an absolute presence of consciousness to itself, retention would obstruct it and thus defer it infinitely. Derrida's proof is that primal impression and retention, while inseparable, do not form a simple unity but a unity synthesizing essentially different data. The present now composes from the origin with the non-now, which in the case of retention is an elapsed now. If the present reduced to the simple and absolute unity of the now, one does not see how that now could become a non-now, or how one would get out of the datum of the instant to accede to the phenomenon of the flux and the flowing of time. This had of course not escaped Husserl, and Derrida shows how Husserl was thus led to abandon the idea of a time whose origin would be found in an absolute now — arriving at the conception of a time which, while preserving a privileged link with the present, would nonetheless have to be understood as originary difference between now and non-now. The irreducible link between primal impression and retention means that the now, from its first emergence, is haunted by the alterity of the no-longer-now. The present thus appears as the result of the originary interlacing of now and non-now. It follows that there exists no simple and instantaneous presence of consciousness to itself, and that the flux presents itself in the form of a difference between what it is now and what it already is no longer (or not yet).

An attentive examination of Husserl's texts on time — even texts Derrida did not take into account or did not know — confirms this interpretation (pp. 283–284). Husserl is several times led by his own phenomenological analyses to question the presuppositions of a metaphysics of presence. Bernet examines three examples: the difficulty of defining the now; the description of the relation between primal impression and retention; and the sketch of a form of self-consciousness which, far from being immediate, is realized only in the *après-coup* of a deferred present. In each case Husserl remains deeply divided between what his analyses make him say and what, in virtue of his metaphysical convictions, he would have wanted to say and maintain. This division, not a Derridean thesis imposed from outside, is what Bernet's independent archival work here establishes.

For Husserl the problem of time concerns principally the perception of the temporal characters of objects of perception (p. 284). These temporal objects belong either to the objective world or to the immanence of lived experiences; either way, as objects of perception they all have a duration centred in the *now*. In the temporal order this now plays a role similar to that of the "here" in the spatial order — which is why Husserl does not manage to rid himself of spatial metaphors in his analysis, a direct consequence of analysing time within the frame of a phenomenology of perception. The same point of departure leads him to conceive retention and protention as constituting a temporal horizon: the perception of the now exceeds itself toward an apprehension of past and future and is thus perception of a duration. Within that duration, past and future nonetheless remain apprehended from the perspective of the present: past is what is *no longer* now, future what is *not yet* now. If for Husserl one does not perceive only the now, everything one perceives nonetheless draws its ontological consistency and its mode of givenness from the now.

The first and most important obstacle this theory strikes concerns precisely its cornerstone, the notion of the now (pp. 284–285). It turns out that this now, said to be the originary source-point (*Urquellpunkt*) of time, is not definable by itself. The text of the *Lectures* is eloquent: the grasp of the now is described as a primal impression, which the text says is the originary temporal form of sensation, the temporal form of the sensation of the now-point and nothing else — but properly speaking this now-point itself can be defined only by originary sensation, so that the proposition just formulated has value only for putting one on the way toward what must be understood. But what must be understood, namely what the now is in itself, cannot be thought without falling into a vicious circle: determining the primal impression as sensation of the now and then determining that now as what gives itself in a primal impression.

The conclusion that imposes itself is that this now said to be at the origin of temporal duration cannot be thought independently of the non-now that follows from it (p. 285). Husserl does not hesitate to assume the consequence, writing in the sequel to the cited text that the punctual now as a whole, the primal impression as a whole, undergoes the modification of the past, and that only with this have we exhausted the concept of the now, insofar as it is *relative* and refers to a "past" as "past" refers to "now". Is this to say that the now depends on the past as much as the past on the now? If so, the now would necessarily lose the privileged status permitting it to present itself as the anchorage point and originary foundation of temporal duration. Rather than revealing the origin of time, the phenomenology of internal time-consciousness would thus stress a relation of *reversibility* between now and non-now. Rather than springing from a unique source, time would be the effect of an irreducible difference, undecidability, spacing. Would Husserl really be Derridean? In any case not wholly safe from the temptation of becoming so.

Husserl also hesitates much as to the right determination of the relation between now and non-now, that is, between primal impression and retention (pp. 285–286). These hesitations testify anew that a precise analysis of temporal phenomena leads him to interrogate seriously the prejudices of a metaphysics of presence. It must be stressed first that retention is a wholly singular phenomenon, difficult to integrate into the great project of a systematic analysis of the types of intentional consciousness. Retention, while intentionally aiming at a past, is not an autonomous intentional lived experience: it is inseparably attached to the perception of the present. A simple moment of that perception, it is nonetheless not a simple perception, since it aims at a datum no longer present. As a moment of the perception of the present, the retention of the past must not be confused either with an act of re-memoration resuscitating the past by traversing it anew. Retention is thus neither an act of presentation (like perception of the present), nor an act of re-presentation (like re-memoration of the past), nor properly speaking an intentional act aiming at an object.

How then is one to understand this unity of the present instant of consciousness in which the grasp of the present and the retention of the past are indissolubly interlaced? (p. 286.) Instead of simply affirming that retention is a supplement of the primal impression, must one not also say that the primal impression of the now is unthinkable without the retention of what is already no longer now? This conclusion seems to impose itself once the now is not definable in itself and presents itself as a limit-point situated at the intersection of two forms of the continuum, that of the not-yet and that of the no-longer. The primal impression of the now, rather than being the trunk on which retention depends as its appendage, would thus be a simple *limit* of retention. Husserl confirms this in an unpublished text of September 1917 stating explicitly that the actuality of the originary hyletic datum and the whole temporal duration belonging to it would not be conscious properly speaking, or conscious only as a limit of the retentional durations preceding them (pp. 286–287). Taking this text seriously implies, first, that the present, far from being an originary and undivided unity, is *difference*, and second, that the present is originally *unconscious* and lends itself to a conscious apprehension only in the *après-coup* of retention. The first conclusion had not escaped Merleau-Ponty, who writes that one must pass from the thing as identity to the thing as difference, that is, as transcendence, as always "behind", beyond, distant — even the present is not absolute coincidence without transcendence, even the *Urerlebnis* involves total non-coincidence; the present too is ungraspable close up. The second conclusion is clearly enunciated in the *Lectures*, even if Husserl refuses to adhere to it: one can raise the question what of the initial phase of a lived experience that constitutes itself — does it too come to be given only on the basis of retention, and would it be "unconscious" if no retention attached to it? Husserl's answer is that if it were conscious only by retention *alone*, it would be impossible to understand what permits characterizing it as "now"; it is a veritable absurdity to speak of an "unconscious" content that would become conscious only belatedly (*nachträglich*). What appears absurd to one who wants to preserve his faith in the founding value of an originary now is nonetheless the unavoidable consequence of a precise analysis of the indissoluble interlacing of the two phenomena. Rather than affirming that the now "is" and the non-now "is not", one must therefore say that the now "is not" without the supplement of the non-now.

One meets the same rending between the demands of a metaphysical faith and the teachings of the phenomena within the analysis of self-consciousness (pp. 287–288). The phenomena speak in favour of a *retentional* self-consciousness, a deferred and non-objectivating grasp of the subject's life; metaphysical faith demands on the contrary the absolute and immediate evidence of the reflective *cogito*. The originary interlacing of primal impression and retention implies that the emergence of a new now entails a modification of the whole retentional horizon: actual retention is thus always a retention of retentions, a form of retrospective resumption of the elapsed flux — each retention is itself submitted to a continuous modification carrying in itself the inheritance of the whole preceding development. Husserl is quoted: the flux of immanent time-constituting consciousness is, but is moreover such that a self-appearance of the flux (*Selbsterscheinung des Flusses*) must necessarily take place in it, so that one must necessarily be able to grasp the flux itself in its flowing; what is brought to appear in the instantaneous actuality of the flux are the past phases of that same flux insofar as they appear through the series of retentional moments contained in that actuality.

Retention thus permits a self-appearance of the flux radically distinguished from a reflective self-consciousness (pp. 288–289). As retentional self-appearance this self-consciousness has nothing instantaneous about it: it is consciousness of the Self in its past duration and not of an actual Self. Nor does it make that Self an *object* of inspection (*inspectio sui*), since retention is neither a perception nor even an objectivating intentional act. In retention, consciousness remains conscious of what it has been, while no longer being it and without reliving it in the form of a re-memoration. It is therefore indeed a self-consciousness accomplished in the *après-coup* of a present forever gone. This retentional self-consciousness is moreover submitted to incessant modification, since it is solidary with the evanescence of the primal impression of the now; as changing consciousness of self it is also consciousness of a changing Self. Retention distinguishes itself from re-memoration precisely in that it never issues in an objective identity of the Self: it assures the subject of its ipseity while refusing it a grasp of its sameness and permanence. The subject apprehends itself there belatedly as the one who has not ceased changing and who for that very reason cannot become the object of a contemplation of itself. In retention there appears a past Self which as such has never been present and can never become so. *The ipseity of the subject is the trace of a past that has never been present.*

As one might expect, Husserl recoils before this teaching of a phenomenon that nonetheless no one before him had brought to light with such clarity (p. 289). Caught by the double and irreconcilable demand of a fidelity to the phenomena and a fidelity to his metaphysical convictions, he extricates himself by a compromise which — like all compromises — has the value of a symptom. He proposes to consider the retentional self-appearance of consciousness as a mere sketch of a genuine self-consciousness which would be accomplished in the form of a reflective intuition of oneself: since I retain the elapsed phases, I can direct my gaze on them in a new act we call a reflection (immanent perception) or a re-memoration; with respect to retention, these acts accomplish a fulfilment (*Erfüllung*). Retentional self-appearance would thus be a mere anticipation of a self-consciousness fully realized only as reflective perception. It goes without saying that maintaining this demand for an introspection in which the subject would apprehend itself fully and without detour risks issuing in an illusion or rather a metaphysical construction of ipseity (pp. 289–290). The subject that contemplates itself as its own *vis-à-vis* attains its ends only by repressing the intimate knowledge it has of the incessant changes its past life has imposed on it. The reflective *cogito*, far from casting light on the implicit and retentional familiarity the subject maintains with its past life, casts it on the contrary into shadow. One cannot opt for a self-consciousness effected in the present without choosing against retention or without betraying the phenomenon of an originary interlacing of primal impression and retention.

As was to be expected, Derrida takes a further step and understands this interlacing as a supplementary figure of the interlacing of expression and indication (p. 290). Expression being necessarily on the side of the primal impression, that is, of the presence of consciousness to itself "in the same instant", retention is perforce supposed to belong to indication. Retention is thus brought close to the differential repetition that constitutes the presence of the ideal object while losing it; it becomes an example of that indicative representation which Derrida claims parasitizes every expressive presentation of thought. The link between primal impression and retention is cited as proof of the necessity of a recourse to the alterity of indicative representation within the very presentation the subject gives itself of itself. The logic of this demonstration leads to pushing retention over to the side of re-presentations such as repetition or reproduction. Where Husserl maintains an essential distinction between retention, which he considers a form or moment of presentation, and re-memoration, which belongs to re-presentation, Derrida makes them two figures of a representation whose common root would be the trace or the movement of *différance* inhabiting the pure actuality of the now.

It is for the reader to judge how far he wants to follow Derrida in the liberties he takes with Husserl's text (pp. 290–291). No reader who has followed the demonstration this far can, however, still lay claim to a full comprehension of Husserl's thought in order to justify his refusal to fall in step with Derrida's interpretation. If Derrida has made us understand one thing, it is that there exists no purely expressive language and therefore no means either of assuring ourselves of the coincidence of an interpretation with the author's *vouloir-dire*. It is not even certain that the author can faithfully reproduce his own thought or that he is in full possession of his thought while thinking it. It is with this last affirmation that Derrida's argumentation reaches its apogee: what he wants above all to show is that the way the subject grasps its thought or hears its own voice is unthinkable without the detour through the alterity of the indicative sign — the subject comports itself toward itself as if it were another. This is the chapter's title-question answered: one cannot listen to the master's voice faithfully, and the demonstration of that is itself the master's teaching.

#### III

In this final stage the self-presence of the subject is analysed in the form of that privileged phenomenon Derrida calls *the voice* (p. 291). The privilege accorded to voice as prototype of all expression goes back to Aristotle, who already opposed the signifying voice (*phōnē sēmantikē*) expressing the *logos* to the meaningless noises (*psophoi*) animals make. In Aristotle already the voice makes itself accomplice of a logocentric understanding of human language. Derrida specifies this privilege by opposing the voice no longer to animal noises but to other forms of the expression of *logos*, above all writing. Whereas the living voice accompanies thought as its shadow, writing comes on stage when this vital link between sound and sense has already been broken, when sense is already on the slope of effacement and slippage toward forgetting: in writing the *logos* no longer lives, it survives in the forms of a mnesic trace, a testament, a funerary inscription. The voice permits the subject to say and hear its own signifying intention without ambiguity, whereas writing distances thought from its author. If writing thus breaks definitively with the self-presence of the thinking subject, the voice on the contrary accomplishes and celebrates the life of reflective consciousness. To hear oneself speak is to hear the pulse of one's own thought beat, and this is worth infinitely more than touching or seeing oneself: in touch and sight the resistance and opacity of the body still dissimulate the interior life of the subject, whereas speech effaces itself before what it makes heard (pp. 291–292). In "the voice" the body of the expressive sign makes itself spirit and spirit grasps itself in a pure auto-affection. The voice that hears itself is thus indeed the linguistic equivalent or even the foundation of reflective consciousness. It is the sign as instituted by the phenomenological reduction — the pure phenomenon in which the transcendental subject appears to itself and appears to itself as constituting, that is, as giving sense to the world. "The voice is the phenomenon" of transcendental subjectivity.

When Derrida deconstructs the possibility of this voice as the phenomenon of a pure auto-affection, it is therefore also a certain notion of the phenomenon and consequently of phenomenology that is put in question (p. 292). The idea of a pure auto-affection realized in the voice presupposes that the subject hearing its own voice grasps "in the same instant" the life of the thought expressed by that voice. If however that present "instant" is impure because produced by the gap between now and non-now, between same and other, then the purity of the auto-affection by means of the voice is equally compromised. The subject that hears its own voice hears itself, but hears itself at least doubly, since its living thought is already doubled by its retentional echo propagating and repeating itself infinitely. This retentional doubling cannot be pure because, for Derrida at least, every retention exceeds the limits of a simple presentation — so this first argument holds only insofar as one is prepared also to consider retention an indicative representation caught in the web of the empirical space of the world.

A second form of impurity befalls the voice from the fact that, in order to be heard, that is, understood, it is forced to make use of a linguistic code (pp. 292–293). In submitting to a trans-individual code the voice loses mastery of itself and recognizes itself only at the end of a detour through anonymity. Derrida establishes this by appeal to the personal pronoun "I" a speaker uses to designate himself: even in the privileged case of monologue where the subject so designates itself for itself, the expression "I" introduces a separation within self-presence, since the sense of the "I" of the act of enunciation and the "I" of the enounced never coincide.

Finally, the third argument: a voice that remained purely expressive, and that thus attempted to stay faithful to the idea of a pure auto-affection, would be a voice that kept silent (p. 293). A voice that hears itself must make itself heard in the world — even if it remains thinkable that it makes itself heard there as "not being of this world".

It is doubtless not by chance that a meditation on the pure auto-affection of transcendental consciousness in the voice awakens associations of a theological order (p. 293). But these must not make us believe that the voice could effectively keep silent without the existence of thought suffering. There is no pure auto-affection in the voice, but there is no *autos* either that would precede an *impure* auto-affection. The transcendental subject needs to represent itself in order to be, and the consciousness it has of itself can come to it only from a representation that separates it from itself and that it undergoes in the form of an alienating affection. If Derrida is right to say that this auto-affection is necessarily impure and that impure auto-affection is necessary, that sounds the knell for many received ideas. The first victim of such a conception, making self-consciousness tributary to the alterity and exteriority of the indicative sign, is of course the idea of a purely expressive language — which implies that there is no immediate, fully intuitive and purely interior self-consciousness either, nor any immutable presence of an ideal object remaining identically the same through its multiple repetitions and representations.

If it is true that *La voix et le phénomène* is something other and much more than a book on Husserl, it can nonetheless usefully contribute to rethinking the fundamental stakes of Husserlian phenomenology (pp. 293–294). Among these must be mentioned first what must indeed be called "the system" or "the order" of presence, organized around the privileged phenomena of self-consciousness and the iterability of ideal objects and asserting itself as an order by distinguishing itself — in the form of exclusion — from the empirical world. Such a metaphysics has certainly left deep traces, above all in Husserl's early writings: one need only think of the presentation of the reduction in *Ideen I* issuing in the hypothesis of an annihilation of the world. But it is clear too that Husserl's analysis of, say, the perception of a spatial object by a bodily subject does not easily integrate into such a system of pure presence. Even if Derrida is well inspired to consider the opposition of presentation and re-presentation the basis of the whole Husserlian analysis of intentionality and constitution, his conclusions seem here too too hasty: there exist not only presentations not assimilable to the system of the metaphysics of presence, but Husserl also describes cases of re-presentation such as imagination which redouble or repeat no prior presentation. And if it is doubtless true that the indicative representations put into play by ordinary language threaten the sovereignty of an intentional thought that would use only purely intuitive presentations, that does not justify making the indicative sign the root of every form of representation.

More generally, both the novelty and the weaknesses of Derrida's reading follow from the attention accorded to the phenomena of *language* (pp. 294–295). One can hardly maintain that the ultimate sense of the reduction reveals itself already in the opposition of expression and indication, but Derrida's interpretation has the very great merit of showing that the sense of that reduction is inseparable from a certain idea of language. The difficulty, for instance, of establishing a language that would be the proper idiom of transcendental phenomenology is the symptomatic expression of that phenomenology's rootedness in the empirical world. The reduction is therefore not the deportation of transcendental consciousness into a separate world; the phenomenon is never a pure voice; and the history of transcendental consciousness is written, as Merleau-Ponty says, in "the prose of the world". One may nonetheless ask whether this exclusive centring of the whole interpretation on language is not also the sign of a new *linguocentrism* to which Husserl had opposed himself with great firmness. To say that Husserl's conception of the ideal language of logic is logocentric is possible but trivial; it is difficult, by contrast, to say as much of his project of a genealogy of logic, which pays quite particular attention to pre-predicative experience. That experience is moreover not limited to pre-logical phenomena such as passive synthesis; it also interrogates *non*-logical phenomena such as the essential facticity of the course of history, ethical responsibility for the state of the world, the project of a rational founding of social institutions, the revelation of a God who is the guarantor of the teleology of reason. A fine array of phenomena whose insistent appeal Husserl would not have heard had he listened only to his own "voice". This is Bernet's final and most substantive objection, and it turns the chapter's title back on Derrida.

But it is true that *La voix et le phénomène* is more than a simple interpretation of Husserl and can also be read as an "Introduction to the problems of the sign in Derrida's phenomenology" (p. 295). The sign understood no longer as the supplement of an origin but as *originary supplement* is the phenomenon guiding Derrida's pen well beyond his first writings. "Writing" is the name of this new signification of a sign that is neither real nor ideal and no longer expresses any transcendental signified. It thereupon becomes difficult to operate an essential distinction between proper and figurative sense, and the double gesture of the deconstruction of the metaphorical language of metaphysics risks prolonging itself infinitely. It is indeed to the infinite that this philosophy of originary representation refers, following the trace of presence by wandering, as the text of Husserl cited by Derrida at his book's opening has it, through the rooms and stopping before a painting by Teniers representing a gallery of paintings (pp. 295–296). Referred indefinitely from one representation to another, the subject is caught in the web of *différance*. It thus undergoes the test of its finitude and its facticity. Its life constantly confronts it with the death of presence; the subject survives only through fascinations each of which nonetheless gives its lack of being to be read. To apprehend the truth of its being, the subject must stage itself, and on that stage its voice signs at the same time its death warrant: like Poe's M. Valdemar, also cited at the opening of *La voix et le phénomène*, it will say what it could already no longer hear — yes, no, I have slept, and now, now, I am dead.

## Conclusion — An Intentional Life Without Subject or Object?

For a long time the theory of the intentionality of the subject's life was a rallying sign for phenomenologists otherwise pursuing very diverse interests and opposing one another in their conception of phenomenological method (pp. 297–298). We know, however, that this theory preceded the invention of phenomenology, and also that phenomenologists mix less and less in current discussions bearing on the concept. Bernet wants to show that despite the multiplicity of directions in which research on intentionality has developed both inside and outside the phenomenological movement, Husserl's work between the *Logical Investigations* (1900/01) and *Ideen I* (1913) has remained present at least in the form of a virtual focus. The development of the analysis of intentionality in Heidegger, Gurwitsch, Sartre, Merleau-Ponty and Henry is at bottom nothing but the putting in question of the representationalist objectivism and the egological subjectivism progressively set in place by Husserl at the beginning of the century. What presents itself as a fundamental critique of Husserl therefore often meets a position Husserl had himself defended and subsequently abandoned. The doctrines concerning the non-egological nature of transcendental consciousness, the insurmountable difference between the intentionality of representations and that of feelings, and the independence of affective sensations with respect to intentional consciousness all have their antecedents in his work. In moving away from these primitive positions — often directly inspired by Brentano — and in accentuating subjectivism and objectivism in his conception of intentionality, Husserl did not for all that turn away from every form of intentionality without object or reflected subject, nor even from a form of the subject's life this side of the intentionality of lived experiences. In introducing a retentional and kinaesthetic self-consciousness and in developing his notion of a world whose pre-givenness precedes the givenness of every intentional object, Husserl already stands on the same terrain as his successors and critics. This is the Conclusion's governing thesis, and it recasts the whole book: the critiques the preceding chapters expounded are largely quarrels within Husserl's own development.

The brief analysis of the changing limits of Husserl's theory in the hinge period 1900–1913 aims to give Husserl the floor so as to renew the dialogue with his successors or predecessors (p. 298). This dispenses Bernet from returning in detail to those points of the Husserlian conception that underwent no important evolution after the *Logical Investigations* — the distinction between matter and quality of the act, the apperceptive apprehension of sensations in perception, the intuitive fulfilment of a partially or wholly empty intention — and equally from the prolongation of this conception into a theory of signification, truth and transcendental constitution.

Going back to the sources of Husserl's theory from its *Wirkungsgeschichte*, the inquiry will confront his successors and critics twice, since the reasons for both the subjectivism and the objectivism of his conception are at issue (pp. 298–299). It will concern itself above all with the introduction of an egological conception of transcendental consciousness and with the progressive effacement of the difference between objectivating representations and feelings. It will turn out that this new subjectivism, far from confirming a latent solipsism, is on the contrary consequent upon a coming to consciousness of the stakes of intersubjectivity; and that the new objectivism seizing the analysis of feelings is due to ethical preoccupations of which Husserl's critics have scarcely taken account. It can then be shown that this imperialism of objectivating reason never reigned unchallenged over the whole of his work: Husserl on the contrary accomplished decisive breakthroughs in envisaging a form of self-consciousness preceding all objectivation of ipseity, and in describing a form of intentional interlacing between subject and object this side of their separation and opposition. This originary intentionality is better known under other names — operative intentionality (Fink), transcendence of being-in-the-world (Heidegger), perceptual faith (Merleau-Ponty). In elaborating this theory of a non-objectivating intentionality and in continuing to interrogate, in the manner of Brentano and Freud, the destiny of the alliance between intentional representations and pre-intentional affects, Husserl will nonetheless never go so far as to say, with Henry, that the essence of subjectivity is an affectivity devoid of all originary link with any form of intentionality. If it is true that the life of the transcendental subject does not reduce to objectivating intentionality, nor even to a pre-intentional attachment to the world, it is no less true that for Husserl the subject that experiences its Self never quite escapes the frame of intentionality.

### The Reasons for the Progressive Reinforcement of Subjectivism and Objectivism in Husserl's Work

Rereading Husserl from the phenomenologists who affirmed their own thought by differentiating themselves from him holds many surprises (pp. 299–300). Their critiques, however justified they may appear, also give to be read aspects of Husserl's thought they ignore. Very often they have merely decided where Husserl left us with an open questioning and with phenomena whose deciphering revealed their undecidable character. The most frequent reproaches concern either his egological, intimist and idealist subjectivism or his intellectualist objectivism — without much concern for the link between the two reproaches or for the internal coherence of the positions Husserl adopted. What could have pushed Husserl to abandon the non-egological conception of intentional consciousness to which Sartre wanted to lead transcendental phenomenology back? And what was the motive for the progressive alignment of feelings on the model of external perception that Henry never stops denouncing? Do these critiques — rooted either in a conception of consciousness as pure intentionality without content of its own, or in a conception of a consciousness withdrawing from all intentionality to feel the beat of its own life's pulse — not both belong to the same fascination, characteristic of French phenomenology as a whole, for a form of subjectivity situated this side of or beneath the Cartesian subject?

#### Genesis of Egological Subjectivism

In defending his thesis of *The Transcendence of the Ego* and criticizing the egological conception of *Ideen I*, Sartre in fact leads us back to the position of the *Logical Investigations*, which in turn only took up Brentano's analysis in the *Psychology from an Empirical Standpoint* (pp. 300–301). Sartre, the early Husserl and Brentano thus share the opinion that the unity of consciousness — whether the unity of different simultaneous experiences or the unity of the stream — constitutes itself all by itself, without appeal to an ego functioning as a principle of unification overhanging the life of consciousness. This spontaneous unity would be of associative nature, belonging to that logic of a whole composed of non-independent parts which Brentano, Meinong and Husserl explored so well in the context of temporally extended objects such as the melody. Even if the *Investigations* call this unity the "phenomenological I", it has in fact nothing egological about it, being nothing but the simple bundle (*Bündel*) of the lived experiences of an empirical I, itself composed of that consciousness and an empirical body. This empirical I is thus indeed a transcendent object, like any physical thing, a house or a tree. It follows that the phenomenological I, being only the spiritual part of the empirical I, has nothing in common with the pure I of Kant and Natorp, the subjective centre linking all the contents belonging to my consciousness. Husserl says he has no use for such a pure I and confesses that he simply cannot manage to dig it up. It must be specified at once that if the belonging of a lived experience to the fluctuating unity of consciousness, and that unity itself, are indeed true phenomena, these nonetheless appear only in favour of a reflective act. When from 1907 Husserl introduces the reduction and thereby specifies the status of that reflection, his aversion to an egological conception will at first only deepen (pp. 301–302): since the reduction consists in purifying consciousness of every empirical apperception and therefore of every link with the empirical I, he concludes very logically that pure consciousness belongs to no one. Sartre says nothing else in considering consciousness a transcendental field, an individuated and impersonal spontaneity.

But if Husserl at a given moment rejoins the Sartrean conception of the transcendence of the ego and the anonymity of transcendental consciousness, he does so for different reasons (p. 302). If he is unable to dig up the pure I, it is quite simply because he has no need of it to understand the unity of *perceptual* consciousness; the pure I will end by appearing to him as soon as he turns to other forms of intentional consciousness, such as empathy. For Sartre, by contrast, the pure I is not only useless but harmful: it assures the subject an illusory retreat outside the world, whereas the transcendence of the ego shows that the human person and the world are cut from the same cloth. Perhaps more serious still, introducing the pure I also falsifies the conception of transcendental consciousness itself by introducing an opacity that deteriorates its interiority. It thus serves as a reassuring refuge for an anonymous transcendental subject whose interior life is in truth without content — a nothing — since it consists in projecting itself entirely into the world of intentional objects. Contrary to Husserl, Sartre therefore understands transcendental life from the start with the impersonal subject of history in mind, of which human individuals are only secondary reflections or effects. It is paradoxical but incontestable that it is precisely this Marxizing interpretation of Husserl by Sartre that served as model for Lacan in developing his conception of the subject of the unconscious. A footnote notes the contrast between Sartre and Heidegger: for Sartre it is the anonymity of transcendental life that is the source of anxiety and the egological apperception that reassures; for Heidegger, on the contrary, the anonymity of inauthentic existence within the "they" is reassuring and the confrontation with the solitude of authentic existence is what is anxiety-inducing.

When in 1910/11, in the course *Fundamental Problems of Phenomenology*, Husserl introduces the pure I into his description of pure consciousness, it is not, as Sartre's argument might suggest, in the name of an attachment to a *solipsistic* idealism (p. 303). It is on the contrary the analysis of *intersubjectivity*, of the diversity of streams of consciousness, that made an egological conception of the unity of consciousness necessary. The notion of the pure I is thus from the start declined into a plurality of pure consciousnesses each possessing its own individual and unique pure I. It is moreover significant that this discussion of empathy is inserted in the wider frame of an analysis of intentional acts of re-presentation, among which figure re-memoration and imagination. Far from being the symptom of a metaphysics of presence celebrating self-presence, the pure I is on the contrary introduced in 1910/11 on the basis of acts of re-presentation implying a division of consciousness (imagination), a temporal distancing of consciousness with respect to itself (re-memoration), or the confrontation of consciousness with another consciousness (empathy). The pure I is required as principle of unification of consciousness *because* consciousness divides itself, absents itself from itself, and projects itself into another consciousness. Husserl is thus very close to Freud, for whom the I has precisely the task of gluing back together the pieces of a subject divided by an experience that entailed its splitting (*Spaltung*).

In the 1910/11 course the unifying role of the pure I in empathy is analysed on the analogy of re-memoration (p. 304). Yet in re-memoration the pure I confirms the unity between my past and my present, whereas in empathy it marks the separation between my consciousness and another's. But can one not also say that in both cases the unity promoted by the pure I is an individual identity, and that this reveals itself only in the face of the other — even where that other is the subject I myself have been in the past? My past does not appear to me in the same way as another's consciousness: the past is given to me originarily (though not in the flesh), the other indirectly and by way of a flesh announcing his presence in the form of an expressive sign. But it is no less true that the unifying role of a pure I remains required even in re-memoration, and precisely because my past has become foreign to me, because the unity of my stream is compromised and can no longer be assured by a simple spontaneous association between contiguous lived experiences. What belongs to me properly — the sphere of ownness or primordial sphere — appears as such only in contrast with the appearing of the other, and this own appears in re-memoration as so holed through by absence and strangeness that the pure I must come to assure me that it is indeed still I who am in question. One is therefore tempted to say that it is re-memoration that *produces* the individual identity of the pure I, and not that self-recognition in re-memoration is made possible by a pre-existing pure I. Husserl would thus rejoin, at least momentarily, the Sartrean conception of the transcendence of the ego by making the pure I a constituted unity rather than the source of all constitution — while distancing himself from Sartre by very explicitly assuming the opacity within a transcendental consciousness marked by division, gap and distancing, in short by the non-coincidence between its *esse* and its *percipi* (pp. 304–305).

*Ideen I* accomplishes a supplementary step in renouncing the affirmation of a privileged link between the pure I and acts of re-presentation, and in attributing an egological structure to all intentional lived experiences, whether acts of re-presentation, of simple presentation, or even axiological and practical lived experiences (p. 305). At the same stroke the pure I, far from being the result of an experience, becomes its ultimate source: every intentional act accomplished in the form of a *cogito* — every act that turns explicitly and attentively toward an intentional object — proceeds from a pure I. And even the other acts, the background intuitions that remain inarticulate, inactual and inattentive, are still egological acts in potency. The pure I is thus no longer a simple bundle of lived experiences; it is the common ground of their life, the radiating centre (*Ausstrahlungszentrum*) of all intentions whose nature is to take a position (*Stellungnahme*) with respect to the world. Rorty's favoured pun bringing together the "I" and the "eye" applies perfectly to the position of *Ideen I*, where the I is presented as the source of the intentional gaze, the centre from which the "look-at" objects radiates.

It is true that Husserl will subsequently attach less importance to this visual metaphor and will considerably widen the field of application of the pure I by including all passive life and notably drive-intentionality (pp. 305–306). The I thus becomes a subject also affected by impressions, moved by appetencies and interests, transported by drives. Does this mean Husserl ends by approaching Henry and his conception of a consciousness whose being consists originarily in nothing but being affected by the movement of its own life? Not at all. If Husserl progressively concedes that the activity undertaken by the subject of intentionality is most often preceded by the passivity of an undergone experience, he will never abandon the idea that the true life of the subject consists in reacting in full lucidity to affective solicitations, in examining them with a critical gaze to decide whether or not to follow them up. Transcendental life, whether theoretical, axiological or practical, always consists for Husserl in taking a position; its intentionality is always, in the idiom of *Ideen I*, doxic in essence. The intentionality of drives therefore demands a resumption or taking in hand by an intentionality of which the subject possesses full mastery: the *subjectum* submitted to multiple impressions and associations (*Einstrahlungszentrum*) must transform itself into an active source of multiple intentional position-takings (*Ausstrahlungszentrum*). The undergone attraction or the heard call must thus change into attentive examination of the object, and every object must then be scrutinized in the teleological horizon of a perfect object — even where that object is a certain idea of the perfection of the subject itself.

Husserl's conception of the pure I thus seems to have evolved much between the 1910/11 course and *Ideen I* (p. 306). In the latter the pure I is no longer simply the principle of unification and individuation of a re-presentative intentional consciousness threatened with bursting or with confusion with another consciousness; it is on the contrary the absolute source from which every intentional act proceeds, even a simple perceptual presentation. This does not mean, however, that the pure I would precede all those intentional lived experiences: Husserl specifies on the contrary that it is only a form or simple identity-pole of transcendental consciousness, and that it is nothing — completely empty — without them. Inseparable from its lived experiences, it is nonetheless distinguished from them and never coincides either with any one in particular or with their totality; it remains the same in the incessant change of consciousness. It is therefore properly speaking "a transcendence within immanence" — atemporal and yet nothing without the temporality of lived experiences, source of the gaze and yet nothing without what gives itself to be seen for it (pp. 306–307). It is thus still and always the diversity of intentional gazes, the disparity of the living subject's interests, its submission to the hard law of a now that ceaselessly renews itself and imposes itself by chasing out the preceding one: all these points of rupture within the subject require the constant, unifying and pacifying presence of a pure I. *Ideen I* has therefore moved less far from the 1910/11 course than it first appeared, and nothing forbids maintaining the understanding of the pure I as a moment at once immanent and transcendent to transcendental consciousness, as a datum at once constituting and constituted, appearing under the double form of a subject and an object of intentional life. The book's governing formula thus reappears at its most literal: the pure I is itself a transcendence within immanence.

But the capital question of the phenomenological appearing of this pure I has become a little more precarious still (p. 307). For it is no longer only a matter of describing how the I appears when consciousness is confronted with an other consciousness (re-memoration) or with another consciousness (empathy), but how the *source* of all intentional consciousness appears. The text of *Ideen I* betrays Husserl's embarrassment: the pure I is said to be in and for itself indescribable — and the text continues: this does not however prevent it from lending itself to a multiplicity of important descriptions. As unique and universal source of every intentional act, the pure I is implicated as soon as an object appears, but what appears is that object and not the egological origin of its appearing. So long as one maintains, as *Ideen I* does most of the time, that everything that appears appears in the form of an intentional object, it follows that the pure I can appear only belatedly and in the form of an object of reflection. Which is to say that the pure I as source of intentional life can never appear in its effectivity. It is thereafter unsurprising that Sartre and Henry — as Heidegger before them — wanted to remedy this situation: the first by making the appearing I a transcendent object produced *ex nihilo* by reflection, the second by affirming that the life of the subject can never appear in the form of the transcendence of an intentional object.

#### Genesis of Objectivism and the Analysis of the Intentionality of Feelings

It is again in Henry that one finds the severest, but doubtless also the most attentive, critique of the parti pris for objectivism as it manifests itself notably in *Ideen I*'s analysis of the intentionality of feelings (p. 308). While recognizing that feelings, emotions, tendencies, drives and other lived experiences of the sphere of affectivity find their origin in pre-intentional sensations or hyletic contents, Husserl's text hastens to apply to this matter the form of intentional consciousness and thus to align the analysis of affectivity on that of acts of representation of an object. One will be astonished, however, that Henry did not push his curiosity so far as to examine also the analyses of the *Logical Investigations* which, while recognizing that intentional feelings do exist, also concern themselves very explicitly with the autonomous existence of non-intentional affects. This comparison is the more fruitful in that it will also permit a better understanding of the reasons that led Husserl to place affectivity under the jurisdiction of objectivating or doxic reason alone.

In considering feelings doxic acts, *Ideen I* makes them intentional acts relating to the existence — certain, doubtful and so on — of a corresponding axiological object, namely a value (pp. 308–309). It follows that in the case of feelings, exactly as for intentional acts of representation, one can observe a perfect correlation between the lived experience and its intentional object, between noesis and noema; the phenomenologist therefore has the choice between intentional consciousness and the value-object in beginning his examination of the being and appearing of affectivity. The structure of the noesis and noema of emotional lived experiences is nonetheless not quite identical to that of acts of representation, since acts of feeling are animated by a double intention referring to a double object: the thing invested with value, and the value itself. Axiological lived experiences are thus complex intentional lived experiences, but this complexity takes nothing from their objectivating character. This structural identity also permits affirming that both sorts of act are susceptible of the same doxic modalizations and that the form of their linguistic expression is rigorously the same. Husserl concludes that theoretical, axiological and practical acts are animated by one and the same doxic reason, and that one can therefore envisage a formal axiology and a formal practics functioning on the model of formal logic. Affectivity thus reduces to a form of objectivating intentionality whose objects are endowed with a supplement of soul.

This triumphal march of objectivism, or more precisely of the monism of objectivating reason, remains nonetheless marked by some signs of hesitation (p. 309). It is true on one hand that Husserl claims nothing occurring in consciousness escapes the hold of an intentionality resembling a universal milieu that finally bears in itself all lived experiences, even those not characterized as intentional; non-intentional sensations, whether representative or affective, thus seem simple incitations to the accomplishment of intentional acts. On the other hand, within the same paragraph and apropos of sensual lived experiences — colour data, touch data, sound data, sensations of pleasure, pain, tickling, as well as the sensual moments of the sphere of the drives — Husserl evokes the possibility of matters without form and poses very explicitly the question whether these sensual lived experiences are everywhere and necessarily bearers of some animating apprehension. Even if he contents himself with answering that this is not the place to decide, one believes one hears in this text a distant echo of the passage of the Fifth *Investigation* where it was said that sensations of pleasure and pain can persist while the act-characters built on them have disappeared: instead of figuring as representative of an agreeable quality of the object, the excitation of pleasure is henceforth simply related to the subject that feels it (pp. 309–310).

Of course this "material phenomenology" of affective impressions is not the last word of the *Logical Investigations* on the nature of feelings (p. 310). Even if that text does make a place for the possible autonomous existence of affective sensations revealing nothing but the state of a consciousness in some way affected by itself, it is nonetheless the analysis of intentional feelings that predominates. But it remains true that the *Investigations* show themselves very sensitive to the subjective-relative character of these intentional feelings and consequently refuse to attribute to them intentional objects *sui generis* as *Ideen I* will do. They thus stay very close to Brentano's position, on which feelings belong among those psychic phenomena called reactive in which a subject takes an affective position with respect to the intentional objects of representations. Brentano even advanced the hypothesis that all representations were accompanied by such an affective colouring — and one finds the same insistence on an originary alliance between object-representations and affects of pleasure and displeasure belonging to the subject's drive-state in his disciple Freud.

For the *Investigations*, feelings distinguish themselves from other intentional acts by referring at the same time and explicitly not only to the presence of an object but to a *state of the subject* (pp. 310–311). According as one insists on one or the other of these two components, the feeling appears either as a complex objectivating act or as an encounter of the subject with itself. In the first case Husserl will say that affective sensations unite with representative sensations and that both enter together into the composition of an intentional apprehension of an object — resulting either in the perception of an object surrounded by an affective aura, or in a new intentional act of feeling that uses an objectivating representation to take an affective position with respect to its object. In the second case one will put forward that feelings affirm nothing about that intentional object, and one will conclude, as in chapter 9 of the Sixth *Investigation*, that acts of feeling distinguish themselves radically from other intentional acts by the form of their verbal expression and by the truth-value they can — or more exactly cannot — claim: whereas perceptual judgments are the expression of a signification relating directly to the perceived state of affairs, feelings are subjective attitudes comparable to doubt or questioning which can be expressed only by way of a reflective judgment relating to those attitudes. The perceptual judgment therefore bears directly on the object of that perception and its truth-value is measured by the fidelity of the correspondence between verbal affirmation and objective reality. The judgment of feeling is on the contrary a judgment *about* the feeling; its object is constituted by the feeling as it appears in the act of reflection, and it is that act of reflection that is brought to linguistic expression. Rather than of truth, one must therefore speak here of *veracity* (*Wahrhaftigkeit*). One cannot ask of a judgment about a feeling that it correspond to any objective reality; even in the best case it is nothing but a sincere and faithful expression of the state of the subject.

The position of the *Investigations* is thus characterized by a great ambivalence as to the originally subjective or objective character of feelings and as to the advantages and disadvantages of their assimilation to objectivating acts (p. 312). On one hand Husserl seems to recoil before the idea of attributing intentional objects *sui generis* such as values to axiological lived experiences; on the other he does not want to renounce the idea that there exists an intentionality of affective type manifesting itself in the form of an emotional attachment (*Zuwendung*) to the intentional object. Again Sartre and Henry hastened to decide where Husserl at first could not: Henry by affirming that the intensely subjective character of feelings excludes from the outset their being considered intentional lived experiences; Sartre by contesting that the intentionality of emotions differs sensibly from that of perceptions and justifies attributing to them an egological or egocentric form lacking in other intentional lived experiences. Husserl, the first moment of indecision past, resolutely opted from *Ideen I* on for a rather objectivist analysis of the intentionality of feelings while affirming their egological character. But we are still far from understanding the motives of that choice.

It seems the decision in favour of the objectivating character of feelings was again taken in the years between the *Investigations* and *Ideen I*, and that it was motivated by the concern for a phenomenological founding of *ethics* (p. 312). It is above all in the ethical texts of this intermediate period that Husserl insists that ethical feelings and the ethical values to which they intentionally refer must be submitted to a critical examination scrutinizing their rational character. It thus seems that this demand for a rational justification of ethical feelings brought Husserl to consider all feelings objectivating intentional acts.

Husserl's idea of rationality implies the conviction that there could not be several sorts of reason each having its own ambitions and demands (p. 313). For him there can be only one reason and one form of rational validation: reason manifests itself within a subjective position-taking, theoretical, axiological or practical, and the rational validation of that position always consists in showing the coincidence between the subject's position-taking and the intuitive givenness of the intentional object posited by the subject. Husserl's phenomenology of reason therefore concerns itself with the validity of that subjective claim to reason — that position or *doxa* — contained in every intentional lived experience. The justification of the rational character of a doxic position operates in what Husserl calls a synthesis of fulfilment, and it is this that gives concepts such as evidence and truth their phenomenological sense.
