This synthesis draws all its validating force from the intuitive givenness of the intentional object, understood as an intentional lived experience of intuitive type whose particularity consists in referring to the object while leaning on hyletic data — these being apprehended in such a way that they become intuitive adumbrations or appearances of the intentional object. As regards the synthesis of fulfilment, it is thereafter clear that it has the form of a synthesis of identification between two objectivating acts (and between their noematic correlates) whose content in intuitive components differs. What legitimates a subjective position-taking by fulfilling it intuitively therefore always has the form of an intuitive apperception of an intentional object.

It is not hard to foresee what such a conception implies for the phenomenological analysis of the validation of a position-taking of *ethical* type (pp. 313–314). If the justification of the rational character of an ethical position is made by means of a synthesis of fulfilment between two objectivating acts, that implies that ethical feelings be understood as objectivating intentional acts of a particular type referring to intentional objects *sui generis*; and that these ethical objects — just like the intentional objects of acts of representation — can be apprehended in intuitive apperceptions capable of serving as intuitive fulfilment for ethical position-takings. That this intuitive apperception of values is to be conceived in close analogy with the perceptions (*Wahrnehmung*) of objects of representation emerges also from Husserl's terminology: he calls it a *Wertnehmung*. One thus better understands the doctrine of *Ideen I*, which is nothing but a generalization of this analysis of ethical feelings: it is now *all* feelings that are considered objectivating or doxic (complex) acts, so that feelings fall under the same form of verbal expression and rational justification as theoretical doxic acts.

It is certain that the treatment reserved for the intentionality of feelings would have been different had Husserl been less preoccupied with their intuitive fulfilment (pp. 314–315). It is precisely because he had not yet worried about this in the *Logical Investigations* that that text remained more faithful to the phenomenon of non-intentional affects, revealing a state of the subject rather than a state of affairs. This greater fidelity to the phenomena extends moreover to the analysis of intentional affects, which, while being intentional acts, are not yet assimilated there without reserve to the model of objectivating acts. One can dream of what his analysis of feelings would have been had he been more preoccupied with *aesthetic* than with ethical feelings, and had he rubbed up against the analysis of those totally atypical lived experiences Kant called the experience of the sublime.

### Overcoming Subjectivism and Objectivism in Husserl

If it is true that objectivism progressively seized Husserl's analysis of the intentionality of feelings, it is not so for a series of other investigations where on the contrary the demand to do justice to a form of non-objectivating intentionality — an "intentionality without object" — asserts itself (p. 315). This non-objectivating intentionality is not, however, incompatible with an objectivating one; on the contrary, the two never exist quite without one another. Non-objectivating intentionality is secretly mixed both into the appearing of intentional objects for the subject and into the appearing of that subject to itself as an object of reflection. Thus the appearing of objects is preceded and made possible by the pre-givenness of a world that no longer appears in the manner of an object, but whose appearing nonetheless presupposes a particular form of intentionality Fink and Merleau-Ponty called operative (*fungierend*) and Heidegger baptized the transcendence of Dasein's being-in-the-world. Likewise the appearing of the subject's life as an object of reflection is preceded and made possible by an appearing of that life to itself in the form of a carnal and temporal auto-affection. On this question of the appearing of the subject's life for the subject it is above all Henry who took over the analyses Husserl had already devoted to the non-objectivating intentionality of kinaesthetic and retentional self-consciousness (pp. 315–316). In doing so he believed he had to distance himself from Husserl as well as from Heidegger and Merleau-Ponty, since he considers this auto-affection of subjective life the business of a purely impressional consciousness without relation to any form of intentionality or transcendence. One may ask whether this does not lead to an ontological dualism opposing subject to world, placing Henry, doubtless despite himself, in the filiation of Sartrean thought. For his own part, far from opposing impressional and intentional consciousness, Bernet would willingly credit Husserl with the discovery of a self-consciousness having the form of an *impressional intentionality*. That phrase is Bernet's own coinage and is the Conclusion's central positive result.

If the examination of the pre-givenness of the world and of the pre-givenness of the Self brings to light above all an intentionality without object, it is not without consequences either for the egological character of the subject and therefore for the question of an intentionality without egological subject (p. 316).

#### The Non-Objectivating Intentionality of the Relation to the World

In Husserl the notion of a pre-given (*vorgegeben*) world certainly does not date from the introduction of a genetic phenomenology, but it is only within the latter that it acquired a proper transcendental status (pp. 316–317). For as long as the world was understood — within a so-called static phenomenology — as the correlate of an actual or even possible consciousness, it remained an object, even if a distinguished one because encompassing and total. The hypothesis of the annihilation of the world in §49 of *Ideen I* is the best example of such an assimilation of the experience of the world to the presumptive experience of a perceptual object. It does not suffice to say that every object of experience is surrounded by a horizon of possible objects, and then to affirm, as Husserl often did, that the world is the horizon of horizons, in order to be rid of the presupposition that this world still reveals itself in the manner of an object. It required that in *Ideen II* Husserl begin to interest himself in the particular essence of the world of cultural objects, the spiritual world, and that he realize the historical transmission of such a world, for the objectivist conception to be shaken. It is from this new interest in the humanized reality of the cultural world — soon called the life-world — that genetic phenomenology asserted itself as a *genealogy* interrogating not only the constitution of the sense of phenomena but the subsequent avatars of sense: its relations of filiation and kinship, its decline and the effects of wear. In interesting itself more in the history of sense and networks of sense than in the mute confrontation of the gaze with its objective *vis-à-vis*, Husserl's transcendental phenomenology progressively gave itself the means to face that "urgency of life" (*Lebensnot*) before which it had at first felt far more destitute than a mere world-view.

The world of cultural tradition remains for Husserl a constituted world, but its sense re-constitutes itself continually in the course of its historical transmission (p. 317). Husserl understood very quickly that such a mode of transmission was at work not only in the succession of generations but already in the course of an individual subject's life. Thus the transcendental sense of the passivity of habitualities revealed itself to him, and more particularly the non-objective presence of a sedimented acquisition of sense continuing to act on the subject's present life and on its anticipations of the future. This givenness of what one might call the transcendental subject's life-experience, and this givenness of the cultural world, precede all effective and actual constitution of an object by a subject; and one does scarcely any violence to Husserl's thought in affirming that this pre-given subjective know-how and this pre-given cultural world are at once constituted and constituting (pp. 317–318). Their presence for the subject differs from that of objects in that they are indeed "given" to it in the true sense of the word. The subject becomes the one for whom this gift of the pre-given is destined, and the intentionality inhabiting its life is defined by its capacity to receive such a gift and make fruitful use of it. The originary response demanded by this gift is therefore less an activity of constitution than a confident acceptance Merleau-Ponty will name perceptual faith. Contrary to objects, the world is absolutely certain, and this unshakeable certainty is presupposed in every attitude of doubt. Consequently the opening onto this new phenomenon of the world necessarily entails a putting in question of the *cogito* and therefore of a Cartesian conception of subjectivity. The first step toward taking non-objectivating intentionality into account within the relation to the life-world and within the pre-reflective relation to self is crossed as soon as the phenomenologist rids himself of his primary fascination for a phenomenon that would be nothing but the perceptual givenness of an actual object present-at-hand.

#### The Impressional Intentionality of the Relation to Self

Turning now to pre-reflective self-consciousness, we shall see that the same non-objectivating intentionality characterizing the subjective relation to the pre-given world is equally at work in the relation the subject maintains with itself (pp. 318–319). This analysis occupied an absolutely central place in the phenomenological theory of intentionality from Brentano on. It was Brentano who fixed the principal stake by making at the outset the distinction between an inner representation (*innere Vorstellung*) and an inner observation (*innere Beobachtung*) — between an inner consciousness that merely accompanies the perception of an object and an inner perception (*innere Wahrnehmung*) in which intentional consciousness becomes for itself an intentional object of reflection. For Brentano there can be no unconscious psychic phenomena: every intentional consciousness is accompanied by a pre-reflective inner consciousness, and this can in turn give rise to a reflective self-consciousness. This pre-reflective self-consciousness is said to be an intentional consciousness whose object remains *secondary*, subordinated to the appearing of the *primary* intentional object of a perception. What this secondary object lacks in order to become primary is an explicit affirmation — Brentano says a judgment — of its existence, which is why pre-reflective self-consciousness is still only a mere representation (*blosse Vorstellung*) and not an inner perception. This pre-reflective self-representation is nonetheless far from maintaining an indifferent relation to that self, and Brentano does not hesitate to affirm that it is habitually accompanied by an affective feeling of self.

To summarize Brentano: every reflection on self or inner perception of self and of one's own lived experiences is preceded by an intentional representation of self, where this self is presented and pre-sensed in the form of a secondary object (pp. 319–320). This originary self-manifestation is already animated by a representative and affective intentionality, but it remains subordinated to the appearing of a primary perceptual object, which is what interests the subject in the first place. (Nothing prevents that primary object from being itself already the object of an inner perception, so that the inner consciousness accompanying it is the unreflected consciousness of a reflective self-consciousness.) One finds practically the same conception in the *Logical Investigations* and *Ideen I*, Husserl's original contribution consisting above all in clarifying the relation between primary and secondary object: for him the pre-reflective relation to self belongs to a horizon-intentionality characterized by the absence of an explicitly thematized object. Rather than a secondary object, the pre-reflective self is for Husserl a *pre-object*, a datum awaiting to become the object of a reflective act. One cannot therefore truly speak of non-objectivating intentionality in its regard. Sartre in turn takes a further step by refining the distinction between the unreflected and non-egological "consciousness (of) self" and reflective "consciousness of self", but he does not contest that reflection makes the self an intentional object, even belatedly and out of the corner of the eye. He separates radically from Brentano and Husserl, however, in considering the "self" of the "consciousness (of) self" as nothing but an impulse toward the intentional object: while being for-itself, this self is thus nothing for itself and scarcely deserves its name.

It is not in Sartre but in Husserl himself that one can find the most convincing alternative to this Husserlian conception making the self of pre-reflective consciousness a mere object in waiting (p. 320). It sufficed that Husserl begin to interest himself in the nature of a consciousness incarnate in a sensible flesh, and in the way the elapsed flux of consciousness appears to itself within that very flux, for non-objectivating intentionality to be placed at the centre and at the origin of every relation to self.

##### The Self-Appearing of the Flesh

As regards the way the bodily subject appears to itself or, more exactly, feels itself as flesh (*Leib*), Husserl always occupies himself with this problem within the frame of his analysis of the perception of a spatial thing (pp. 320–321). This does not mean that my carnal being appears to me originarily in the manner of a spatial object, but rather that it is given to me conjointly with the appearing of such a thing. It is in perceiving a spatial thing that the subject becomes conscious of the pre-spatiality of its perceiving flesh; it is the appearing of the perceived thing that is the occasion of the pre-appearing of the flesh as organ of perception. Husserl attributes this self-feeling of the perceiving flesh to a particular type of sensation or impression he calls kinaesthetic. For nineteenth-century psychology (Bain), kinaesthetic sensations were muscular sensations caused by the movement of a limb. Husserl gives them a wholly different interpretation by considering them originary impressions of movement which, far from presupposing the representation of one's own body and of the thing, represent nothing but condition — motivate — the representation of every spatial object. The movement to which kinaesthetic sensations testify is therefore at the origin neither the movement of things nor even the movement of one's own body in space, but the *emotion* of a perceiving subject affected by its invisible flesh, that is, by its carnal being. Whoever has never experienced his carnal condition could never perceive anything as extended in space. But Husserl adds: whoever perceives nothing, that is, does not use his flesh to explore the world, will not have the occasion to feel himself a carnal being.

The perceiving subject is therefore a carnal subject, and to perceive is to set one's flesh in movement or at work (*fungierende Leiblichkeit*) without thereby making it an object of perception (pp. 321–322). Who sets the flesh to work and uses it as organ of the will? One must admit that for Husserl this will always remain a pure I able to command its flesh because its being is not exhausted in that incarnation. This does not prevent this egological intentionality using the flesh to perceive an object from remaining dependent on that flesh's proper functioning, nor the fact that this flesh, before being colonized by the perceiving subject's "I can", already functioned in its own manner. This proper functioning is evoked at length by Husserl under the heading of the constitution of sensory fields (*Sinnesfelder*) and of synaesthesias. These issue from links, above all associative, woven among sensations, and one is therefore tempted to use in their regard the paradoxical term "impressional or hyletic intentionality". The passive constitution of these sensory fields, which are like the extension of an invisible flesh traversed by the shiver of life, is moreover not the only example of a carnal intentionality Husserl evokes: other texts mention a drive-intentionality (*Triebintentionalität*) whose thrust is directed not toward an object but toward a goal. It is not forbidden to make the link with Freud's theory of the drives, and it is certain that this goal belongs to a sensible state of the flesh before referring to a worldly state of affairs.

Every perception of an extended thing would thus bring into play a double intentionality: the impressional intentionality circulating among sensations, and the objectivating intentionality putting this network of sensations in relation with worldly things (p. 322). To this double intentionality corresponds a double mode of manifestation: the impressional or affective manifestation of the flesh, and the perceptual manifestation of the thing. If the two forms do not go one without the other, there is no doubt that an originary consciousness of self is realized only within the impressional intentionality of the kinaesthetic sensations revealing to the subject the movement of its own carnal life. Objectivating intentionality, by contrast, wholly turned toward the spectacle of the world, is *forgetting* of the carnal Self (pp. 322–323). One must therefore say that the sensible revelation of the invisible flesh does not go without its withdrawal and covering-over in favour of the massive and visible presence of the things of the world. This does not prevent the two forms of appearing from being confused, nor the presence of the flesh in the form of an object of the world from being held originary. That transformation of the appearing of the invisible flesh into an in-itself presence of a thing of the world is the result of a secondary elaboration or taking in hand of the primary self-manifestation of the flesh: it objectivates the flesh (*Leib*) so as to make of it a flesh-body (*Leibkörper*) or even a body (*Körper*) of the physical world.

##### The Self-Appearing of the Flux of Consciousness

This sensation of one's own carnal being is not the only form of impressional self-manifestation examined by Husserl (p. 323). Since the subject's life is effected within a pre-temporal flux, it is by being impressionally affected by the incessant pulsation of that life that the subject appears to itself originarily. Even abstracting from the capital question of how carnal and temporal self-manifestation are linked to one another, one cannot but be struck by the fact that Husserl approaches both forms of the originary appearing of the subject's ipseity by strongly accentuating their resemblance: just as the objective spatiality of a thing can be perceived only by a carnal subject, so the perception of that thing's temporal characters is attributed to a subject whose life is indelibly marked by an inner temporality; just as the carnal being of the subject reveals itself only conjointly with the visible appearing of the worldly thing, so the self-manifestation of the flux of absolute consciousness is said to be inseparable from the appearing of temporal objects (pp. 323–324); and just as the originary self-manifestation of carnal being is accomplished within an impressional intentionality, so the elapsed life of the subject appears thanks to a retentional intentionality depending on a primal impression.

Faithful to the regressive style of his constitutive analysis, Husserl leads us in the *Lectures* from the objective time of natural events back to the pre-temporal flux of absolute consciousness by way of the immanent temporality of lived experiences (p. 324). At the centre stands the notion of the present, understood at once as what appears now and as the appearing of that now. For Husserl the appearing of the now of a lived experience is not itself a now, and is called primal impression. This is indeed an impressional form of the appearing of the now, but that does not prevent this primal impression from being inseparable from a temporal horizon of retentions and protentions referring intentionally to other primal impressions, already elapsed or still to come. Thanks to this horizon, the present of what appears and the present of the appearing itself are temporally extended and constitute a field of presence (*Präsenzfeld*). With each primal impression there appears not only a singular instant but a duration of a lived experience and also a quasi-duration of the appearing or of the "having" of that duration. Taking account of the fact that a primal impression is only an abstract moment of the flux — preceded and followed by other primal impressions within that same flux — it follows that each of these gives another temporal adumbration of that duration and of that quasi-duration (pp. 324–325). The flux thus makes the duration of immanent lived experiences and its own quasi-duration appear conjointly. In Husserl's terms: the flux is animated by a double impressional intentionality, a transverse intentionality aiming at the temporality of lived experiences and a longitudinal intentionality aiming at the flux itself.

Bernet will not enter into a more precise description of how this double intentionality is accomplished, nor try to understand why Husserl treated it only within the frame of retention (p. 325). What matters exclusively is that the longitudinal intentionality of retention, which from a primal impression recapitulates the phases of the flux in their elapsed quasi-succession, indeed represents an originary manifestation of the temporal being of the subject. As in the case of carnal being, this temporal being reveals itself here again within an experience of movement or change. This self-manifestation of a non-egological and fluctuating Self — while being the work of a non-objectivating and impressional intentionality — is nonetheless not free of all attachment to another form of intentionality which, through the appearing of intentional lived experiences, aims at and represents genuine objects. It turns out once more that impressionally-intentional self-appearing does not exhaust being-self, since to be a self is also to be animated by an objectivating intentionality that makes the things of the world appear.

### Conclusion

We must therefore concede that the inquiry has not corroborated the hypothesis of "an intentionality without subject" (p. 326). We have rather been led to revise our conception both of the egological subject and of objectivating intentionality. But we have effectively succeeded in bringing to light in Husserl the existence of "an intentionality without object". This is at work both in the subject's relation to a pre-given world and in the impressionally-intentional relation of the subject to itself. This intentionality of the impressional relation to self, far from being an objectivating intentionality in the service of a pure ego conceived as source of life, has appeared rather as an originary manifestation of the very life of the subject, that is, of its carnal and temporal existence. This life does not, however, confine itself to being the impressional self-manifestation of itself, since its intentionality carries it also, and at the same time, toward the objects of the world. A life that coincided totally with its self-manifestation would scarcely still have the right to call itself subjective. We have observed how subjective life is animated by a double intentionality making it appear to itself while making worldly objects appear or while being affected by them. *For Husserl, to be a subject means to be divided* — to be the subject of a double intentionality and a double affection. It follows that the subject appears to itself as transcending itself toward things and therefore also in its difference from the things of the world. The title-question is thus answered by halves: without object, yes; without subject, no.

Henry was therefore right to stress the impressional or affective, that is, invisible character of the self-manifestation of subjective life and to make of it a mode of appearing *sui generis* (pp. 326–327). But he was doubtless not attentive enough to the fact that this originary self-manifestation is still realized thanks to an intentionality *sui generis* that is not incompatible with the impressional character of that self-manifestation. Bernet also believes — with Husserl — that Henry is wrong to consider this self-manifestation more originary than the manifestation of worldly things and as constituting in some way the very essence of manifestation. It has turned out not only that the two modes of appearing necessarily go together, but also that the subject does not coincide with the self-manifestation of its life.

The Husserlian conception of the divided subject of intentionality defended here has found a more favourable echo in Levinas (p. 327). In insisting on the ontological difference between the existent and existence, Levinas showed how its own existence appears to the existent precisely insofar as the latter differentiates itself from it. It is in experiencing its existence as a *charge* — as what it "has" to bear as much as what it "is" — that the existent is affected by the appearing of its existence. This setting at a distance of its own existence is also what permits the existent to open itself to the world and to the Other. This does not mean, of course, that the subject can establish itself as impartial spectator of its life or of the life of others: if it does not coincide with its life, it cannot escape from it to the point of making it a worldly object either. The subject's relation to (the appearing of) its life is therefore a supplementary figure of that "transcendence within immanence" which does indeed seem to be the key to the Husserlian theory of intentionality. The book thus closes on the formula it opened with — and on the impartial spectator of the Introduction, now refused to the subject in its relation to its own life.
